# Everything A Sound Stage Needs

Component by component, for the four stages at Sugar Land: Askja at 6,000 m² and 30 m clear, Lón at 3,200 m² with a 2,400 m² tank, and the paired 2,000 m² rooms.

Every figure here either cites the operator, standard or study it came from, or is marked as an estimate. Where a number is an estimate it says so in its own line — the distinction is the point, because a specification that cannot tell you which of its numbers are load-bearing is a wish list.

## What is in here

11 domains, 891 items. Each item says what the thing is, what it has to do, why it matters, and — where it differs — what Iceland does to it. Items are tiered: **Essential** is the thing not working without it, **Expected** is what a professional operator has, **State of the art** is what puts a facility ahead of the ones it competes with.

### The domains

1. **Structure and building envelope** — 69 items (34 essential, 24 expected, 11 state of the art)
2. **Acoustic isolation and room acoustics** — 62 items (42 essential, 12 expected, 8 state of the art)
3. **Electrical supply and distribution** — 75 items (27 essential, 34 expected, 14 state of the art)
4. **Climate, air and smoke** — 65 items (42 essential, 14 expected, 9 state of the art)
5. **Rigging, grid and overhead** — 63 items (22 essential, 19 expected, 22 state of the art)
6. **Sound stage floor and substructure** — 63 items (23 essential, 18 expected, 22 state of the art)
7. **Doors, access and logistics** — 61 items (23 essential, 30 expected, 8 state of the art)
8. **Water, tanks and underwater work** — 74 items (43 essential, 21 expected, 10 state of the art)
9. **Virtual production and LED volume** — 60 items (33 essential, 22 expected, 5 state of the art)
10. **Sound stage complex** — 154 items (86 essential, 44 expected, 24 state of the art)
11. **Fire, safety, compliance and operations** — 145 items (97 essential, 33 expected, 15 state of the art)

---

## 1. Structure and building envelope

*Askja (6,000 m², 100 × 60 m, 30 m clear, 180,000 m³), Lon (2,400 m² tank stage) and the paired 2,000 m² stages, Sugar Land Studios, Mosfellsbær, Iceland*

### Essential

#### Ground investigation and site characterisation

Rotary-cored boreholes ≥5 m into sound rock, min. 1 per 900 m² plus 1 per column line change; CPTu in soft ground; MASW/downhole for Vs,30 to set EN 1998-1 ground type A–E; EN 1997-2, ISO 22475-1, ISO 22476-1/-2. Water chemistry and sulfate for EN 206 XA class.

**Why.** Without Vs,30 you cannot pick the EN 1998-1 response spectrum; without rockhead contours you cannot price piling or blasting, the two biggest foundation cost risks.

**Here.** Capital-area ground is post-glacial basalt lava over till with pockets of soft marine clay and peat in low ground; rockhead can step several metres between adjacent bays. Expect scoria and lava tubes — voids that swallow concrete.

#### Site-specific seismic hazard and response study

PSHA to 475-year and 2,475-year return; ÍST EN 1998-1:2004/NA:2010 hazard map gives agR from 0.10 g to 0.50 g across Iceland on Type A ground (475-yr mean return). Capital region taken as agR ≈ 0.10–0.15 g (ESTIMATE — confirm against the NA zone map for the exact site).

**Why.** The Icelandic NA spans a 5× range in agR. Guessing the zone either overbuilds the frame or leaves the stage under-designed against a South Iceland Seismic Zone event.

**Here.** Mosfellsbær sits between the Reykjanes oblique rift and the SISZ; the 2000 and 2008 Suðurland M6.3–6.5 events and the 2021–2024 Reykjanes sequence are the design analogues. Near-fault pulse effects should be screened, EN 1998-1 §3.2.2.3.

#### Foundations — pad and strip footings to rock

Mass or reinforced pads to sound basalt, allowable bearing 1,500–3,000 kPa on unweathered lava (ESTIMATE, confirm by testing); EN 1997-1 design approach as set by the Icelandic NA; blinding and levelling concrete to bridge irregular rockhead.

**Why.** A 60 m truss delivers 2,000–4,000 kN per column base plus large uplift; the footing must carry down-load, net uplift and horizontal shear at once.

**Here.** Rock is often close to the surface, which is a gift — but the irregular lava surface means every pad is a bespoke depth and dig-and-pour rates are unpredictable.

#### Grade beams and seismic foundation tie system

Continuous tie beams or a tied slab connecting all pad foundations, sized for the axial force in EN 1998-5 §5.4.1.2 (≈0.3–0.6 · agS · NEd depending on ground type). Reinforced concrete, min. 500 × 800 mm (ESTIMATE).

**Why.** Eurocode 8 requires foundations on non-rock ground to act as one body; untied pads move differentially in an earthquake and shear the column bases off.

**Here.** Mandatory on the mixed lava/clay profile typical of the capital region. Only omittable where every pad sits on demonstrably continuous rock (EN 1998-5 §5.4.1.2(2)).

#### Frost protection to foundations

Foundation depth or horizontal/vertical wing insulation to EN ISO 13793 (frost-protected shallow foundations); design frost penetration for the capital region 0.8–1.5 m (ESTIMATE — confirm against Icelandic practice and Byggingarreglugerð 112/2012).

**Why.** Frost heave under a stage slab lifts the floor and cracks it; a stage floor is a precision surface, not a warehouse slab.

**Here.** Free geothermal district heat makes a heated sub-slab or heated perimeter trench cheap to run, which is the neat Icelandic answer: heat the ground instead of digging deeper. Reykir in Mosfellsbær has supplied the capital's hot water since 1943.

#### Sub-slab drainage and groundwater control

Granular drainage blanket ≥300 mm with perforated land drains to EN 1610, geotextile separation to EN 13252, gravity discharge to attenuation; no reliance on pumping under the stage floor.

**Why.** Hydrostatic pressure under a slab lifts it and forces water through joints; a wet stage floor is unusable and a shock hazard around 24 kW HMIs.

**Here.** Heavy driven rain and rapid snowmelt give high, flashy groundwater; lava is highly permeable so infiltration rates are enormous but flow paths are unpredictable.

#### Damp-proof and gas membrane under slab

Reinforced LDPE or bituminous membrane to EN 13967, ≥0.5 mm, taped and welded laps, protected both faces; radon barrier not required.

**Why.** Ground moisture into a stage floor destroys sprung dance floors, sub-floor cabling and any wood set built directly on the deck.

**Here.** Radon risk in Iceland is negligible — young basalt is uranium-poor — so the membrane is a damp and gas barrier only, not a radon system. That is a genuine saving over a UK or Nordic spec.

#### Stage floor slab

Ground-bearing reinforced or steel-fibre concrete, 250–350 mm on rock/engineered fill; design UDL 20 kN/m² plus 100 kN axle and 60 kN scissor-lift outrigger point loads (owner-set; ESTIMATE); jointless or sawn-joint layout to Concrete Society TR34 / EN 1992-1-1.

**Why.** Set builds, telehandlers, tracked cranes, water tanks and Technocranes all land on this slab. Cracks and joints telegraph through painted floors and show on camera.

**Here.** Concrete must be air-entrained and ASR-mitigated (see mix design); cold-weather curing to EN 13670 with heated enclosures is normal for a Mosfellsbær winter pour.

#### Concrete mix design — ASR and freeze-thaw

EN 206 exposure XC4/XF4/XS1 as applicable; silica-fume-blended cement for alkali-silica reaction control; air entrainment 4–6% for XF; w/c ≤0.45; aggregate reactivity screening to ASTM C1260/C1293 or RILEM AAR-2.

**Why.** ASR is a slow structural cancer with no cure once cast; freeze-thaw scaling ruins external concrete within a decade in this climate.

**Here.** Iceland's defining concrete problem. Gudmundsson and Ólafsson (Cement and Concrete Research, 1999) record serious ASR damage in Icelandic housing 1961–1979 from rhyolitic and altered-basalt aggregate, unwashed sea-dredged sand and cement at ~1.65 wt% Na₂O-equivalent alkali; since 1979 silica fume has been blended into all Icelandic cement. Wash sea-dredged aggregate, test every source.

#### Reinforcement steel and ductility class

EN 10080 ribbed bar, B500B or B500C; EN 1998-1 requires ductility Class B or C in dissipative zones (Class C for DCH); couplers to ISO 15835; cover to EN 1992-1-1 for the relevant exposure class, ≥40 mm externally.

**Why.** Class A mesh in a seismic zone fractures without warning; the whole capacity-design logic of Eurocode 8 assumes bar elongation the steel actually has.

**Here.** All reinforcement is imported; specify the ductility class at order stage because substitution at the mill is the common failure.

#### Holding-down bolt assemblies and cast-in anchors

EN 1992-4 design for cast-in anchors; hot-dip galvanised or stainless grade A4 anchor assemblies; oversize cones/pockets with 75 mm adjustment; anchor plates and shear keys sized for base shear plus uplift; grouting to EN 1504-6.

**Why.** Column bases in a wind-dominated tall shed carry large uplift and shear simultaneously — the anchor is the most common weak link in a big shed's load path.

**Here.** Uplift, not down-load, governs — a 36 m/s basic wind and a 40 m tall face put column bases into net tension in the corner bays.

#### Primary long-span roof structure — 60 m clear-span trusses

Welded or bolted steel lattice trusses spanning 60 m at 10 m centres (10 bays over 100 m); truss depth span/10–span/12 = 5.0–6.0 m; pre-camber for dead load plus 50% of the rigging allowance; steel tonnage 90–140 kg/m² of roof (ESTIMATE) ≈ 650–850 t for Askja alone.

**Why.** The column-free span is the whole point of a sound stage; there is no acceptable internal column and no acceptable compromise on the clear height.

**Here.** Every tonne is imported — through Sundahöfn or Grundartangi — so pieces are sized to ship and bolt on site, not to weld. Splice for a 30 m maximum transport length, and design connections a small local erection crew can complete quickly between weather windows.

#### Columns and lateral stability system

Moment-resisting portal action in the span direction or braced bays; column base fixity chosen deliberately (fixed bases cut sway but load the foundations); behaviour factor q per EN 1998-1 Table 6.2 — q = 4 for a DCM moment frame, q = 4 for concentric bracing with diagonals in tension.

**Why.** Sway limits, not strength, usually govern a tall shed: the acoustic linings, the door seals and the cladding all fail before the steel does.

**Here.** Wind and seismic base shears are of the same order here — roughly 10–15 MN each (ESTIMATE) — so neither can be treated as the obvious governing case; both must be run with all EN 1990 combinations.

#### Roof plan bracing and diaphragm action

Horizontal wind girders at eaves and at truss chord level; either a braced plan bracing system or a designed metal-deck diaphragm to EN 1993-1-3; diaphragm fastener pattern designed, not assumed.

**Why.** Without a working roof diaphragm the wind load on a 100 m wall has no path to the braced bays and the gable posts fail.

#### Perms — permanent rigging grid

Steel grid at 30 m to underside, on a 3 m × 3 m or 4 m × 4 m module; distributed design load 0.75–1.0 kN/m² over the whole grid (≈15–20 psf) plus 500 kg (5 kN) per pick point at any node, non-concurrent; US theatrical gridiron practice historically used 250 psf (12 kPa) locally.

**Why.** The perms are the product. Under-rate them and every heavy rig needs ground-supported truss, which eats floor area and shooting days.

**Here.** Design so a small crew can rig from above without external scaffold — the weather rarely allows extended external work at 30 m.

#### Steel grade and low-temperature fracture toughness

S355 J2 or K2 to EN 10025-2 as a minimum; sub-grade selected per EN 1993-1-10 Table 2.1 using a reference temperature that accounts for a −25 °C service minimum and full plate thickness; through-thickness properties to EN 10164 Z25/Z35 at heavily restrained welded nodes.

**Why.** Brittle fracture in a thick welded node at −20 °C is a sudden, total failure of a member carrying a 60 m span.

**Here.** Reykjavík's record low is −24.5 °C (Veðurstofa Íslands). The combination of low service temperature, thick plate and restrained welded joints is precisely the EN 1993-1-10 scenario; do not default to JR.

#### Robustness and disproportionate collapse strategy

EN 1990 consequence class CC3, reliability class RC3, KFI = 1.1; EN 1991-1-7 Consequence Class 3 requires a systematic risk assessment; horizontal and vertical tying, key element design at 34 kN/m² accidental pressure, or notional member removal analysis.

**Why.** A single truss loss over a 6,000 m² floor with 300 crew below is a mass-casualty event; CC3 forces a designed answer rather than an assumed one.

**Here.** CC3 is justified by occupancy and by span, and it also sets the seismic importance class — EN 1998-1 importance class III with γI = 1.2 is the defensible choice for a stage holding hundreds of people.

#### Expansion and movement joints

EN 1991-1-5 thermal actions; steel uniform temperature component roughly −25 °C to +55 °C (dark membrane in summer sun) → ΔT ≈ 80 K → free movement 12×10⁻⁶ × 100 m × 80 K ≈ 96 mm over the length. Either a full movement joint at mid-length or a continuous frame designed for the restrained force.

**Why.** 96 mm of unaccommodated movement tears cladding, breaks acoustic seals and cracks the slab — and a movement joint through a stage is an acoustic hole.

**Here.** Iceland's summer air is cool but the sub-arctic sun sits low and long in June, and a dark membrane still reaches +55 °C. The winter minimum is the harder end. A continuous, jointless frame with designed restraint forces is usually preferred over cutting an acoustic joint through the envelope.

#### Roof deck — composite metal deck with structural concrete topping

Deep composite steel deck (e.g. 80–100 mm profile) to EN 1994-1-1, with 130–150 mm normal-weight concrete topping; combined ≈300–350 kg/m²; through-deck shear studs; deck to EN 1993-1-3 acting as the roof diaphragm.

**Why.** A profiled-steel-only roof is acoustically transparent to rain and transmits aircraft and wind noise straight to the stage floor.

**Here.** This is the single most important Icelandic decision in the package. Rain drumming on a long-span roof is the worst acoustic problem a big stage has, and Iceland delivers heavy driven rain constantly. Mass is the only reliable answer.

#### Air and vapour control layer

Continuous self-adhered or torch-applied vapour control layer, sd ≥100 m, over the deck and continuous with the wall air barrier; interstitial condensation assessed to EN ISO 13788 (Glaser) and, for the marginal cases, hygrothermal simulation to EN 15026.

**Why.** The air barrier is also the acoustic seal and the wind-infiltration barrier; a discontinuity at the wall/roof junction leaks air, sound and heat at once.

**Here.** Cold, wet, high-wind climate with a warm humid interior (300 crew, lighting load, water tanks on Lon) gives a strong vapour drive outward — get the layer order wrong and the roof deck rots from the inside.

#### Roof waterproofing membrane

Single-ply or two-layer bituminous system to EN 13956/EN 13707; mechanically fastened or fully adhered — not loose-laid ballast unless the ballast is designed as uplift restraint; falls ≥1:60 to outlets; BBA/ETA certificate with a ≥30-year durability declaration; welded seams tested.

**Why.** Water ingress into a live stage stops the shoot and can destroy hundreds of thousands of euros of set and electrics in an afternoon.

**Here.** Mechanically fastened membranes in this wind regime need much denser fastening patterns than mainland Europe, and the fastener rows are also thermal and acoustic bridges through the build-up. A fully adhered system over a concrete topping is the safer answer.

#### Wind uplift resistance of the roof assembly

Assembly tested to EN 16002 (mechanically fastened) or ETAG 006/EAD 030350; FM 4470 with FM 1-28/1-29 wind ratings as a parallel benchmark; corner and edge zone fastener densification per EN 1991-1-4 zone F/G/H mapping; peak local uplift in corner zones roughly 6–7 kPa (see the wind calculation below).

**Why.** Roofs do not fail in the field; they fail at a corner, peel, and then the whole sheet goes. Zone F fixing density is the entire game.

**Here.** 6–7 kPa of local uplift is ~700 kg/m² — more than the weight of the entire build-up. There is a happy synergy: the heavy acoustic roof needed for rain noise is also the roof that resists Icelandic uplift.

#### Gutters, outlets, overflows and trace heating

Internal or perimeter gutters designed to EN 12056-3 with a minimum freeboard; independent overflow weirs or scuppers sized to discharge the full design flow with all primary outlets blocked; electric or geothermal trace heating in gutters, outlets and the first 3 m of every downpipe.

**Why.** A blocked outlet turns a flat roof into a 6,000 m² tank; 100 mm of standing water is 100 kg/m² the roof was never designed for.

**Here.** Freeze-thaw blockage is the realistic failure mode, not leaf litter. Geothermal-heated gutter and outlet trace, running off district heat return water, is the cheap Icelandic answer and avoids the electrical load.

#### Snow load design and drift management

ÍST EN 1991-1-3:2003/NA:2010, five Icelandic zones; Zone 1 including Reykjavík sk ≈ 1.0 kN/m² (attributed to an Icelandic engineering summary of the NA — confirm against the NA itself). Flat-roof μ1 = 0.8 → s ≈ 0.8 kN/m². Drift against a taller adjacent stage per EN 1991-1-3 Annex B: μ up to 8, giving up to 8.0 kN/m² over a band ls up to 15 m.

**Why.** The uniform snow load on an Icelandic roof is modest; the drift load against a 40 m tall neighbouring stage is eight times larger and is what actually collapses long-span roofs.

**Here.** With four stages of very different heights on one lot, drift is the governing snow case, not uniform fall. Wind here redistributes snow aggressively — Iceland scours flat roofs and dumps everything in the lee, so drift factors should be treated as real, not conservative.

#### Wind load design basis

ÍST EN 1991-1-4:2005/NA:2010. Fundamental basic wind velocity vb,0 = 36 m/s is the figure shown by Dlubal's Geo-Zone Tool for the Icelandic NA (confirm the site's zone value). qb = 0.5 × 1.25 × 36² = 810 Pa. Terrain category II. At z = 40 m, ce ≈ 3.1 → qp ≈ 2.5 kPa. Net lateral face load ≈ 3.5 kPa; base shear on the 100 × 40 m face ≈ 14 MN.

**Why.** Wind, not snow or seismic, sizes the frame, the cladding, the doors and the fixings. Every envelope component must be specified against qp, not against a generic 'high wind' note.

**Here.** Iceland is among the windiest inhabited places on earth. Veðurstofa Íslands records a 10-minute mean of 62.5 m/s at Skálafell (20 Jan 1998) and a gust of 74.5 m/s at Gagnheiði (16 Jan 1995), with 63.9 m/s mean and 76.5 m/s gust at Skálafell on 17 Feb 2003. Also: cold Icelandic air is denser (ρ ≈ 1.29 kg/m³ at 0 °C vs the 1.25 default), adding ~3% to every pressure.

#### External wall construction — mass wall

Precast concrete panels (200–250 mm, ≈500–600 kg/m²) or twin-skin composite with a mass inner leaf; connections designed for out-of-plane wind at qp ≈ 2.5 kPa and for seismic panel accelerations per EN 1998-1 §4.3.5; movement/rotation-tolerant fixings.

**Why.** The wall carries the same acoustic duty as the roof. A lightweight cladding on a shed frame will not deliver an outdoor-to-indoor difference of 65–75 dB.

**Here.** Precast in Iceland means either a local yard (limited capacity, small labour pool) or shipped panels (heavy, expensive freight). Either way the panel schedule, not the steel, is likely the critical path.

#### Weather sealing, joints and driven-rain performance

Two-stage drained and pressure-equalised joints, not face-sealed silicone; sealants to EN ISO 11600 class 25LM with ≥25% movement accommodation; laboratory watertightness to EN 12155 or EN 1027 at a test pressure of ≥1,200 Pa (curtain walling class RE1200 to EN 12154).

**Why.** Face-sealed joints fail in a decade; at these wind pressures water is driven uphill through any single-line defence.

**Here.** Standard mainland test pressures are not adequate. Specify watertightness testing at a pressure derived from the actual qp — 1,200 Pa or more — and require site hose testing to AAMA 501.2 / EN 13051 on completed samples.

#### Elephant doors

Two per stage minimum; clear opening 12 m wide × 10 m high (owner-set); acoustically rated sliding leaves with compression seals and drop seals, target Rw ≥50 dB; leaves designed for qp ≈ 2.5 kPa on a 120 m² face = 300 kN total; motorised with manual override; EN 13241 / EN 12604 / EN 12453 for power-operated doors.

**Why.** Sets, vehicles and 40 ft trailers come in through here. The door is simultaneously the largest structural panel, the weakest acoustic element and the biggest air-leakage path.

**Here.** Wind loading on a 120 m² leaf is severe and the operating case matters as much as the closed case — specify a maximum safe operating wind speed and an anemometer interlock, or the door will be destroyed on the day someone opens it in a gale.

#### Personnel doors and acoustic sound locks

Paired acoustic doors forming a lobby at every personnel entry, each leaf Rw ≥45 dB with magnetic and drop seals, interlocked or clearly signed; EN 14351-1 for external doorsets, weather classes to EN 12207 (air, class 4), EN 12208 (water, class 9A) and EN 12210 (wind, class C5).

**Why.** A single door is an acoustic hole and a pressure leak; a sound lock is the only construction that works while people keep using it.

**Here.** Doors here must also survive pressure differences that slam them; specify closers and restraints rated for the wind, and a wind lobby on the exposed elevations so people can actually get in.

#### Earthing and equipotential bonding

Foundation earth electrode (ring conductor cast into the perimeter footing) to IEC 62305-3 Annex E and IEC 60364-5-54; all structural steel, perms, rails, tank steel and door tracks bonded; target earth resistance <1 Ω for the technical earth given the film power loads.

**Why.** A stage is a metal cage full of people, water and 400 A three-phase distribution. The earth is a life-safety system, not a lightning afterthought.

**Here.** Basalt bedrock is a poor conductor and can give very high soil resistivity; achieving a low earth resistance may need an extended ring, deep-driven rods into groundwater, or conductive backfill. Test, do not assume.

#### Fall arrest and edge protection

Permanent parapet ≥1.1 m high (preferred over any line system), or horizontal lifeline anchor devices to EN 795 Class C with energy absorbers, tested and certified annually to EN 365; anchor points to EN 795 Class A at every plant island; rescue plan and equipment on site.

**Why.** A collective measure (parapet) protects everyone including untrained visitors; a lifeline only protects the person who clipped on.

**Here.** Wind makes line-based systems and rope access unusable for much of the year — the parapet is the only measure that works in a gale, and a 1.1 m parapet also helps with wind uplift at the corner zones.

#### Lon water tank structure

In-floor tank, 2,400 m² with a 20 × 20 m dive well at 6 m depth. Watertight reinforced concrete to EN 1992-3 (liquid-retaining), crack width limit wk ≤0.2 mm (tightness class 1) or ≤0.05 mm for class 2; water-stops at all construction joints; hydrostatic pressure at 6 m = 59 kPa on the well walls.

**Why.** A leaking tank under a stage floor undermines the slab and cannot be repaired without emptying and closing the stage.

**Here.** Freeze-thaw at the waterline is severe; specify XF4 concrete there. Geothermal heat makes tank heating trivially cheap — a real Icelandic advantage for underwater work, which elsewhere is a large energy cost.

#### Massing, ridge height and planning envelope

Askja: 30 m to underside of perms + 5.5 m truss depth + 0.7 m build-up + 1.2 m parapet ≈ 37.4 m to parapet top; rooftop plant screen adds ~4 m → ~41 m overall. Footprint 100 × 60 m. Four stages give a stepped massing from roughly 25 m to 41 m.

**Why.** A 41 m windowless box is the largest object for kilometres; planning consent, and the wind interaction between the four boxes, both turn on the massing.

**Here.** Mosfellsbær is a low-rise town in an open valley — the visual impact case is significant and the planning process (skipulag) is local and slow. Also: the boxes shelter and channel wind onto each other, which is why the wind tunnel model must include all four and the surrounding terrain.

#### Erection methodology and temporary works

EN 1991-1-6 actions during execution; a stated maximum permissible wind speed for lifting (typically 9–12 m/s for crane operations, per the crane's own chart); temporary bracing designed for the partial-completion cases; large ground cranes or a strand-jacked ground-assembly-and-lift methodology for the trusses.

**Why.** A half-braced 60 m truss is far weaker than the finished frame and sees the same wind.

**Here.** This is the schedule killer. Wind above the lifting threshold is common for large parts of the year, and December daylight in Mosfellsbær is roughly four hours. Ground-assemble trusses and lift complete units in rare windows; build in float, and expect a two-season programme where a mainland site would take one.

### Expected

#### Rock excavation, blasting and vibration control

Controlled blasting or hydraulic breaking of basalt; peak particle velocity limits to DIN 4150-3 / BS 7385-2, typically ≤5 mm/s at sensitive neighbours; pre- and post-blast condition surveys; continuous PPV monitoring.

**Why.** Basalt at shallow depth cannot be dug. Uncontrolled blasting cracks neighbouring structures and, once other stages are live, stops shooting on the lot.

**Here.** Icelandic lava is hard, fractured and abrasive — drill-and-blast is routine and contractors are experienced, but the small labour pool means one specialist crew and a queue.

#### Piled foundations where rock is deep or ground is soft

Driven precast or steel tube piles to EN 12699, or bored piles to EN 1536; capacity verified by static load test to EN ISO 22477-1 (min. 1% of piles, ≥2 tests) plus dynamic testing and low-strain integrity testing on 100%.

**Why.** Differential settlement across a 100 m stage tilts the perms and throws the rigging grid out of level, which the grips will notice before the engineers do.

**Here.** Driving through lava crust into soft clay below is a classic Icelandic refusal/punch-through problem — pre-drilling through the lava cap is normally required.

#### Stage floor flatness and levelness specification

Flatness/levelness to TR34 FM1 (or ACI 117 FF35/FL25 equivalent); global level tolerance ±10 mm across 100 m; local 3 m straightedge deviation ≤3 mm; power-float, laser screed, then grind to tolerance.

**Why.** Dollies, camera track and Steadicam read every ripple. A stage floor out of flat is re-levelled by the production with plywood and shims, at the studio's reputational cost.

#### Settlement and movement monitoring network

Permanent precise-levelling sockets on every column base and at slab grid nodes; baseline survey at handover, then 3/6/12/24-month readings; alert threshold 10 mm total, 1/500 differential between adjacent columns (ESTIMATE).

**Why.** Differential settlement shows first as a perms grid out of level and doors that stop sealing — both silent until they are expensive.

#### Catwalks, greenbeds and access galleries

Perimeter and cross catwalks at 900 mm clear width, guardrails to EN ISO 14122-3, toe boards, imposed load 3.0 kN/m² or 1.5 kN point; open galvanised grating or solid plate where drop hazards over the set exist.

**Why.** Lamps get focused from the perms by people. No safe access means every note from the DP becomes a scissor-lift move on the floor.

#### Bolted connections — preloaded, slip-resistant

HV or HR assemblies to EN 14399-3/-4 with washers to EN 14399-5/-6; Category C slip-resistant at ULS for connections in the seismic load path; faying surfaces prepared to a specified slip factor (Class A, μ = 0.50) and verified per EN 1090-2 Annex G.

**Why.** Bearing-type bolts slip under cyclic seismic and wind load; slip in a 60 m truss chord shows as permanent grid movement and loosening over years.

**Here.** Preloading in wind and cold on site is error-prone — specify DTI washers or calibrated torque with 100% inspection records, because rework at 30 m in Mosfellsbær weather is brutal.

#### Execution class, fabrication tolerances and CE marking

EXC3 minimum, EXC4 for the primary long-span trusses and base-isolated elements; EN 1090-2 for execution, EN 1090-1 for factory production control and CE marking; welding to EN ISO 3834-2; NDT extent per EN 1090-2 Table 24; erection tolerances per EN 1090-2 Annex; setting-out to ISO 4463.

**Why.** EXC4 buys full NDT and traceability on the members whose failure closes the stage. It is the cheapest insurance in the package.

**Here.** Fabrication will be off-island (Poland, Baltics, Spain are the usual sources); factory inspection and shipment sequencing matter more than site supervision.

#### Corrosion protection system

EN ISO 12944-2 corrosivity category C5-M (marine) for external steel, C3 internal; hot-dip galvanising to EN ISO 1461 (85 µm) plus a duplex paint system to EN ISO 12944-5 for a 'very high' (VH, >25 yr) durability range; stainless A4/316 fixings at the envelope.

**Why.** Rust on a 30 m truss cannot be repainted without a shooting shutdown; specify for life, not for handover.

**Here.** Iceland is coastal, salt-laden and windy, and volcanic events add sulphurous, acidic and chloride-rich fallout — the corrosivity case is closer to CX than C5 at the envelope. Aluminium and galvanised steel are attacked by acidic ash leachate; wash-down after ashfall is a maintenance requirement, not an option.

#### Fatigue and wind-induced vibration check

EN 1993-1-9 fatigue assessment of welded details in the roof bracing, cladding rails and any slender external elements; vortex-shedding and galloping checks to EN 1991-1-4 Annex E for masts, stacks, ladders and free-standing screens.

**Why.** Iceland delivers gale-force wind for a large fraction of the year; details that would never fatigue elsewhere accumulate millions of cycles here.

**Here.** This is the specifically Icelandic structural check that gets skipped. High mean wind speeds sustained over decades put ordinary cladding fixings and secondary steel into a fatigue regime.

#### Structural fire protection

Fire resistance period per Byggingarreglugerð nr. 112/2012 (Iceland's building regulation) and EN 1993-1-2; intumescent coating or board to the required section factor; EN 13501-2 classification; fire engineering with FDS/CFD to justify reduced periods given the volume and the smoke reservoir.

**Why.** A 180,000 m³ volume with a 30 m ceiling is a huge smoke reservoir; a code-literal 60- or 90-minute spray on every member is both expensive and probably unnecessary once modelled.

**Here.** Byggingarreglugerð 112/2012 governs, with ÍST-adopted Eurocodes as the technical basis. Icelandic fire authorities accept performance-based design but the review capacity is small — allow programme time.

#### Roof thermal insulation

Rigid mineral wool (EN 13162) or PIR (EN 13165) in two layers with staggered joints; U-value ≤0.15 W/m²K (owner target, better than the maxima tabulated in Byggingarreglugerð 112/2012 tafla 13.01 — ESTIMATE of the regulation's figure, confirm current amendment); compressive strength ≥80 kPa under trafficked areas.

**Why.** Insulation here is bought for condensation control, acoustic damping and comfort more than for fuel cost.

**Here.** The Icelandic economics invert the usual argument: geothermal district heat is close to free, so the payback case for deep insulation is weak — but the condensation and acoustic cases still hold. Mineral wool is preferred over PIR partly for the added mass and fire performance.

#### Roof drainage — siphonic system

Siphonic roof drainage to BS 8490 / EN 12056-3; design rainfall intensity to the Icelandic NA of EN 12056-3 (ESTIMATE: 0.030–0.050 l/s·m² for the design event — confirm from Veðurstofa Íslands IDF data); pipework and outlets sized for full-bore siphonic operation; separate emergency overflow system.

**Why.** Gravity drainage on a 6,000 m² roof needs enormous downpipes that must land somewhere; siphonic clears the roof with far fewer, smaller stacks and no falls inside the stage.

**Here.** Iceland's design event is not a short intense convective storm but prolonged heavy driven rain, often with meltwater. Size for duration and for simultaneous snowmelt, and note that outlets can be blocked by ice.

#### Snow retention and roof snow management

No snow guards on a flat roof; instead design for the drift, provide safe access for mechanical clearance, and set an operational trigger depth. Where any pitched or curved surface exists, snow fences to EN 516 / EN 1991-1-3 §6.2 sliding-snow provisions.

**Why.** Snow sliding off a 40 m high roof onto a loading yard is lethal.

**Here.** An operational snow-clearance procedure with a load-monitoring trigger is worth more here than hardware; wet Icelandic snow can approach 400 kg/m³.

#### Airtightness performance and testing

Envelope air permeability q50 ≤ 1.0 m³/(h·m²) at 50 Pa, tested to EN ISO 9972 method 1; envelope area for Askja ≈ 21,600 m² (6,000 roof + 9,600 walls + 6,000 floor) → 21,600 m³/h leakage, i.e. n50 ≈ 0.12 h⁻¹.

**Why.** Air leakage is sound leakage, and in a persistent 20 m/s wind it is also an unmanageable infiltration load that no air handling plant can hold against.

**Here.** Use q50 (per m² of envelope), not n50 (per volume). For a 180,000 m³ box, n50 flatters the result absurdly — 0.12 h⁻¹ sounds Passivhaus-grade while the actual leakage is 21,600 m³/h. Also: testing a volume this large needs a fan array and a calm day, which in Mosfellsbær must be scheduled, not assumed.

#### Thermal bridging and condensation risk analysis

Linear thermal transmittance ψ calculated to EN ISO 10211 for every junction; ground heat flow to EN ISO 13370; surface temperature factor fRsi ≥0.75 (or ≥0.90 at wet areas) to avoid mould and surface condensation; thermographic survey to EN 13187 at handover.

**Why.** Cold spots at the wall/roof junction and around the elephant door heads drip onto the stage floor and the electrics.

**Here.** Lon's water tank makes the interior humid; a 6 m dive well of open water in a heated 180,000 m³ volume drives condensation onto every cold surface. The tank stage needs its own hygrothermal model.

#### Lightning protection system

EN/IEC 62305-1 to -4; risk assessment to IEC 62305-2 determines the lightning protection level. Air termination mesh or rods, down conductors at ≤10 m spacing for LPL III, earth termination Type B ring electrode with resistance <10 Ω; surge protective devices to IEC 61643-11 at every service entry.

**Why.** The perms, the rigging and the power distribution are all bonded metal at 30 m; a strike without a designed path finds the camera and lighting electronics.

**Here.** The genuinely Icelandic answer: lightning risk is almost nil. Iceland sees roughly 250–600 flashes nationally per year over 103,000 km² — around 0.003–0.006 flashes/km²/yr, against 0.5–3.0 for most of Europe (Enno et al., Vaisala ATDnet ten-year study). The IEC 62305-2 risk calculation will very likely conclude that structural LPS is not required. Surge protection on services is still worth having, and the earthing system is needed regardless.

#### Roof access — stairs, hatches and walkways

An enclosed internal stair to roof level (not a cat ladder) plus a goods hoist or roof-level crane davit; access hatches to EN 1873; permanent walkway systems on protective paving to keep traffic off the membrane; guardrails to EN ISO 14122-3 and EN 13374 for temporary edge protection.

**Why.** A 6,000 m² roof at 36 m needs frequent visits — outlets, plant, ash, snow, membrane. Difficult access means it stops being inspected.

**Here.** Roof work windows are short and rare. Enclosed, heated access and a place to shelter at roof level is not luxury — it is what makes maintenance happen at all in a Mosfellsbær winter.

#### Parapet, edge trim and coping

Continuous parapet ≥1.1 m with mechanically fixed metal coping to EN 1991-1-4 edge zone pressures; secret-fix or clipped copings with expansion joints at ≤3 m; no exposed face fixings; edge trim tested for uplift.

**Why.** Copings are the most commonly lost element in a windstorm and become high-energy projectiles from 36 m.

**Here.** Standard coping clip spacings from mainland catalogues are inadequate at these pressures; halve them and specify stainless fixings.

#### Bird control and exclusion

Physical exclusion first: mesh to all openings ≥20 mm, sealed eaves and verges, no accessible ledges >50 mm; anti-perch systems only where exclusion is impossible; no netting that can trap birds; nesting-season constraints on works.

**Why.** Birds nesting in the perms drop guano onto sets and lamps, block outlets, and their calls land on the sound recordist's track.

**Here.** Iceland's breeding seabirds and waders are legally protected under Icelandic nature conservation law and works may be restricted in the nesting season — Arctic terns in particular will colonise open gravel roofs and will then dive-bomb anyone servicing the plant. A ballasted gravel roof is a bad idea here for that reason alone.

#### Snow and ice management at doors, aprons and the moat

Hydronic snow-melt loops in the elephant door aprons, the loading yard and the seismic moat covers, run off geothermal district heat return; slip resistance to EN 13036-4 (PTV ≥36 wet); ice-fall protection over pedestrian routes below high parapets.

**Why.** An elephant door that cannot open because 400 mm of drifted snow is frozen against it stops the shoot as effectively as a structural failure.

**Here.** This is where nearly-free geothermal heat is a genuine competitive advantage over any mainland studio — snow melting that would be prohibitively expensive elsewhere costs almost nothing here. Note the grid caveat is about electricity, not heat: district heat is unconstrained, so put every de-icing duty on hot water rather than electric trace where you can.

#### Fixings, fasteners and sealants schedule

All external fixings stainless A4/316 to EN ISO 3506; no carbon steel fasteners in the envelope; EPDM-backed washers; sealants to EN ISO 11600 with an ISO 11600 class 25LM movement rating; fastener pull-out values verified by site test on the actual substrate.

**Why.** Envelope failures start at fasteners, and in a corrosive coastal atmosphere carbon steel fixings stain, then fail, long before the panels do.

**Here.** Salt spray plus acidic volcanic fallout gives an effectively CX environment. A4 is the minimum; consider duplex or A5 at the most exposed elevations.

#### Rooftop plant support steel and acoustic breaks

Dedicated plant support frames tied to truss nodes, never to purlins or deck; anti-vibration mounts sized for the plant's disturbing frequency with ≥90% isolation efficiency; acoustic break at every penetration; plant screens designed as wind-loaded elements to EN 1991-1-4.

**Why.** Rooftop plant is a structure-borne noise source sitting directly on the acoustic envelope; mounting it wrong puts the AHU straight into the sound take.

**Here.** Plant screens in this wind are substantial structures in their own right — they attract very high pressures and add materially to the base shear and to the massing height.

#### Structural steel logistics and piece-size discipline

Maximum shipped piece 30 m long × 3.5 m wide (ESTIMATE, set by the vessel, port handling at Sundahöfn/Grundartangi and the road route to Mosfellsbær); all field connections bolted, not welded; trial assembly in the fabrication yard for the primary trusses.

**Why.** A single member that cannot be delivered or lifted stops the whole erection sequence, and there is no second fabricator on the island to recover it.

**Here.** Everything structural is imported. Sailing schedules, not fabrication capacity, set the programme; order long-lead steel before the design is fully frozen and carry spares on the manifest — a missing splice plate is a three-week delay, not a three-hour one.

#### Handover documentation, O&M and structural asset register

As-built model to ISO 19650, full EN 1090-2 fabrication and NDT records, mill certificates, bolt preload records, membrane warranty and installer certification, isolator test certificates to EN 15129, annual inspection regime with defined intervals for perms, anchors, lifelines and membrane.

**Why.** The perms are a lifting structure; without a documented inspection regime and a rating register, the studio cannot certify to a production what it is safe to fly.

**Here.** With a small local labour pool, the institutional knowledge lives in the documents rather than in the people who built it.

### State of the art

#### Seismic base isolation — elastomeric or friction pendulum bearings

Lead-rubber bearings or double friction pendulum to EN 15129 (anti-seismic devices) and ISO 22762 (elastomeric isolators); target isolated period 2.5–3.5 s; design displacement 300–500 mm (ESTIMATE); prototype and production testing per EN 15129 §8.

**Why.** Isolation cuts superstructure demand by 3–5×, protecting the perms, the rigging, the acoustic linings and the shooting schedule — a stage that survives the frame but loses its ceiling is still out of service.

**Here.** Genuinely justified here rather than gold-plating: Iceland's hazard is real, the building is enormous and single-storey (light and stiff, so isolation works well), and downtime on a lot this size is the expensive failure mode.

#### Seismic moat, cover plates and flexible service crossings

Continuous perimeter gap ≥ 1.2 × design displacement (i.e. 400–600 mm); sliding cover plates rated for vehicle wheel loads at the elephant doors; all pipes, ducts, cable and drainage crossings on flexible loops or seismic joints to EN 15129.

**Why.** An isolation system is only as good as the softest thing bridging the gap — one rigid drain line across the moat locks the building and defeats the bearings.

**Here.** The moat must be heated or covered so it does not fill with drifted snow and freeze solid, which would also lock the isolators.

#### Acoustic decoupling of the stage slab (floating floor or isolated raft)

Full-area resilient bearings or a jack-up isolated slab where required, natural frequency 8–12 Hz; alternatively a structurally separate slab with a 25 mm perimeter isolation joint packed with closed-cell foam and sealed.

**Why.** Structure-borne noise from plant, traffic and the neighbouring stages arrives through the ground; the floor is the flanking path nobody remembers until a take is ruined.

**Here.** Iceland adds a rare structure-borne source — volcanic and tectonic microseismicity and harmonic tremor during unrest episodes, which registers on a quiet stage floor.

#### Rigging grid flatness and deflection limits

Roof truss deflection ≤ span/500 under the rigging live load (120 mm over 60 m); differential deflection between adjacent pick points ≤25 mm; total dead-plus-live sag pre-cambered out (ESTIMATE — owner-set, tighter than any code minimum).

**Why.** Chain hoists and motorised trusses need a flat, predictable reference; sag that varies bay-to-bay makes flying a level 30 m set piece a fight.

#### Roof acoustic mass and rain-noise build-up

Total roof surface mass target ≥400 kg/m² (≈4.0 kPa) including topping, insulation, membrane and ballast; ideally a discontinuous construction (mass deck / resilient break / suspended massive ceiling) rather than a single leaf. Rain noise verified to ISO 10140-5:2010/Amd 1:2014 (heavy artificial rainfall, 3.5 m fall height, 30° specimen pitch; formerly EN ISO 140-18).

**Why.** Target internal ambient NC-20 to NC-25 must hold during heavy rain, not only on a calm day. An uninsulated steel deck can put 70–80 dB(A) into the space under heavy rain (typical figure, ESTIMATE).

**Here.** Specify and test the rain-noise case explicitly, with an agreed rainfall intensity representative of Icelandic driven rain, not a mild-climate default. Driven rain arrives near-horizontally here, so the wall build-up needs the same treatment as the roof.

#### Wind tunnel study

Boundary-layer wind tunnel testing to ASCE 49-21, 1:200 to 1:400 rigid pressure model plus a site-surroundings model; cladding pressure taps at ≥400 locations; and a topographic study of the local terrain multiplier co(z) per EN 1991-1-4 §4.3.3.

**Why.** Four large boxes of different heights on one site create channelling, vortex shedding and local suctions that no code zone map captures.

**Here.** Fully justified here rather than optional. Icelandic topography produces severe local speed-up and downslope acceleration, and the code's terrain multiplier is not designed for it. A tunnel study typically pays for itself in reduced cladding fixing density across the field zones.

#### Volcanic ash loading design case

Additional roof load for wet tephra: USGS gives dry compacted ash ≈1,000 kg/m³ and 50% saturated wet ash ≈1,500 kg/m³ (dry loose 400–700 kg/m³, saturated >2,000 kg/m³). 100 mm of wet ash ≈ 1.5 kPa. Recommend a 1.0 kPa design allowance combined with snow at reduced factor (owner-set; ESTIMATE).

**Why.** Published fragility work puts roof collapse at ~1–10 kPa of tephra, and specifically flags non-engineered long-span low-pitch roofs as vulnerable at thicknesses under 100 mm.

**Here.** Eyjafjallajökull 2010 and Grímsvötn 2011 both put ash across populated Iceland. A flat roof sheds nothing — laboratory work (Osman et al., Journal of Applied Volcanology) found tephra only sheds at pitches ≥32° on metal. Design the load in, and provide safe roof access and washdown points for mechanical removal.

#### Roof washdown and ash removal provisions

Hose bibs at roof level on a frost-proof charged main, fed from geothermal return water; membrane and outlets specified to survive mechanical shovelling (protection boards or a paver walkway on clearance routes); ash-tolerant outlet gratings with removable silt buckets.

**Why.** Ash must come off within days before rain saturates it; if the only way to remove it damages the membrane, you trade a load problem for a leak problem.

**Here.** Ash slurry is abrasive and acidic; it blocks siphonic outlets fast and attacks aluminium and galvanising. Also note the wider lot risk: the same ash destroys the air handling filtration.

#### Tank buoyancy, uplift and seismic sloshing

Empty-tank flotation check against the design groundwater level with a factor ≥1.1 (EN 1997-1 UPL limit state); seismic hydrodynamic pressures and sloshing wave height to EN 1998-4 Annex A (Housner impulsive/convective model); freeboard to the dive-well coping sized for the convective wave.

**Why.** An empty tank in high groundwater floats and cracks; a full tank in an earthquake sloshes water onto the stage floor and into the electrics.

**Here.** Both cases are live here: groundwater in permeable lava is high and flashy, and the seismic case is real. This is the one place where Icelandic hazard and a water tank interact directly.

#### Acoustic interconnect between the two 2,000 m² stages

Full-height movable acoustic partition or paired sliding doors giving a combined 4,000 m² clear space; structurally independent frames either side with a double-leaf separation, target Rw ≥65 dB closed; the frames must not share a column line or a foundation.

**Why.** If the two stages share structure, closing the partition does not give you two stages — vibration flanks straight through the steel and one production ruins the other's sound take.

**Here.** A wide combined span in this wind regime means a substantial temporary-condition check: the partition is not a structural element and each half must be stable alone.

#### Structural health and seismic instrumentation

Triaxial accelerometers at foundation, above the isolation plane and at roof level; strain gauges on selected truss chords; automated post-event alert with a pre-agreed inspection trigger threshold; data logged continuously.

**Why.** After an earthquake the studio must decide within hours whether the stage can be re-occupied. Instrumentation turns that from a judgement call into a measurement.

**Here.** Uniquely valuable in Iceland, where felt events are frequent and each one otherwise triggers a precautionary shutdown and a structural inspection. It also verifies that the base isolation is behaving as designed.

### What gets got wrong

- Rain noise is the defining acoustic problem and it is a STRUCTURAL decision, not an acoustic finish. A lightweight profiled roof cannot be fixed later with linings. The mass has to be in the deck (130–150 mm concrete topping, ≥400 kg/m² total build-up) and it has to be specified before the frame is sized, because it doubles the roof dead load. Verify by test to ISO 10140-5:2010/Amd 1:2014, with an agreed rainfall intensity representative of Icelandic driven rain, not a mild-climate default.
- Design uplift beats design gravity. With vb,0 = 36 m/s and a 40 m tall building, qp ≈ 2.5 kPa and local corner-zone uplift reaches 6–7 kPa — around 700 kg/m², more than the whole roof build-up weighs. Mainland European fastener densities and coping clip spacings are simply wrong here; halve them, and test the assembly to EN 16002 or ETAG 006 rather than trusting a catalogue.
- Snow drift, not uniform snow, governs. Reykjavík's ground snow load is only about 1.0 kN/m² (Zone 1 of the ÍST EN 1991-1-3 NA), so the uniform case looks trivial — but a 40 m step between Askja and its neighbours gives EN 1991-1-3 Annex B drift coefficients up to μ = 8, i.e. 8.0 kN/m² over a 15 m band. Iceland's wind scours the flat roof and dumps everything in the lee. Teams from milder climates dismiss Icelandic snow and get this wrong.
- n50 flatters a big volume into meaninglessness. At 180,000 m³ with a 21,600 m² envelope, a mediocre q50 of 1.0 m³/(h·m²) reads as n50 ≈ 0.12 h⁻¹, better than Passivhaus. Specify q50 per m² of envelope. And book the EN ISO 9972 test on the weather forecast, because you cannot pressure-test a stage in a 20 m/s wind.
- Alkali-silica reaction is Iceland's signature concrete failure. Icelandic aggregate carries rhyolitic and altered basaltic material and Icelandic cement runs at about 1.65 wt% Na₂O-equivalent alkali; ASR wrecked Icelandic housing built 1961–1979. Silica fume has been blended into Icelandic cement since 1979 and sea-dredged sand must be washed (Gudmundsson & Ólafsson, Cement and Concrete Research, 1999). Test every aggregate source; do not assume an imported mix design is safe with local stone.
- Specify the steel sub-grade for cold. EN 1993-1-10 with a −25 °C reference temperature and thick, restrained welded truss nodes rules out S355JR immediately — J2 or K2 minimum, with EN 10164 Z-quality where plates are loaded through thickness. This is the item most commonly carried over unchanged from a mainland spec, and its failure mode is brittle and total.
- Lightning protection is the thing Iceland lets you skip, and nobody believes it. At roughly 0.003–0.006 flashes/km²/yr nationally against 0.5–3.0 across most of Europe, the IEC 62305-2 risk calculation will almost certainly say no structural LPS is needed. Run the calculation and keep it on file, because every insurer, consultant and reviewer will query it. Earthing and surge protection are still required.
- Erection wind and December darkness are the programme, not the steel. Crane lifting stops around 9–12 m/s, which in Mosfellsbær is common, and December gives about four usable hours of daylight. Ground-assemble the 60 m trusses and lift complete units in rare windows. Budget two seasons where a mainland site would need one, and design EN 1991-1-6 temporary bracing for a frame that will stand part-complete through gales.
- The elephant door is the weakest element in four different ways at once — structurally (a 120 m² leaf sees ~300 kN of wind), acoustically, thermally and for air leakage. Add an anemometer interlock and a stated maximum operating wind speed, or the first person to open it in a gale destroys it.
- Ash falls on a flat roof and stays there. Wet tephra is about 1,500 kg/m³, so 100 mm is 1.5 kPa, and published fragility work puts long-span low-pitch roof collapse in the 1–10 kPa band. Tephra only sheds at pitches above about 32°, so a flat stage roof sheds nothing. Design a load allowance, and make sure the roof can be shovelled without destroying the membrane and that outlets have silt buckets.
- Don't let the seismic tie system be value-engineered out. On the capital region's mixed lava-over-clay profile, EN 1998-5 §5.4.1.2 requires tied foundations; the exemption applies only where every pad demonstrably sits on continuous rock, and Icelandic rockhead steps between adjacent bays. Untied pads look like an easy saving on a drawing and are a failure mechanism in an event.
- Corrosion here is worse than the coastal category suggests. Salt spray gives C5-M, but volcanic events add acidic, sulphurous, chloride-rich fallout that attacks aluminium and galvanising specifically. Spec toward CX, use A4 stainless fixings throughout the envelope, and treat post-ashfall washdown as a scheduled maintenance duty with the water supply already installed.
- A gravel-ballasted roof invites Arctic terns to nest on it. They are protected under Icelandic law, they colonise open gravel, and they will then attack anyone servicing rooftop plant through the whole breeding season. Choose the membrane finish with that in mind.
- Wind and seismic base shears here are the same order of magnitude — roughly 10–15 MN each on Askja. Engineers used to a wind-governed shed or a seismic-governed frame tend to assume one case dominates and check the other loosely. Both must be run through the full EN 1990 combination set, and the isolation system design has to work with wind restraint as well as seismic release.

---

## 2. Acoustic isolation and room acoustics

*Askja (6,000 m² / 180,000 m³), Lon (2,400 m² tank stage) and the paired 2,000 m² stages, Sugar Land Studios, Mosfellsbær*

### Essential

#### Box-in-box inner structural frame

Fully independent inner steel frame carrying floor, walls, perms and all rigging; zero rigid contact with the outer weather shell. Predict composite performance with EN 12354-1:2017. Envelope target D_w ≥ 75 dB overall and ≥ 55 dB at 63 Hz (estimate — set by consultant modelling, not a published figure).

**Why.** A single-leaf shell tops out near Rw 60 at any sane mass. Without a second decoupled leaf the stage is unusable in Icelandic wind and rain.

**Here.** The outer shell alone takes the 36 m/s class design wind (ÍST EN 1991-1-4:2005/NA — verify vb,0 by zone for Mosfellsbær) and seismic drift; the inner box sees neither, so it can be built purely for acoustics.

#### Structural isolation bearings under the inner box

Elastomeric bearings under every inner-box column and under the floating slab: Getzner publish Sylomer SR 42 at 0.042 N/mm² through SR 850 at 0.85 N/mm² and SR 1200 at 1.2 N/mm² static range of use; or helical steel springs. Mason Industries publish 8 Hz in a 50 mm gap for TV/theatre jack-up floors, and 3.6–4.5 Hz at 12–19 mm deflection for spring floors.

**Why.** Sets all structure-borne isolation. At fn = 8 Hz transmissibility is roughly −19 dB at 25 Hz, −36 dB at 63 Hz, −48 dB at 125 Hz (1/((f/fn)²−1)).

**Here.** Bearings must be rated for seismic vertical and lateral excursion and for wind-driven sway of the shell above. Demand creep data and stiffness characterised to −20 °C, not just 23 °C lab values.

#### Isolation gap / acoustic void

Continuous void on all six faces. Width = greater of acoustic need (≥100 mm) and structural demand: EN 1998-1 seismic separation √(d1²+d2²) plus outer-shell wind sway plus thermal movement. Expect 250–400 mm here (estimate). Must be inspectable, drainable and permanently free of debris.

**Why.** One solid bridge — grout squeeze-out, a dropped tool, a conduit — short-circuits the whole isolation and costs 20–30 dB.

**Here.** Icelandic seismic and wind drift force a wider gap than elsewhere, which is an acoustic gift: 300 mm puts mass-air-mass at ~8.8 Hz for 400 + 250 kg/m² leaves, versus ~15.3 Hz at 100 mm.

#### Cavity absorption in the isolation void

50–100 mm mineral wool, 30–60 kg/m³, hydrophobic, Euroclass A1 to EN 13501-1, on the cavity face of one leaf. Suppresses the depth resonance at c/2d — 343/(2 × 0.3) = 572 Hz — and cavity standing waves; worth 5–10 dB of double-leaf TL (estimate).

**Why.** An untreated cavity resonates and re-radiates, and the double leaf can fall 8 dB short of prediction across 500 Hz–2 kHz.

**Here.** Must be rot-proof and hydrophobic — the void will see condensation and wind-driven moisture. Seal the void head and base so volcanic ash cannot infiltrate and foul the wool.

#### Seismic snubbers and restraint of the isolated mass

All-directional restraints with 6–12 mm working clearance, engaging only above serviceability displacement, rated to the EN 1998-1 design demand for the full inner-box mass. Check aGR for Mosfellsbær against the ÍST EN 1998-1:2004 NA map (capital-area values in the 0.1–0.2 g range, estimate; the South Iceland Seismic Zone is codified up to ~0.5 g at 10%/50 yr).

**Why.** An isolated box on soft bearings walks in an earthquake or a gale; restrain it rigidly and you have thrown the isolation away.

**Here.** Non-negotiable. Reykjanes volcano-tectonic swarms are current and felt in the capital region; the snubber gap is what stops a swarm from bridging the acoustic void.

#### Outer leaf wall build-up

200–250 mm in-situ or precast reinforced concrete, 480–600 kg/m², or heavy cassette panels. No lightweight profiled metal cladding. Critical frequency of 200 mm concrete ≈ 90–100 Hz (estimate). Mass law gives ~6 dB per doubling of surface mass and ~5 dB per octave field-incidence.

**Why.** The outer leaf does the low-frequency work and takes the weather. Light cladding fails at 63–125 Hz, exactly where wind and plant live.

**Here.** Horizontal driven rain makes the walls rain-noise radiators, not just the roof. Profiled metal walls drum under sideways rain. Concrete also answers wind load, ash abrasion and the cost of imported steel.

#### Inner leaf wall build-up

Independent inner leaf at 200–300 kg/m², e.g. 100 mm dense blockwork or a second concrete skin plus 2 × 15 mm dense gypsum (~24 kg/m²) and 18 mm ply, mounted on the inner frame only. Staggered board joints, every edge sealed with permanently plastic acoustic sealant, no rigid ties across the void.

**Why.** The second mass of the double leaf. Its surface mass and airtightness — not the outer shell — set high-frequency performance.

**Here.** Every board is imported by sea. Specify panel sizes that ship in standard containers and a build-up a small local crew can install from a scissor lift without specialist plant.

#### Outer roof build-up for rain noise

Concrete or composite metal deck with structural topping, ≥300–400 kg/m², warm-roof insulation, adhered or mechanically fixed membrane. Rain performance rated as sound intensity level L_I,A in dB re 1 pW/m² to ISO 140-18:2006, now carried into EN ISO 10140-1/-3.

**Why.** Rain drumming on a long-span roof is the single worst acoustic problem a big stage has. A bare profiled metal deck can put 60–75 dB(A) into the room (estimate).

**Here.** Mosfellsbær sees frequent heavy driven rain and sleet. Set the acceptance target as rain contribution ≤ 25 dB(A) inside under the ISO 'heavy' rain condition — 5 mm drops at 7 m/s (per BSI/ISO); 'intense' is 2 mm drops at 4 m/s.

#### Anti-drumming / constrained-layer damping on the roof skin

Bituminous or polymeric damping membrane at 4–8 kg/m² bonded (not loose-laid) to any metal element rain can strike. Products such as Sound Reduction Systems Raincheck exist for exactly this. Avoid rigid PIR directly under a metal skin — it acts as a sounding board.

**Why.** The rain source is a thin, undamped, lightly loaded plate. Damping the plate beats adding absorption in the room by a wide margin.

**Here.** Also damps sleet and graupel, which are more common here than true hail, and the hydraulic hammering of near-horizontal rain.

#### Roof form, ballast strategy and snow retention

Flat or ≤2° pitch, adhered or mechanically fixed membrane, no loose gravel ballast, no planes that shed snow. Parapets and secondary overflows sized for rain that climbs rather than falls. Design retained snow as load to ÍST EN 1991-1-3 NA.

**Why.** Snow sliding off a pitched roof is a loud impulsive event mid-take; gravel ballast is the classic rain-noise fix but becomes a projectile in a gale.

**Here.** Gravel ballast and sedum roofs — the two standard rain-noise answers — are both effectively unusable at Icelandic design wind. Retained snow at ~200 kg/m² per metre is free acoustic mass, but only if the structure carries it.

#### Decoupled inner ceiling / perms deck

Perms, grid and all rigging hung from the inner box only. Where a discrete inner ceiling is used, hang on resilient hangers (Sylomer or spring, fn 8–12 Hz), ≥300 mm void, 100 mm mineral wool. No hanger, conduit, sprinkler drop or cable tray may touch the outer roof deck.

**Why.** The commonest rain-noise failure is not the roof build-up — it is one rigging point or sprinkler drop bolting the perms to the drumming outer deck.

**Here.** With 30 m of clear height there is room for a deep, heavily damped decoupled zone above the perms. Spend it on rain-noise buffering; you will not get the height back.

#### Rainwater drainage routing and lagging

All rainwater outside the inner box. Siphonic or gravity downpipes in the outer zone only, lagged with 25–50 mm mineral wool plus a 5–8 kg/m² limp mass barrier; cast iron or mineral-filled PP pipe, resilient clips throughout, no rigid fixing to both leaves.

**Why.** A downpipe inside the acoustic envelope is a permanent waterfall in the middle of every take.

**Here.** Short-duration design rainfall intensity for the capital region is high (estimate 60–90 mm/h at the design return period — confirm against Veðurstofan Íslands records). Outlets must also resist wind-driven backflow.

#### Acoustic elephant doors

Sliding stage doors, typically 12 × 9 m. Specialty Doors / Elephant Doors publish STC 53 with full-perimeter double acoustic seals, electric roller-chain drive with manual override, and installations to 30–40 ft high; they report a site NIC of 50 on an 18 × 20 ft install. For European procurement demand Rw to EN ISO 10140-2 / ISO 717-1.

**Why.** The largest single hole in the envelope. Its rating caps the whole wall — see the composite budget.

**Here.** Must double as a weather door at design wind and stay operable with snow and ash in the track. Specify a heated, drained, sweepable sill and a track that does not rely on a floor recess.

#### Sound-lock lobbies

Every opening is a pair: outer weather/goods door and inner acoustic door, mechanically or electrically interlocked, with the lobby lined on all faces in 100 mm Class A absorber (α_w ≥ 0.90 to EN ISO 11654). Lobby length ≥ 6 m for elephant doors, ≥ 2 m for personnel doors.

**Why.** Two doors in series with an absorptive lobby give far more than the arithmetic sum; a single door, however good, gives zero isolation while it is open.

**Here.** The sound lock is simultaneously the wind lobby, the snow lobby and the ash lobby. Without it, every truck movement in a gale drives snow onto the set.

#### Personnel acoustic doorsets

Double-leaf sets in sound-lock configuration, each leaf Rw 45–50 dB tested to EN ISO 10140-2, giving Rw ~60+ composite. Steel or heavy timber leaf 60–100 mm at 40–60 kg/m², rebated perimeter seals and a 10–15 mm travel threshold drop seal.

**Why.** Crew doors are used hundreds of times a day and are the component most likely to be left ajar or have its seals damaged.

**Here.** Fit closers rated for the pressure differential a gale produces. A door that a 1 kPa differential holds open is a permanent 40 dB hole in a 75 dB envelope.

#### Door seals and gasket regime

Inflatable pneumatic perimeter seals at 0.5–1.5 bar on the large doors; mechanical drop seals plus dual compression gaskets on personnel doors; magnetic seals on light leaves. Scheduled replacement interval, not replace-on-failure.

**Why.** Seals are the only consumable in the isolation system and the only part that degrades silently, without a visible symptom.

**Here.** UV is low but temperature cycling, ash abrasion and salt-laden air attack elastomers. Specify EPDM or silicone characterised from −30 to +60 °C.

#### Composite transmission loss budget

R_comp = −10·log10(Σ (S_i/S_total)·10^(−R_i/10)). For a 6,000 m² envelope at Rw 80 containing 200 m² of Rw 55 door, the composite is 69.4 dB — the door alone costs 11 dB. Publish an element-by-element budget before anything is procured.

**Why.** Envelopes are always governed by their worst element. Money spent raising the wall above the door's ceiling is money burnt.

**Here.** With no glazing anywhere in the shell, doors and service penetrations constitute the entire weak-element budget here — which makes the budget unusually simple and unusually unforgiving.

#### Leak and gap sensitivity

A continuous 1 mm gap around a 12 × 9 m Rw 55 door — 0.042 m² of open area in 108 m² — drops the assembly to about 34 dB by the same composite arithmetic (idealised illustration; a real slit has its own resonant behaviour). Airtightness, not mass, governs above 500 Hz.

**Why.** Every decibel above about 50 is won or lost at the joints, not in the build-up.

**Here.** Re-verify airtightness after the first winter: wind loading, thermal cycling and seismic settlement open joints that passed at handover.

#### Acoustic interconnect between the two 2,000 m² stages

Two independent walls — one on each stage's own inner frame — with a walk-through void between, each leaf Rw 55–60, giving Rw 75+ composite. Operable/folding partitions (Skyfold, Dorma Hüppe, Gerriets) top out around Rw 55–60 in manufacturer tests and cannot do this alone.

**Why.** A single operable partition is ~20 dB short of a stage envelope. Sold as an interconnect, it turns two stages into one whether you wanted that or not.

**Here.** Two independent walls also mean the two structures are not seismically coupled through a shared partition — a single-wall interconnect would transfer drift between buildings.

#### Zero glazing in the shooting envelope

No rooflights, windows or vision panels in the shell. Viewing by camera feed and through sound-lock vision panels only: 2 × 12.8 mm laminate on separate frames, cavity ≥100 mm, Rw ~50–55 (estimate).

**Why.** Glazing is always the weakest element and the coldest surface, and a shooting stage has no daylighting requirement to trade against it.

**Here.** December in Mosfellsbær gives roughly four hours of daylight and June gives near-continuous light. Neither is usable for controlled shooting, so a windowless envelope costs nothing and removes the biggest weak point.

#### Penetration policy and isolated sleeves

Written rule: nothing penetrates the inner leaf. Where unavoidable — oversized cast-in sleeve, service centred on a resilient collar, packed with mineral wool, sealed both faces with a permanently plastic acoustic sealant. Two-stage seal, never one.

**Why.** Penetrations are the flanking path nobody draws. One unsealed 50 mm conduit will undo a 75 dB wall.

**Here.** Seals must accommodate seismic and thermal displacement without cracking, so specify permanently plastic sealants rather than rigid mortar or hard-setting fire compound alone.

#### Pipework crossings and resilient hangers

All pipes crossing the envelope on resilient hangers (Sylomer or spring, fn 8–12 Hz), with a flexible connection at the crossing and lagging of 25–50 mm wool plus a 5–8 kg/m² barrier for at least 5 m either side. No pipe rigidly clipped to both leaves.

**Why.** Pipes are perfect structure-borne bridges and carry pump noise straight into the room from networks you do not control.

**Here.** Veitur district heat arrives hot and pumped. The network's own pump and control-valve noise is an imported source you did not design, cannot switch off, and must isolate at the boundary.

#### Sprinkler system acoustic integration

Design for 30 m clear height to NFPA 13 or EN 12845 — beyond standard ESFR limits, so expect a performance-based ceiling-level design, plus deluge over Lon's tank. Every drop, main and hanger supported from the inner box only; resilient hangers on the wet main.

**Why.** Sprinkler steel is the heaviest pipe run in the building and the most likely to be hung off whatever is convenient — which is the outer shell.

**Here.** Geothermal supply is not a cold main. Run a separate cold water main and trace-heat any run in the cold outer void; a freeze-cracked main is both a flood and a noise event.

#### Duct silencers / splitter attenuators

Rectangular splitter attenuators, 200 mm splitters on 100 mm airways, 2,400–3,000 mm long; expect roughly 15/30/45/50/45/35/25 dB insertion loss at 63 Hz–4 kHz for 100–200 Pa (estimate). Rate to ISO 7235:2003 (insertion loss, flow noise, pressure loss); in situ to ISO 11820:1996.

**Why.** Without them the AHU is audible in the room; with the wrong ones the silencer's own regenerated flow noise becomes the binding limit.

**Here.** Ambient near 5 °C annual mean makes 100% outside-air free cooling tempting and cheap. The acoustic price of free cooling is silencer length and cross-section — budget for it up front.

#### Cross-talk attenuators and the one-AHU-per-stage rule

No shared ductwork, plenum or riser between stages; one dedicated AHU per stage. Where any duct must serve two spaces, size cross-talk attenuators for the full inter-space target (D_w 75 dB), which usually proves the duct should not exist.

**Why.** A shared duct is a 75 dB wall with a 40 dB hole in it — the cheapest possible way to destroy inter-stage isolation.

**Here.** Separate plant also lets one stage shut down completely during an ash event or storm without taking its neighbour off air.

#### Terminal devices, dampers and air velocity limits

Fabric sock diffusers at ≤0.2 m/s discharge face velocity; main duct ≤5 m/s, final branches ≤3 m/s. Rate terminal and damper sound power to ISO 5135:2020. No balancing damper within 5 m of a terminal inside the stage.

**Why.** Regenerated noise at dampers and grilles, not fan noise, is what actually breaks NC-25 in a properly silenced stage.

**Here.** Fabric socks also catch the last of any ash carryover and are removable and washable — a real operational benefit in the weeks after an eruption.

#### Plant room isolation and inertia bases

AHUs, pumps and chillers on concrete inertia bases of 1.5–2× machine mass, on spring isolators at 25–50 mm static deflection (fn ~1.5–3 Hz), in plant rooms structurally separate from the stage box, with flexible connections on every duct and pipe. Sound power to ISO 3744.

**Why.** Plant vibration entering the structure bypasses the whole airborne design and arrives as a low-frequency hum no wall build-up addresses.

**Here.** Site plant to meet ISO 1996-1/-2 at the nearest Mosfellsbær dwellings. Any diesel genset used to firm supply — likely, given Landsvirkjun's refusal of new industrial load — is the loudest object on the lot and needs its own enclosure.

#### Filtration for volcanic ash, and silencer sizing at final resistance

Multi-stage filtration to ISO 16890: ISO Coarse pre-filter, then ePM10 and ePM1 stages, with an ash bypass/shutdown mode and differential-pressure alarms. Size fans, silencers and regenerated-noise limits at FINAL filter resistance, never at clean.

**Why.** As filters load the fan speeds up. A silencer sized on clean-filter flow starts to whistle and the stage drifts off its NC target.

**Here.** Icelandic tephra is largely sub-63 μm and highly abrasive. A single ash event can load filters in days, abrade acoustic scrims and clog porous absorbers, permanently reducing α.

#### Lighting fixture fan noise

Specify convection-cooled or user-selectable silent-mode LED fixtures. A single fan-cooled 1.2 kW LED can radiate 30–35 dB(A) at 3 m (estimate). Acceptance-test the stage with the full rig at 100% and HVAC at design flow.

**Why.** A rig of 200 fan-cooled fixtures will exceed NC-25 on its own, and no amount of envelope performance will help.

**Here.** Cheap, low-carbon electricity and free heat make large rigs affordable to run; the real constraints here are grid capacity and fixture noise, not energy cost.

#### Stage floor construction and flatness

Solid and dead, not sprung. Power-floated slab plus bonded 25–32 mm ply or MDF overlay, paintable, camera-dolly grade flatness — typically ±3 mm under a 3 m straightedge, premium stages ±1.5 mm. Live load 15–25 kN/m² (estimate) with point loads for scissor lifts.

**Why.** A sprung or hollow floor drums under footfall and dolly wheels, and every boom mic on the stage picks it up.

**Here.** Free geothermal heat makes underfloor heating attractive; keep the pipe screed within the structural slab, not in a separate floating layer that would decouple and drum.

#### Slab decoupling from dock, mill and workshops

Full-depth movement/isolation joint in the slab at every boundary between a stage and a scenery dock, construction mill, workshop or corridor, filled with compressible closed-cell filler and sealed both faces.

**Why.** The commonest flanking failure on any studio lot: a continuous ground slab carrying mill saw and nail-gun impact directly into the stage.

**Here.** Icelandic lots concentrate workshops indoors because outdoor working is impossible for much of the year, so the mill sits closer to the stages than it would elsewhere and the joint matters more.

#### Lon water tank mass and isolation strategy

2,400 m² tank with a 20 × 20 × 6 m dive well. The dive well alone holds 2,400 m³ = 2,400 t; the whole tank plausibly 5,000–5,500 t full (estimate). Do not float the tank — bear it on grade and decouple the tank walls from the inner box, or use load-adaptive bearings.

**Why.** A spring-mounted mass whose weight varies roughly 3:1 between empty and full cannot hold a natural frequency; the isolation is correct in one state and wrong in the other.

**Here.** Tank water at Icelandic ambient needs continuous heating. Geothermal makes that essentially free, but the plate heat exchanger and circulation pumps then become permanent in-envelope noise sources.

#### Background noise criterion — shooting stage

NC-25 with all plant and rig running; Sky Studios Elstree publish NC-25 as best-in-class for film and TV. NC-25 octave limits 54/44/37/31/27/24/22/21 dB at 63 Hz–8 kHz (≈34 dB(A)); NC-20 is 51/40/33/26/22/19/17/16. Evaluate per ANSI/ASA S12.2-2019; NR 20 (ISO/R 1996 curve family) is the European equivalent.

**Why.** Above NC-25 the boom operator hears the building. Below NC-20 you are buying silence that lighting fans and crew will destroy anyway.

**Here.** The criterion must be met in a 30 m/s wind with heavy rain on the roof, not on a calm day. Write the weather condition into the acceptance test or the number means nothing.

#### Background noise and RT criteria — mix, ADR and Foley

Mix stage NC-15 (47/36/29/22/17/14/12/11 dB, ≈25 dB(A)); ADR and Foley NC-10 to NC-15. RT 0.25 s ±0.05, broadly flat 100 Hz–8 kHz, in a 300–500 m³ room, per ITU-R BS.1116-3 / EBU Tech 3276 practice. B-chain response to SMPTE ST 202:2010, which applies to rooms over 150 m³.

**Why.** A mix stage is a measuring instrument; a shooting stage is a workshop. Applying the shooting stage criterion to post is a 10 dB error.

**Here.** Put post in a separate structure from the stages. Neither the shooting envelope's own plant nor the wind on its shell can be attenuated to NC-15 within the same building.

#### Reverberation time target and the volume limit

Sabine T = 0.161V/A. Askja's 180,000 m³ has only ~21,600 m² of surface (6,000 floor + 6,000 ceiling + 9,600 wall). Even with walls and ceiling (15,600 m²) at α 0.95 and a hard floor, A ≈ 15,120 m² sabins and T(500 Hz) ≈ 1.9 s. Add 3,000 m² of hanging baffles at α 0.9 to reach ≈1.4 s (estimate).

**Why.** A 180,000 m³ stage physically cannot be as dry as a 36,000 m³ one. Specifying 1.0 s is specifying something no amount of money can buy.

**Here.** Set the expectation with the owner in writing at concept stage; the small local consulting pool means nobody on site will independently challenge an impossible number later.

#### Absorption treatment build-up

100 mm mineral wool at 45–60 kg/m³ on a 50 mm air gap behind black acoustically transparent scrim and expanded metal; α_w ≥ 0.90–0.95, Class A to EN ISO 11654, tested to EN ISO 354. Euroclass A1 or A2-s1,d0 to EN 13501-1. Full wall coverage above dado and the full ceiling zone.

**Why.** This is the entire absorption budget. There is no spare surface in a room with a 33 m mean free path.

**Here.** Facings must be washable or cheaply replaceable after an ash event; open-cell wool that has taken tephra loses α permanently and cannot be cleaned in place.

#### Echo control, mean free path and critical distance

Mean free path 4V/S = 33 m, i.e. ~97 ms between reflections. A far-wall reflection across 100 m returns at 292 ms — a discrete echo far beyond the ~50 ms integration window for speech. Critical distance r_c = 0.057·√(V/T) ≈ 18 m at T = 1.8 s.

**Why.** In a 100 m room the problem is not reverberation, it is a distinct slap. Treat the end walls as echo control first and RT control second.

#### Flanking path register and EN 12354 budget

Numbered flanking-path register, each path budgeted with EN 12354-1:2017 (airborne), -2 (impact) and -5 (equipment noise). Named paths: foundations, shared slab, cable containment, sprinkler and HVAC pipe, ducts, drainage, doors and frames, cavity bridging, roof plant, catwalks, interconnect wall, cable pits.

**Why.** Flanking, not the wall, decides the field result. Without a register the paths get discovered during the first shoot.

**Here.** Add two Icelandic paths: wind-driven vibration of the outer shell transmitted through the bearings, and post-seismic bridging where the void has closed through settlement or debris.

#### Verification — airborne sound insulation

Lab type-tests to EN ISO 10140-2:2021, rated Rw (C; Ctr) per EN ISO 717-1:2020. Field: EN ISO 16283-1:2014 — note its stated scope is 10–250 m³, so record the deviation formally — plus ASTM E336 for NIC/ASTC and ISO 15186-2:2003 sound-intensity scanning to locate leaks. Uncertainty per EN ISO 12999-1:2020.

**Why.** Askja is over 700× the upper volume of the standard field method. Without an agreed deviation and an intensity method, acceptance testing has no defensible basis.

**Here.** Intensity scanning is also the only practical way to find a seal that has opened after a seismic event or a winter of thermal cycling, without dismantling anything.

#### Verification — room acoustics and background noise

RT to EN ISO 3382-1:2009 (precision grade, integrated impulse response) with stated source and microphone counts; ISO 3382-2:2008 for ancillary rooms.
