Absorption product data to EN ISO 354 / EN ISO 11654 (α_w) or ASTM C423 (NRC/SAA). Noise per ANSI/ASA S12.2-2019 with IEC 61672-1 Class 1 meters, IEC 61260-1 filters, IEC 60942 calibrator.

**Why.** Unwitnessed, unstandardised numbers are how a stage is handed over at NC-25 and shoots at NC-35.

**Here.** Specify that the test be repeated in a named winter window — wind above 20 m/s with rain — because that is the condition the stage actually has to work in.

#### Site layout, stage separation and door orientation

Physically separate stage buildings with a minimum 15–20 m gap (estimate); elephant doors of adjacent stages not facing one another; door openings leeward of the prevailing wind; workshops, mill and plant grouped downwind and on separate slabs.

**Why.** The cheapest isolation on the lot is distance and geometry, and neither can be retrofitted once the buildings are up.

**Here.** Prevailing winter winds in the southwest are broadly easterly to north-easterly (verify against Veðurstofan records for Mosfellsbær). Leeward door openings stop a gale loading the seals every time a truck moves.

#### Inter-stage SFX budget and lot-wide lock-up

At D_w 75 dB between stages, a 130 dB peak effect in one arrives at ~55 dB in its neighbour — clearly audible against NC-25. No envelope closes that gap. Run a lot-wide SFX and lock-up schedule with cue lights, bells, red-light interlocks and a shared calendar.

**Why.** The isolation target is a floor, not a solution. Gunfire, explosions and stunt days must be coordinated, not attenuated.

**Here.** A small labour pool means one sound department covers several stages, so the lock-up schedule is a single shared operational document rather than a per-stage one.

### Expected

#### Mass-air-mass and coincidence management

f0 = 60·√((m1+m2)/(m1·m2·d)); 400 + 250 kg/m² at d = 0.3 m gives 8.8 Hz. Keep f0 below 20 Hz and keep the two leaves' critical frequencies apart — concrete ~90–100 Hz, 12.5 mm gypsum ~2.5–3 kHz (estimates) — by using dissimilar thicknesses and constrained-layer damped board.

**Why.** The two dips are where a double wall leaks. Matched leaves put both dips at the same frequency and double the loss.

**Here.** Viscoelastic damping polymers stiffen below 0 °C. Specify a damping layer characterised down to −20 °C for anything in the outer leaf.

#### Verified concrete density (surface mass, not thickness)

Specify walls in kg/m², not mm, and verify hardened dry density of the actual mix to EN 12390-7 rather than assuming 2,400 kg/m³. Check aggregate vesicularity and alkali-silica reactivity.

**Why.** Sound insulation follows surface mass. A wall specified by thickness and poured from lighter aggregate quietly loses 1–2 dB across the entire envelope.

**Here.** Icelandic basaltic and rhyolitic aggregates vary widely in density and some are vesicular; Iceland also has a documented ASR history. Test the real local mix before the acoustic model is signed off (estimate of risk).

#### Smoke ventilation strategy

Prefer mechanical smoke extract through silenced, damper-isolated shafts over natural ventilators. Where EN 12101-2 NSHEVs are unavoidable, specify acoustically rated double-skin units and count every one in the composite budget.

**Why.** Each roof-mounted natural ventilator is at once an acoustic leak, a rain drum, a snow trap and a wind-load concentration.

**Here.** NSHEV wind-load class and snow-load class matter far more than usual; a unit rated adequate in temperate Europe may be under-specified at Icelandic exposure, and a wind-damaged vent is an open hole in the envelope.

#### Acoustically rated cable and pipe transits

Modular re-openable transits (e.g. Roxtec) with published Rw to EN ISO 10140-2, sized so every block is fully filled — unfilled cores are the classic failure. Separate transits in the outer and inner leaves; never one sleeve spanning both.

**Why.** Production cabling changes constantly. Without a re-openable rated transit, someone will drill a new hole rather than pull a block.

**Here.** Iceland is 230 V / 50 Hz, so incoming US productions bring step-down transformers and extra distribution. Cable counts run higher than a US stage — size transits with 50% spare cores.

#### Duct breakout lagging

Lag any duct inside or crossing the acoustic void with 50 mm mineral wool plus an 8–10 kg/m² limp mass barrier, fully sealed — worth 10–20 dB of breakout reduction (estimate). Prefer round or oval spiral duct; flat rectangular duct radiates strongly at low frequency.

**Why.** Breakout is transmission through the duct wall downstream of the silencer, and it bypasses every attenuator you installed.

#### Geothermal pipework and control-valve noise

District heat arrives around 80 °C and pumped. Use pressure-independent control valves sized to keep the outlet above the fluid's vapour pressure, check the cavitation index σ against the manufacturer's limit, and use multi-stage trim on high-drop stations.

**Why.** A cavitating control valve produces a broadband hiss that travels the pipework the full length of the lot and cannot be silenced downstream.

**Here.** This is the Icelandic failure mode: because heat is nearly free, pressure drops get taken carelessly at the building — and the resulting noise then lives inside the acoustic envelope.

#### Transformer and dimmer room siting (100 Hz, not 120 Hz)

All transformers, dimmer racks and VFDs outside the inner box, on isolated bases, in rooms with Rw ≥ 60 walls. Iceland is 230 V / 50 Hz, so magnetostrictive hum lands at 100 Hz with harmonics at 200 and 300 Hz — not the 120 Hz of a US stage.

**Why.** 100 Hz is where a very large room has the least absorption and where the double leaf's mass-air-mass behaviour is least forgiving.

**Here.** Any low-frequency treatment copied from a US stage design will be tuned an octave-third off. Tune LF traps to the 100 Hz family.

#### Perms and catwalk impact noise

Solid decked walkways with bonded resilient underlay and rubber-topped grating rather than bare steel mesh; target ~10 dB footfall reduction (estimate). Rate impact to EN ISO 10140-3, field-verify to EN ISO 16283-2 with L'nT,w per ISO 717-2.

**Why.** Someone always walks the perms during a take. Bare steel grating delivers the footfall straight down onto the shooting floor.

#### Ground-borne vibration baseline and foundation flanking

Pre-design baseline survey per ISO 14837 / BS 6472-1 / DIN 4150-2 and -3. Separate footings for outer and inner structures where ground allows; where a shared raft is unavoidable, isolate at the bearing plane. Assess Route 1 traffic, plant and any quarry blasting.

**Why.** Ground-borne energy bypasses the entire airborne envelope and arrives at 20–80 Hz, which nothing in the wall build-up addresses.

**Here.** Add volcano-tectonic tremor as a genuine baseline source. Reykjanes episodes produce sustained low-frequency ground motion felt across the capital region, and it is not a one-off event.

#### Lon's absorption deficit from the water surface

A 2,400 m² water surface has α ≈ 0.01 — acoustically a mirror. Lon's entire absorption budget must be met by walls and ceiling, and the surface adds a strong specular reflection directly under every source.

**Why.** Losing the floor as an absorber raises RT across the band and puts a hard early reflection under every boom mic.

**Here.** Rain noise matters more in Lon than in a dry stage, because there is no absorbent floor to soak up what the roof radiates.

#### Low-frequency absorption

A quarter wavelength at 63 Hz is 1.36 m, so porous absorbers under ~300 mm do essentially nothing there. Add tuned membrane or Helmholtz absorbers at 63–125 Hz, or 1 m deep porous zones behind the perms, to stop the LF RT running 1.5–2× the mid band.

**Why.** An LF reverberation rise is heard as boom on dialogue, cannot be fixed in post, and is the commonest complaint on very large stages.

**Here.** The dominant in-envelope LF sources here are wind buffeting on the shell and 100 Hz transformer hum — both landing exactly in the band the room absorbs worst.

#### Modal behaviour and Schroeder frequency

First axial modes: 343/(2×100) = 1.7 Hz along, 2.9 Hz across, 5.7 Hz vertical — all sub-audible. Schroeder frequency f_s = 2000·√(T/V) = 2000·√(1.8/180,000) ≈ 6 Hz. The field is statistically diffuse across the entire audio band.

**Why.** No modal treatment is needed or useful in Askja. The same arithmetic on a 500 m³ mix room gives f_s ≈ 45 Hz, where modal control is the whole job.

### State of the art

#### Aeroacoustic tone control on the envelope

No exposed slender members. Vortex shedding f = St·U/d with St ≈ 0.2: a 20 mm bar in a 36 m/s wind sheds at 360 Hz; a 10 mm cable at 720 Hz. Round or fair every parapet edge; no perforated cladding, mesh screens, free-standing roof handrails or guy wires.

**Why.** A tonal whistle cuts straight through a 75 dB envelope because ear and microphone both lock onto the tone; broadband wind noise does not.

**Here.** Among the windiest inhabited places on earth — this is a routine condition, not an extreme. CFD- or wind-tunnel-screen every exposed edge, louvre blade and lightning conductor.

#### Envelope pressurisation and airtightness

Hold the stage at +10 to +25 Pa relative to outside via the HVAC. Test the shell to EN ISO 9972 (blower door) with a target ≤1.0 m³/h·m² at 50 Pa. Dynamic control, because wind swamps the fan pressure.

**Why.** Positive pressure makes leaks blow outward. A negatively pressurised envelope in a gale draws outside noise, rain and ash through every imperfect seal.

**Here.** A 36 m/s wind puts roughly ±1 kPa on the façades — two orders of magnitude above the fan pressurisation — so seals must hold under reversal and the control must respond to wind direction.

#### Elephant door wind load and seal compression

A 12 × 9 m leaf at q_p ≈ 2 kPa (estimate at 30 m height, Icelandic exposure) carries about 216 kN. Limit leaf deflection to L/240 under design wind and specify seals whose compression range exceeds that deflection, verified under both positive and negative pressure.

**Why.** A door that measures Rw 55 in the lab and bows 40 mm in a gale is an Rw 35 door on the day it matters.

**Here.** The single most Icelandic detail on the stage. Elsewhere elephant-door wind load is a formality; here it is the governing case for the seal design.

#### Displacement ventilation to shrink the air volume

Condition only the occupied zone — the lower 5–6 m of 30 m. Low-level displacement supply, high-level extract at the perms. Mixing 6,000 m² against ~800 kW of lighting load at ΔT 8 K needs about 83 m³/s (≈300,000 m³/h) (estimate); displacement cuts that materially, and silencer size follows volume.

**Why.** Every m³/s not moved is silencer cross-section, fan power and regenerated noise you never have to attenuate.

**Here.** A 30 m tall stage stratifies naturally, and with ambient near 5 °C for most of the year the extract-side free cooling does most of the work for nothing.

#### Winch, hoist and motion-control noise

Chain hoists, point hoists and motion-control rigs on resilient mounts with brake-release damping; require sound power to ISO 3744 and a silent-run mode. Motion-control track flatness and the floor's vibration criterion — VC-A, 50 μm/s RMS (estimate) — govern camera stability.

**Why.** Rigging noise is intermittent and impulsive, which is worse for a take than steady noise, and brake clunks transmit structurally rather than through the air.

#### Hydro-acoustics and tank plant

Water carries sound at ~1,480 m/s and couples pumps, filtration and wave machines straight into the set. All tank plant on inertia bases, flexible couplings at every tank-wall crossing, silent-run modes for takes, and hydrophone monitoring for underwater dialogue and diver comms.

**Why.** A running filtration pump is inaudible in air and unmissable on an underwater microphone or to a diver at 6 m.

#### Air absorption as a design term

Include the 4mV term: T = 0.161V/(A + 4mV), with m from ISO 9613-1:1993. At 180,000 m³, m ≈ 0.009 /m at 4 kHz, 20 °C, 50% RH gives roughly 7,200 m² sabins of free absorption (estimate) — comparable to half the treated surface area.

**Why.** Above 2 kHz the air, not the treatment, sets the decay in a room this size, and the effect swings with temperature and humidity.

**Here.** Icelandic ambient humidity is high, frequently 75–85% RH, and stage air is conditioned from that. HF air absorption differs from a dry-climate stage — do not copy a Californian model's high-frequency decay.

#### Event-driven re-test and inspection regime

Re-test envelope and isolation after 12 months, after any felt earthquake, after any significant ash fall, and after any door or seal replacement. Retain the original intensity scan as baseline. Inspect the isolation void annually for bridging, debris and water.

**Why.** Isolation degrades by event, not by age. A stage that passed at handover proves nothing three winters later.

**Here.** Iceland supplies all three degradation events routinely — seismic swarms, tephra fall and 36 m/s storms — so the regime here is event-driven, not calendar-driven.

### What gets got wrong

- Specifying walls in millimetres instead of kg/m². Sound insulation follows surface mass, and Icelandic basaltic and rhyolitic aggregates vary widely in density — a wall built to the drawn thickness can quietly come in 1–2 dB light across the whole envelope. Specify surface mass and test density to EN 12390-7.
- Matching the two leaves of the double wall. Identical thicknesses put the mass-air-mass dip and the coincidence dip at the same frequency and double the loss. Make the leaves deliberately dissimilar.
- Commissioning NC with the lighting rig off and the HVAC at part flow. A rig of fan-cooled LED fixtures will breach NC-25 on its own. The acceptance test must run the full rig at 100% and the plant at design flow, and — in Iceland — in wind and rain.
- Reaching for gravel ballast or a sedum roof as the rain-noise fix. Both are effectively unusable at Icelandic design wind. Put the mass in the deck instead and damp the metal skin with a bonded constrained-layer membrane, not a loose-laid one.
- Hanging a sprinkler drop, a cable tray or one rigging point off the outer roof deck. This is the single commonest rain-noise failure and it undoes the whole roof build-up for the cost of one bracket.
- Pouring a continuous ground slab from the construction mill into the stage. The most frequent flanking failure on any lot, and worse in Iceland where the mill has to be indoors and therefore close.
- Sizing silencers and fan speed on clean-filter resistance. One ash event loads the filters in days, the fan speeds up, the silencer regenerates noise, and the stage drifts off NC with nothing visibly wrong.
- Assuming EN ISO 16283-1 applies. Its stated scope is 10–250 m³; Askja is 180,000 m³. Agree a formal deviation plus ISO 15186-2 sound-intensity scanning before anyone signs an acceptance criterion.
- Specifying RT 1.0 s in a 180,000 m³ room. With only 21,600 m² of surface, even 100% Class A coverage of walls and ceiling gives about 1.9 s. This is physics, not budget, and it must be settled at concept stage.
- Treating the interconnect between the two 2,000 m² stages as an operable-partition problem. Folding and vertical-lift partitions top out around Rw 55–60 in manufacturer tests, roughly 20 dB short. Use two independent walls with a walk-through void.
- Forgetting that a 12 × 9 m elephant door leaf carries around 216 kN in an Icelandic gale. Leaf deflection breaks seal compression, and an Rw 55 door becomes an Rw 35 door in exactly the weather where you needed it.
- Building a sprung stage floor because the word sounds better than 'dead'. A sprung or hollow floor drums under footfall and dolly wheels and every boom mic hears it. Bond the overlay to the slab.
- Copying a Californian stage's high-frequency decay model. At 180,000 m³ the 4mV air-absorption term dominates above 2 kHz, and Iceland's 75–85% ambient humidity gives a different m than a dry climate does.
- Tuning low-frequency traps to 120 Hz. Iceland is 50 Hz, so transformer and dimmer hum sits at 100/200/300 Hz — an octave-third off any US-derived design, and in the band the room absorbs worst.
- Letting a rainwater downpipe, a smoke vent or a shared duct inside the acoustic envelope. Each is a permanent noise source or a 40 dB hole in a 75 dB wall, and each is far cheaper to design out than to retrofit.
- Believing a 75 dB inter-stage wall solves special-effects days. A 130 dB peak lands at 55 dB next door, clearly audible against NC-25. The wall is a floor; the answer is a lot-wide lock-up schedule.
- Leaving the isolation void uninspectable. If nobody can walk it, nobody ever finds the bridge — and in Iceland the void will be bridged eventually by settlement, seismic movement, ash or debris.

---

## 3. Electrical supply and distribution

*Askja (6,000 m2 / 180,000 m3), Lon water stage and Stages 3-4, Sugar Land Studios, Mosfellsbaer, Iceland*

### Essential

#### Incoming HV service and connection agreement (Veitur DSO / Landsnet TSO)

Dual radial 33 kV 50 Hz from Veitur's nearest adveitustod; 8 MVA contracted firm + 4 MVA curtailable. 8 MVA at 33 kV = 140 A. Iceland's DSO structure (published): 33 kV between substations, 11 kV substation-to-distribution-transformer, 0.4 kV to customer.

**Why.** One feeder is a single point of failure for the whole lot. Contracted capacity sets the monthly capacity charge (aflgjald) and caps every load calculation below it.

**Here.** Landsvirkjun has publicly stated it will not supply new smelters and has refused e-fuel projects citing system capacity constraints, so capacity must be negotiated years ahead. Nearest Landsnet injection point is Korpa (132 kV, Reykjavik). Buy forgangsorka (priority) for stages, skerdanleg orka (curtailable) for tank heating and EV charging.

#### Whole-lot load calculation and diversity model

Connected ~15,000 kVA: Askja 2x2,500 kVA production + 1,600 kVA house; Lon 2,000 + 1,250 kVA; Stages 3/4 2x1,600 + 1,000 kVA; mill/workshops 1,600 kVA; offices/post 1,000 kVA; backlot/EV 1,250 kVA. Diversity 0.45 gives ~6.8 MVA maximum demand. Estimate.

**Why.** Sized on connected load you buy 15 MVA you cannot get; sized on average you brown out the night a full unit lights a 6,000 m2 build.

**Here.** No electric space heating anywhere in the model - geothermal district heat carries the whole 180,000 m3 thermal load, which removes several MW that the same shed would need in Canada or Norway. That is the single largest Iceland effect on the load schedule.

#### Askja production lighting load density

Design 60 W/m2 LED-first = 360 kW connected; legacy tungsten/HMI capability 150 W/m2 = 900 kW. Diversified stage peak taken at 1,400 kW / 1,475 kVA at 0.95 PF. Tie-in capacity provided far above this at 5,196 kVA. Estimate, calibrated to modern LED-first stage practice.

**Why.** Under-provisioning forces productions onto diesel generators outside a stage that was sold as fully serviced.

**Here.** Cheap low-carbon power makes generator-free shooting the commercial pitch; the grid constraint makes it hard to deliver. Resolve with storage, not with a bigger connection.

#### Package substations at each stage

11/0.4 kV cast-resin transformers, Dyn11, class F1 flammability / C2 climatic / E2 environmental per IEC 60076-11, 6% impedance, EU Ecodesign Reg. 548/2014 Tier 2 losses. Askja: 2 x 2,500 kVA production + 1 x 1,600 kVA house. 2,500 kVA gives 3,608 A at 400 V.

**Why.** Long 400 V runs from a central substation are unaffordable in copper and unacceptable in volt-drop for a 100 m stage.

**Here.** Cast resin, not oil - no oil bund to freeze, no fire load next to a stage, and no oil to import and dispose of on an island.

#### Production/house transformer separation

Physically separate transformers, separate LV boards, separate neutrals and separate earthing conductors for production power and house power. No shared neutral, ever. House = HVAC, hoists, work light, offices, life safety. Production = anything a crew plugs into.

**Why.** A chiller starting or a door motor tripping must never appear as a level shift on a camera-critical lighting feed.

**Here.** House load is unusually small here because heating is hydronic geothermal, so the house transformer can be modest and the split is cheap to make.

#### LV main switchboard

IEC 61439-1/-2, Form 4 Type 7, 4,000 A tinned-copper busbar, Icw 65 kA 1 s (specify 80 kA), IP31 in switchroom / IP54 external. Air circuit breakers with electronic trip units (LSIG), draw-out, motorised, with ZSI zone-selective interlocking.

**Why.** Form 4 Type 7 lets you work on one outgoing way with the rest live - unavoidable on a lot that never fully shuts down.

#### Bus-section breaker held normally open

2 x 2,500 kVA at 6% Z gives 60.1 kA each; paralleled gives ~120 kA, beyond any economic LV device. Run split with an interlocked, auto-transfer bus-section ACB.

**Why.** Paralleling the two production transformers doubles prospective fault current past the switchgear rating and past any sane arc-flash incident energy.

#### Company switches / tie-in points, Askja

6 x 1,250 A TP&N (866 kVA each, 5,196 kVA total) at 25 m centres on both long walls; plus 8 x 630 A (436 kVA) and 8 x 400 A (277 kVA). Each with lockable isolator, phase rotation indicator, MID-class metering and a bonded earth stud.

**Why.** A gaffer arriving with a 1,000 A order and finding 400 A outlets loses a day. Tie-ins on both long walls kill 100 m cable runs.

#### Powerlock / Powersafe connectors

ITT Cannon Powerlock and compatible Powersafe: T4 400 A and T6 660 A, uprated to 500 A and 800 A on the higher-rated contact band; IP67 mated, IP2X finger-proof, 1,000 V, mechanically keyed and secondary-locked, source/drain/line variants. 1,250 A tie-in = 2 sets of 800 A per phase.

**Why.** Keying and secondary lock are what stop a neutral being pulled under load or a live source connector being touched.

**Here.** IP67 matters more here than anywhere: connectors get made up on a wet apron in horizontal rain. Specify the drain-side covers and use them.

#### IEC 60309-2 outlet ladder

Per stage: 24 x 125 A 5-pin (86.6 kVA), 48 x 63 A (43.6 kVA), 96 x 32 A (22.2 kVA), 400 V 3P+N+E, 6 h clock position, IP67; plus 230 V 16 A CEE and Schuko (Type F) at 6 m centres round the wall. Colour red for 400 V, blue for 230 V.

**Why.** Most of what plugs in on a stage is 32 A and 63 A; running everything off 400 A headers wastes distro and time.

**Here.** Iceland is 230 V / 50 Hz with Type C/F sockets, so Schuko is the house standard for practicals, chargers and video village.

#### Cable ramps and drive-over bridges

5-channel yellow-jacket ramps (up to 55 mm cable) for crossings; ADA-compliant low-profile ramps in walkways; steel drive-over bridges rated 44 t gross for backlot roads and the loading apron; hinged lids, high-visibility yellow, non-slip surface.

**Why.** A crushed 240 mm2 feeder is a fire; a tripped crew member is a claim.

**Here.** Rubber ramps go rigid and crack below freezing and lift in high wind. Specify low-temperature-grade compound (-40 C) and pin or ballast every ramp - a loose ramp in a 36 m/s gust is a projectile.

#### Central battery emergency lighting

EN 50171 central power supply, 3 h duration per EN 1838 / EN 50172, 1 lux minimum on escape routes and 15 lux at high-risk task areas, automatic monthly function and annual duration testing with addressable per-luminaire reporting.

**Why.** A 30 m tall blacked-out stage with no daylight is unevacuable without emergency lighting.

**Here.** There is no borrowed daylight to fall back on: a Reykjavik December day runs from roughly 11:20 to 15:30, about 4 h 17 min at the solstice, so a power loss at any normal call time is total darkness inside and out.

#### Essential services board

Dedicated generator- and UPS-backed board per stage: emergency lighting, fire alarm, smoke extract, elephant door drives, personnel door releases, tank filtration and dive comms at Lon, hoist brakes and the perms access lift.

**Why.** You need to be able to get people out, and get divers out of a tank, on a dead grid.

#### Earthing arrangement and system type

TN-S from the star point of each 11/0.4 kV transformer; no PEN downstream of the transformer. Installed to the IST HD 60364 series, mandated in Iceland by Reglugerd nr. 678/2009 um raforkuvirki; HV earthing to EN 50522 with touch/step voltage verification.

**Why.** A PEN conductor shared between house and production puts load current on the earth of everything a crew touches.

**Here.** Icelandic practice follows CENELEC HD 60364, not the NEC. A US-trained gaffer's Article 530 assumptions do not apply and the site rules must say so in writing.

#### Earth electrode system

Buried ring electrode (70 mm2 bare copper) round each stage bonded to the foundation reinforcement, plus deep-driven rods to the water table and conductive backfill. Target <=1 ohm at the substation, <=10 ohm at each stage; measure by fall-of-potential to IEC 61557-5.

**Why.** High earth resistance means high touch voltage on a fault, and unstable reference for every piece of technical equipment.

**Here.** Young Icelandic basalt and lava have very high soil resistivity - hundreds to thousands of ohm-metres - so a rod array that works in London clay will not reach 1 ohm here. Budget for foundation earthing, deep electrodes and conductive backfill from the start; it cannot be retrofitted under a poured stage floor.

#### Equipotential bonding of stage structure

Bond perms steelwork, catwalks, wall rails, pipe grid, the elephant door leaf and frame, tank steel, lifts, rigging beams and the water-tank grating to the main earthing terminal with 50-120 mm2 copper, to IEC 60364-5-54, with accessible test clamps.

**Why.** A lamp faulting to a perms rail 30 m up energises the entire grid a rigger is clipped to.

**Here.** Seismic movement works bonding connections loose. Use exothermic welds or bolted, spring-washered joints and re-test on the annual cycle rather than relying on clamps.

#### Lon water tank electrical zoning

IEC 60364-7-702 zones 0/1/2 applied to the 2,400 m2 tank and 20 x 20 x 6 m dive well. Zone 0: SELV 12 V AC / 30 V DC only, source outside the zones. Supplementary equipotential bonding of every conductive part including ladders, grating, rails and pipework.

**Why.** This is the one place on the lot where an electrical fault kills someone in seconds.

#### Residual current protection

Type A 30 mA on all socket outlets up to 32 A; Type B 30 mA where VFDs, EV chargers or DC-injecting loads are present (per IEC 62423, since Type A is blinded by smooth DC residual current); 300 mA time-delayed selective RCDs upstream for fire protection.

**Why.** A Type A device in front of an EV charger or a drive can be rendered inoperative by DC leakage and will not trip when it matters.

**Here.** Iceland's very high EV penetration means the DC-leakage problem is present all over the lot, not just at the chargers.

#### Emergency switching-off (stage kill switches)

Emergency switching-off devices to HD 60364-5-537 at every personnel door and at the stage manager's desk, latching, red-on-yellow, dropping all production power but retaining emergency lighting, fire alarm, smoke extract and door drives.

**Why.** Someone in contact with a live conductor 30 m up needs the whole floor dead in one action by anyone standing at a door.

**Here.** Do not label these to NEC Article 530 wording; Iceland runs on HD 60364 and the signage must match the code the inspector uses.

#### Fire detection interfaces and effects inhibit

EN 54 system: aspirating smoke detection to EN 54-20 Class A at high level plus EN 54-12 beam detectors, because stratification in a 30 m volume defeats point detectors. Keyswitch-supervised two-stage inhibit for atmospheric effects, logged, with a permit regime and automatic reinstatement.

**Why.** Haze and smoke effects false-alarm a normal system daily, and a crew that has learned to ignore the alarm is the real hazard.

#### Fire-resistant cabling and fire-fighter's switch

Enhanced-grade fire-resistant cable (BS 8434-2 / EN 50200 PH120) for alarm, emergency lighting, smoke extract and door drives. Fire-fighter's isolating switch for production supply at the fire brigade access point, clearly identified.

**Why.** Life safety circuits have to keep working while the thing that started the fire is still burning.

**Here.** The Mosfellsbaer brigade is small and mutual aid from Reykjavik takes time; the switch has to be findable by someone who has never been on the lot.

#### Revenue metering

DSO revenue meter to IEC 62053-22 class 0.5S (specify 0.2S), with separate registers for priority and curtailable energy, 15-minute demand, and reactive energy. Landsnet transmission charges pass through the DSO tariff.

**Why.** The bill is the second-largest line item on a stage's operating cost and it is settled on this meter alone.

**Here.** Verify how the curtailable/priority split is metered and settled before signing - it is the difference between a cheap tariff and a shoot stopped mid-day.

#### DMX distribution and splitters

DMX512-A (ANSI E1.11) on 5-pin XLR (ANSI E1.27-2 cable, 110 ohm), opto-isolated splitters at each perms zone, 32 devices maximum per unterminated run, 120 ohm terminators, star topology from gateways rather than long daisy chains.

**Why.** A single bad terminator on a 100 m stage takes out a whole grid zone and is nearly impossible to find at height.

#### Seismic restraint of electrical plant

Anchorage and qualification to IEC 60068-3-3 / IEEE 693 (high performance level) for switchgear, transformers, battery racks, UPS and BESS. Design per IST EN 1998-1:2004/NA:2010; the Icelandic National Annex uses reference peak ground acceleration agR steps of 0.05, 0.10, 0.15, 0.20, 0.30, 0.40 and 0.50 g.

**Why.** An unrestrained 2,500 kVA transformer that walks off its plinth tears its LV bushings off and arcs.

**Here.** Mosfellsbaer sits in the capital region rather than the South Iceland Seismic Zone, so expect the lower end of the national range (estimate 0.10-0.15 g agR), but the design earthquake is real and must be taken from the National Annex for the actual site, not assumed. Flexible braided links at every rigid transformer/busbar interface.

#### External plant wind and snow design

Substation buildings, BESS containers, generator enclosures, cable ladder on external walls and lighting columns designed to IST EN 1991-1-4:2005/NA:2010 with fundamental basic wind velocity vb,0 = 36.0 m/s, plus site topography and exposure factors. Snow to IST EN 1991-1-3.

**Why.** Anything outdoors that is not designed for this wind becomes debris that damages the stage envelope it sits next to.

**Here.** 36 m/s is the fundamental value; local gusting in Iceland regularly exceeds it. Louvres, radiator faces and ventilation openings need snow hoods and baffles, and door swings need to be checked for wind-catch on a lot where a door can be torn off a hinge.

#### Neutral conductor sizing for harmonic load

Full-size or 200% neutral on all production final circuits, busway and transformers where triplen content is expected; neutral current can approach 173% of phase current with heavy 3rd-harmonic load. Apply the IEC 60364-5-52 harmonic derating factors (0.86 at 15-33% 3rd harmonic content, 0.86 on neutral current basis above 33%).

**Why.** The neutral has no overcurrent device on it; it just gets hot and eventually sets fire to the trench.

#### Testing, certification and commissioning

Initial verification to IST HD 60364-6: continuity, insulation resistance (>=1 Mohm at 500 V), polarity, earth fault loop impedance, RCD trip times, phase rotation, prospective fault current. HV commissioning to EN 50522 including step/touch voltage measurement. Full power quality baseline before handover.

**Why.** Without a measured baseline you cannot later prove whether a fault is yours or the DSO's.

**Here.** Certification must be by an Icelandic-registered electrical contractor under Reglugerd nr. 678/2009 and notified to the DSO; an imported contractor's paperwork will not satisfy the inspector.

### Expected

#### On-site primary substation, 33/11 kV

2 x 10 MVA ONAN 33/11 kV transformers, Dyn11, ONAN/ONAF, OLTC +/-10% in 1.25% steps, IEC 60076-1/-2/-5. 33 kV side 175 A, 11 kV side 525 A. Separate founded building outside the acoustic envelope of every stage.

**Why.** An 11 kV-only service will not carry 15 MVA of connected load across four stages plus a mill.

**Here.** Site the building and its bund for ISO 12944-2 C4/C5-M coastal corrosivity (Kollafjordur) and for basic wind velocity vb,0 = 36.0 m/s per IST EN 1991-1-4:2005/NA:2010. Radiators and louvres need snow hoods against driven snow.

#### 33 kV and 11 kV switchgear

IEC 62271-200, 36 kV / 12 kV, 630-1250 A busbar, 25 kA 1 s short-time withstand, internal arc classification IAC AFLR 25 kA 1 s. Vacuum interrupters, SF6-free (dry air or fluoronitrile) insulation. Motorised, remote-operable.

**Why.** Arc containment protects the operator; SF6-free is now a compliance requirement, not a preference.

**Here.** Iceland is in the EEA, so Regulation (EU) 2024/573 applies: SF6 prohibited in new MV switchgear up to and including 24 kV put into operation from 1 Jan 2026, and 24-52 kV from 1 Jan 2030. A new-build in 2026 must specify SF6-free at 11 kV outright.

#### MV protection and substation automation

Numerical relays (SEL-751 / ABB REF615 / SIPROTEC 5 class) with ANSI 50/51, 50N/51N, 87T, 27/59, 81U/81O, 25, 32, 46, 49, 67N. IEC 61850-8-1 MMS and GOOSE on a redundant PRP/HSR ring; IEEE 1588 PTP time sync.

**Why.** Grading between the DSO fuse, the 11 kV feeder and the 400 V ACB is what stops a stage fault taking the lot dark.

**Here.** Include 81 rate-of-change-of-frequency and wide frequency settings: EN 50160 allows 50 Hz +/-2% for 95% of a week and +/-15% for 100% of the time on systems with no synchronous connection to an interconnected grid - which is exactly Iceland.

#### 11 kV site ring main and RMUs

Closed-loop 11 kV ring, 3-core 240 mm2 Al XLPE (IEC 60502-2, 12/20 kV), ~400 A per leg, in a segregated duct bank. Extensible RMUs at each package substation with fault passage indicators reporting to the EPMS.

**Why.** A ring lets you lose one leg and keep every stage lit; radial spurs cost you a shoot day.

**Here.** Everything underground. Iceland is among the windiest inhabited places on earth and Icelandic DSOs have been undergrounding distribution precisely because overhead lines do not survive the storms.

#### Factor-K / 200% neutral production transformers

K-13 (or factor-K to BS 7821-4 / EN 50464-3 method) with 200% rated neutral terminal and neutral bar; electrostatic screen between windings, earthed to the technical earth bar. Delta primary to trap triplen circulating currents.

**Why.** LED drivers and dimmers push 3rd-harmonic current that adds arithmetically in the neutral; a standard transformer runs hot and its neutral runs hotter than the phases.

#### Production power busway to the company switches

Sandwich busbar trunking, 4,000 A copper, IP55, IEC 61439-6, with tap-off boxes; 200% neutral bar. Routed at high level outside the acoustic envelope, on spring/neoprene AV hangers.

**Why.** Cable of this size cannot be routed or re-routed; busway lets you add a tie-in point later without a shutdown.

**Here.** Keep the busway magnetic field below ~0.5 microtesla at stage-floor level and at the mic positions; 4,000 A of unbalanced busbar is an audible hum source in a stage designed for NC-20.

#### Company switches, Lon and Stages 3/4

Lon: 3 x 1,250 A + 6 x 400 A, all above the maximum tank flood line and outside splash zones. Stages 3/4: 2 x 1,250 A + 6 x 400 A each, with a cross-stage tie so a single production can take both stages through the acoustic interconnect on one supply and one earth.

**Why.** Two stages joined acoustically become one set; two separate earthing systems bonded by a set piece is how you get circulating currents and audible hum.

#### US-format tie-in kit (Cam-Lok / stage pin)

Adaptor sets: Powerlock to 400 A Cam-Lok E1016 series, plus 100 A Bates / stage-pin panels. Colour-code to both EU (brown/black/grey/blue/green-yellow) and US (black/red/blue/white/green) conventions with a permanent legend at every tie-in.

**Why.** An American crew that mis-reads blue as a phase instead of neutral energises a neutral bar.

**Here.** Iceland sells itself to US and UK productions. Assume every crew arrives with the wrong tails and stock both.

#### Perms distribution boxes

~120 boxes on a 10 x 10 m grid at 30 m, each with 1 x 125 A + 2 x 63 A + 4 x 32 A + 4 x 16 A CEE, 2 x DMX512-A 5-pin, 2 x etherCON Cat6A, 1 x duplex OS2 fibre, 1 x intercom. Fed from busway drops with local 125 A isolator.

**Why.** Hauling a 63 A cable 30 m to the deck for one lamp is the tax you pay for skipping this.

**Here.** Boxes must be dead silent - no cooling fans, natural convection only. Anything that hums at 30 m is directly over the mic in a stage this quiet.

#### In-floor cable trenches and floor boxes

300 x 200 mm trenches on a 10 m grid with flush, load-rated, gasketed lids (5 t point load, 40 t distributed on the main crossings). Floor boxes with 63 A + 32 A + data at 10 m centres. Trench lids acoustically sealed and gasketed.

**Why.** Surface cable on a 100 m stage is a trip hazard, a dolly-track obstacle and a nightmare on a strike.

**Here.** Trench drainage matters: a stage floor at 6,000 m2 with an elephant door open in driven rain will get water in, and a flooded live trench is fatal. Drain to a pumped sump, and put the trench earth bar above the flood line.

#### Generator hardstanding and shore-power tie-in

Two pads per stage, 12 x 6 m, reinforced for 40 t, bunded to contain 110% of the largest fuel tank, with 2 x 630 A and 2 x 400 A reverse-fed Powerlock inlets in a heated, IP66, lockable enclosure. Mechanical/electrical interlock against the utility supply (Kirk key plus ANSI 27/59).

**Why.** An unsynchronised back-feed onto the DSO network is the fastest way to lose your connection agreement and kill a lineworker.

**Here.** Heat the enclosure and trace-heat the interlock mechanism (from geothermal hot water, not electrically); frozen Kirk locks are a real winter failure mode.

#### Standby generating sets

2 x 2,000 kVA prime-rated, N+1, ISO 8528-1 performance class G3: steady-state frequency band +/-0.5%, steady-state voltage +/-1%, transient frequency -7/+10% recovering in <=3 s, transient voltage -15/+20% recovering in <=4 s (ISO 8528-5). 15 s to on-load. Acoustic enclosure <=65 dB(A) at 7 m.

**Why.** Class G2 or a commercial standby set will show as visible flicker and pitch shift on camera the moment a 300 kW lighting block strikes.

**Here.** Storms take out distribution here, so standby earns its keep even on a very clean grid. Enclosures must be rated for the wind and for driven snow ingress at the radiator face.

#### Fuel system

HVO100 to EN 15940 in bunded double-skin tanks to EN 12285-2, 48 h at full load (~2 x 40,000 L for 2 MVA sets), with polishing, water separation and monthly fuel analysis to ISO 4406/ASTM D7462.

**Why.** Untreated stored fuel goes off; a standby set that will not run is worse than none because you planned around it.

**Here.** All liquid fuel is imported to Iceland and can be weather-delayed for days, so bunker capacity is a resilience item not a convenience. HVO also keeps the lot's carbon claim honest against nearly zero-carbon grid power. Note HVO's poorer cold filter plugging point - specify arctic-grade and heat the tank from the geothermal loop.

#### Technical UPS

2 x 500 kVA modular, double-conversion, classified VFI-SS-111 per EN 62040-3, N+1 modules, 10 minutes autonomy at full load, input THDi <5%, unity output power factor. Serving machine rooms, post, DIT carts, the lighting console rooms and stage comms.

**Why.** A media server or a lighting network processor rebooting mid-take costs more than the UPS.

**Here.** Also decouples the sensitive load from the island grid's permitted 50 Hz +/-2% band, which matters for anything running on mains-derived clock.

#### Power factor correction, detuned

Automatic PFC with detuned reactors, p = 7% (tuned ~189 Hz) or 14% (~134 Hz), target 0.98 lagging, contactor stages with pre-charge resistors plus thyristor stages for fast-varying lighting loads. Capacitors 440-525 V rated for harmonic-rich duty.

**Why.** Undetuned capacitors resonate with the 5th and 7th harmonics of a stage full of LED drivers and blow themselves apart.

**Here.** Confirm the DSO's reactive-power charging threshold; correcting harder than the tariff requires wastes money and risks leading power factor at 03:00 when the lot is idle.

#### Screened isolation transformers for camera, video village and audio

Single-phase and 3-phase 1:1 isolating transformers, 10-150 kVA, double electrostatic screen, attenuation >=100 dB common mode, separately derived neutral referenced to the technical earth bar.

**Why.** Breaks the earth loop between the sound cart, the video village and the lighting distro, which is the usual source of mains hum on a recording.

#### Step-down transformers for US-format equipment

12 x 150 kVA 400 V delta to 120/208 V wye, Dyn11, K-13, 200% neutral, on castors with Powerlock in and Cam-Lok/stage-pin out. Total 1,800 kVA of step-down capacity across the lot.

**Why.** American lighting and grip packages arriving on a 230/400 V lot are otherwise useless.

**Here.** Iceland is a 230 V country; assume every incoming US production needs this and price it as a stage service rather than a rental.

#### Technical earth and single-point earthing

Insulated technical earth bar (TE) per stage, 250 mm2 copper, star-connected back to one point at the production transformer neutral. Separately identified from the protective earth. Bonded, never floating.

**Why.** Multiple earth references across a 6,000 m2 floor create circulating currents that appear as hum on audio and banding on video.

#### Surge protective devices

Type 1+2 at each LV main board (Iimp 25 kA per pole, 10/350 us), Type 2 at each stage sub-board (In 20 kA, 8/20 us), Type 3 at technical equipment, all to IEC 61643-11, coordinated per IEC 61643-12 with remote status contacts to the EPMS.

**Why.** Switching surges from 4,000 A contactors and generator tie-ins destroy LED drivers and network gear whether or not there is lightning.

**Here.** Thunderstorms are rare in Iceland (a handful of days a year), but volcanic plumes generate intense lightning, and the site is at high geomagnetic latitude where quasi-DC geomagnetically induced currents can bias transformer cores. Do the EN 62305 risk assessment rather than assuming a low-lightning country needs nothing.

#### Lightning protection system

EN/IEC 62305 risk assessment; if required, class III/IV air termination on the roof and stack, down conductors bonded to the ring earth, with all penetrations bonded. Roof-mounted terminations only where a penetration-free strategy allows it.

**Why.** The roof is the acoustic barrier; every fixing through it is a leak, a thermal bridge and a drumming point.

**Here.** Rain and hail drumming on a long-span roof is the worst acoustic problem this stage has, so the electrical rule is simple: no cable, no conduit, no earth tape penetrating the roof. Route everything through the wall at low level and take the LPS down the outside.

#### Arc flash study and PPE

IEEE 1584-2018 incident energy study at every board, labels in Icelandic and English showing incident energy (cal/cm2), arc flash boundary and required PPE. Arc-rated clothing to IEC 61482-2 with ATPV matched to the calculated energy. Maintenance-mode (reduced-let-through) switch on every ACB.

**Why.** Without a study you are guessing at PPE in front of a board with 60 kA prospective fault current.

**Here.** Bilingual labels are not a courtesy; the crew rotates internationally and the local electricians work in Icelandic.

#### Lighting control network

Dedicated lighting VLAN, redundant OM4/OS2 fibre backbone in a ring, Cat6A to every outlet, sACN (ANSI E1.31) and Art-Net 4 as transports, RDM (ANSI E1.20) for device management, PTP or Art-Net sync for frame-locked output. Managed switches with IGMP snooping and multicast filtering.

**Why.** Unmanaged multicast from 64 universes of sACN will flatten a shared network and stall the lighting board mid-take.

#### Dimmer and constant-power racks

Per stage: 4 x 96-way racks, dual-mode (ETC Sensor3 ThruPower class), 3 kW and 6 kW modules, switchable dimmer/relay per circuit, with 400 us rise-time chokes. Sited in a remote, acoustically isolated dimmer room, not on the stage.

**Why.** Even an LED-first stage still needs dimmed tungsten for practicals and firelight, and dimmer chokes buzz audibly.

**Here.** A stage designed to be quiet enough to shoot dialogue under a driven-rain roof cannot then have racks humming on the floor. The dimmer room is an acoustic decision as much as an electrical one.

#### Architectural control processor and house work light

ETC Paradigm / Mosaic class processor, independent of the production console, controlling DALI-2 (IEC 62386) house luminaires. Work light 500 lux at floor, 100 lux housekeeping, blackout override, blue running lights, and a hard 'lights out' key at the stage door.

**Why.** House light must be controllable by a stage manager who is not a lighting programmer, and must never be on the production console.

#### Backlot, roadway and apron lighting

LED, DMX-addressable, dimmable to zero, on photocell plus presence detection rather than an astronomical timeclock. Column bases and brackets designed for vb,0 = 36.0 m/s (IST EN 1991-1-4:2005/NA:2010). Separate blackout circuit for night exteriors.

**Why.** Site lighting that cannot be fully extinguished on command contaminates every night exterior on the backlot.

**Here.** Sub-arctic light is the driver both ways: from November to February the lot runs on artificial light for most of the working day, a large sustained load; in June there is civil twilight all night, so genuine night exteriors outdoors are only possible in the dark months, and the backlot lighting has to be capable of creating darkness as well as light.

#### EV charging with dynamic load management

30 x 22 kW AC (IEC 62196-2 Type 2) and 4 x 150 kW DC (CCS2, IEC 61851-23) = 1,260 kW connected, managed by OCPP 2.0.1 with dynamic load balancing that throttles to as low as 6 A per point when a stage is at peak.

**Why.** 1.26 MW of uncontrolled charging will set the monthly capacity charge on its own.

**Here.** Iceland has one of the highest EV shares in the world, so crew and unit vehicles are electric by default and charging is a base-load, not a nicety. It is also the ideal curtailable load to shed against the aflgjald peak.

#### Unit base shore power

Serviced pads for trailers, honeywagons and production offices: 24 x 63 A and 12 x 125 A 400 V CEE outlets, individually metered, RCD-protected, on a heated pedestal.

**Why.** Trailers running their own diesel sets outside a stage put exhaust and engine noise directly into the shoot.

**Here.** Cheap low-carbon power makes shore power obviously right here, and it is the visible proof of the lot's carbon claim. Pedestals need to survive being buried in drifted snow.

#### Construction, mill and workshop power

Separate 'build power' distribution from shoot power: 1,600 kVA transformer feeding 63 A welding sockets, 32 A tool circuits, dust extraction VFDs, CNC and paint booth (with ATEX-classified zones per IEC 60079-10-1 in the spray area). 110 V CTE (55-0-55) site transformers for hand tools.

**Why.** A welder striking on the same board as a lighting rig is a visible dip on camera; and reduced low-voltage tools are the standard for wet, temporary work.

#### Motor control centres and VFDs

MCCs for AHUs, smoke extract, elephant door drives, stage lifts, tank circulation and filtration (~800 m3/h turnover, ~90 kW), wave generators and dump-tank compressors. VFDs with integral EMC filters to EN IEC 61800-3 category C2, and dV/dt or sine filters on runs over 50 m.

**Why.** Unfiltered drives inject broadband noise onto the same LV system a sound recordist is working off.

**Here.** Lon's tank heating is a geothermal heat exchanger, not electric: heating 4,800 m3 from 5 C to 30 C is about 139 MWh (2.9 MW thermal over 48 h). Doing that electrically would be a third of the lot's whole connection. The electrical load is only the pumps.

#### Perms hoist and winch power

100 x 1 t point hoists at ~3 kW each = 300 kW connected, on a dedicated hoist distribution with direct-acting brakes, dual-brake redundancy for loads over people, phase-failure and phase-reversal protection, and a supervised emergency stop ring covering the whole grid.

**Why.** A phase reversal on a chain hoist drives a load up when the operator pressed down.

#### Cable containment and corrosion specification

Internal: hot-dip galvanised to EN ISO 1461. External and within 1 km of the shore: 316L stainless or GRP, ISO 12944-2 corrosivity C5-M, ISO 9223 category C5. All fixings A4-80 stainless. Seismic bracing and transverse restraint on every tray run.

**Why.** Galvanised tray in coastal salt spray is rusting within two winters and the fixings fail before the tray does.

**Here.** Coastal salt on Kollafjordur plus geothermal hydrogen sulphide is an aggressive combination. Iceland's 24-hour H2S health limit is 50 microgram/m3 under Reglugerd nr. 514/2010, set because Hellisheidi's plume regularly reaches the capital area.

#### Cable sizing with cold-ambient rating factors

Sized to IEC 60364-5-52. Iceland's low ambient permits an uprating factor: roughly 1.12 (PVC) and 1.15 (XLPE/EPR) at 20 C against the 30 C reference (IEC 60364-5-52 Table B.52.14). Indicative single-core rubber flexible free-air ratings: 185 mm2 ~500 A, 240 mm2 ~590 A. Verify against the manufacturer's tables; derate for grouping and harmonics.

**Why.** Getting the ambient factor wrong in either direction either wastes six figures of copper or overheats a 240 mm2 feeder buried in a trench.

**Here.** Genuine Iceland advantage: mean annual temperature in the capital area is around 5 C and design maxima are low, so the same conductor legitimately carries more current here than in Los Angeles. Do not take the uprating on cable inside a hot stage under lights, and do not take it on buried duct-bank runs sized on soil thermal resistivity.

#### Critical spares holding and maintenance contracts

On-site spares: one 2,500 kVA transformer (or a contracted 72 h replacement), a full set of ACB trip units, RMU vacuum bottles, BESS inverter modules, doubled filter sets, and 500 m of each feeder cable size with made-up Powerlock tails.

**Why.** Lead time on a cast-resin transformer is months, and a stage with no power has no revenue.

**Here.** Everything structural and electrical is imported and shipping is weather-dependent; a part that is three days away in mainland Europe can be three weeks away in February. Hold spares that would be uneconomic anywhere else.

#### Icelandic-language site electrical rules and competence regime

Written stage electrical rules in Icelandic and English covering tie-in procedure, lockout/tagout, permitted connector types, the emergency switching-off devices, effects inhibit permits and dive-well electrical control. Competence register of who may operate the company switches.

**Why.** The connectors, colour codes, code base and earthing arrangement all differ from what a US or UK crew expects; the rules have to say so before someone assumes.

**Here.** A small local labour pool means most of the crew on any given show is foreign and rotating. The written regime is doing the work that institutional memory does at Pinewood or Burbank.

### State of the art

#### Optical arc-flash detection on LV and MV boards

Point and lens sensors on every busbar and cable chamber, trip in <2.5 ms plus breaker time (ABB REA / Arcteq AQ-100 class), current-supervised to avoid flash-photography false trips.

**Why.** At 60 kA prospective fault an arc in the production board is a life-changing injury; conventional grading takes hundreds of milliseconds.

**Here.** A small labour pool means the person who opens that board may be the only qualified person on site that night. Engineer the risk out rather than relying on procedure.

#### Sealed cable transfer ports and generator duct bank

Weather-sealed, gasketed cable ports (brush-seal plus inflatable-bladder inner seal) in the stage wall at each tie-in, plus a below-grade duct bank from the generator hardstanding to each stage cable trench: 6 x 150 mm and 4 x 100 mm ducts with draw ropes.

**Why.** Without this, every generator tie-in means propping the elephant door open.

**Here.** This is the highest-value Iceland-specific item on the list. Holding a 20 m elephant door ajar for cables in horizontal rain and a 30 m/s wind wrecks the acoustic seal, drives water 30 m into the stage and makes the door itself a structural risk.

#### Generator synchronising and paralleling panel

Check-sync relay ANSI 25 with 5 degree / 0.2 Hz / 3% voltage windows, reverse power ANSI 32 at 5% for 5 s, ANSI 46/49/27/59/81. Closed-transition, soft-load transfer with a ramp rate of 10-20% per second so the change-over is invisible on camera.

**Why.** Open-transition transfer drops power for 100-200 ms - a lost take, a rebooted media server and a re-racked light board.

**Here.** Also the mechanism for peak-shaving against the contracted capacity: parallel the BESS or a genset for the two hours a night shoot exceeds the aflgjald threshold instead of buying capacity you cannot get.

#### Battery energy storage system (BESS)

4 MW / 8 MWh LFP, outdoor containerised, grid-forming inverters, sub-cycle response, round-trip efficiency >=88%. Fire and siting to NFPA 855 and EN IEC 62933-5-2, >=15 m from any occupied structure, with deflagration venting.

**Why.** It buys you the peaks the connection agreement will not sell you, and it makes the supply stiff enough that nobody sees a dip on camera.

**Here.** The direct answer to a grid at capacity and a generator refusing new industrial load. Contract 8 MVA firm, shave everything above it, and charge overnight and in the shoulder months. Also rides through the frequency excursions an isolated island grid is permitted under EN 50160.

#### Peak-shaving and demand controller

Predictive controller reading the incoming meter, the shooting schedule and the BESS state of charge, with a 15-minute rolling demand window matched to the DSO's aflgjald measurement interval. Sheds curtailable loads (EV charging, tank heating, workshop kilns) before it discharges the battery.

**Why.** Capacity charges are set by one 15-minute peak a month; without automation that peak will be a lighting test nobody logged.

**Here.** Load-shedding order should follow the priority/curtailable contract split so the shed loads are the ones you already bought on a cheaper interruptible tariff.

#### Active harmonic filters

Modular AHF, 3 x 300 A per production board (expandable), correcting harmonics 2nd-50th with >97% attenuation on selected orders, response <1 ms, with neutral-current (triplen) compensation. Target THDv <=3% at the production busbar.

**Why.** LED drivers, dimmers and VFDs together push THDi well past what passive means can hold, and voltage distortion is what the camera eventually sees.

#### Camera-quality power quality targets

Contract targets at the production busbar, tighter than EN 50160's 8%: THDv <=3%, individual harmonics h5/h7 <=2%, THDi <=5% at the PCC per IEEE 519-2022, flicker Pst <=0.8 / Plt <=0.6 per IEC 61000-4-15, voltage unbalance <=1%, IEC 61000-2-4 class 1 (5%) as the ceiling.

**Why.** Distortion and flicker show up as banding on high-frame-rate takes and as buzz in the audio, both of which are unfixable in post.

**Here.** EN 50160 is a public-supply floor, not a studio spec, and it explicitly permits 50 Hz +/-15% for 100% of the time on non-interconnected island systems. Write the tight numbers into the internal spec because the DSO will not guarantee them.

#### 60 Hz frequency converter

1 x 250 kVA static solid-state converter, 400 V 50 Hz in, 120/208 V 60 Hz out, THDv <2%, for legacy 60 Hz motion-control rigs, process projection, turntables and any equipment with a mains-synchronous motor.

**Why.** A transformer changes voltage, not frequency; a 60 Hz synchronous motor runs 17% slow on 50 Hz and every speed-critical effect is wrong.

**Here.** Only bites in 50 Hz territory. A US crew shooting 23.976 fps under legacy magnetic-ballast HMIs on a 50 Hz supply also has a flicker problem that no amount of clean power fixes - it needs flicker-free electronic ballasts or a matched shutter angle.

#### IT system with insulation monitoring for tank and dive operations

Medically-derived unearthed IT supply for the dive-well area: isolating transformer plus insulation monitoring device to IEC 61557-8, alarm at 50 kohm, with audible/visual annunciation at the dive supervisor's position and at the stage manager's desk.

**Why.** On an IT system the first earth fault does not produce a dangerous current - it produces an alarm, and you get the diver out before the second fault.

#### Production sub-metering and recharge

Meter at every company switch and every stage board: PMD class 0.5 to IEC 61557-12, kWh, kW peak, PF and per-phase demand, Modbus TCP to the EPMS, with per-production reporting and automatic export to the booking system.

**Why.** Power is a rechargeable stage service; without per-tie-in metering it goes into overhead and the lot subsidises its tenants.

**Here.** Cheap power is the sales pitch, so the metering has to be able to prove the saving to a production that is comparing Iceland against a diesel-generator budget in Hungary.

#### Power quality analysers

Class A instruments to IEC 61000-4-30 Ed.3 permanently installed at the incomer, at each production busbar and at each generator tie-in; continuous logging of dips, swells, interruptions, THD, flicker and frequency with 1-year retention.

**Why.** When a production claims your power ruined a day of high-speed plates, the log is the only thing that settles it.

**Here.** Also documents the island grid's frequency behaviour against EN 50160's wider island limits, which is evidence you need when the fault was upstream.

#### Electrical power management system (EPMS/SCADA)

IEC 61850 station bus plus Modbus TCP gateway, single-line mimic, alarm management, breaker status, transformer temperature (PT100 per winding, alarm 140 C / trip 155 C for class F cast resin), BESS state, generator status, and metering, on a segregated OT VLAN with no direct internet path.

**Why.** A lot this size cannot be operated by walking to switchrooms; and the OT network is a genuine attack surface.

**Here.** A small labour pool means remote diagnosis and vendor support over a controlled path is not optional - the specialist may be in Copenhagen.

#### Switchroom environmental control and filtration

Positive pressure at 25-50 Pa, ISO 16890 ePM1 60% (ex-F7) prefilter plus ePM1 85% (ex-F9), plus activated-carbon/potassium-permanganate chemical filtration. Target ISA 71.04 severity level G1 (mild) inside; monitor with copper and silver reactivity coupons quarterly.

**Why.** Sulphide creep corrosion on PCBs and silver-plated contacts kills relays, drives and control cards silently, then all at once.

**Here.** This is the single most under-specified item for an Icelandic build. Geothermal H2S attacks copper and silver in electronics; volcanic ash events load the filters catastrophically in days. Specify differential-pressure alarms, a doubled filter stock on site, and an ash-event mode that recirculates on 100% return air with outside air dampers shut.

#### Thermographic and condition monitoring programme

Annual thermographic survey to ISO 18434-1 under >=40% load, with permanently installed IR viewing windows on all boards above 400 A; continuous busbar and cable-termination temperature monitoring on the production boards, reporting to the EPMS.

**Why.** A loose 1,250 A termination announces itself as heat months before it announces itself as an arc.

**Here.** IR windows matter more where the corrosive atmosphere degrades terminations faster and where opening a live board means flying in a specialist.

### What gets got wrong

- Sizing the connection on connected load rather than on a negotiated capacity. Landsvirkjun has publicly refused supply to new industrial load citing system capacity, so the answer is not a bigger service - it is 8 MVA firm plus a 4 MW/8 MWh BESS and a peak-shaving controller matched to the 15-minute demand window the capacity charge is billed on.
- Assuming NEC Article 530 applies. Iceland runs on the IST HD 60364 series under Reglugerd nr. 678/2009 um raforkuvirki. Colour codes, earthing arrangement, connector types, RCD rules and certification are all different, and only an Icelandic-registered contractor can sign the installation off.
- Treating EN 50160 as a studio spec. It is a public-supply floor of 8% THDv, and for systems with no synchronous connection to an interconnected grid - Iceland exactly - it permits 50 Hz +/-2% for 95% of a week and +/-15% for 100% of the time. Camera-critical targets (THDv <=3%, Pst <=0.8) have to be written into the internal spec and delivered by filters, storage and UPS, not requested from the DSO.
- Running generator tie-in cables through the elephant door. In horizontal rain and a 30 m/s wind that destroys the acoustic seal, drives water deep into the stage and puts the door itself at structural risk. Below-grade duct banks from the generator pad to the floor trenches plus sealed cable transfer ports are the fix, and they have to be poured in on day one.
- Putting anything on the roof, or through it. Rain drumming on a long-span roof is this stage's worst acoustic problem and every conduit, earth tape and cable penetration is a leak, a thermal bridge and a drumming point. Take all services through the wall at low level and run the lightning down conductors on the outside.
- Specifying SF6 switchgear. Regulation (EU) 2024/573 prohibits putting new SF6 MV switchgear up to and including 24 kV into operation from 1 January 2026, and 24-52 kV from 1 January 2030. Iceland is in the EEA. A 2026 build must be vacuum plus dry air or fluoronitrile from the outset.
- Under-specifying switchroom air. Geothermal hydrogen sulphide corrodes copper and silver in electronics, and volcanic ash events blind filters in days. Positive pressure, ISO 16890 ePM1 60%/85% plus chemical filtration to hold ISA 71.04 severity G1, reactivity coupons, a doubled filter stock and an ash-event recirculation mode.
- Assuming a standard rod array will reach 1 ohm. Young Icelandic basalt and lava run to hundreds or thousands of ohm-metres. Foundation earthing, ring electrodes and deep rods to the water table have to go in with the slab; it cannot be retrofitted under a poured 6,000 m2 floor.
- Paralleling the two 2,500 kVA production transformers. Each gives 60.1 kA prospective fault at 6% impedance; paralleled, roughly 120 kA - past any economic LV switchgear and past sane arc-flash incident energy. Run split with an interlocked, auto-transfer bus-section breaker.
- Forgetting that Iceland is 50 Hz. Step-down transformers give a US package 120/208 V but not 60 Hz, so any mains-synchronous motor runs 17% slow. Legacy magnetic-ballast HMIs on 50 Hz also flicker at 23.976 fps. Budget a static frequency converter and insist on flicker-free electronic ballasts.
- Sizing neutrals as reduced. LED drivers and dimmers make triplen harmonics that add in the neutral, which can approach 173% of phase current, and the neutral carries no overcurrent device. 200% neutrals on production transformers, busway and final circuits, with the IEC 60364-5-52 harmonic derating applied.
- Fitting Type A RCDs in front of EV chargers and VFDs. Smooth DC residual current blinds a Type A device so it will not trip on a real fault. Iceland's EV penetration means this appears all over the lot, not just at the chargers - Type B per IEC 62423 where any DC-injecting load exists.
- Ignoring the sub-arctic light in both directions. Roughly 4 h 17 min of daylight at the Reykjavik winter solstice makes site and emergency lighting a sustained daily load; roughly 21 hours of daylight and no true darkness at midsummer means the backlot lighting has to be able to produce darkness on command as well as light.
- Putting dimmer racks, transformers or busway inside the acoustic envelope. Cast-resin transformers hum at 100 Hz, dimmer chokes buzz, and 4,000 A of busbar radiates a field that gets into microphones. Remote dimmer room, separately founded substation building, and a target of under about 0.5 microtesla at stage floor level.
- Treating the Lon tank as ordinary wet-location work. IEC 60364-7-702 zoning, SELV in zone 0, supplementary equipotential bonding of all tank steel, and an IT supply with an insulation monitoring device to IEC 61557-8 annunciating at the dive supervisor's position - so the first earth fault is an alarm rather than a fatality.
- Buying spares as if the supply chain were European. Everything is imported and shipping is weather-dependent; a cast-resin transformer is a months-long lead time and a February delivery can slip weeks. Hold a spare transformer, trip units, vacuum bottles, inverter modules and made-up feeder tails on site.

---

## 4. Climate, air and smoke

*Askja (6,000 m² / 180,000 m³), Lón tank stage and the two 2,000 m² interconnect stages, Sugar Land Studios, Mosfellsbær*

### Essential

#### Outdoor design conditions basis

Est. heating 99.6% DB −13 °C; cooling 0.4% DB ~16 °C / 13 °C MCWB. Reykjavík Jan normal 0.6 °C, 77% RH, 22.7 km/h wind (1991–2020). Extremes −19.7 °C / +26.2 °C. Design wind vb,0 = 36.0 m/s per ÍST EN 1991-1-4/NA:2010 (Dlubal geo-zone).

**Why.** Every coil, damper, louvre and door air curtain is sized off these. Wrong wind figure and the smoke inlets and relief paths are all undersized.

**Here.** Cooling design DB barely reaches 16 °C so envelope gain is near zero — the cooling load is 100% internal. But the 36 m/s design wind sets every intake, relief and smoke opening on the building.

#### Internal gain schedule / connected production load

Industry planning figure 45 W/ft² = 484 W/m² for production space (TV Technology); 35–40 W/ft² achievable on large stages. Askja worst case 6,000 × 484 = 2,904 kW. Design basis here (est.): 300 W/m² full-floor simultaneous plus 4 × 250 kW movable hot zones.

**Why.** Size on 484 W/m² everywhere and you buy twice the plant you will ever run. Size on LED-only and the first tungsten-heavy period show cooks the stage.

#### Cooling plant capacity / tonnage

Askja est. 1,800 kW base + 1,000 kW boost = 2,800 kW ≈ 796 TR (1 TR = 3.517 kW). Whole lot incl. Lón, the two 2,000 m² stages, mill and offices est. 5.5–6.0 MW ≈ 1,560–1,700 TR, N+1.

**Why.** Undersize and you cannot hold 21 °C under a full lighting period; oversize and the chillers short-cycle at the 5% load the Icelandic climate actually asks for.

**Here.** Installed tonnage is high but annual chiller run hours are tiny — free cooling covers nearly the whole year. Specify for turndown, not for annual efficiency.

#### Free-cooling dry coolers / waterside economiser

Glycol dry coolers, 30% ethylene glycol, EC fans, sized for 12/18 °C chilled water at 8 °C ambient DB. Est. 97–99% of annual hours available (Mosfellsbær mean annual ~5 °C; hours above 15 °C est. 200–400 h/yr). No cooling towers anywhere on the lot.

**Why.** Removes almost all mechanical cooling energy and, more usefully here, almost all mechanical cooling electrical demand.

**Here.** Towers are ruled out three times over: freezing, plume in a wind-scoured site, and ash-fouled fill. Dry coolers with ash-tolerant fin pitch (≥2.5 mm) are the answer.

#### District heating substation (hitaveita connection)

Indirect connection to Veitur. Plate HXs; Veitur cools well and plant water to ~80 °C for distribution. Secondary 70/40 °C for AHU coils, 45/35 °C for the slab. Heat meters to EN 1434.

**Why.** Single source of essentially free heat for coils, slab, snow melt, tank heating and reheat. Everything thermal on the lot hangs off it.

**Here.** Cascade the water three times — absorption chillers, then coils, then snow melt — before it goes to return. Standard Icelandic practice and it triples the value of every litre.

#### Primary air handling units

6 × bespoke double-skin, est. 32 m³/s each (190 m³/s, ~684,000 m³/h total). EN 1886 casing class D1 / L1 / T2 / TB2, filter bypass F9. Coil face ≤1.8 m/s, filter face ≤2.0 m/s → est. 17.6 m² face area each, about 4.4 × 4.0 m.

**Why.** Face velocity is the acoustic lever. Dropping from a normal 2.5–3.0 m/s to 1.8 m/s halves coil-generated noise and pressure drop, at the cost of a much bigger box.

**Here.** Units and casings are imported by sea in sections; specify split lines and a flanged bolt-together build, not a monolithic factory unit that will not fit a Sundahöfn crane schedule.

#### EC plug fan arrays

8–12 EC plug fans per AHU, redundant fan-wall, ISO 12759 efficiency, Reg (EU) 327/2011 compliant. Est. SFP 0.8 kW/(m³/s) against a typical 1.8–2.0 because external static is ~250 Pa not 1,000 Pa. 190 m³/s × 0.8 ≈ 152 kW total fan power.

**Why.** Redundancy without a standby AHU, and the turndown that makes a graceful quiet mode possible instead of an air-off stage.

**Here.** The low-velocity design that NC-25 forces on you also saves est. 230 kW of continuous fan power — real money against a rationed electrical connection.

#### Vibration isolation of all rotating plant

Spring isolators ≥50 mm static deflection on inertia bases ≥1.5× machine mass; flexible duct connections; resilient pipe hangers for the first 30 m; plant building on its own foundation with no shared slab or steel with the stage box.

**Why.** Structure-borne noise bypasses every attenuator you buy. It is the failure mode that shows up only after the stage is finished.

**Here.** Seismic restraint and vibration isolation pull against each other. Use seismically-rated snubbered spring mounts (ASHRAE Applications Ch. 56 / SMACNA Seismic Restraint Manual) — a plain spring mount walks off its base in a design event.

#### Particulate filtration train

Four stages: ISO Coarse 60% panel → ISO ePM10 75% bag → ISO ePM1 80% compact (all ISO 16890-1:2016) → gas phase. Differential pressure monitored per section, alarm at 250 Pa, final change at 450 Pa.

**Why.** ePM1 is what keeps volcanic and resuspended ash off coils, LED walls, lens glass and dimmer racks.

**Here.** Filter life is set by ash and resuspended dust, not by hours. Plan on changing at pressure drop, and stock est. three full sets per AHU because a change is needed exactly when Keflavík is shut.

#### Volcanic ash event mode

Outdoor air dampers to 5% minimum, stage held at +25 Pa, dedicated ash make-up train with HEPA H13 final (EN 1822-1:2019). Eyjafjallajökull 2010 ash was ~58 wt% SiO₂ with up to 25% of mass respirable below 10 µm (PNAS 2011; Thorsteinsson et al. 2012, JGR).

**Why.** Ash loads a bag filter in hours, abrades fan wheels and coil fins, and blocks the fresh-air path exactly when spares cannot be flown in.

**Here.** The particles are jagged volcanic glass, not soft dust. Specify abrasion-resistant fan wheels, ≥2.5 mm coil fin pitch and washable pre-filters, and write the ash mode into the BMS as a one-button state.

#### Heat recovery — run-around coil

Glycol run-around, ≥68% thermal efficiency to Reg (EU) 1253/2014 Tier 2 (from 1 Jan 2018; ≥73% for non-run-around systems). Applies in Iceland through the EEA. Rotary wheels and plate exchangers rejected.

**Why.** A rotary wheel carries haze glycol, ash and Lón's humidity from extract straight back into supply. Run-around is the only zero-carryover option.

**Here.** Geothermal heat is nearly free so the payback is poor — you fit heat recovery because Ecodesign makes it mandatory, not because the fuel bill asks for it. Say so in the business case rather than pretending it pays.

#### LPHW heating coils

70/40 °C district heat, ≤1.8 m/s face velocity, 6–8 rows, copper/aluminium with an applied corrosion coating (Blygold/Heresite class). Frost trip on coil return at 5 °C.

**Why.** Uncoated aluminium fin in an H₂S and salt-laden coastal atmosphere loses fin bond and capacity within a few years.

**Here.** Geothermal water is mildly mineralised and the site air carries H₂S and marine salt. Coat every coil and use titanium or 316L plates in the substation.

#### Glycol frost / preheat coil

Ahead of the filter bank, lifting −13 °C outdoor air to +2 °C before it reaches the run-around coil and the bag filters. Two-port control, glycol pumped, freeze stat on the leaving air.

**Why.** Wet bag filters freeze solid. Ice on a filter face is a total blockage, not a partial one, and it takes the AHU offline mid-shoot.

**Here.** Non-negotiable at −13 °C design with high ambient RH. This is the item that most often gets value-engineered out by a non-Icelandic design team.

#### Cooling / dehumidification coil

6/12 °C chilled water, ≤1.8 m/s face velocity (well under the 2.5 m/s carryover threshold), stainless drain pan falling to twin trapped drains sized for the full fan static. No eliminator plates.

**Why.** Above 2.5 m/s the coil throws condensate into the duct; eliminator plates fix that and whistle, which is the one thing NC-25 cannot tolerate.

#### Duct velocity limits

Mains ≤4.0 m/s (787 fpm), branches ≤2.3 m/s (450 fpm), terminals ≤1.5 m/s (300 fpm) — TV Technology cites 750 / 450 / 300 fpm for studio work. 190 m³/s at 4.0 m/s needs 47.5 m² of total main cross-section, est. 7.9 m² per AHU (about 3.5 × 2.3 m).

**Why.** This one line is what makes an NC-25 stage expensive: roughly 2.5 times the cross-sectional area of a commercial system, and every riser, opening and hanger sized to suit.

**Here.** All that sheet metal is imported. Specify site-rolled spiral where possible or accept 6–8 week lead times on rectangular sections.

#### Duct construction and leakage class

Galvanised to DW/144 or EN 1505/1506 dimensions; air leakage class C to EN 12237 (circular) and EN 1507 (rectangular). Heavier gauge and closer stiffener pitch than the tables require, to push the panel resonance out of the speech band.

**Why.** A large-panel duct at low velocity still drums. The gauge is chosen for acoustics, not for pressure.

#### External duct lagging / breakout treatment

Mass-loaded barrier ≥10 kg/m² plus 50 mm mineral wool on every duct inside the acoustic envelope. Internal acoustic lining avoided inside the stage — it sheds fibre and absorbs haze fluid; use lined plenums outside the box instead.

**Why.** Breakout through a 3.5 × 2.3 m duct wall is a bigger noise path than the terminal is, and it is invisible on a calculation sheet.

#### Splitter attenuators

Rectangular splitters 2,400–3,000 mm long, 50% free area, ≤5 m/s face velocity, insertion loss est. 35–45 dB across 250–2,000 Hz. Two in series per path (supply and return), one each side of the acoustic envelope. Non-shedding facing cloth.

**Why.** The AHU is around 95 dB(A) sound power. Reaching NC-25 — roughly 30 dB(A) in room — needs 60 dB-plus of insertion loss and there is nowhere else to find it.

#### Attenuator regenerated noise limit

Self-noise must sit ≥10 dB below the NC-25 target in every octave band. Achieved by keeping attenuator face velocity ≤5 m/s and avoiding abrupt transitions either side. Method per ASHRAE Handbook—HVAC Applications Ch. 49.

**Why.** An undersized silencer becomes the loudest thing in the duct. It is a very common way to spend money and make the stage worse.

#### Cross-talk attenuation between stages

Every stage on its own dedicated AHU with no shared supply or extract duct. Where a route is unavoidable — the interconnect between the two 2,000 m² stages — Dn,e ≥ 65 dB, tested to EN ISO 10140-2 / ISO 10848-1.

**Why.** A shared duct is a speaking tube. Two units shooting simultaneously in adjacent stages is the normal case, not the exception.

#### Detached acoustic plant building

All rotating plant in a separate structure, est. 540 m² per large stage — around 9% of stage floor area, against 4–5% for a normal building — on its own foundation, connected only by duct runs through the attenuator wall.

**Why.** Isolating plant structurally is cheaper and more reliable than trying to silence it inside the acoustic envelope.

**Here.** Plant buildings on this site are themselves exposed to 36 m/s wind and driven snow; louvre faces need snow hoods and drainable blades, not standard weather louvres.

#### Balancing and control dampers

Opposed-blade dampers sited at least 5 duct diameters upstream of any terminal and, wherever the layout allows, outside the acoustic envelope. Leakage class 1 to EN 1751. No VAV terminal boxes inside the stage.

**Why.** A partly-closed damper blade in a moving air stream is a broadband noise generator sitting downstream of all your attenuation.

#### High-level return and relief path

Extract at the perms, est. 10% more free area than supply (studio convention per TV Technology), acoustically lined transfer plenums, motorised low-level relief at the elephant doors. Extract air arrives stratified at est. 30–35 °C.

**Why.** Return is where most designs lose their NC rating, because it is treated as an afterthought and grilles end up sized on face area alone.

**Here.** That 30–35 °C stratified extract is the highest-grade heat on the lot and should feed the run-around coil before anything else.

#### Air change rate and fresh air minimum

Est. 3.8 ach on the full 180,000 m³ at 190 m³/s; est. 11 ach over the 10 m occupied zone. Minimum fresh air 10 l/s per person to EN 16798-1 Category II — 300 crew = 3 m³/s, trivial against the thermal airflow.

**Why.** The airflow driver is heat removal and haze clear-down, not CO₂. Anyone sizing this stage on ventilation rates will be out by two orders of magnitude.

#### Temperature and humidity setpoints and tolerances

21 °C ±1 K in the occupied zone (EN 16798-1 Cat. I; ISO 7730 Category A, PPD < 6%), 45–55% RH ±5%. Vertical gradient ≤3 K between 0.1 m and 1.8 m. Recovery to setpoint within 20 min after a full elephant-door cycle.

**Why.** Hair, make-up, wardrobe and scenic paint all move with RH, and continuity across a multi-week shoot depends on holding it.

**Here.** Holding 45% RH against −13 °C outdoor air is the hard direction here, not the cooling. Sizing the humidifier, not the chiller, is what the Icelandic winter tests.

#### Air velocity limit in the acting area

≤0.15 m/s (30 fpm) at head height in the acting area; ≤0.10 m/s where a candle, cigarette or practical flame is in shot. Verified with a hot-wire anemometer at commissioning on a 5 m grid at 1.5 m.

**Why.** Anything faster moves costume, hair, smoke, dust and flame, and the DP will simply ask for the air to be turned off.

#### Haze-tolerant fire detection

Optical beam detectors with drift compensation, aspirating detection (VESDA class) and video smoke detection, multi-criteria on smoke, heat and CO. Alarm verification / double-knock during a declared haze period under a named fire watch.
