**Why.** Point smoke detectors alarm on haze, and the usual field fix is to isolate the zone — taking detection out of a building full of naked flame, pyro and megawatts of lighting.

**Here.** Sign-off runs through HMS (Húsnæðis- og mannvirkjastofnun) and the capital-area brigade SHS under Byggingarreglugerð nr. 112/2012. Neither has approved a hazed sound stage before — budget a fire-engineered case to BS 7974 / PD 7974 and a long pre-application.

#### Powered smoke and heat exhaust (SHEVS)

Powered ventilators to EN 12101-3:2015 class F400 (400 °C for 120 min), est. 8 × 15 m³/s = 120 m³/s. Design fire 5 MW sprinklered (PD 7974-1). Thomas plume at 25 m rise gives est. 236 kg/s ≈ 207 m³/s of smoke, so steady-state clearance is unachievable and the design is time-based.

**Why.** At 30 m to the smoke base the plume entrains so much air that chasing a steady clear layer would need twice the extract and still fail.

**Here.** Natural EN 12101-2 vents are the wrong answer here. At 36 m/s a windward vent reverses and drives smoke back down, and an Icelandic roof vent must also carry snow load class SL and low-temperature class T(−25) and still open.

#### Make-up air for smoke extract

Inlet free area at ≤2.5 m/s for 120 m³/s = 48 m² minimum; ≤5 m/s absolute limit so the inlet does not shred the plume (BS 7346-4 / EN 12101-13 commissioning). Motorised low-level louvres plus nominated elephant doors.

**Why.** Extract without matching inlet just depressurises the box, stalls the fans and pulls smoke through every leak into adjacent stages.

**Here.** Which doors open must be selected by live wind direction. Opening a 15 × 12 m leaf on the windward face in a 30 m/s gust loads it with roughly 500 kN and destroys the smoke layer.

#### Sprinkler / SHEVS interaction sequencing

Extract delayed est. 60–90 s after first sprinkler operation so the system is not extracting a plume the sprinklers have already cooled and dragged to floor level. Sequence fixed in the fire strategy, not left to the control panel installer.

**Why.** Running both simultaneously is the classic conflict — sprinkler cooling destroys buoyancy and the vents then pull cold smoke down through the escape route.

**Here.** A 30 m ceiling is beyond standard sprinkler reach anyway, so the strategy needs perms-level heads plus deluge under the grid, and the smoke sequencing must be written against that arrangement.

#### Fire and smoke control dampers

Motorised fire dampers to EN 15650, classified EI 120 (ve i↔o) S to EN 1366-2; smoke control dampers to EN 12101-8, class E600 120. Each detailed acoustically — a bare blade in a 4 m/s main is a tonal source.

**Why.** Compartment integrity through a 7.9 m² duct penetration, and the dampers are also the mechanism that makes ash mode and haze mode possible.

#### Acoustic vestibules and airlocks

Two-leaf vestibule at every personnel entry, both leaves Rw ≥ 50 dB, electrically interlocked so both cannot open together, with heated matting and a drained grille well.

**Why.** Single-leaf doors are a sound path and a cold draught path, and both fail in exactly the same weather.

**Here.** The grille well needs the snow-melt circuit under it or it fills with compacted ice and the door stops closing.

#### Lón tank water heating

Est. 5,400 m³ (2,400 m² × 1.5 m plus a 20 × 20 × 6 m dive well). Raising 4 → 28 °C is est. 542 GJ ≈ 150,700 kWh: 3.1 MW over 48 h or 900 kW over a week. Titanium plate HX on the filtration return, on district heat.

**Why.** Cold water stops a shoot. Divers and performers work to a floor temperature, and the fill-and-heat time sets the stage turnaround.

**Here.** Elsewhere the heating bill is why tank stages are expensive to run. On geothermal it is close to a rounding error, and that is a genuine competitive advantage for the lot.

#### Lón humidity control and space pressurisation

Air held 1–2 K above water temperature (ASHRAE Handbook—HVAC Applications Ch. 6, natatorium practice), 30 °C / 50–55% RH, space at −10 to −15 Pa relative to adjacent. Est. evaporation 360 kg/h from 2,400 m² of open water ≈ est. 245 kW latent.

**Why.** Negative pressure stops moisture migrating into the acoustic cavity, the perms steel and the adjacent stages, where it becomes rot and corrosion.

**Here.** Negative pressure against a 36 m/s wind is not a single setpoint. It needs wind-compensated pressure control referenced to a shielded external tapping, not a naive differential.

#### Interstitial condensation and vapour control

Assessed to EN ISO 13788 (Glaser) and validated by transient hygrothermal simulation to EN 15026. Air and vapour control layer on the warm side, sd ≥ 100 m, continuous through the double-leaf acoustic build-up. Thermal bridge ψ audit at perms, doors and every duct penetration.

**Why.** A 30 m tall envelope at ΔT 34 K with a humid tank stage next door will drive moisture into a cold cavity and quietly rot the acoustic construction.

**Here.** The conflict is structural: acoustics wants a sealed cavity, moisture wants a ventilated one. Resolve it by keeping the cavity warm on the inside of the insulation, never by venting an acoustic cavity.

#### Rain and hail noise control on the roof

Measured to ISO 10140-8 (formerly ISO 140-18:2006) at the standardised intense rate of 40 mm/h. Target internal ≤NC-25 under that rate. Heavy roof: est. ≥150 mm concrete or ≥100 kg/m² ballast on isolators with a fully separate inner acoustic ceiling. No exposed profiled metal deck anywhere.

**Why.** Rain drumming is the single worst acoustic problem a long-span stage has, and it cannot be fixed after the roof is on.

**Here.** The defining problem on this site. Also specify hail resistance (EN 13583 / FM 4473) and note that driven rain at 30 m/s hits the WALLS — a case ISO 10140-8 does not test at all, so the walls need mass too, not just the roof.

#### Acoustic roof drainage

Gravity drainage to EN 12056-3 with mass-loaded lagging on every stack, or siphonic to BS 8490 with all full-bore pipework routed outside the acoustic envelope. Outlets and gutters heated from the district-heat return; overflow provision assuming the primary is iced.

**Why.** 6,000 m² of roof at Icelandic rainfall intensity produces a lot of water, and the pipe carrying it runs down the inside of the stage wall.

**Here.** Siphonic runs full bore and is very loud — the right hydraulic answer and the wrong acoustic one. If you use it, it must be outside the box, and the outlets must be heated or the whole system fails the first time it ices.

#### Envelope airtightness

Target q50 ≤ 1.0 m³/(h·m²) over an est. 21,600 m² envelope; tested to EN ISO 9972 method 1. The acoustic envelope and the air barrier are the same line on the drawing and must be drawn, specified and inspected as one element.

**Why.** Leakage is simultaneously a heat loss, a sound path and, in this wind, a source of audible whistling that no amount of HVAC specification can fix afterwards.

**Here.** Pressure-testing a 180,000 m³ volume in Iceland requires a wind-calm window that may not appear for weeks. Programme the test, and have a fallback multi-fan method agreed in advance.

#### Building management system

BACnet/IP head end (ISO 16484-5) with native controllers, plus a stage-side touchpanel exposing only four things to the AD, gaffer and mixer: temperature, quiet mode, haze hold, purge. Trend logging at 1-minute resolution, retained 24 months.

**Why.** A stage crew will not learn a BMS. If the interface is not four buttons, someone opens an elephant door instead and you lose the whole climate.

**Here.** The ash, wind-direction door policy and quiet modes are all BMS states. This building is more control-dependent than a normal stage, which raises the stakes on the small local support pool.

#### Commissioning and acoustic verification

Air balance to CIBSE Code A / EN 12599 (±10% branches, ±5% terminals), then an unoccupied room noise survey to ANSI/ASA S12.2-2019 at four positions per stage with the plant at design duty, plus background level and reverberation to ISO 3382-2.

**Why.** NC-25 is a measured outcome, not a design intent, and it is proved once — on site, with everything running, before the first production books.

**Here.** Schedule the survey for a calm night. Wind noise through the envelope at 20 m/s will swamp any NC-25 measurement and you will not know whether you failed on the mechanical or the fabric.

#### Seismic restraint of mechanical services

All plant, ducts over 600 mm and pipes over 65 mm restrained per ASHRAE Handbook—HVAC Applications Ch. 56 and the SMACNA Seismic Restraint Manual, to the Icelandic NA to ÍST EN 1998-1. Est. agR 0.1–0.15 g for the capital region; the South Iceland Seismic Zone reaches ~0.4 g.

**Why.** A 7.9 m² duct or a 400 m³ buffer tank coming off its supports in an earthquake is a life-safety event and takes the smoke system with it.

**Here.** Iceland's EN 1998 National Annex has not been substantially revised in over two decades and its zoning has been criticised as inadequate. Commission a site-specific hazard study rather than reading a value off the map.

#### Maintenance access design

Every filter, coil, drive, damper and attenuator reachable from the plant building without entering a stage or crossing a shooting floor. Duct access hatches at every attenuator and every 20 m of main run.

**Why.** If a filter change needs stage access, it happens during a shoot, or it does not happen at all.

**Here.** Small labour pool. If a routine task needs four people and a permit, it will not get done between two shoots. Design every recurring task for two people and a hand trolley.

### Expected

#### Electric chillers (trim and latent duty)

2 × 900 kW oil-free magnetic-bearing centrifugal (Turbocor class), R-1234ze(E), GWP < 1, full-load COP ≈ 5.5, IPLV ≈ 9–11. 6/12 °C flow/return. F-gas Reg (EU) 517/2014 applies through the EEA.

**Why.** Dehumidification needs 6 °C water regardless of how cold it is outside — free cooling cannot make an apparatus dew point.

**Here.** Sized as trim, not as the primary machine, precisely because the electrical connection is the constrained resource.

#### Hydronic snow melting (snjóbræðsla)

Elephant-door aprons, crew routes, loading dock, roof valleys and gutters on district-heat return water at 35–40 °C. Est. 250–300 W/m² design output, zoned, with slab sensors.

**Why.** Without it the elephant door freezes shut, the roof drains ice up and the loading dock becomes unusable in the four months that matter.

**Here.** Universal Icelandic practice and effectively free on return water. It is also what stops snow being tracked onto a 6,000 m² shooting floor.

#### Humidification

Resistive or electrode steam, or high-pressure adiabatic on RO water. Est. 250 kg/h per large stage to hold 45% RH at −13 °C outdoor. Distribution manifold inside the AHU with a proven absorption distance, never in the stage-side duct.

**Why.** Scenic plaster cracks, timber sets move, wardrobe changes hand and radio mics pick up static below about 35% RH.

**Here.** Winter outside air at 0.6 °C / 77% RH carries only ~3.1 g/kg. Heat it to 21 °C and you have 20% RH — drier than most deserts. Iceland needs humidification far more than it needs dehumidification on dry stages.

#### Dehumidification with geothermal reheat (dry stages)

Chilled water to a 9 °C apparatus dew point, then reheat on district heat to the 17–18 °C displacement supply temperature. Sequenced so reheat never fights active cooling on the same air unless dew point control demands it.

**Why.** Reheat is normally forbidden on energy grounds. Here the reheat fuel is free geothermal, so the usual objection does not apply.

**Here.** One of the few places on earth where simultaneous cooling and reheat is the correct, defensible answer rather than a design failure.

#### Fabric duct / sock diffusers

Micro-perforated permeable-face fabric socks (Prihoda/DuctSox class), 900–1,400 mm diameter, washable, EN 13501-1 reaction-to-fire class B-s1,d0, hung on rails so a run can be pulled clear of a camera line in minutes.

**Why.** Discharges through the whole surface at effectively zero velocity, which is the only way to move that much air at NC-25 without a wall of metal diffusers in shot.

#### Haze hold mode

Recirculation-dominant, outdoor air to minimum, extract throttled to an est. 0.5–1.0 ach so a level set by the DP holds est. 20–30 min. ANSI E1.23-2020 governs design, execution and maintenance of atmospheric effects; ANSI E1.5-2003 covers di- and trihydric alcohol fogs.

**Why.** Without a hold mode the hazer runs continuously to fight the ventilation, and crew exposure and fluid cost both climb for no picture benefit.

#### Haze purge / clear-down mode

100% outdoor air, extract to full, est. 6–8 ach, clearing a hazed 180,000 m³ to visual clear in est. 12–20 min. Interlocked so purge cannot run while a fire-effect or pyro permit is open.

**Why.** Between units, before hot work and before any evacuation drill you need the volume clear on a known, provable timescale.

**Here.** Purge in January means dumping 190 m³/s of −13 °C air into the stage. Without the geothermal preheat and the slab, a purge would leave the stage unusable for an hour.

#### Smoke curtains and reservoirs

Automatic fabric curtains to EN 12101-1:2005+A1:2006, gravity-fail (down on power loss), dividing the 6,000 m² floor into est. four reservoirs of ≤1,600 m² with a ≥3 m deep layer. Also used to seal the interconnect between the two 2,000 m² stages.

**Why.** Reservoirs stop the layer spreading thin across 6,000 m² where it cannot be extracted and cannot be seen through.

#### Effects and vehicle exhaust extraction

Local capture at 2–4 m³/s per point, hose-reel tailpipe extraction for picture cars, a dedicated fire-effects hood. Fixed CO and NO₂ monitoring — CO alarm at 30 ppm 8-h TWA and 200 ppm ceiling, NO₂ at 0.5 ppm (EU IOELV lineage, Dir. 2000/39/EC).

**Why.** Running a picture car or a practical fire in a sealed 180,000 m³ box builds CO slowly and invisibly until someone is unwell.

**Here.** With a hard-sealed, airtight envelope built for wind and cold, natural leakage cannot be relied on to dilute anything. Mechanical capture is the only control.

#### Elephant door air curtain

Recirculating, geothermally heated, est. 25 m³/s per door, nozzle velocity 12–15 m/s, effective against a cross wind of about 10 m/s on manufacturer's ISO 27327-1 data.

**Why.** Holds the thermal and humidity boundary for routine door cycles so the stage recovers in minutes rather than an hour.

**Here.** A 15 × 12 m opening in a 36 m/s wind is 180 m² of hole and no air curtain wins that fight. The real controls are a drive-through lobby and a wind-direction door policy in the BMS.

#### Sensors and instrumentation

Occupied-zone temperature at 1.5 m on an est. 10 × 10 m wireless grid (battery, no floor cabling), RH and dew point per stage, NDIR CO₂, differential pressure across every filter section and stage-to-outside, water temperature and turbidity in Lón.

**Why.** Without an occupied-zone sensor grid a displacement system is controlled on return air temperature, which in a 30 m stratified volume tells you nothing useful.

**Here.** External reference tappings for pressure control need shielded, wind-compensated heads, and the whole external sensor set needs heated, ash-shedding enclosures.

#### Metering and measurement/verification

Heat meters to EN 1434 on every district heat branch, electrical sub-metering per stage to IEC 61557-12 class 0.5S, chilled water thermal metering. Data exposed per stage per day.

**Why.** You cannot recharge a production for what it used, or prove the green claim, without metering at the stage boundary.

**Here.** With low-carbon hydro and geothermal supply, per-stage metering is the evidence base for a carbon claim that is genuinely worth money to an incoming studio.

#### Resilience and standby provision

N+1 on chillers, AHU fan arrays and pumps; dual district heat service from separate mains; standby generation covering smoke control, life safety, Lón filtration and est. 30% of climate — deliberately not full lighting load.

**Why.** A lost stage day is the unit of loss here. Everything that stops a shoot needs a second path; the lighting does not, because the production carries its own generators.

**Here.** The grid is at capacity and Landsvirkjun has refused new large industrial supply, so standby sizing is also a hedge against a constrained or curtailable connection, not only against outage.

#### Spares and consumables holding

Est. three full filter sets per AHU, one spare fan module per array, spare plate packs, gaskets, glycol, and haze and fog fluid stock. Est. 4–8 week sea-freight lead on anything mechanical.

**Why.** A blocked filter bank with no spare on the shelf takes an AHU, and therefore a stage, out of service for a month.

**Here.** During an ash event the airport closes and everything is on a ship. Holding stock is not inventory inefficiency here, it is the operating plan.

### State of the art

#### Geothermal-fired absorption chiller

Single-effect LiBr, hot-water fired 85–95 °C, COP ≈ 0.7, e.g. 2 × 1,200 kW. Reykir/Reykjahlíð wells run at 86 °C (Veitur operates 22 boreholes at Reykir plus 7 new). Electrical draw only the solution and refrigerant pumps, est. 15 kW per machine.

**Why.** Converts nearly-free geothermal heat into cooling and removes roughly 500 kW of electrical demand from a grid connection you may not be able to get.

**Here.** The central Iceland move. Landsvirkjun has publicly refused supply to new large industrial load on system-capacity grounds; absorption trades scarce kW for abundant kWh-thermal from Reykir, 4–7 km away.

#### Chilled water buffer / thermal store

Stratified atmospheric buffer, est. 250–400 m³, giving est. 20–30 min at full lighting load. Charged overnight on free cooling.

**Why.** Lets you shave the electrical peak the connection agreement is priced on, and ride a chiller changeover through the middle of a take.

**Here.** Peak-shaving has real money in it when the transmission constraint means your connection capacity is rationed rather than just billed.

#### Stage floor heating (hydronic slab)

PE-RT/PEX-a in a 350 mm slab, ≥100 mm cover, 200 mm pitch, 40 °C flow, surface ≤27 °C (EN 1264-2), est. 60–80 W/m², 6,000 m² in 24 zones. Full as-built survey; cover meter kept on stage.

**Why.** Radiant heat is silent. Every kW delivered through the floor is a kW you do not have to move as air, which is the direct route to smaller ducts.

**Here.** Free geothermal heat makes a radiant slab the cheapest heating on the lot, and it removes the winter air volume that would otherwise drive the NC-25 duct sizes even larger.

#### Gas-phase / chemical filtration for H₂S and SO₂

Impregnated activated carbon plus potassium permanganate media on all outdoor air. Iceland Reg. 514/2010 sets a 50 µg/m³ 24-hour H₂S limit in ambient air — the only such limit in Europe. Target ISA 71.04-2013 severity level G1 inside.

**Why.** H₂S tarnishes silver, corrodes copper and kills dimmer racks, LED driver boards, camera contacts and console pots.

**Here.** Hellisheiði and Nesjavellir vent H₂S 25–30 km east of the site and the odour reaches Reykjavík. Volcanic SO₂ episodes are a separate, sharper event on the same media.

#### Low-level displacement air distribution

Perimeter and column-base plenums plus removable floor-level terminals. Terminal face velocity ≤1.0 m/s (197 fpm); est. 190 m² of total face area for 190 m³/s. Supply air 17–18 °C, not 13 °C.

**Why.** Displacement conditions the bottom 10 m and lets the top 20 m stratify. Trying to mix 180,000 m³ is what makes big stages noisy and expensive.

**Here.** Displacement also stacks the warm, humid, hazed air at the perms where the extract and the recovery coil want it — worth est. 10–15 K on the extract temperature into the run-around.

#### Quiet mode / red-light interlock

Shooting-bell interlock drops the fan array to est. 40–50%, using the heated slab and the 180,000 m³ as thermal flywheel. Ramp ≤2% per second so the change is inaudible on a running take. Auto-restore on cut, or on a temperature, dew point or CO₂ limit.

**Why.** Even at NC-25 a mixer will ask for air off. Offering a graceful 6 dB instead of a hard stop is the difference between a usable stage and an air-off stage.

**Here.** The heated slab makes this work far better here than elsewhere: with 6,000 m² of warm concrete you can coast a winter take with the fans nearly off and lose almost nothing.

#### Smoke filling and tenability calculation

The 30 m height and 180,000 m³ are the asset: est. 10.6 min to fill from 30 m to a 12 m clear layer with a 5 MW fire and no extraction (NFPA 92 / PD 7974-6 filling method), over 20 min with 120 m³/s running. Est. escape time from a 6,000 m² floor 4–6 min.

**Why.** This calculation is the argument that buys 120 m³/s of extract instead of 210. Present tenability time, not a steady-state layer depth.

**Here.** An unfamiliar approach for an Icelandic authority working from a prescriptive building regulation. Bring an independent peer review from the outset.

#### Equipment acclimatisation lobby

Tempered room between loading dock and stage floor held at 12 °C / 35% RH. The dew point of a 21 °C / 50% RH stage is 10.2 °C, so anything colder than that fogs and condenses the moment it comes through the door.

**Why.** Camera bodies, lens glass, LED panels and lighting fixtures condense internally; the damage is delayed, expensive and blamed on the vendor.

**Here.** Kit arrives off a truck at −10 °C for four months a year. Almost nobody builds this room and every Icelandic stage needs it.

#### Lón 100% outdoor air dehumidification

Winter outdoor air at 0.6 °C / 77% RH is ~3.1 g/kg against 14.6 g/kg indoors, so each kg removes 11.5 g. Est. 8.7 kg/s (7.2 m³/s) covers the whole evaporation load with est. 257 kW of geothermal reheat. Summer at 12 °C / 80% still works at est. 13 kg/s.

**Why.** Removes the refrigerant dehumidifier or desiccant wheel that a natatorium normally needs, along with its electrical load and its maintenance.

**Here.** Cold dry air plus free heat is the best natatorium climate anywhere. It is the single clearest case where Iceland turns a cost centre into a giveaway — but only if the reheat is on district heat, not electric.

### What gets got wrong

- What NC-25 actually costs: duct mains at 4 m/s instead of a commercial 10 m/s means roughly 2.5x the cross-sectional area, plus 3 m splitter attenuators on every path in both directions, plus AHU coil and filter faces derated to 1.8 m/s, plus a detached plant building at est. 9% of stage floor area against 4-5% normally. Est. uplift on the mechanical package 50-70%, taking mechanical from around 12-15% of construction cost to 20-25%. The counter-intuitive part is that it saves running cost: external static drops to around 250 Pa, SFP falls to an est. 0.8 kW/(m3/s) against 1.8-2.0, and total fan power for 190 m3/s comes out at est. 152 kW instead of est. 380 kW. NC-25 is a capital cost, not an energy cost.
- Sizing on 484 W/m2 (45 W/ft2) across the whole 6,000 m2. That is the industry planning figure for connected load in a production space, not a simultaneous whole-floor load. Nobody lights 6,000 m2 at once. Design a moderate full-floor base with movable high-capacity hot zones instead, or you buy 1.5 MW of chiller that never runs.
- Ventilation-rate sizing. Someone will size this stage on EN 16798-1 fresh air rates and arrive at 3 m3/s for 300 crew. The real airflow is est. 190 m3/s, driven by heat removal and haze clear-down. The two numbers differ by a factor of 60.
- Controlling a displacement system on return air temperature. In a 30 m stratified volume the return reads 30-35 C while the acting area sits at 21 C. Without an occupied-zone sensor grid at 1.5 m the control loop is measuring a different building.
- Rotary heat recovery wheels on a hazed or tank stage. A wheel carries haze glycol, ash and Lon's moisture from extract back into supply. It is the default choice everywhere else and it is wrong here; run-around coil is the only zero-carryover option that still meets Reg (EU) 1253/2014.
- Underfloor heating pipes in a floor that scenic carpenters nail into. This is a genuine, recurring conflict. The mitigation is 100 mm minimum cover, a strict 200 mm laying grid, a surveyed as-built drawing issued to every production, pre-set floor inserts for all fixings, and a cover meter kept on the stage as a condition of the floor permit.
- Acoustic cavity versus moisture. A double-leaf acoustic wall wants to be sealed; a cold cavity in an Icelandic winter wants to be ventilated. Venting an acoustic cavity destroys the sound insulation. The resolution is to keep the cavity on the warm side of the insulation and control vapour with an sd >= 100 m barrier, never to compromise the seal.
- Natural smoke vents in a 36 m/s design wind. A windward EN 12101-2 vent reverses and pressurises the space with its own smoke. In Iceland the smoke system should be powered (EN 12101-3, F400), and any roof vent that does exist must additionally carry snow load class SL and low-temperature class T(-25) and still be certified to open.
- Wind-blind make-up air. Opening the wrong elephant door for smoke make-up in a 30 m/s gust puts roughly 500 kN on a 15 x 12 m leaf and blows the smoke layer apart. Door selection must be driven by live wind direction in the fire control matrix, which most smoke cause-and-effect schedules have no provision for.
- Haze against point smoke detection. The field workaround is to isolate the zone, which removes detection from a building containing naked flame, pyro and megawatts of lighting. Multi-criteria detection with drift compensation, video smoke detection and a documented double-knock regime under a named fire watch is the only defensible answer, and it needs agreeing with HMS and SHS long before construction.
- Siphonic roof drainage inside the acoustic envelope. Full-bore flow is very loud. It is the right hydraulic answer for 6,000 m2 of roof and the wrong acoustic one, so it has to be routed outside the box or replaced with lagged gravity drainage.
- Testing airtightness or NC-25 on a windy day. Mosfellsbaer will swamp both measurements. Both tests need a calm window that may not appear for weeks, and neither can be squeezed into the last fortnight of a programme.
- Assuming heat recovery pays back. Geothermal heat is nearly free, so the run-around coil has a poor financial case. You fit it because Ecodesign Reg (EU) 1253/2014 applies through the EEA and makes it mandatory. Say that plainly in the business case rather than fabricating a payback.
- Forgetting that ash arrives with the airport shut. Volcanic ash loads bag filters in hours and abrades fan wheels and coil fins, and it does so at exactly the moment air freight stops. Holding three full filter sets per AHU is the operating plan, not surplus inventory.
- Electric reheat. Simultaneous cooling and reheat is normally a design failure. Here it is correct, because the reheat fuel is free geothermal water. But if anyone value-engineers the reheat coil to electric duct heaters, the design flips from clever to indefensible and puts load on the one utility the site cannot get more of.

---

## 5. Rigging, grid and overhead

*Askja (6,000 m², 100 × 60 m, 30 m to underside of perms, 180,000 m³), Lon, and the two 2,000 m² interconnect stages, Sugar Land Studios, Mosfellsbær*

### Essential

#### Primary long-span roof trusses (rigging-carrying)

Welded steel trusses spanning 60 m at 7.5 m centres, depth ≈6 m (span/10). EN 1993-1-1, joints EN 1993-1-8, fatigue EN 1993-1-9; fabrication EN 1090-2 EXC3, welding EN ISO 3834-2. Design target (estimate): 0.50 kN/m² aggregate rigging reserve held clear of snow/ash/wind cases.

**Why.** Every overhead kilogram lands here. Reserve capacity you do not buy on day one can never be retrofitted across a 60 m span.

**Here.** vb,0 = 36 m/s for the whole of Iceland per ÍST EN 1991-1-4:2005/NA:2010, so qb = 0.5·1.25·36² = 0.81 kN/m²; at ~35 m in terrain category II, qp ≈ 2.3–2.4 kN/m² (calculated). Net roof uplift reverses chord forces, and rigging down-load is a variable action you may not count on as relieving.

#### Secondary rigging grid steel (the perms)

Hot-rolled beams, typically 254×146 UB or 305×165 UB equivalent, running the 60 m direction at 3.0 m centres (33 lines across 100 m), with intermediate rail beams at 1.5 m. Deflection limit L/360 under characteristic rigging load. Galvanised EN ISO 1461, ≥85 µm mean coating on ≥6 mm steel.

**Why.** The perms are the product. Point spacing and per-point WLL decide what a production can hang and therefore what the stage can be booked for.

**Here.** Imported. Section lengths are set by shipping: 12.2 m ISO container via Eimskip/Samskip, or breakbulk to Sundahöfn/Grundartangi for longer. Design bolted splices at 12 m rather than pay for charter freight.

#### Acoustic decoupling of the grid from the roof structure

Grid hung from the inner acoustic shell, or on resilient hangers (bonded-neoprene or spring, natural frequency ≤8 Hz, static deflection 6–25 mm) with no rigid steel-to-steel path to the outer roof. Verify by rainfall test to ISO 140-18 / EN ISO 10140-1 Annex methods (heavy rain ≈40 mm/h; intense ≈100 mm/h).

**Why.** A bolted grid conducts rain drumming and wind roar straight into the shooting volume. It is the single hardest noise path to fix after handover.

**Here.** This is the item Iceland punishes hardest. Driven rain and hail on a 6,000 m² roof, plus wind-induced roof-deck flutter, are the dominant noise sources; a rigidly coupled grid turns the whole perms system into a loudspeaker over the set.

#### Rail beams and the suspension point matrix

Design target (estimate): 1,000 kg WLL at any single point on a rail beam; 500 kg per point where two points fall within 1.5 m; matrix 1.5 m across × continuous along the beam. Over 6,000 m² that is ≈2,600 discrete addressable positions. All WLLs proof-verified in situ, not calculated only.

**Why.** Riggers plot to the matrix. Coarse spacing forces bridles, bridles eat headroom and add load, and every bridle is another connection to inspect.

#### Grid access stairs

Two protected stair cores to grid, 30 m rise, EN ISO 14122-3: rise 150–200 mm, going ≥225 mm, pitch 30–38°, 1,000 mm clear width, landings every ≤3.0 m of rise. Fire-rated enclosure and part of the means of escape.

**Why.** A single access route means a rescue from the grid has to come back the way the casualty went up. Two cores is the minimum for a 30 m grid.

**Here.** Seismic separation of the stair cores from the acoustic inner shell so a design earthquake cannot rack the stairs and trap crew at 30 m.

#### Electric chain hoists — D8+ class

1,000 kg and 2,000 kg, 4 m/min, EN 14492-2, FEM 9.511 mechanism group 1Bm (M4), grade T (grade 80) load chain to EN 818-7, double-braked, 4:1 minimum design factor on the mechanism. D8+ may HOLD a static load over people but not move it (DGUV V17/18, formerly BGV C1).

**Why.** The everyday workhorse. Confusing D8 with D8+ is how loads end up moving over a crew.

**Here.** Condensation is the enemy: a stage that cycles from -5 °C outside to a warm geothermal-heated interior will wet the inside of brake housings. Specify IP55 minimum and heated storage.

#### Truss couplers, spigots and pins

Conical spigot connections with retaining pins and R-clips, matched to the truss manufacturer; no mixed-manufacturer joints. Half couplers and swivel couplers to BS EN 74-1 class B (≥6.1 kN slip resistance for a right-angle coupler on 48.3 mm tube).

**Why.** A mixed-brand spigot joint has no rating at all. Coupler slip is a silent failure that shows up as a lamp drifting off its mark.

#### Rigging hardware stock — shackles, slings, roundslings

Forged bow shackles to EN 13889 (or ASME B30.26), 0.5–25 t, all WLL-stamped; roundslings and flat webbing to EN 1492-2 / EN 1492-1 with 7:1 factor; wire rope slings to EN 13414-1 at 5:1; components (master links, hooks, eye bolts) to EN 1677-1 to -6. Malleable wire rope clips prohibited (ANSI E1.6-1 §6.4.2.2).

**Why.** Consumables that get lost, mixed and abused. Unstamped or uncertified hardware in a bin is the fastest route to a prohibition notice.

**Here.** Colour-code by year and hold deep spares. Replacements are a 5–10 day sea freight or an expensive air freight from Keflavík; a run-out of 2 t shackles stops a shoot.

#### Safety bonds / secondary suspension

Steel wire bonds sized to arrest the actual load with a drop of ≤200 mm, rated to at least 10× the suspended mass, on every lamp, speaker, sensor, camera and accessory hung from truss or pipe. Colour-coded by WLL, discarded after any arrest.

**Why.** The only thing between a 20 kg fixture and the floor when a clamp bolt backs out.

**Here.** A 0.5 kg shackle falling 30 m from Askja's grid reaches 24.3 m/s and 148 J (calculated). At this grid height a dropped object is a fatality, not an incident.

#### Chain bags, chain buckets and cable socks

Chain bags sized for 35 m of 7.1 mm chain (Askja's grid height plus reserve), fitted to every hoist; woven cable socks with safety bond eyes on every vertical cable drop.

**Why.** Loose chain at 30 m swings, snags and pulls. A cable sock is what stops a 60 m cable run pulling out of its connector.

#### Horizontal lifeline fall-arrest system

Permanent stainless cable HLL along every open perm and catwalk edge, EN 795:2012 Type C with CEN/TS 16415 for multiple simultaneous users, energy absorber at each end, maximum 2 users per span unless engineered, clearance calculation posted at each access point.

**Why.** Thirty metres above a hard floor there is no such thing as an acceptable unrestrained edge.

**Here.** Stainless (316) not galvanised: the marine-influenced Kollafjörður air puts the building at ISO 12944-2 corrosivity C4–C5-M, and a corroded lifeline is worse than none because it is trusted.

#### Structural anchor points and eyebolts

EN 795 Type A single-point anchors at every hoist maintenance position, rated 12 kN (or 15 kN for a two-person rescue load), marked, individually numbered, and re-tested at each periodic examination.

**Why.** Riggers will tie off to something. If you do not provide rated anchors they will use a rail beam, a sprinkler pipe or a cable tray.

#### Fall-protection PPE issue and store

Full body harnesses EN 361, work positioning belts EN 358, energy-absorbing lanyards EN 355, retractable type fall arresters EN 360, guided type on flexible anchor line EN 353-2, connectors EN 362. Periodic examination at least every 12 months per EN 365, plus pre-use check every use.

**Why.** A grid without a controlled PPE store ends up with kit of unknown age and history hanging on a nail.

#### Suspension trauma rescue kit and written rescue plan

Rescue descenders EN 341 class A, rescue lifting devices EN 1496, a rescue pole per grid zone, trained rescuers on every grid call, target time to relieve suspension ≤10 minutes. Rescue plan specific to Askja's 30 m grid, exercised twice a year.

**Why.** Suspension trauma can be fatal in 15–30 minutes. Calling the fire service is not a rescue plan at 30 m.

**Here.** Slökkvilið höfuðborgarsvæðisins is the responding service; brief them on the building and rehearse with them. Winter access to Mosfellsbær by road can be slow enough to blow the 10-minute window on its own.

#### Guardrails, toe boards and infill

1,100 mm top rail, mid-rail with ≤500 mm gap, ≥100 mm toe board to EN ISO 14122-3, plus 50 mm mesh infill on all catwalk edges over occupied deck. Removable sections keyed and register-controlled.

**Why.** Toe boards are what stop a dropped bolt. Mesh infill is what stops a dropped spanner going through the gap above the toe board.

#### Grid working lighting and emergency lighting

LED worklights on the catwalk soffit at 6 m centres, 200 lux on the walkway, switched in zones from grid and from the stage floor; separate escape lighting to EN 1838 (1 lux minimum on the escape route centre line, 3 h duration) on the stairs, catwalks and at every hatch.

**Why.** A blacked-out stage plus a 30 m grid plus a power failure is the classic fatal combination.

**Here.** From late November to mid-January there is effectively no daylight ingress to fall back on, and the roof has no rooflights on an acoustic stage anyway, so the escape lighting is the only light there will ever be.

#### Fire detection and suppression at grid level

Aspirating smoke detection (VESDA class) at grid soffit, EN 54-20 Class A; sprinkler or water-mist heads above and below the grid deck per EN 12845 / NFPA 13, with the grid deck treated as an obstruction requiring intermediate-level heads. Draft curtains coordinated with the smoke reservoir.

**Why.** A walk-on grid shields the roof heads from the fire below; without intermediate-level protection the ceiling system never operates.

**Here.** Dry or pre-action systems on any run at risk of freezing at the roof interface; and design the heat detection thresholds for a building whose stage-floor-to-grid temperature gradient is large in winter.

#### Seismic restraint of the grid and all suspended plant

Design to EN 1998-1 §4.3.5 for non-structural elements with importance factor γa; ÍST EN 1998-1:2004/NA:2010 uses agR bands of 0.05 g to 0.50 g. Sway bracing on every suspended service, seismic gaps at the inner-shell interface, and restrained cable trays and light fittings.

**Why.** In a design event the grid does not fail — the things hung off it swing, collide and fall. Bracing is what keeps services attached.

**Here.** agR is 0.50 g in the South Iceland Seismic Zone; the capital region is far lower (estimate: 0.10–0.15 g band — confirm against the ÍST NA map for the exact site). Note the pendulum problem: a pick on 30 m of chain has T = 2π√(30/9.81) ≈ 11 s, so low acceleration but large displacement — design swing clearance between adjacent picks, not just strength.

#### Wind uplift and load reversal check on the rigging steel

Full load-reversal check of perms, hangers and connections under net uplift, with γG,inf applied to permanent load and rigging load taken as zero. Hanger rods checked in compression/buckling or replaced with sections that work both ways.

**Why.** Hangers sized only for tension buckle when the roof lifts, which is the one case Iceland guarantees will happen.

**Here.** qp ≈ 2.3–2.4 kN/m² at grid height (calculated from vb,0 = 36 m/s, ÍST EN 1991-1-4 NA), roughly three times a typical UK inland site. Add the internal pressure case with the 12 m elephant doors open during a load-in on a windy day.

#### Inspection regime — periodic thorough examination

Adopt EN 17206:2020 (+AC:2021) plus the DGUV V17/18 interval model: inspection at least annually by a competent person (Sachkundiger), and every four years by an authorised expert (Sachverständiger), the expert year replacing the competent-person inspection. Wire rope discard to ISO 4309; chain wear limits per EN 818-7.

**Why.** Iceland has no stage-specific inspection law, so without a contractually adopted regime there is no regime at all.

**Here.** The statutory hooks are lög nr. 46/1980 (working environment) and reglugerð nr. 367/2006 um notkun tækja, enforced by Vinnueftirlitið — both generic. EN 17206 and DGUV V17/18 must be written into the operating manual as the house standard.

#### Point identification, WLL placards and signage

Every point stencilled and tagged with ID, WLL in kg, and beam reference; zone placards at each hatch stating maximum persons, maximum aggregate load and operator restrictions; signage per ANSI E1.6-1 §9 (operational limits, authorised persons only, supplier contact) in Icelandic and English.

**Why.** Riggers rig what they can read. An unmarked beam is either used at zero or used at guesswork.

**Here.** Bilingual signage is not optional with a mixed local and visiting international crew, and Icelandic is the legal language of workplace safety information.

#### Corrosion protection specification for all overhead steel and hardware

Design to ISO 12944-2 category C4 (C5-M over the Lon tank and at any external interface), ISO 12944-5 protective paint systems for a durability class 'very high' (>25 years), hot-dip galvanising to EN ISO 1461 with duplex coating where exposed. Stainless 316 for lifelines, anchors and all tank-zone hardware.

**Why.** Corroded overhead steel is a 30 m problem: you cannot easily inspect it, and you cannot easily replace it with a stage in use.

**Here.** Marine-influenced air off Kollafjörður plus airborne volcanic acids (SO2 and fluoride during eruptions, which reached the capital region in 2010, 2014–15 and repeatedly on Reykjanes from 2021) makes Iceland far more aggressive than the inland UK sites this steel is usually specified for.

### Expected

#### Walk-on perm decking

Open-mesh steel grating, 30×30 mm or 30×10 mm bearing bar, load class ≥5.0 kN/m² UDL and 3.0 kN concentrated on 200×200 mm, slip resistance R11/serrated. Walkway clear width 900 mm minimum, 1,200 mm on main runs, per EN ISO 14122-2.

**Why.** Open steel forces every trip to grid level to be a rope-access or MEWP job, which triples the cost and time of every lighting change.

**Here.** Open mesh lets volcanic ash fall through to the deck rather than accumulate; solid plate would need sweeping after every ash event and would also block downlight from grid worklights during the December shooting day.

#### Beam trolleys / rolling beam clamps

Plain and geared trolleys, WLL 1,000–5,000 kg, adjustable flange width 50–220 mm, EN 13157 (hand powered) / EN 14492-2 (powered). Anti-drop plates and end stops on every rail beam. Trolley wheels selected for the beam flange taper actually supplied, not the nominal.

**Why.** Without trolleys every point move is a de-rig and re-rig. End stops missing is how a trolley leaves the beam.

#### Bridle plates, spreader beams and steel

Forged or plate steel bridle rings/plates to EN 1677-1; spreader beams to EN 13155 (non-fixed load lifting attachments) with WLL, tare and CoG marked. Wire rope 'steels' 6×36 IWRC to EN 12385-4, slings to EN 13414-1 with 5:1 minimum breaking-load coefficient.

**Why.** Bridles are how you reach a point the matrix does not offer. Sized by eye they load the perms at angles the perms were never checked for.

#### Catwalks — perimeter and cross

Perimeter catwalk at grid level, 1,200 mm clear, continuous around 320 m of Askja; cross catwalks at 12 m centres. Guardrails ≥1,100 mm with mid-rail and ≥100 mm toe board per EN ISO 14122-3. Design live load 5.0 kN/m², 20 persons max per span noticed on the placard.

**Why.** Catwalks are how the stage gets worked without a MEWP. They also carry the cable runs, worklights and fall-arrest anchors.

#### Grid hatches, drop shafts and a material handling opening

Hinged, interlocked hatches with 1,100 mm guardrail collars; a dedicated 3.0 × 3.0 m load-in shaft at each corner with a permanently rigged 1,000 kg hoist. Hatch interlocks tied to the exclusion-zone signage system.

**Why.** Every kilogram that goes up must come down. Ad hoc drop routes over the set are how lamps get dropped onto scenery.

#### MEWPs — scissor and articulating boom

Fleet (estimate) for Askja: 3 × 32 m working-height articulating booms (e.g. Genie Z-135/70 class), 4 × 20 m rough-terrain scissors, 2 × 12 m electric slab scissors. EN 280 design, annual thorough examination, non-marking tyres, spill trays on a stage floor.

**Why.** The only way to reach a point that is not on the matrix, and the only way to work over a set that is already dressed.

**Here.** Battery-electric only inside, which Iceland's cheap low-carbon electricity makes trivially affordable — but the machines are imported and there is no local rental depot deep enough to cover a breakdown, so hold spares on the lot.

#### Pipe grid — the two 2,000 m² stages

48.3 mm OD (1.9 in) schedule 40 black steel pipe on 1.2 m (4 ft) centres, hung on 6 mm threaded rod or 1/4 in wire drops at ≤1.5 m, WLL 45 kg per 300 mm of pipe with a total 250 kg per drop pair. Deflection limit L/180 between drops per ANSI E1.6-1 §6.6.2 batten criterion.

**Why.** Cheap, dense, infinitely re-clampable. For television and small-set film work a pipe grid beats a hoist grid on speed.

#### Motor power distribution and hoist drop boxes

Grid-level distribution at ~150 m intervals: 400 V 3-phase, 63 A and 125 A CEE (IEC 60309) outlets, RCD 30 mA on control circuits, plus 16-way motor control drops. Design target (estimate): 300 A per quadrant of Askja, 1,200 A total for motors alone.

**Why.** Trailing 60 m of motor cable across a dressed set is the most common cause of a hoist call running long.

**Here.** Landsvirkjun has refused supply to new industrial load and the grid is at capacity, so the whole lot's connection is a fixed number. Motor power is peaky and short-duration: size it against a site battery buffer rather than against the utility connection.

#### Aluminium truss inventory

400/500/600 mm box truss, 6082-T6 alloy, EN 1999-1-1 design, manufactured and rated per ANSI E1.2-2012 (called up by ANSI E1.6-1 §6.6.3) or DIN 4113; supplied with load tables for the actual span and load case. Design target (estimate): 1,200 m of 500 mm box across the lot.

**Why.** Every hung load spreads onto truss. Truss used past its published span/load table is the most common overhead failure mode.

#### Steel truss and heavy pick beams

Steel box truss for spans over 20 m or loads over 2,000 kg; welded to EN 1090-2 EXC3 with a per-configuration load table. Fatigue-checked to EN 1993-1-9 where the truss carries a moving load.

**Why.** Aluminium runs out at long spans and heavy point loads; substituting more aluminium truss instead of steel is how deflection becomes failure.

#### Grade 8/10 chain slings

EN 818-4 grade 8 (T) chain slings, single to four-leg, 7, 10 and 13 mm, WLL tagged with the leg angle range; shortening claws to EN 1677-1. Grade 10 (V) where weight matters, to the same standard family.

**Why.** Chain is the only slinging medium that survives contact with a hot lamp body or a sharp scenic edge.

#### Grid drop boxes — power, data, video, audio

Combined drops at 12 m centres: 63 A and 32 A CEE 3-phase, 16 A single, 4 × 5-pin DMX (ANSI E1.11 DMX512-A), 4 × RJ45 and 2 × duplex LC fibre for sACN (ANSI E1.31) and Dante, 2 × 12G-SDI BNC, and a timecode/intercom tie line.

**Why.** Without drops at grid level every signal run becomes a 60 m cable down the wall and across the floor.

**Here.** Fibre rather than copper on the long runs: it is immune to the transient earth potential differences a geothermally-heated, heavily-earthed building on volcanic ground can present.

#### Cable management — trays, catenaries and picks

Perforated cable tray on every perm line, segregated power/data by ≥300 mm or a divider; catenary wires for temporary runs; dedicated 250 kg cable-pick hoists at 24 m centres so cable bundles are motorised, not hand-hauled.

**Why.** Cable weight is real load and it is never in the rigging plot. A dressed 6,000 m² stage carries several tonnes of cable overhead.

#### Debris netting and drop-object control

Deployable safety netting over the working area during rig and de-rig; tethered tools on every grid call (tool lanyards rated to 2× tool mass); a hard-hat and exclusion-zone regime enforced by the hatch interlocks and floor signage.

**Why.** Everything the grid drops lands on people. Tool tethering is cheaper than one head injury.

#### Grid communications

Wired intercom outlets (4-wire and partyline) at every drop box, plus in-building DAS or leaky feeder covering the full grid volume for Tetra/UHF radio, and a dedicated rigging channel separate from production.

**Why.** A rigger 30 m up with no comms cannot be stopped and cannot call for help.

**Here.** 180,000 m³ of steel-lined acoustic shell is a Faraday cage; handheld radio will not work grid-to-floor without in-building distribution.

#### Cyclorama and greenscreen suspension track

Continuous curved I-beam track on the perimeter at ~2 m inboard of the wall, 500 kg/m distributed, with motorised carriers; plus straight cross-stage tracks for travellers and blacks. Drape weight (estimate): 500 g/m² wool serge, so a 30 m × 30 m black is ≈450 kg plus chain hem.

**Why.** Drapes are permanent overhead load that never appears on a rigging plot and never comes down.

#### Rigging store, motor store and chain wash

Heated, dehumidified store at grid or gallery level: motor racking for 300 hoists, chain wash and re-lube bench, hardware bins by WLL and colour year code, harness hanging store at ≤65% RH, and a quarantine cage for anything awaiting inspection.

**Why.** Kit that lives in a damp corner fails inspection. A quarantine cage is what stops a failed shackle going back into service.

**Here.** Geothermal heat makes a large heated store almost free to run, which is one of the few places Iceland's economics are strictly better than a UK or US lot.

#### Permit to work and grid access control

Card-controlled access to the stair cores and lift, a grid occupancy board, a permit for any work over an occupied deck, and hatch interlocks tied into the exclusion-zone lighting on the stage floor.

**Why.** Two crews on a grid unaware of each other is how loads move over people.

#### Rigger competence, certification and crewing

ETCP Certified Rigger (Arena and Theatre) or an equivalent European competence scheme as the house standard for head riggers; rescue-trained personnel on every grid call; documented training records tied to the inspection system.

**Why.** The regime is only as good as the person signing it off. A competent person who is not defined is not a competent person.

**Here.** There is no depth of certified entertainment riggers in Iceland. Budget for a resident head rigger plus a trained local crew, and accept 12–24 months of training lead time; flying riggers in during winter is hostage to Keflavík closures and road conditions.

### State of the art

#### Fixed heavy dead-hang points

Design target (estimate): 5,000 kg WLL points on truss node lines — 7.5 m across × 10 m along, ≈100 points over Askja — plus 4 × 12,500 kg mega-points at the mid-span nodes for gimbals, motion bases and large LED ceilings. Each stamped with WLL, unique ID and last-test date.

**Why.** Node-line points let heavy picks go straight into the truss instead of being spread through secondary steel that was never sized for it.

#### Greenbeds / loading galleries

Two intermediate galleries at ~12 m and ~21 m AFFL on the long walls, 2.0 m deep, 7.5 kN/m² live load, with 2,000 kg goods hoist landing on each. Removable handrail sections for lowering pipe and truss to the deck.

**Why.** Mid-height working platforms halve the vertical travel for lamps, drapes and cable, and let you rig a set wall from behind without the deck.

**Here.** Mid-height galleries are where the sub-arctic build cycle is won: crews can prep in the warm and dry above the deck rather than waiting for the floor to clear, which matters when winter weather stops all exterior work.

#### Grid personnel and goods lift

Machine-room-less passenger/goods lift to grid level, 2,000 kg / 26 persons, car 1.5 × 2.7 m to take a 12 ft pipe or a stretcher, EN 81-20/50, with firefighter's and evacuation modes to EN 81-72 / EN 81-70 accessibility.

**Why.** Thirty metres of stairs with a lamp on your shoulder is why grid work is slow and why grids go uninspected.

**Here.** With a small labour pool you cannot solve grid access with bodies; you solve it with plant. A lift is the difference between a two-person and a six-person grid call.

#### Tension wire grid (TWG) — the two 2,000 m² stages

3.2 mm (1/8 in) 7×19 galvanised or black aircraft cable woven on 50 mm (2 in) centres in both axes on a steel channel perimeter frame; ~94% optical transparency. Published ratings: 20 lbf/ft² (0.96 kN/m²) live load, ≤50 mm deflection under a 136 kg (300 lb) point load (per Tiffin Scenic / SkyDeck TWG literature).

**Why.** Lets one person walk anywhere overhead and focus a lamp with both hands, with no ladder and no fall-arrest tether.

**Here.** A TWG is a labour multiplier: it turns a two-person focus (one up, one spotting) into one person. That matters more in a market where you cannot hire a fourth grip at short notice.

#### Electric chain hoists — C1 class (loads moved over people)

1,000/2,000 kg C1 to DGUV V17/18: 10:1 design factor (vs 5:1 for D8), two independent brakes, four position limits (two working, two emergency), load monitoring and slack-chain detection. Control to EN ISO 13849-1 PL d Cat 3 minimum.

**Why.** The only lawful way to fly a load while a crew works beneath it. Without C1 you clear the floor for every move, which costs shooting days.

#### Variable-speed automation hoists

Servo or VFD winches, 250–1,000 kg, 0–120 m/min, wire rope lifting media, absolute encoder position to ±2 mm, EN 17206:2020 (+AC:2021). ANSI E1.6-1 §6.4.1.1 requires lifting media minimum tensile of 5× characteristic load, 8× static load and 1.33× peak load.

**Why.** Cued, repeatable, on-camera moves. Also the safe way to lower a heavy pick slowly onto a set.

#### Hoist and automation control system

Central console with dual-redundant safety PLC, e-stop ring, group-move monitoring (ANSI E1.6-1 §7.5.3 systems monitoring), Category 0 stop via a contactor or STO separate from the drive (§7.6.4.1.2), SIL 3 for software limits (§7.6.4.2/§7.6.4.5). Electrical to IEC 60204-32 (hoisting machines) or NFPA 79.

**Why.** A hundred hoists moving as one group needs synchronised monitoring; one hoist stalling in a group move tears the truss apart.

**Here.** Specify the console for a bilingual crew — Icelandic and English labelling — and for operators who may be contract riggers flown in for one show and unfamiliar with the desk.

#### Per-point load cells and live load monitoring

Shackle-pin or in-line load cells, 0.1% FS accuracy, wireless to a grid console, on every heavy point and every automation line; ANSI E1.6-1 §7.6.6.1/§7.6.6.2 treat overload and slack-line sensors as risk-driven additions. Annual calibration, certificate retained.

**Why.** Each point can be under its WLL while the aggregate on a truss is over. Nobody sees that without live monitoring.

**Here.** Also the earthquake instrument you already own: after a felt event, a load-cell log tells you which picks shifted, before anyone climbs.

#### Grid load management model and software

Live structural model of the grid, fed by the load cells and by the plotted rig, showing per-beam, per-truss and per-quadrant utilisation against the 0.50 kN/m² aggregate allowance and a concentrated-zone rule (design target, estimate: ≤1.5 kN/m² over any 200 m²).

**Why.** Turns 'is this rig legal' from an engineer's week into a screen a rigging gaffer can read at 06:00.

**Here.** The load budget is shared with snow, drift and ash. In February the software must show the roof's current snow load and shrink the available rigging allowance accordingly.

#### As-built point survey and BIM point database

Every point surveyed by total station to ±10 mm in x, y, z, tagged with a unique ID, WLL, beam reference, last inspection date and test history; exported to Vectorworks/AutoCAD and to the load model.

**Why.** Drawings lie. A rig plotted against nominal grid lines and hung against as-built steel is a day lost finding out which is right.

#### Motorised lighting bars / battens

Design target (estimate): 60 motorised bars in Askja, each 12 m of 48.3 mm pipe on four 500 kg lift lines, 0–18 m/min, integrated power and data on the bar. Deflection ≤ span/180 between lift lines per ANSI E1.6-1 §6.6.2.

**Why.** Lets a lighting rig be lowered to head height for relamping and refocus instead of a full MEWP call.

#### Structural load budget for snow, drift and volcanic ash

A published, dated allocation between snow (ÍST EN 1991-1-3 NA, with drift shape coefficients on a 100 m roof), ash, services and rigging. Wet volcanic ash bulk density ≈1,000–1,500 kg/m³ (USGS/IVHHN guidance): 100 mm of wet ash ≈1.0–1.5 kN/m², two to three times the design rigging allowance.

**Why.** Snow, ash and rigging draw on the same reserve. Nobody tells the rigging gaffer the roof is loaded unless someone owns this budget.

**Here.** The defining Iceland item. Ash does not melt and does not blow off, and it arrives with no notice. Provide roof-clearing access, a written trigger depth, and a rule that automatically reduces the permitted grid load when the roof load monitors read high.

#### Roof load monitoring and clearing access

Load cells or snow-depth sensors on the roof feeding the grid load model; permanent roof anchor lines (EN 795 C) and a lit, railed maintenance route so clearing can be done in darkness and wind.

**Why.** Clearing a 6,000 m² roof safely is a designed task, not an improvised one.

**Here.** Clearing will happen in December, in the dark, in 25 m/s wind. Design the route and the anchors for that, or the roof will not be cleared.

#### Grid over the water tank — Lon

Full-coverage grid over the 2,400 m² tank and the 20 × 20 m dive well at 6 m, on the same point matrix; all hardware over water in 316 stainless or duplex, no galvanised steel over the tank. Dedicated 1,000 kg dive-well picks on a 2.5 m matrix for rescue, camera and rigging drops.

**Why.** There is no floor to work from over a full tank. Everything must be reachable from the grid or from a boat.

#### Underwater and tank-edge rigging points

Cast-in stainless anchor sockets in the tank floor and walls on a 2 m grid, WLL 500 kg each, flush and capped when unused; tank-edge dead-hang beams for wave machines, rain bars and dump tanks.

**Why.** Set pieces underwater float; they must be tied down, and the tie-down points must exist before the tank is poured.

**Here.** Geothermal heat makes a heated tank cheap to run, which means warm, humid air rising into the grid all year — hence the stainless specification and a dehumidification interlock with the rigging store.

#### Overhead LED ceiling / virtual production support

Design target (estimate): 500 kg/m² localised capability over a 30 × 20 m zone via the 5,000 kg node points, with pre-run 400 A power, fibre and chilled-water routes. Panel weight for a 2.6 mm-pitch ceiling tile runs ≈25–40 kg/m² plus frame and processing.

**Why.** An LED ceiling is the heaviest thing a modern stage hangs and it is always a retrofit request. Provide the route now or refuse the booking later.

**Here.** Cheap low-carbon electricity is the selling point for a power-hungry volume, but the Landsvirkjun capacity constraint means the LED zone's peak draw must be inside the lot's fixed connection or served from on-site storage.

#### Acoustic interconnect detail in the grid — the two 2,000 m² stages

Grid steel structurally discontinuous at the party wall, with a removable or hinged bridging section rated 1,000 kg per point, keyed to the acoustic door state. Resilient breaks and no continuous rail beam, tray or conduit crossing the line.

**Why.** A continuous grid across an acoustic separation is a flanking path that defeats the wall, whether the door is open or shut.

#### Performer flying system provision

Dedicated flying winches and track to ANSI E1.43 (2016, revised 2025): design factors of 10:1 or greater for life-safety, redundant braking and backup, defined harness attachment and inspection. Control to EN ISO 13849-1 PL e / SIL 3. Independent secondary suspension on every performer line.

**Why.** Performer flying is the only rigging where a single failure is immediately fatal. It cannot be improvised onto a scenery grid.

#### Speaker hoists and bomb-bay doors

Dedicated 500–1,000 kg hoists on the playback/PA positions with interlocked bomb-bay doors and door limit switches in the hoist control (ANSI E1.6-1 §7.6.7 interlocks; the standard names exactly this case).

**Why.** Lets a speaker cluster be serviced from the deck without a MEWP and without disturbing the set.

#### Grid monorail crane for plant handling

Underslung monorail on the main perm lines with a 5,000 kg electric hoist, EN 15011 / EN 14492-2, serving the rigging store, the goods hoist landing and the AHU plant positions at grid level.

**Why.** Grid-level plant — air handlers, hoists, LED processing — has to be moved to be maintained, and there is no crane access at 30 m.

**Here.** Grid-level AHUs are large in Iceland because ash filtration means multi-stage filters (ISO 16890 ePM1 with heavy pre-filters) that need frequent, heavy changes — the monorail is what makes that a routine job.

#### Hoist test bed and proof-load rig

In-house load test frame to 10,000 kg with calibrated reference cell, for the ANSI E1.6-1 §11.3 regime: static test at ≥1× WLL, dynamic test at ≥1× WLL at minimum and maximum rated speed and acceleration, e-stop tested at 1× WLL and maximum speed in both directions, each load securing device tested independently.

**Why.** Testing 300 hoists a year offshore is impossible; a lot this size must own its own test capability.

**Here.** With a small labour pool and no local test house, in-house capability is not a luxury — the alternative is shipping hoists to mainland Europe and waiting.

#### Commissioning proof load and periodic re-proof of the fixed grid

Proof load every point class at commissioning, with deflection measured and recorded (ANSI E1.6-1 §11.3.1 requires tests be non-destructive; §11.1.3 requires re-test after installation, mishap, repair or modification). Re-proof a statistical sample every 4 years and any point after a seismic event or a shock load.

**Why.** A calculated WLL that has never been proved is a number on a drawing. The commissioning deflection record is also the baseline for detecting later damage.

**Here.** Iceland adds two mandatory re-test triggers most lots never write down: a felt earthquake, and any ash or drift event that took the roof past its trigger load.

### What gets got wrong

- Rain drumming is a rigging problem, not just an acoustics one. If the grid steel is bolted to the roof trusses, the perms conduct roof rain noise into the shooting volume and become the flanking path that defeats the whole acoustic design. Hang the grid off the inner shell or on resilient hangers with no rigid path to the outer roof, and prove it with a rainfall test to ISO 140-18 / EN ISO 10140-1 Annex methods (heavy rain ≈40 mm/h, intense ≈100 mm/h).
- Point load and aggregate load are different budgets and only one gets checked. Every point can be inside its 1,000 kg WLL while a single truss is overloaded. Without live per-point load cells feeding a structural model, nobody sees this until deflection is visible from the floor.
- The structural reserve is shared between snow, drift, volcanic ash, services and rigging — and only rigging has a person whose job it is to use it. Someone must own the budget and be able to reduce the permitted grid load when the roof is loaded.
- Wet volcanic ash is roughly 1,000–1,500 kg/m³ (USGS/IVHHN), three to five times settled snow, and unlike snow it does not melt, does not slide and does not blow off. A 100 mm fall is ≈1.0–1.5 kN/m², two to three times a typical grid's whole rigging allowance, and it arrives with hours of warning.
- Wind uplift reverses the load in the hangers. Designers habitually treat rigging as a permanent down-load that relieves uplift; Eurocode does not let you, and hanger rods sized only for tension will buckle. At vb,0 = 36 m/s (ÍST EN 1991-1-4 NA) and 30 m grid height, qp is around 2.3–2.4 kN/m² — roughly three times a typical UK inland site.
- The seismic risk to a grid is not strength, it is swing. A pick on 30 m of chain has a pendulum period of about 11 seconds; the acceleration is low but the displacement is large. Design clearance between adjacent picks and restraint on every suspended service, or the design event will be a collision event rather than a collapse event.
- D8, D8+ and C1 get used interchangeably by crews who have not read DGUV V17/18. D8 may lift only with the floor cleared; D8+ may hold static over people but not move; only C1 (10:1 design factor, dual brakes, four limits, load monitoring) may move a load over people. Confusion here is the most common serious breach on any stage.
- Iceland has no stage-specific machinery inspection law. Lög nr. 46/1980 and reglugerð nr. 367/2006 um notkun tækja are generic work-equipment rules enforced by Vinnueftirlitið. If you do not contractually adopt EN 17206:2020 and the DGUV V17/18 interval model as your house standard, you have no inspection regime at all — and no insurer will accept that.
- There is no depth of certified entertainment riggers in the Icelandic labour pool, and no local test house for hoists. Both have to be built on the lot: a resident head rigger, a trained local crew on a 12–24 month lead time, and an in-house proof-load test bed. Flying riggers in during winter is hostage to Keflavík closures.
- Everything structural is imported and shipping length caps section length at 12.2 m for containers. Design bolted splices at that module up front, or discover the charter freight cost after the steel is drawn.
- A tension wire grid is not a floor. Published ratings are around 20 lbf/ft² (0.96 kN/m²) with ≤50 mm deflection under a 136 kg point load. Crews put ballasted bases, road cases and generators on them because they look like floors.
- A dropped object at 30 m is a fatality, not an incident: a 0.5 kg shackle falling from Askja's grid arrives at 24.3 m/s with 148 J. Toe boards, mesh infill and tool tethering are not paperwork items.
- Cable weight is real overhead load and it never appears on a rigging plot. A dressed 6,000 m² stage carries several tonnes of cable, drape and hardware that no drawing accounts for.
- A grid that runs continuously across the acoustic separation between the two 2,000 m² stages defeats the separation whether the interconnect door is open or shut. The grid, the cable trays and the conduit must all break at the line.
- The 180,000 m³ steel-lined acoustic shell is a Faraday cage. Handheld radio will not reach from grid to floor without in-building distribution, and a rigger with no comms cannot be stopped mid-move.
- Escape lighting at grid level is the only light there will ever be. An acoustic stage has no rooflights, and from late November to mid-January there is no daylight to fall back on outside either.
- Load cells drift and nobody calibrates them once the building is in service. An uncalibrated load-management system is worse than none, because it is trusted.
- Grid-level air handlers in Iceland are oversized because ash filtration needs heavy multi-stage filters, and they compete with rigging for the same overhead volume. Coordinate the plant zones against the point matrix before the steel is fabricated, not after.
- Nobody re-proofs a fixed grid. Iceland gives you two triggers most lots never write down — a felt earthquake, and any drift or ash event that took the roof past its trigger load — and both should force a point re-test before the next rig.
- Galvanised hardware over the Lon tank will not last. Warm, humid, chemically treated water plus marine air plus geothermal heat is a C5-M environment; specify 316 stainless for everything over water, including the fall-arrest lifelines.

---

## 6. Sound stage floor and substructure

*Askja (6,000 m², 100 × 60 m), Sugar Land Studios, Mosfellsbær, Iceland. Slab, foundations, tolerances, finish, traps and pits, under-floor services, drainage, heating, isolation and load paths.*

### Essential

#### Ground investigation and geotechnical model

Rotary-cored boreholes to 25 m on a 30 m grid + CPT + MASW shear-wave profile to classify EN 1998-1 ground type A–E. Plate load tests to BS 1377-9 / DIN 18134. Target allowable bearing ≥300 kPa on compact moraine or sound lava. ÍST EN 1997-1 (Eurocode 7) GC3 supervision.

**Why.** Ground type sets the seismic design spectrum and the slab support modulus. Both are unrecoverable once 6,000 m² is poured.

**Here.** Mosfellsbær sits on lava, hyaloclastite and moraine over the Reykir low-temperature geothermal field — Veitur runs 22 wells there, originally 95–110 °C. Boreholes can hit thermal water; drilling needs Veitur/Orkustofnun clearance and hot ground changes slab thermal design.

#### Modulus of subgrade reaction (k) and proof-roll regime

Design k ≥ 0.05 N/mm³ (50 MN/m³) at formation, verified by 750 mm plate load test on a 20 m grid; Concrete Society TR34 4th ed. slab design consumes k directly. Proof-roll 100% with a loaded 20 t tipper, no visible rutting or heave.

**Why.** TR34 slab thickness is a direct function of k. A soft patch under a 6,000 m² jointless slab appears later as a cracked, dished panel under a telehandler.

**Here.** Icelandic moraine is variable and lava can be cavernous; blind local variation is common and demands a denser test grid than a UK equivalent.

#### Capping layer and crushed-basalt sub-base

150–250 mm crushed basalt Type 1 (0/40) laid in 150 mm compacted layers to ≥95% modified Proctor per ÍST EN 13286-2, frost-susceptibility screened (fines <5% passing 63 µm), CBR ≥30%. Blinding of 4/10 to protect the membrane.

**Why.** Uneven support and frost-susceptible fines produce differential heave that no slab thickness fixes.

**Here.** Basalt aggregate is abundant and cheap locally — one of the few structural materials not imported. Estimated frost penetration in the capital region 1.0–1.5 m; specify frost-free depth at the perimeter and under unheated aprons.

#### Damp-proof membrane / vapour control layer

0.3 mm (1200 gauge) LDPE DPM to ÍST EN 13967, lapped 300 mm, taped, dressed up the perimeter to DPC level, protected by a 4/10 blinding and a slip sheet.

**Why.** Rising moisture ruins floor paint adhesion and blisters any glued green-screen or vinyl overlay.

**Here.** No radon barrier required. Geislavarnir ríkisins' 2012–13 national survey recorded an indoor mean of 13 Bq/m³ (20 Bq/m³ in N. Iceland) against 200–300 Bq/m³ reference levels — young basaltic bedrock is uranium-poor. A whole layer of UK/US detailing is deleted.

#### Slip membrane / friction-reducing layer

Two layers of 0.125 mm polythene, or a proprietary low-friction sheet with coefficient of friction <0.5, laid loose over the insulation.

**Why.** Restrained shrinkage in a 100 m long jointless slab cracks it. Letting the slab slide is the cheapest crack control there is.

#### Concrete mix with ASR suppression

C32/40 to ÍST EN 206, exposure XC1 (XC4/XF3/XA1 at aprons and tank), Dmax 22 mm, w/c ≤0.45, silica-fume-blended cement, aggregate screened to ASTM C1260 (14-day expansion <0.10%) and ASTM C1567 for the combination.

**Why.** Icelandic aggregates are highly ASR-reactive; an unsuppressed 6,000 m² slab will map-crack and expand within 10–20 years.

**Here.** This is the single most Icelandic thing about the slab. Rhyolitic glass, altered basalt and unwashed sea-dredged sand caused Iceland's national ASR problem; the 1979 national measures — silica-fume blending, washing sea-dredged aggregate, tighter reactive-material limits — effectively ended it. Do not import a foreign mix design.

#### Ground-bearing slab

300 mm nominal, steel-fibre-reinforced, jointless construction, designed to Concrete Society TR34 4th ed. and ÍST EN 1992-1-1. Executed to ÍST EN 13670 tolerance class 2. Laser screed placement, single-pour bays up to 2,500 m².

**Why.** Thickness is what lets a 20 t boom lift, a crane outrigger and a two-storey set stand on the same floor with no load plan argument.

**Here.** Everything but the aggregate is imported — cement, fibres, admixtures, laser screeds and the crew who run them. One weather window, one batching plant; a broken supply chain mid-pour is a cold joint you live with forever.

#### Isolation joints at columns, bases and walls

Compressible closed-cell isolation strip, 25 mm, full slab depth, around every column, pad footing, plinth and wall base; diamond or circular isolation detail at columns.

**Why.** A slab cast hard against a column cracks radially at the first thermal or seismic movement.

**Here.** Seismic drift makes the joint width a real calculation, not a default: the slab must be able to move relative to the frame without pounding.

#### Acoustic separation joint at the box-in-box line

Full-depth discontinuity between the stage floor and every structure outside the acoustic envelope, filled with closed-cell resilient filler and sealed both faces with a two-part acoustic-grade sealant. No reinforcement, no conduit, no pipe crosses the line rigidly.

**Why.** One rigid crossing short-circuits the whole box-in-box and returns the roof, plant and road noise straight into the floor.

**Here.** With driven rain on a 6,000 m² roof, the flanking path through a bridged floor joint is the loudest thing on stage. This joint is worth more than any wall build-up.

#### Seismic snubbers and lateral restraint of the floating slab

Elastomeric snubbers around the floating slab perimeter with 10–20 mm free gap, designed to ÍST EN 1998-1. Icelandic PSHA puts the highest national zones at agR ~0.5 g (10% in 50 years); capital-region agR is lower — estimated 0.10–0.20 g, confirm against the ÍST EN 1998-1 National Annex.

**Why.** An isolated slab with no restraint walks off its bearings in a design earthquake and shears every service crossing it.

**Here.** Iceland is one of the few places where floating-slab acoustics and seismic restraint genuinely conflict — the snubber gap has to be big enough for isolation and small enough for the design drift.

#### Curing regime

Spray-applied curing compound to ÍST EN 14754-1 (efficiency ≥90%) plus 7-day sheeting; evaporation rate held below 0.5 kg/m²/h using ACI 308 nomograph. Wind screens mandatory during and after finishing.

**Why.** Plastic shrinkage cracking is driven by wind speed more than by temperature, and a cracked stage floor is visible in shot at 8K.

**Here.** This is where Icelandic wind bites the concrete. At 15 m/s over fresh concrete the evaporation rate exceeds 1 kg/m²/h even at 8 °C air. The pour must be inside a wind-tight envelope or it will craze.

#### Shot-fired fixing regime and nailability

Powder-actuated fasteners permitted into the top 100–120 mm only; declared safe-nailing depth recorded on the load plan. No post-tensioning, no top reinforcement mat, no services within 150 mm of the surface in the field. Make-good with an epoxy mortar between productions.

**Why.** Sets are fixed down with shot-fired nails. A floor that cannot be nailed into is a floor the crew will drill anyway.

#### Slab design loading — UDL and point loads

General field 25 kN/m² (2,550 kg/m²); dock, vehicle route and set-build zones 50 kN/m² (5,100 kg/m²). Design point load 100 kN (10.2 t) on a 300 × 300 mm contact patch, any position, plus a 4-wheel patch load case (estimates for best-in-class; TR34 4th ed. governs the check).

**Why.** This is the number every production asks for and no published stage spec gives. Get it wrong and a 20 t boom lift punches through in the middle of the field.

#### Dock apron, threshold and vehicle route

Slab thickened to 350–400 mm on the route from the elephant door to the dock, exposure XF3/XF4 externally with air entrainment 4–6% per ÍST EN 206, laid to falls externally only (never inside), edge armoured with galvanised angle.

**Why.** The dock takes every load twice — in and out — and freeze-thaw scaling destroys an unentrained external apron in two winters.

**Here.** Freeze-thaw cycling in the capital region hovers around zero for months, giving many more cycles than a colder climate. De-icing salt plus XF4 is the correct external spec even though snowmelt should make salt unnecessary.

#### Water tank structure (Lon)

2,400 m² in-floor tank, reinforced watertight concrete to ÍST EN 1992-3 tightness class 1 (crack width wk ≤0.2 mm), 400–500 mm walls and base, integral waterproofing admixture, PVC/hydrophilic waterstops at every construction joint, no through-wall ties.

**Why.** A tank that weeps loses water, undermines the surrounding slab and destroys the acoustic isolation of everything around it.

**Here.** Seismic design to ÍST EN 1998-4 — impulsive plus convective (sloshing) hydrodynamic pressures and freeboard for the sloshing wave. 6,000+ m³ of water is the largest single mass on the lot.

#### Tank buoyancy and uplift relief

Empty-tank flotation check against the design groundwater table with a minimum factor of safety 1.1 on uplift; under-base drainage blanket, perimeter land drain, and hydrostatic pressure-relief valves in the base slab as a fallback.

**Why.** An empty 6 m tank in wet ground floats. It cracks upward and the failure is terminal.

**Here.** High rainfall plus permeable lava gives perched water tables that respond fast to weather. This is a live Icelandic risk, not a theoretical one, and the tank is drained between productions.

#### Tank watertightness testing and commissioning

Fill test to ÍST EN 1992-3 / BS EN 1508 protocol: fill at ≤2 m/day, stabilise 7 days, measure over 7 days, permissible drop ≤1/500 of average water depth allowing for evaporation and rainfall via a control vessel.

**Why.** Leaks found after commissioning are found by the stage next door.

**Here.** Rain and evaporation corrections matter more here than in a dry climate — run the control vessel outdoors and indoors.

#### Acoustic and smoke sealing of every floor penetration

Every drain, duct, socket and trap sealed with a bolted gasketed cover achieving the wall's rating; deep-seal or waterless traps to hold seal when dry; fire-stopping to ÍST EN 1366-3 with the same integrity/insulation rating as the slab it passes through.

**Why.** An open gully is an acoustic hole, an air leak and a smoke path straight through a 60 dB envelope.

**Here.** On a 30 m tall stage the stack effect in an Icelandic winter is severe — an unsealed floor drain becomes an air inlet that whistles and drags cold air across the set.

#### Threshold drainage at elephant doors

Trace-heated stainless slot channel to ÍST EN 1433 class D400 immediately outside each door leaf, with a heated grating and a wind-driven-rain-rated threshold seal; external apron laid to 1:60 away from the door.

**Why.** The floor inside is dead level, so anything that crosses the threshold runs into the stage.

**Here.** This is where the wind and rain actually get in. At 36 m/s basic wind velocity (ÍST EN 1991-1-4:2005/NA:2010) rain travels horizontally and crosses a level threshold without hesitation. A heated, drained trench at every door is not optional here.

#### Foundation earth electrode and equipotential bonding

Ring earth conductor in the slab perimeter — 30 × 3.5 mm hot-dip galvanised strip or 10 mm stainless round — to DIN 18014 / ÍST EN 62305-3, bonded to the reinforcement at ≤20 m intervals, target electrode resistance ≤2 Ω, with test points at each corner.

**Why.** Every metal thing on the floor — track, boxes, sets, tank — must sit at the same potential, and a wet stage makes that a life-safety matter.

**Here.** Dry basalt and scoria have very high resistivity (estimated >1,000 Ω·m and often far higher), so driven rods often fail to reach target. The foundation electrode cast into the slab is frequently the only earth that works, and it is unrecoverable if omitted.

#### Holding-down and wind uplift anchorage

Foundations and slab designed as ballast against net roof uplift.
