With ÍST EN 1991-1-4:2005/NA:2010 vb,0 = 36.0 m/s (basic velocity pressure ~810 Pa, peak velocity pressure at 30 m estimated 2.0–2.5 kPa), uplift over 6,000 m² of roof is on the order of 15,000–18,000 kN — my estimate, to be confirmed by the wind study.

**Why.** On a lightweight long-span roof the uplift can exceed the dead load, and the only thing holding the building down is what is under the floor.

**Here.** Iceland is among the windiest inhabited places on earth. Hold-down, not bearing, is the governing foundation case — the opposite of the usual assumption, and the reason the substructure here is heavier than a comparable mainland stage.

#### Structural separation between stages and from plant

Full-depth separation joint between Askja, Lon and each 2,000 m² stage; every plant item (tank pumps, filtration, AHUs, chillers) on inertia bases with isolators tuned below 8 Hz; no shared slab, no shared foundation.

**Why.** Lon's filtration plant is a hundred-plus kW of rotating machinery; on a shared slab it is audible in Askja during a quiet take.

#### Published floor load plan and signage

A drawing issued to every production showing: field UDL 25 kN/m², dock 50 kN/m², point load 100 kN/300 mm², trap panel ratings, pit and tank extents, safe nailing depth, under-floor heating pipe layout, anchor socket coordinates and floor box positions. Painted zone markings at the dock.

**Why.** Productions bring their own machines. Without a published plan the first they know of a limit is when they exceed it.

**Here.** With a small local labour pool, the incoming crew is often foreign and unfamiliar with the building; the drawing has to do the work that institutional memory does elsewhere.

### Expected

#### Sub-slab thermal insulation

200 mm XPS to ÍST EN 13164, compressive strength class CS(10/Y)700 (700 kPa at 10% strain), λ 0.034 W/m·K, water absorption WL(T)0.7, creep-checked for 50 years. 300 mm under the tank/dive-well base.

**Why.** Without it a heated 300 mm slab bleeds geothermal heat to ground continuously and the floor surface never stabilises.

**Here.** Heat is nearly free from district supply, so insulation is a comfort and control measure, not an energy one. Where the slab sits over warm Reykir ground, insulation works in the opposite direction — keeping ground heat out so the floor holds a set-point.

#### Steel fibre reinforcement

Hooked-end cold-drawn wire fibre to ÍST EN 14889-1, 60 mm × 0.9 mm, tensile ≥1,100 MPa, dosage 35–45 kg/m³, residual flexural strength verified to EN 14651 (fR1, fR4) as required by TR34. Anti-crack mesh A252 only at pits and re-entrants.

**Why.** Fibres give the post-crack ductility TR34 relies on and remove the top mat, so shot-fired fixings do not hit bar.

#### Power-float and power-trowel finish with dry-shake topping

Monolithic finish, dry-shake hardener 4–6 kg/m² (corundum or metallic-free quartz), burnished to a closed matt surface. No liquid densifier that raises specular sheen.

**Why.** An open, dusty surface sheds fines into camera bodies, lenses and filtration all day.

**Here.** Volcanic ash tracked in on boots and tyres is basaltic glass at Mohs 5–6 — harder than the cement paste. A hardened surface is the only thing that survives it.

#### Abrasion resistance class

BS 8204-2 abrasion resistance class AR1 (special) for the shooting field and dock, tested to BS 8204-2 Annex B. Surface hardness by dry-shake, not by a topping screed.

**Why.** Steel-wheeled road cases, scaffold and skates chew a class AR3 floor into dust within a season.

**Here.** Ash acts as a lapping compound under every wheel that crosses the threshold. Iceland justifies the top abrasion class where a mainland stage might not.

#### Floor sealer and stage black finish

Two-pack water-based PU or epoxy, matt black, gloss ≤5 GU at 60° per ISO 2813, slip resistance PTV ≥36 wet (BS 7976-2) / R10–R11 (DIN 51130), fire class Bfl-s1 to ÍST EN 13501-1, recoatable without full removal.

**Why.** A shiny floor throws lamp reflections into every low-angle shot and reads as a mirror in a volume.

#### Chroma green floor zone

Defined bays coated in a specified chroma paint (Rosco Chroma Key Green 5710 / Digital Green are the reference products), matt, with a documented recoat cycle and a spectrophotometer reference chip retained.

**Why.** Repainting to a different green mid-shoot breaks the key and costs post.

#### Self-levelling compound and make-good stock

Cementitious SLC, ≥30 MPa compressive, 3–20 mm, for set-build make-good; epoxy patch mortar for nail-hole repair. Held on site as consumable stock with a documented cure time before repaint.

**Why.** Between-production turnaround is measured in days; a floor that needs a specialist to repair is a floor that stays damaged.

**Here.** Small labour pool — repair has to be doable by the in-house lot crew, not flown in.

#### Vehicle, crane and MEWP load cases

Check against real machines: JLG 1350SJP telescopic boom, 20,300 kg with ground bearing pressure 107 psi / 7.52 kg/cm² (738 kPa) per JLG's published spec; 55 t city crane outriggers at ~300–400 kN per pad with mandatory spreader mats; 3.5 t and 26 t vehicle axles on the dock route.

**Why.** Outrigger pads and telehandler wheels are the governing point loads on any stage floor, not the sets.

**Here.** Crane and MEWP fleets on the island are small; the machines that turn up are whatever Iceland owns. Design to the actual national fleet, not a catalogue.

#### Tank dewatering, sump and discharge

Duty/standby submersible pumps sized to empty 6,000 m³ in 12 h (≈140 l/s each), graded base falls 1:100 to a sump, plus a floor-level squeegee sump for the shallow zone. Discharge via silt trap and consented outfall.

**Why.** Turnaround between a water shoot and a dry build is set by how fast the tank empties and dries.

**Here.** Discharge of heated, chemically treated water is consented locally; Veitur and the municipality set limits. Warm discharge to a cold receiving water is an environmental issue on its own.

#### Under-floor service trenches

Perimeter trench 600 × 600 mm on all four walls plus two cross-stage runs, chequer-plate or GRP lids rated to the field point load, with segregated compartments for HV/LV, data and services per ÍST EN 50174-2 separation distances.

**Why.** Cable on the floor is a trip hazard, a dolly obstruction and something a telehandler crushes.

#### Floor boxes — power

Recessed boxes on a 12 m field grid plus a denser perimeter run. Per box: 2 × 63 A and 1 × 125 A 5-pin IEC 60309 at 400 V 3ph, 4 × 16 A CEE 7/4 Schuko at 230 V. 400 A/660 A Powerlock (BS EN 61984) terminations at the four company-switch positions only.

**Why.** Lamps, motors and effects all draw at floor level, and running everything from the perms wastes cable and time.

**Here.** Iceland is 230/400 V 50 Hz TN-C-S with Schuko outlets. Grid capacity is the constraint, not price — Landsvirkjun has refused supply to new industrial load, so the floor distribution has to be sized for a peak the connection may not deliver, with on-site battery or generation shaving it.

#### Floor boxes — compressed air, water and waste

Air at 7 bar (¾" claw coupling), potable cold water at 3 bar, and a 100 mm dry-break waste connection at eight positions around the perimeter, each with an isolating valve and a screw-down acoustic cover.

**Why.** Wet work, plaster, smoke machines, air rams and pneumatic effects otherwise need hoses run from outside through an open door.

**Here.** Icelandic cold water is exceptionally clean and needs no treatment; hot water is geothermal and sulphurous, so specify materials accordingly and never plumb it to anything that touches a set.

#### Floor box lids and load rating

Recessed-tray lids infilled with the floor finish, gasketed, flush ±1 mm, rated to ÍST EN 124 class D400 (400 kN test load) on the vehicle route and C250 elsewhere; every lid lockable and openable by one person with a standard key.

**Why.** A telehandler wheel finds the weakest lid on the floor. A crushed box takes the circuit and the fibre with it.

#### Floor drainage — gullies and slot channels

Stainless gullies to ÍST EN 1253 with 75 mm minimum trap seal, and stainless slot channel to ÍST EN 1433 load class D400 across the dock threshold. Field gullies at defined wet-work positions only, each with a bolted, gasketed blanking plate.

**Why.** You need drainage for wet work and rain FX, and you need it sealed and invisible the rest of the time.

#### Silt trap and oil separator

Silt trap upstream, then a class I full-retention separator to ÍST EN 858-1 (residual hydrocarbon <5 mg/l), sized to the contributing area, with an oil-level alarm and a documented emptying contract.

**Why.** Vehicles on stage, plaster wash-out, tank drain-down and set paint all reach the drain, and discharge consent is enforced.

**Here.** Volcanic ash washed off vehicles is fine, dense and abrasive — the silt trap fills far faster than a mainland equivalent and needs a bigger chamber and a shorter emptying interval.

#### Cascaded snow melting to apron and thresholds

Snow-melt loops in the external apron, dock and pedestrian routes, output ~250 W/m², fed from the underfloor-heating return at ~35 °C, with a snow/ice sensor and outdoor reset control.

**Why.** 6,000 m² of roof sheds snow, and the wind piles it against the doors. A blocked elephant door stops the production.

**Here.** Snjóbræðsla off district-heating return water is standard Icelandic municipal practice — it uses heat that would otherwise be thrown away, so the marginal cost of a heated apron here is close to zero. Nowhere else can justify heating an apron this size.

#### Floor protection materials and regime

6 mm hardboard/Masonite or 4 mm fluted polypropylene under all set builds, scaffold and vehicle standing; taped seams; spreader mats (minimum 1.0 × 1.0 m timber or composite) mandatory under every MEWP and crane outrigger.

**Why.** The cheapest way to protect an FF 45 floor is to stop people standing on it directly.

#### Maintenance, resurfacing and re-survey cycle

Repaint the field between productions; full FF/FL re-survey and grind/rectify on a 5-year cycle or after any water-tank campaign; annual anchor socket proof-test on a 10% sample; annual gully, separator and sump service.

**Why.** Flatness degrades. A floor surveyed once at handover and never again quietly stops being a superflat floor.

**Here.** Freeze-thaw at the dock and ash abrasion in the field both accelerate the cycle relative to a mainland stage.

### State of the art

#### Joint strategy: jointless field, armoured joints at bays

No sawn induced joints in the shooting field. Bay construction joints armoured with proprietary steel-plate load-transfer (dowel plate) systems, ≤3 mm lipping, sealed with a semi-rigid epoxy filler Shore A ≥50.

**Why.** A sawn joint under a dolly track is a bump on every take, and joint arrises spall under wheeled traffic.

#### Resilient bearing layer / floating slab

Full-surface PU elastomer bearing mat (e.g. Getzner Sylodyn/Sylomer class) or discrete bearings, tuned to system natural frequency 8–14 Hz, static deflection 1.5–4 mm, tested to ÍST EN 12354-2 / DIN 53513 for dynamic stiffness. Isolation efficiency >15 dB above 30 Hz (estimate).

**Why.** Ground-borne rumble arrives as low-frequency structure-borne energy that no wall build-up stops.

**Here.** Iceland runs continuous volcanic tremor and frequent M3–5 swarms on Reykjanes, ~30–40 km away. Persistent low-frequency ground motion is an operating condition, not an accident.

#### Flatness and levelness specification and survey

ASTM E1155 FF 45 / FL 35 overall ('very flat', the class the industry applies to television studios), or Concrete Society TR34 4th ed. FM1 (Property E ≤4.5 mm, Property F ≤1.8 mm at the 95th percentile; FM2 is E ≤6.5, F ≤2.0). Survey within 72 h of finishing, 3 m grid.

**Why.** Dolly and motion-control work reads every millimetre. Flatness cannot be assessed after the floor is painted and dressed.

**Here.** There is no Icelandic FM1/superflat laser-screed crew. Specialist contractors (CoGri/Face-type) fly in with their own kit for a single campaign — the pour programme is set by their availability, not the builder's.

#### Local surface regularity for dolly and track zones

BS 8204-1 class SR1 (±3 mm under a 2 m straightedge) as the site minimum; target ±1.5 mm under 3 m across designated dolly runs (estimate for best-in-class). No joint lipping >1 mm anywhere in the field.

**Why.** A grip shims track over a bad floor and loses the shot to a wobble at the shim.

#### Permanent cyclorama cove

Coved floor-to-wall junction, 600–900 mm radius, GRG or plaster on a rigid former, cast integral with a thickened slab edge, crack-isolated from both slab and wall by a debonded joint.

**Why.** A hard-edged wall junction reads as a line; a cracked cove reads as a worse line.

**Here.** The cove sits at the box-in-box line — it must be structurally coupled to neither the outer shell nor the slab beyond it, which is a genuine detailing problem.

#### Nailable lay-in timber sub-floor bays

Recessed bays, or loose-laid 18 mm WBP plywood to ÍST EN 636 on 50 × 100 battens, screwed to cast-in sockets. Bay perimeter flush with the slab, ±2 mm.

**Why.** Gives dressing, set fixing and cable burial without perforating the slab; essential where under-floor heating pipes forbid nailing.

#### Cast-in anchor socket grid

Stainless M24 cast-in threaded sockets (ÍST EN 1993 / ETA-assessed) on a 3 m grid, each proof-tested to 50 kN tension and 30 kN shear, flush-capped with a stainless screw plug, positions surveyed and published as a CAD layer.

**Why.** Motion bases, gimbals, wind machines, car rigs, stunt rigs and cinebot pedestals all need to be tied to the ground.

**Here.** Removes the need to ballast precision track — MRMC's Bolt X track needs 2 × 80 kg plates per section, or bolting down. Shipping 160 kg of steel per track section to Iceland is a real cost the anchor grid deletes.

#### Survey control monuments and stage grid

Permanent brass survey monuments cast flush into the slab at the four quadrant points and centre, coordinated to a site datum and to ISO 17123 instrument tolerances; painted 1 m stage grid or discreet floor fiducials for camera-tracking re-registration.

**Why.** Virtual production and LED-volume tracking need a physical datum to re-register against; so does every set that gets rebuilt.

**Here.** Iceland is deforming — plate spreading and volcano-tectonic displacement move survey control at measurable rates. Tie monuments to the national ISN2016 frame and re-observe periodically.

#### Trap room / under-stage basement

1,000–1,500 m² under a defined quadrant, 3.0 m clear height, floor loading 10 kN/m², with a goods lift, two protected stairs and a dedicated ventilation and drainage system. Structure to ÍST EN 1992-3 for water-retaining/water-excluding grade.

**Why.** Traps are where rising units, water FX return, cable runs and under-set effects live; retrofitting one under a live stage is impossible.

**Here.** Below-grade excavation near the Reykir wellfield risks intercepting warm groundwater and is permit-controlled. Tanking to BS 8102 Type B plus a drained cavity is the safe assumption.

#### Removable floor panels and panel handling

2.4 × 1.2 m panels over the trap room, rated 10 kN/m² UDL and 20 kN point, flush ±2 mm, gasketed for acoustic and smoke seal, lifted by a vacuum panel lifter; 20% of trap area panelled, the rest solid.

**Why.** A trap you cannot open quickly is not a trap. Panels that leak sound or smoke break the acoustic envelope and the fire strategy.

#### Pits: camera, elevator, lift and effects

Minimum one 4 × 4 × 2 m camera/effects pit and one scissor-lift pit, drained, tanked, with load-rated removable covers matching the field rating (100 kN point), fitted with 230 V/400 V, data and a sump.

**Why.** Low-angle camera work and rising units need real depth; a floor with no pit forces every low shot onto a built-up platform.

#### Turntable / revolve pit

12 m diameter recessed revolve, capacity 30 t, 0–3 rpm variable, flush ±2 mm with the field, drive under the trap, with a fill-in deck so the pit disappears when unused (dimensions and capacity are best-in-class estimates).

**Why.** Car and product work, and any rotating set piece, otherwise needs a hired above-floor turntable that eats 400 mm of clear height.

#### Motion-base / gimbal foundation

Dedicated reinforced plinth or pit with an isolated inertia block, sized for a 6-DOF hexapod of 6,000 kg payload class, dynamic reactions taken to foundation not to the floating slab (payload class is an estimate; confirm against the chosen platform).

**Why.** A hexapod running a sea state puts cyclic load into the floor that will crack a plain slab and broadcast into the neighbouring stage.

**Here.** Structurally separate stages matter here: Askja's motion base must not be audible in Lon.

#### Dive well

20 × 20 m at 6 m depth = 2,400 m³, walls to ÍST EN 1992-3, static head 60 kPa (6.1 t/m²) at base, with underwater lighting conduit, mounting sockets, viewing ports and a graduated depth marking to ÍST EN 15288-1.

**Why.** Underwater work needs real depth and hard points; a shallow tank limits you to surface effects.

**Here.** Pinewood's Underwater Stage is the published benchmark — 20 × 10 × 6 m, 1,200,000 litres, held at 30 °C with UV filtration to cut chlorine. Iceland can hold that temperature for almost nothing.

#### Tank filtration, heating and water treatment plant

Sand or regenerative-media filtration with turnover ≤6 h, UV primary disinfection with residual chlorine ≤1.5 mg/l, plate heat exchanger off district heat holding 28–30 °C, dosing and monitoring to ÍST EN 15288-2. Plant on inertia bases, isolated from the slab.

**Why.** Cast and crew spend hours immersed. Water clarity is a camera requirement and water temperature is a safety one.

**Here.** Heating 6,400 m³ from 4 °C to 30 °C is ~696 GJ (193 MWh) — my calculation. On Reykjavík geothermal district heat that is trivially cheap. This is the single largest Icelandic operating-cost advantage on the whole lot.

#### Tank deck / cover system

Demountable steel or aluminium deck over the full 2,400 m², rated to the field UDL of 25 kN/m², flush ±3 mm with the surrounding slab, installable in ≤2 shifts by four people.

**Why.** A tank stage that cannot be decked is a tank stage that sits idle whenever nobody is shooting water.

**Here.** Small labour pool — the deck has to be quick to lay with a small crew, which pushes toward larger, crane-handled modules.

#### Floor boxes — data, fibre and comms

Per box: 4 × Cat6A shielded to ISO/IEC 11801-1 (permanent link ≤90 m), 12-core OM4 and 12-core OS2 fibre on LC/SC, 2 × 12G-SDI BNC, and a duplex opticalCON. Segregated compartment, IP66 lid gaskets, individually labelled to a published patch schedule.

**Why.** Camera, tracking, LED-volume and virtual-production traffic all originate at floor level, and copper runs across a 100 m stage exceed the standard's limit.

#### Under-floor hydronic heating

PE-Xa or PE-RT pipe, 20 × 2 mm at 200–300 mm centres, to ÍST EN 1264-1..5, in the lower third of the slab, blended down from the 80 °C district supply to 35–45 °C flow, surface temperature capped at 29 °C occupied / 35 °C peripheral per EN 1264-2. Pipe positions surveyed and issued as a drawing.

**Why.** A 6,000 m² floor at 20–22 °C is the cheapest comfort and condensation control there is, and the heat is nearly free.

**Here.** Veitur supplies at 80 °C to the capital region, sourced partly from the Reykir and Reykjahlíð fields in Mosfellsbær which together provide 44% of the region's hot water. The stage sits on the wellfield. The catch is that heated pipes and shot-fired set fixings cannot occupy the same depth.

#### Slab thermal mass, response and condensation control

A 300 mm heated slab has an estimated 12–24 h thermal response; control on slab temperature not air temperature, with a weather-compensated curve and no fast setback. Dew-point interlock to shut heating or raise ventilation when a door opens.

**Why.** A warm slab and a 12 m door opened into Icelandic fog puts condensation on the floor, which is a slip and an electrical hazard.

**Here.** Long summer daylight and near-dark December swing the shell load enormously; the slab cannot follow. Also, flooding Lon's tank with cold water fights the underfloor system directly — they need to be zoned and interlocked.

#### Ground-borne vibration criterion and verification

Target ≤50 µm/s rms in 1/3-octave bands 8–80 Hz (VC-A, per the Ungar & Gordon generic vibration criteria adopted in IEST-RP-CC012), with an audibility target of NR 15 or better on stage. Baseline survey before construction, verification survey at handover.

**Why.** Sets the isolation design and gives you something to test against instead of an argument after handover.

**Here.** Baseline in Iceland includes volcanic tremor, Reykjanes swarms and wind-induced ground motion — measure across a winter storm, not on a calm day, or the baseline is meaningless.

#### Acoustic interconnect threshold between the two 2,000 m² stages

Floor continuous and flush ±2 mm across the interconnect line so a dolly can run through, with a demountable floor-level acoustic seal — a drop-in gasket track and threshold — restoring the rating when the stages are separated. Target flanking limit: no worse than the interconnect door's rating.

**Why.** The interconnect only earns its keep if the combined 4,000 m² floor is one usable plane, and only stays useful if the two stages can be independently quiet.

#### Ash control at the floor line

Entrance airlock with a recessed grille pit and matting well ≥6 m of walk-off, boot wash with heated water and a silt-trapped drain, and a wheel wash on the vehicle route, all upstream of the stage floor.

**Why.** Ash carried onto the floor becomes lapping compound under every wheel, gets into dolly track, pit mechanisms and tank filtration, and ends up in lens barrels.

**Here.** Icelandic ash events deposit fine basaltic glass across the region for weeks after an eruption. This is the one entry-control measure that a mainland stage would never build and this one must.

### What gets got wrong

- Under-floor heating and shot-fired set fixings are mutually exclusive at the same depth. The obvious answer — geothermal heat is free, so heat the floor — collides with the fact that every set is nailed down with powder-actuated fasteners. Resolve it by putting pipe in the bottom third of a 300 mm slab, declaring a safe nailing depth of 100–120 mm, surveying the as-built pipe layout, and issuing it as a drawing. A GPR or thermographic scan before any big build is cheap insurance. Get this wrong and the first day of the first build punctures the heating.
- A sound stage floor is dead level, not laid to falls. Every drainage instinct from industrial and commercial work is wrong here: falls make dolly moves read as a tilt and put sets out of plumb. Drainage is handled by localised gullies at defined wet-work positions, by a squeegee-and-pump regime, and by falls that exist only outside the threshold.
- The floor cannot be acoustically absorptive, and in a 180,000 m³ volume that governs the room. Rough arithmetic (Sabine, my calculation): with roughly 28,800 m² of surface, even if every surface but the floor achieved α = 0.8, RT60 lands near 1.5 s, and reaching 1.0 s would need an average α of about 1.0 across the whole envelope, which is impossible. A stage this big is reverberant empty and always will be. Do not let anyone specify an absorptive floor to fix it — the floor has to be hard and workable, and the volume is the thing you accepted when you chose 30 m of clear height.
- One rigid crossing kills the floating slab. A drainage pipe, a conduit, a bonding strap or a lazy bit of reinforcement bridging the acoustic separation joint short-circuits the entire box-in-box. Every crossing must be flexibly coupled, and the joint has to be inspected before the slab is poured over it, because after that you can never find the offender.
- An empty water tank floats. Iceland's combination of high rainfall and permeable lava produces perched water tables that respond within hours to weather. Lon's tank spends much of its life drained. Check uplift with a factor of safety on flotation, and provide an under-base drainage blanket plus hydrostatic relief valves as the fallback.
- Plastic shrinkage cracking in Iceland is a wind problem, not a heat problem. The ACI 308 evaporation nomograph is driven hard by wind speed: at 15 m/s over fresh concrete you exceed 1 kg/m²/h even at 8 °C air temperature. The pour has to happen inside a wind-tight envelope. A crew that has only poured in mainland Europe will not expect this and will craze 6,000 m².
- Floor boxes in the middle of the field get built over, and then crushed. Sets go up on top of them, cable gets trapped, and a telehandler finds the weakest lid. Keep the field grid coarse (12 m), put the density in the perimeter trench, rate the lids to EN 124 D400 on the vehicle route, and publish the coordinates so a set designer can avoid them.
- Earthing is genuinely hard on lava. Dry basalt and scoria have very high resistivity and driven rods routinely fail to reach a usable value. The foundation earth electrode cast into the slab perimeter is often the only earth that works — and it is completely unrecoverable if it is left out of the pour. This is the classic Icelandic omission.
- The governing foundation case is uplift, not bearing. With a basic wind velocity of 36 m/s and a 6,000 m² low-pitch roof, net uplift can exceed the roof's dead load, and the substructure becomes ballast. Engineers arriving from milder climates size foundations for downward load and get the wrong answer.
- Iceland deletes the radon barrier and adds an ash airlock. Foreign specifications get imported wholesale and carry a radon membrane that the national indoor mean of 13 Bq/m³ makes pointless, while omitting the entrance airlock, boot wash and wheel wash that basaltic ash absolutely requires. Both are floor-line decisions.
- Do not import a concrete mix design. Icelandic aggregates are ASR-reactive — rhyolitic glass, altered basalt, sea-dredged sand — and the national fix since 1979 is silica-fume-blended cement with washed aggregate and ASTM C1260/C1567 screening. A mix that is perfectly sound in the UK will map-crack here within a couple of decades.
- There is no local superflat crew. FF 45 / FL 35 across 6,000 m² needs specialist laser-screed contractors who fly in with their own equipment for one campaign. The pour programme is dictated by their availability and the weather window, not by the main contractor's sequence, and the flatness survey must be done within 72 hours of finishing — after that you are grinding, not specifying.
- The tank and the heated floor fight each other. Flooding 6,000 m³ of cold water into Lon while the underfloor system holds the slab at 22 °C wastes heat and destabilises control in both. Zone them and interlock them; put the tank on its own heat exchanger off district supply rather than letting it drag the building's heating circuit down.
- Under-floor power distribution has to be sized for a peak the grid may not give you. Icelandic electricity is cheap and low-carbon, but Landsvirkjun has refused supply to new industrial load and capacity, not price, is the binding constraint. Size the floor boxes and trenches for the full lighting and effects peak, then plan on battery or on-site generation to shave it — the cabling is the part you cannot add later.

---

## 7. Doors, access and logistics

*Askja (6,000 m²) and the Sugar Land Studios lot, Mosfellsbær, Iceland*

### Essential

#### Primary elephant door, Askja east elevation (acoustic sliding leaf set)

Proposed 24.0 m clear width x 12.0 m clear height, 4 biparting leaves ~6.1 m wide, ~90 mm (3.5 in) steel acoustic panel. Benchmarks: Pinewood 007 Stage dock door 84 ft x 32 ft (25.6 x 9.75 m) (thestudiotour.com); Specialty Doors quote 53 STC on 3.5 in panels. Size is my estimate.

**Why.** Nothing else admits a 12 m truck-mounted crane, a standing set wall in one piece, or a shipping-container-sized build. Undersize it once and every set is cut and re-joined on stage.

**Here.** Site it on the lee elevation. With ÍST EN 1991-1-4/NA:2010 vb,0 = 36 m/s (Dlubal), a 288 m² leaf set is the single biggest sail on the lot.

#### Secondary elephant door, west elevation

Proposed 12.0 m x 9.0 m, 2 leaves, same acoustic build. Shinfield Studios publish two elephant doors per stage (16 ft x 16 ft on Stage 1, 20 ft x 20 ft on Stages 3 and 14). Size is my estimate.

**Why.** One door means every load-out blocks the load-in and a set can only be struck the way it came. Two doors give a through-route and a second escape for vehicles.

**Here.** Second door on the opposite face lets you always work the sheltered side whatever the wind direction that day.

#### Elephant door acoustic performance and flanking control

Target Rw 50–55 dB / NIC 50 for the closed moving element, tested EN ISO 10140-2, rated EN ISO 717-1 (US equivalent ASTM E90 / E413). Specialty Doors publish 53 STC and a field NIC 50 at an 18 x 20 ft install. Head, jamb and sill flanking detailed to match the shell's Rw 65+.

**Why.** A stage is only as quiet as its worst opening. A 45 dB door in a 65 dB wall sets the whole stage at roughly the door.

**Here.** The exterior noise to keep out is wind roar and rain-on-roof re-radiated round the opening, not traffic — broadband and low-frequency, which punishes seals.

#### Bottom track, floor recess, drainage and track heating

Flush stainless track in a heated recess, slot drain outboard, 100% of track length trace-heated or geothermal-loop heated, self-draining so no standing water. Track flatness matched to stage floor tolerance (±3 mm under a 3 m straightedge, typical stage spec — estimate).

**Why.** A frozen or silted bottom track jams a 30-tonne leaf set and a proud track rail derails every dolly and scenery cart that crosses it.

**Here.** Geothermal district heat is nearly free, so run a hydronic snowmelt loop in the track and apron rather than electric trace — it removes an electrical load from a grid that has none to give.

#### Full-perimeter acoustic seals, double compression plus inflatable sill

Double acoustical seals to the full perimeter of every moving element (the Specialty Doors arrangement), plus an inflatable/drop seal at the sill giving a positive squeeze on close. Silicone or EPDM rated to -40 °C, ozone- and UV-stable.

**Why.** Seals are the whole acoustic rating. A 10 mm gap at a sill will cost 10–15 dB of an otherwise 53 dB door.

**Here.** Sub-arctic cold stiffens standard EPDM and ash abrades it. Specify low-temperature-flexible compound and treat seals as an annual consumable, not a 20-year part.

#### Anti-lift restraints and seismic hold-downs on door assemblies

Top guides and bottom shoes restrained against uplift and out-of-plane displacement; door head beam and track designed as a non-structural element to EN 1998-1 §4.3.5 with the Icelandic NA (ÍST EN 1998-1:2004/NA:2010; agR across Iceland 0.1 g–0.5 g, capital area toward the low end — estimate).

**Why.** A door leaf that walks off its track in a quake blocks the only vehicle escape route from a 6,000 m² space.

**Here.** Iceland is seismically active and the site sits in the capital region; the design earthquake is a real load case, not a formality.

#### Door head transfer beam / lintel truss

Deep transfer truss over the 24 m opening carrying roof and perm loads round the door, deflection limited to ~span/500 and absolutely capped at the door's seal compression range (~15 mm) under full snow plus wind — estimate.

**Why.** If the head deflects more than the seal can take up, the door leaks sound and water at exactly the moment weather is worst.

**Here.** Snow load plus a 36 m/s wind uplift reversal on the same night means the head sees both directions; seals must work under both.

#### Door safety system to EN 12453 / EN 12445

Light curtains full leaf height, resistive safety edges on leading edges, force limitation tested per EN 12445, hold-to-run plus deadman for the last 500 mm, audible/visual warning during travel, emergency stop at each control point.

**Why.** A powered 30-tonne leaf against a crew member is unsurvivable. The standard is also the CE/UKCA route to market for the door under EN 13241.

**Here.** Icelandic building control accepts the harmonised EN suite (adopted as ÍST EN), so the same certification path applies.

#### Personnel sound lock (double-doorset vestibule)

Two acoustic doorsets in series, vestibule 2.5 x 3.0 m minimum, absorptive lining (NRC ≥0.85 on walls and ceiling), interlocked or self-closing so both are never open together. Target combined Rw ≥55 dB.

**Why.** A single personnel door onto a live stage is an open microphone to the outside world and a light leak onto the set.

**Here.** Also the wind lobby. A single door onto a 30 m/s wind is physically hard to close and slams hard enough to be heard in the take.

#### Acoustic personnel doorsets

44–54 mm steel or composite leaf, Rw 45–52 dB tested EN ISO 10140-2, magnetic and drop-seal perimeter, EN 1154 controlled closers, EN 1935 grade 13 hinges. Jewers publish an Acoustic Matrix range at 27–48 dB reduction across configurations.

**Why.** Sets the acoustic floor for every routine entry and exit — a hundred cycles a day.

#### Escape doors, hardware and travel distances

Outward-opening final exits with EN 1125 panic bars (public/crew) or EN 179 emergency exit devices (staff-only), fire-rated where crossing compartments to EN 16034 / EN 13501-2, sized and spaced per Icelandic Byggingarreglugerð nr. 112/2012.

**Why.** A 6,000 m² windowless volume with a working set is a high occupant load in poor visibility; the doors are the whole life-safety case.

**Here.** Byggingarreglugerð 112/2012 governs, referencing the ÍST EN suite, and requires two independent escape routes to a safe place at ground level.

#### Loading dock bays, dock-height

Proposed 4 bays per large stage at 1,200 mm dock height, 3.5 m bay centres, bay openings 3.0 m w x 3.5 m h, dock approach apron falling away 1:100 from the building. Estimate.

**Why.** Consumables, props, wardrobe, catering and waste all move at dock height on pallets; without it every box is hand-balled off a tail-lift.

**Here.** Apron must fall away from the building for meltwater, but not so steeply that a trailer on ice slides toward the dock.

#### Hydraulic dock levellers

To EN 1398 (Dock levellers — Safety requirements). Suggested 2,000 x 2,500 mm platform, 6 t or 10 t rated, 400 mm lip with 5° curvature, working range about +360/-300 mm about dock level (a published EN 1398 example, Armo UK), hydraulic with lip and platform safety supports.

**Why.** Truck bed heights vary by 400 mm+; without a leveller the pallet truck either cannot cross or tips.

#### At-grade drive-in ramp

One ramp per stage from apron to stage floor level at 1:12 maximum (8.3%), 6 m wide, transition curves top and bottom to avoid grounding a low-loader, heated surface.

**Why.** Camera cars, picture vehicles, scissor lifts and low-loaders cannot use a dock; they drive in.

**Here.** Heat the ramp from the geothermal loop. An iced 1:12 ramp with a loaded low-loader on it is uncontrollable.

#### Stage apron hardstanding

Reinforced concrete apron, proposed 40 m deep across the full elephant door face, designed for 44 t vehicles and for point loads from crane outriggers; surface regulated so a scenery dolly runs true. Estimate.

**Why.** This is where the truck stands, the crane sets up and the set gets assembled outdoors before it goes in.

**Here.** Concrete not asphalt: asphalt ruts under outrigger pads and degrades under freeze-thaw and studded winter tyres, which are near-universal in Iceland.

#### Internal haul roads and road loading

Roads designed to EN 1991-2 Load Model 1 with the Icelandic NA, 7.5 m carriageway two-way plus 1.5 m verges, 12.5 m minimum kerb radius, no adverse crossfall on the scenery route. Estimate on geometry.

**Why.** Scenery moves on multi-wheel trailers with concentrated loads; a road built to car standards fails within a season.

**Here.** Frost-susceptible subgrade demands a frost blanket and a deeper pavement than the equivalent UK spec; freeze-thaw is the design driver, not traffic count.

#### Swept-path geometry and turning circles

Yard proven by swept-path analysis for a 16.5 m artic and a 18.75 m drawbar combination (Iceland's limit before an ICETRA exemption is needed, per island.is), plus abnormal loads over 4.20 m high or 2.55 m wide by permit. Turning head 30 m minimum diameter — estimate.

**Why.** A truck that cannot turn must reverse blind across a live lot, or must be uncoupled and shunted, which costs an hour a load.

**Here.** Iceland's thresholds are height >4.20 m, width >2.55 m, rigid length >12 m and combination >18.75 m before an exemption permit is required (island.is / ICETRA).

#### Height-restriction envelope across the scenery route

Continuous minimum clear height of 8.0 m from gate to every stage door, and no overhead services crossing the route: all power, data and pipework buried or ducted, all lighting on breakaway or fold-down columns clear of the swept envelope. Estimate.

**Why.** One 5 m cable across a road caps every set piece at 4.5 m and the stage's 30 m of height is wasted.

**Here.** Overhead lines are a poor idea here anyway — wind and icing bring them down — so burying them serves two purposes at once.

#### Scenery route from mill and paint shop to stage

Direct, level, covered route with no door narrower than the mill's own door and no threshold over 15 mm; route width to pass a 12 m x 8 m flat standing on edge on a dolly; turning bays at every direction change. Estimate.

**Why.** The size of the biggest thing the mill can build is set by the narrowest point between the mill bench and the stage floor, not by the mill.

**Here.** Cover it. An uncovered route means scenic finishes are weather-damaged between build and set, and the paint shop reworks everything.

#### Inbound freight route and port/airport interface

Route from Sundahöfn (Iceland's main container port, in Reykjavík) and from Keflavík airport to the lot at Mosfellsbær — the town is 12 km east of Reykjavík (Wikipedia). Route surveyed for abnormal loads, bridge and roundabout constraints logged. Road distances are my estimate.

**Why.** Everything structural is imported; the lot's capability is capped by the biggest thing that can be landed and driven to the gate.

**Here.** Everything structural is imported. Sailings are weekly, not daily, so a missed container is a week, and winter weather closes Route 1 sections without notice.

#### Snow, ice and ash clearance regime for access routes

Geothermal hydronic snowmelt to the apron, ramp, dock approach and door tracks; mechanical clearance fleet for the rest; ash wash-down with a silt interceptor on the yard drainage; documented clearance priority order with the scenery route first.

**Why.** Every access asset on this list is worthless the morning it is under 400 mm of drifted snow or 20 mm of ash.

**Here.** This is the defining Icelandic access item. Geothermal district heat makes snowmelt across thousands of square metres economic here in a way it is nowhere else — use the free heat rather than the constrained electricity.

#### Traffic management plan, marshals and pedestrian segregation

One-way lot circulation, marked pedestrian routes physically separated from the scenery route, banksman required for all reversing, no-reversing design at the docks where possible, 15 km/h lot limit.

**Why.** The highest-risk moment on any studio lot is a reversing artic near a crowd of crew leaving a stage.

**Here.** Reduced visibility from darkness, driving snow and ash haze makes marshalling and high-visibility marking do more work here than elsewhere.

#### Emergency and fire service vehicle access

Perimeter access road giving appliance access to at least two faces of every stage, hardstanding for a pumping appliance and an aerial platform within reach of the elephant doors, hydrants per the Icelandic fire authority requirement under Byggingarreglugerð 112/2012.

**Why.** A stage fire load is enormous and the building is windowless; the fire service fights it from the doors, so the doors must be reachable by appliance.

**Here.** The access road must be kept clear of snow as a life-safety duty, which puts it high in the clearance priority order alongside the scenery route.

### Expected

#### Door drive: roller chain concealed in track, VFD soft start/stop

Chain-in-track drive (as Specialty Doors describe for their stage doors), inverter-controlled, ~0.1–0.15 m/s leaf speed, drive noise target NR 25 at 3 m so a leaf can be moved between takes. Powered operation to EN 12453, mechanical aspects EN 12604, product standard EN 13241.

**Why.** Rack-and-pinion or exposed rope drives chatter and shed grease; a screaming door means door moves only between setups, which costs shooting hours.

**Here.** Cheap low-carbon electricity makes powered operation on every leaf economic; the constraint is grid connection capacity, not tariff.

#### Removable centre wind post / king mullion

Demountable structural mullion at mid-span of the primary opening, pinned top and bottom, removable by two people with a pallet truck in under 15 minutes; leaves latch to it for storm loading. Designed for the full EN 1991-1-4 peak velocity pressure across the closed opening.

**Why.** Without a mullion the closed leaves must span 24 m against wind alone, which drives leaf depth, weight and cost through the roof.

**Here.** With vb,0 = 36 m/s the mullion is not optional engineering vanity; it is what keeps leaf depth at ~90 mm instead of ~300 mm.

#### Wicket (pass) door within the elephant leaf

1,000 x 2,100 mm minimum clear, threshold ≤15 mm, in-leaf, self-closing, with its own acoustic seals; interlocked so the leaf cannot move while the wicket is unlatched. Panic hardware to EN 1125.

**Why.** Without it, one person fetching a C-stand cycles a 30-tonne leaf and 288 m² of weather.

**Here.** Cuts the number of full-leaf opens per day sharply, which is the main way you keep heat and dry air inside during a Mosfellsbær winter.

#### Manual and backup operation of the main doors

Hand-crank or auxiliary hydraulic drive per leaf, and a generator/UPS-backed control circuit; target full close of the opening within 20 minutes with no mains — estimate.

**Why.** Power fails, and a stage that cannot close its 24 m door in a storm loses the set, the lighting rig and the schedule.

**Here.** The grid is reliable but the site's own connection is the weak point; storm-driven local outages are the realistic failure case.

#### Door thermal, air and water performance classification

Thermal transmittance to EN 12428, air permeability to EN 12426 (target class 4–6; class 1 is 24 m³/m²·h at 50 Pa), watertightness to EN 12425, wind resistance to EN 12424 class 5 or a specified higher design pressure with EN 12444 test.

**Why.** Untested doors are the leak path that defeats the stage's pressurisation, its humidity control and its heating bill.

**Here.** Class 5 is the top published EN 12424 class and is not enough here; specify a project-specific design pressure above class 5 and require an EN 12444 test to it.

#### Door leaf parking pockets / external leaf housings

Enclosed, weathered pockets either side of the opening, drained and heated, deep enough for the full leaf stack plus 300 mm, with removable panels for leaf extraction by crane.

**Why.** Leaves parked in the open ice up, fill with ash and jam; and a leaf that cannot be lifted out cannot be repaired.

**Here.** Non-negotiable here. Volcanic ash plus driven snow into an open track pocket is a guaranteed jam.

#### Escape route snow and wind management at final exits

Final exits set behind storm porches or in recessed reveals, doors opening into a swept and heated zone, snow-drift-clear width of 1.2 m maintained, outward swing protected from drift build-up and from wind pressure exceeding the 100 N opening force limit.

**Why.** A fire exit with a metre of drifted snow against it or a 400 N wind load on the leaf is not a fire exit.

**Here.** This is the Icelandic failure mode that kills people: drifted snow against an outward-opening exit after a night of driven snow.

#### Blackout light-lock in every personnel lobby

Offset door leaves, matt black finish (LRV <5%), no glazing on the inner leaf, light-tight brush and drop seals, lobby lighting on a red/low-level circuit with a hot-set interlock.

**Why.** A sliver of daylight across a black-drape set at 15:00 is a re-shoot.

**Here.** Cuts both ways: a June midnight is bright enough to fog a shot, and December's few hours of low, raking sun cut through a lobby at exactly the worst angle.

#### Hot-set interlock, red lights and bell/klaxon system

Recording lamp circuit ties every stage opening: red lamp and bell outside each door, elephant-door drive inhibited while recording, wicket alarms on prop, override key at the AD's station.

**Why.** A door cycled during a take costs the take; a door cycled during a wire stunt costs more.

#### Door-prop alarms and access control on all openings

Card/biometric readers with anti-passback and anti-tailgate on personnel doors; prop alarms on every restricted opening as required by MPA Content Security Best Practices; perimeter gates locked at all times; all events logged and retained.

**Why.** Content security is contractual. A studio that cannot pass a TPN assessment cannot host a major title.

**Here.** Small labour pool means fewer guards; electronic control and logging substitutes for the headcount a US lot would put on a gate.

#### Segregated cast/crew entrance and unit-base gate

Separate personnel entrance with turnstile, pass office and holding lobby, away from the goods dock and the elephant door apron; vehicle route to unit base kept clear of the scenery route.

**Why.** Mixing 200 crew on foot with reversing 16.5 m artics at one opening is the classic lot fatality.

#### Interconnect floor threshold and services crossing

Flush, load-rated floor track with removable acoustic infill, drained; power, data and lighting-control tie-lines crossing at the same point in a sealed acoustic penetration; combined-mode floor flatness matched across the joint.

**Why.** A step or a lip at the interconnect makes the combined stage useless for camera movement across the join.

#### Dock shelters and inflatable dock seals

Inflatable or high-retention foam shelters sealing head and both jambs to the trailer body, rated for repeated high-wind cycling, with breakaway frames.

**Why.** Sealed docks stop the weather and stop the noise path straight into the stage while a truck is on the bay.

**Here.** Standard curtain shelters shred in this wind. Specify inflatable seals with wind-rated frames, and expect to replace curtains far more often than a mainland lot would.

#### Dock bumpers, wheel guides and vehicle restraints

Laminated rubber bumpers 250 mm projection, steel wheel guides to the bay approach, and powered wheel-hook or chock restraints interlocked to a red/green traffic-light system inside and outside per EN 1398 practice.

**Why.** Trailer creep and early pull-away are the two ways a loader ends up in the gap.

**Here.** Ice under the drive wheels makes chocks unreliable; the powered hook restraint is the right answer here, not a rubber wedge.

#### Dock canopy and storm protection

Cantilever canopy over the bays, minimum 4.0 m projection, designed for the site snow load (EN 1991-1-3 with Icelandic NA) and full uplift, with heated gutters and downpipes.

**Why.** Loading in horizontal rain destroys cardboard-packed props and soaks electrical stock.

**Here.** Canopies are uplift-critical here and are a common structural failure in Icelandic wind; design them as a primary wind element, not an add-on.

#### Crane hardstanding and outrigger pad design

Designated crane pads sized for a 500 t class all-terrain (four outriggers, worst-case ~100 t per pad). Working example from published guidance: 49.4 t per pad on a 0.58 m² mat needs about 85 t/m² (≈835 kPa) allowable bearing; design pads and mats to keep imposed pressure inside the verified ground capacity.

**Why.** An outrigger punching through the apron drops the boom. It is the single most consequential ground-bearing decision on a studio lot.

**Here.** Icelandic ground is often young lava and compacted fill with pockets of soft ash; ground investigation at each crane pad, not a lot-wide assumption.

#### Trailer marshalling and lay-down yard

Hardstanding for proposed 20 trailers plus 40 containers, marked bays, tied-down points for empty containers and skips, lighting to EN 12464-2 class for external work areas. Estimate on counts.

**Why.** Sets arrive in advance of the shoot and leave after it; without a lay-down yard they sit on the stage floor and the stage cannot be turned around.

**Here.** Empty containers and skips are lethal projectiles in this wind. Every empty needs a ground anchor or ballast, and that has to be designed in.

#### Mill, paint and plaster shop doors matched to stage doors

Mill out-door and paint shop out-door at least 8.0 m w x 8.0 m h, sliding, on the same axis as the scenery route; plaster shop door sized for a full moulded piece on a pallet. Estimate.

**Why.** A mill that can build bigger than it can post is a mill that cuts everything in half and loses the joint on camera.

#### Set-piece handling kit

Air castor sets (4 x 12 t modules), scenery dollies, Bishop trolleys, steel road plates for crossing soft ground and cable runs, pallet trucks, and a 15 m telescopic handler with a 4 t at 10 m chart. Estimate on quantities.

**Why.** Between the truck bed and the mark on the stage floor, everything moves on this kit. Without it you rent a crane for a wall.

#### Forklift, telehandler and MEWP fleet plus charging

All-electric fleet with fast-charge points at the docks and in the yard; indoor units electric only (no IC engines on stage); load-rated for the apron and the stage floor.

**Why.** Diesel plant on a stage is a noise, exhaust and air-quality problem you then have to ventilate away.

**Here.** Cheap low-carbon electricity makes the electric fleet obviously right, but the site's grid connection has to be sized for the charging load — and Landsvirkjun has refused supply to new industrial load, so charging demand must be inside the connection you already hold.

#### Goods and scenery lift to gantry/perms level

Proposed 5,000 kg goods lift, 3.0 x 5.0 m platform, serving stage floor, gantry levels and the perms at 30 m, EN 81-20/50 for a lift or EN 1570-1 for a lifting table. Estimate.

**Why.** Lamps, cable, rigging steel and effects gear all have to reach 30 m. Hand-hauling that up a ladder is where rigging injuries come from.

#### Roof and perms access, with fall protection

Internal stair to perms plus an external protected stair to roof level; EN 795 class C anchor line system across the roof; roof access hatch weathered and interlocked to the fire alarm.

**Why.** Roof plant, drains, and the rain-noise treatment all need routine access, and this roof is the last place to improvise access.

**Here.** Roof access in winter is a genuine hazard: ice, darkness for most of the working day in December, and gusts that exceed safe working limits. Plan for a documented wind cut-off for roof work.

#### Crash-rated main vehicle gate

Cantilever sliding crash gate impact-tested to IWA 14-1 / PAS 68 (ISO 22343-1 supersedes PAS 68). Published example: Barkers Vulcan RCS stops a 7,500 kg vehicle at 64 km/h with 4.5 m penetration. Automated, with manual release and fail-secure control.

**Why.** The gate is the single asset that decides whether unauthorised vehicles reach a stage full of a studio's unreleased film.

**Here.** A cantilever gate of this mass is also the only gate type that behaves in 36 m/s wind; swing gates and lightweight sliders are unusable here.

#### Gatehouse, ANPR and pass office

Manned gatehouse with ANPR on both lanes, in/out lanes plus a rejection lay-by, visitor pass issue, delivery booking check, and a hold area off the carriageway for a 16.5 m artic that has arrived without a booking.

**Why.** A truck refused at the gate must have somewhere to stand that is not the public road.

**Here.** Small labour pool means the gatehouse cannot be triple-manned around the clock; automation plus remote monitoring carries the night shift.

#### Perimeter fence and detection

Proposed 3.0 m mesh fence to LPS 1175 or equivalent security rating, wind-load-designed to EN 1991-1-4 with the site vb,0, with fence-mounted vibration detection or a taut-wire system, and a clear 3 m sterile zone inside. Estimate on height.

**Why.** MPA Content Security Best Practices treat the perimeter as the first control; insurers and studios audit it.

**Here.** A 3 m solid or close-mesh fence is a large sail. Use permeable mesh with wind-rated posts and deep foundations, and expect drift accumulation on the lee side to bury the sterile zone.

#### CCTV, perimeter lighting and detection

IP cameras to IEC 62676 series, IP66/IK10, heated housings, coverage of every stage door, dock and gate with 30-day retention minimum; lighting to EN 12464-2 with cut-off optics.

**Why.** Content security audits require verifiable coverage of every point where material can leave.

**Here.** December means the lot is dark for most of the working day, so 'lighting' is not just a security item — it is the working light for load-in from roughly 15:30 onward. Cameras need heated, ash-resistant housings and frequent lens cleaning.

#### Customs bonded store and carnet office

Secure, segregated bonded area at the goods yard with its own access control, for goods under temporary import. Iceland honours the ATA Carnet for cinematographic and professional equipment (Iceland Chamber of Commerce; Skatturinn/Iceland Revenue and Customs).

**Why.** Incoming production kit arrives under carnet or temporary admission and must be storable, countable and re-exportable against the document.

**Here.** Iceland is in the EEA but not the EU customs union, so goods from the EU still clear customs. This is the item mainland producers do not expect and it is the commonest cause of a delayed load-in.

#### Yard drainage with silt and ash interception

Slot drains across the apron and dock, silt traps and a full-retention interceptor sized for the ash load, oil separator to EN 858-1 class I on all vehicle areas, with heated or self-draining gullies.

**Why.** Ash-laden runoff blinds a conventional drainage system in one event and floods the apron and then the stage.

**Here.** Volcanic ash events dump fine, abrasive solids across the whole yard at once. Design for a sediment load a mainland lot would never see, and plan a mechanical clean-out after every event.

#### Lon tank stage access provisions

Tank fill and drain connections reachable by tanker without crossing the scenery route; removable floor sections over the 20 x 20 m x 6 m dive well; crane access to the dive well from the apron; dive equipment and safety-boat store at the tank door. Estimate.

**Why.** A dive well is a confined-space rescue problem; access for lifting gear and rescue services has to be designed, not improvised.

**Here.** Free geothermal heat means the tank can be run at working temperature year-round, which is a genuine competitive advantage — but it makes the tank a year-round asset, so its access has to work in February.

#### Waste, recycling and strike-out dock

Separate dock with roll-on/roll-off skip bays, timber and steel segregation for set strike, wind-rated skip lids and anchors, sized for a full 6,000 m² strike inside 5 days. Estimate.

**Why.** Striking a set generates more tonnage in a week than the stage receives in a month; if it queues at the goods dock, the next build cannot start.

**Here.** Open skips in this wind scatter set debris across the lot and into the neighbours' land. Lidded, anchored containers only.

### State of the art

#### Outer weather (storm) leaf forming a vehicle sound-and-wind lock

Second leaf set 6–9 m outboard of the acoustic leaf, insulated steel or Megadoor-type vertical-lift fabric (ASSA ABLOY publish Rw 15 dB to ISO 717, wind class 2–5 to EN 12424 = 0.45–1.6 kPa). Vestibule sized for a 16.5 m artic to stand inside with both closed.

**Why.** Opening a single acoustic leaf in weather dumps rain, ash and a 30 m/s gust straight onto the set and blows the stage's air balance apart.

**Here.** This is the item Iceland forces. On a normal lot it is a luxury; here it is the difference between shooting and not shooting in January.

#### Anemometer interlock and storm-lock mode

Roof-mounted ultrasonic anemometer (heated, no moving parts), door control inhibits opening above a set threshold (suggest 18 m/s / 65 km/h operating limit, storm-lock and pin above 25 m/s — estimate) and auto-closes and pins on rising wind.

**Why.** An elephant leaf caught half-open in a gust is a write-off and a fatality risk. Human judgement at 3 a.m. in a gale is not a control measure.

**Here.** Among the windiest inhabited places on earth. Expect the interlock to fire dozens of times a winter and to appear in the shooting schedule as lost time.

#### Interconnecting acoustic doors between the two 2,000 m² stages

Full-height moveable acoustic partition or sliding leaf pair, proposed 12 m x 9 m clear, Rw ≥55 dB closed so either stage can shoot while the other builds; flush floor track with removable infill so the joined floor takes dolly track. Sizes are my estimate.

**Why.** The whole point of the interconnect is a 4,000 m² combined volume for one show and two isolated stages for two shows. A weak door means you only ever get one of those.

**Here.** Also an internal weather-free scenery route between stages, which in this climate is worth more than it would be in Los Angeles.

#### Askja-to-Lon covered scenery link

Covered, heated link corridor between Askja and the tank stage, proposed 8 m clear width x 8 m clear height, acoustic doors both ends, floor rated for a 20 t loaded scenery dolly. Sizes are my estimate.

**Why.** Tank work uses parts built dry on the neighbouring stage; carrying them outside in Icelandic weather ruins painted and dressed surfaces.

**Here.** Rain, ash and salt spray destroy scenic paint and plaster. Every outdoor metre of the scenery route is a repaint.

#### Weighbridge at the gate

Pit-mounted or surface 18 m x 3 m weighbridge, 60 t capacity, class Y(b) to OIML R76 / EN 45501, integrated with the gate system and the ANPR read.

**Why.** Axle-load compliance on public roads, waste-transfer records, and a check on what actually leaves the lot.

**Here.** Icelandic axle-load and seasonal weight restrictions (umferdin.is publishes them) apply to the road outside the gate; weighing in-house avoids a turned-back load.

#### Hostile-vehicle mitigation and bollards at stage doors

Static and retractable bollards to IWA 14-1 across the stage apron approaches, keeping vehicles off the door face outside booked movements; foundation design certified for the exact tested configuration.

**Why.** An elephant door is a 24 m hole; the standoff has to be provided in the yard because the door itself cannot resist a vehicle.

#### Vehicle search and inspection bay

Covered bay off the inbound lane, lit to 500 lux, with an inspection pit or mirror set, sized for a 16.5 m artic, adjacent to the gatehouse.

**Why.** Searching in the open in Icelandic weather does not happen; a bay is the only way the policy survives contact with January.

**Here.** Cover and heat it or the search is waved through, which is the same as not having the policy.

#### Delivery booking and dock scheduling system

Slot-booking software tied to the gate ANPR and the weighbridge, with per-production dock allocation and a queue-hold area; every movement logged for the content-security audit trail.

**Why.** Four productions striking and building at once will collide at the docks and the elephant door apron on the same Friday.

**Here.** Weather cancels slots wholesale. The system must handle a day's worth of re-booking when a storm closes the route, or the yard gridlocks the day after.

### What gets got wrong

- Sizing the elephant door to the biggest set piece rather than to the biggest crane. The set fits; the 60 t telehandler or the crane that has to place it does not, and you find out on the day.
- Treating the elephant door's acoustic rating as an isolated number. Rw 53 dB on the leaf next to a flanking path at the head, or a track recess that bypasses the seal, delivers 40 dB in the field. Test the whole opening, not the leaf.
- Publishing an acoustic rating for a moving element without saying what it means. A door only performs closed, sealed and inflated; a rating with no sealing sequence and no maintenance regime behind it is fiction within eighteen months.
- Detailing the bottom track flush with a floor that is not flat. Stage floor tolerance and door track tolerance are set by different trades with different standards, and the mismatch is the reason dollies bump at the door line on almost every stage ever built.
- Forgetting that the elephant door is the largest single wind element on the site. With vb,0 = 36 m/s under the Icelandic NA to EN 1991-1-4, EN 12424 class 5 (the top published class) may be below what the site needs; you must specify a project design pressure and require an EN 12444 test to it, not accept a class number.
- Buying dock shelters and canopies to a mainland spec. Curtain shelters shred and canopies fail in uplift in Icelandic wind; both need to be treated as wind-designed elements.
- Believing snowmelt is the expensive option. In Iceland geothermal district heat is nearly free while electricity, though cheap, is capacity-constrained — so hydronic snowmelt across the apron, ramp and door tracks is the cheap answer and electric trace heating is the expensive one. Getting this backwards wastes the site's single biggest natural advantage.
- Sizing the electrical connection for the charging fleet and the door drives as an afterthought. Landsvirkjun has refused supply to new industrial load; every kW of yard and door load has to come out of the connection you already hold.
- Designing the route in but not the height envelope. Any overhead line, gantry, sign or lighting column across the scenery route silently caps the size of everything the mill can build, no matter how big the stage door is.
- Assuming EU freight rules apply because Iceland is in the EEA. It is not in the EU customs union, so goods from the EU still clear customs, and the ATA Carnet paperwork for production kit is where load-ins actually get stuck.
- Ignoring seasonal axle-load restrictions on the road outside the gate. A perfectly legal load in September can be refused in the spring thaw, and the yard has to be able to hold the truck that gets turned back.
- Ordering doors from a hangar-door catalogue. Aircraft hangar doors are wind- and span-engineered but acoustically almost useless (a vertical-lift fabric door is around Rw 15 dB to ISO 717), which is fine as the outer storm leaf and fatal as the only leaf.
- Planning the yard on the assumption of a deep local contractor pool. With a small labour pool, anything that needs a specialist to unjam it will sit jammed for days; specify for manual recovery by the crew you actually have on site.
- Letting empty containers, skips and parked leaves sit unanchored. In the Icelandic wind they become projectiles, and this is the most common way a well-designed lot damages its own stage doors.
- Designing the docks and the elephant door apron as one shared space. Four productions loading and striking in the same week will collide there, and the resulting jam blocks the only route into a 6,000 m² stage.

---

## 8. Water, tanks and underwater work

*Lon (2,400 m² tank, 20x20 m dive well at 6 m) and wet provision for Askja, Sugar Land Studios, Mosfellsbær*

### Essential

#### Lon tank basin — reinforced concrete, monolithic

2,400 m² wet footprint (60x40 m). Main basin structural depth 2.40 m, working 0–2.00 m; dive well 20x20 m to −6.00 m. Volumes (my calc): 6,400 m³ full, 4,320 m³ at 1.20 m, 2,400 m³ well-only. Walls 600 mm, base 700 mm, C35/45, CEM III/B, w/c ≤0.45.

**Why.** Everything hangs off the basin. Wrong geometry or wrong mix and you are resin-injecting a live tank for thirty years.

**Here.** Cement, rebar and formwork all imported via Grundartangi or Sundahöfn; one pour season Jun–Sep. Capital-area ready-mix capacity is thin for a 3,000 m³ pour programme.

#### Watertightness design class, EN 1992-3:2006

Tightness Class 2 (no through-cracks; compression zone ≥ min(50 mm, 0.2h)) for the dive well; Class 1 with wk1 0.05–0.20 mm, interpolated on hD/h = 5→35, acceptable for the shallow basin. Class 3 (no leakage) delivered by the bonded liner, not by the concrete.

**Why.** A leaking film tank loses heat, loses chlorinated water to ground and stains whatever is below it. Crack width is the design variable, not 'waterproof concrete'.

**Here.** Early-age thermal cracking is worse with a 4 °C mains fill against a 20 °C pour. Specify pour sequencing, pipe cooling and a 28-day cure before first fill.

#### Waterproofing — crystalline admixture plus bonded membrane

Integral crystalline admixture at 1% by cement weight (Xypex Admix C-1000NF, Penetron Admix or Kryton KIM), hydrophilic waterbars at all construction joints, plus a 2.0 mm reinforced PVC bonded liner (Renolit Alkorplan 2000 or Sika Sikaplan WP), hot-air welded, every seam spark- or vacuum-tested.

**Why.** Belt and braces. The admixture self-heals hairlines behind the liner; the liner is the actual barrier and is repairable without draining the tank.

**Here.** Liner welding needs 15–25 °C and a dry substrate — a heated, dehumidified temporary enclosure through the Icelandic build season or the weld quality is a lottery.

#### Dive well

20x20 m clear, floor at −6.00 m, 400 m², 2,400 m³. 1.2 m submerged rest bench at −1.20 m on three sides. Floor falls 1:100 to a 2x2x0.6 m sump. Corner radii 1.0 m so a diver never swims into a hard internal corner.

**Why.** 6 m gives a working column deep enough for a full-body vertical beat and a 4–5 m camera pull-back, with no decompression obligation incurred.

**Here.** 6 m keeps the operation inside reg. 535/2001's easiest team case (SCUBA, clear water, ≤10 m): three divers rather than four or five.

#### Balance and surge tank

400 m³ (my estimate) below the plant room, including 150 m³ dedicated to dump-tank and rain return surge. The Umhverfisstofnun pool handbook benchmark is about 25 m³ for a 25x12.5 m (312 m²) pool; that ratio gives 192 m³, and a wave-making film tank needs roughly double.

**Why.** Takes displacement of crew, boats and set, plus wave surge, and receives everything the dump tanks throw. Undersize it and the deck floods on the first big gag.

**Here.** Insulate and keep it warm: 400 m³ of 30 °C water in a partly buried chamber is a free thermal flywheel through a January supply interruption.

#### Seismic design of the basin, EN 1998-4

Impulsive plus convective (sloshing) masses per EN 1998-4 Annex A — 6,400 t of water is the largest single seismic mass on the lot. Freeboard sized for the convective wave; flexible articulated joints at every pipe penetration. ÍST EN 1998-1 NA:2010 carries a national hazard map with agR up to 0.5 g in the South Iceland Seismic Zone.

**Why.** Half the water mass moves out of phase with the structure. Ignore the convective component and the walls are designed for the wrong load case.

**Here.** Mosfellsbær is outside the SISZ so agR is well below the 0.5 g peak. I could not read the exact local value off the NA map — treat 0.10–0.15 g as an unverified placeholder and confirm from the hazard map.

#### Acoustic separation of the tank from the isolated stage box

Tank founded outside the box-in-box isolation, with a continuous 50 mm resilient separation joint at the deck edge (Getzner Sylomer or equivalent), watertight and acoustically broken. Deck panels resiliently seated. Target NR 20 in the stage with plant running.

**Why.** 6,400 t of water on rigid ground is a direct flanking path. Water carries structure-borne noise at 1,480 m/s, so a pump anywhere on the loop is audible underwater.

**Here.** Rain drumming on the long-span roof is already the worst acoustic problem on this site; a hard-coupled tank hands the same stage a second uncontrolled path.

#### Anti-entrapment inlets and outlets, EN 13451-3:2020

Twin floor drains per suction circuit, ≥1.0 m apart, approach velocity ≤0.5 m/s through each grate, sumps sized to EN 13451-3, safety vacuum release on every circulation pump, no single main drain anywhere, grates removable only with a tool.

**Why.** A 1,600 m³/h suction on a 6 m dive well will hold an adult against a grate. This is the classic way a film tank kills someone.

**Here.** Reg. 814/2010 puts the inspection duty on the local heilbrigðisnefnd; expect physical testing of grate retention at each starfsleyfi renewal.

#### Floor inlet distribution grid

Floor inlets on a 3x3 m grid across all 2,400 m², nozzle exit velocity ≤0.5 m/s so the volume turns over uniformly and heated return is diffused. Adjustable-throat nozzles in 316L or ABS. Inlet-to-tank ΔT held ≤2 K.

**Why.** Uniform, slow distribution is what holds the tank to ±0.5 K top to bottom and keeps thermal schlieren off camera.

**Here.** Make-up arrives at roughly 4 °C from the Heiðmörk groundwater (my figure for temperature). Temper it before it reaches the tank or it lays a visible cold layer across the shot.

#### Circulation pumps

4 duty + 1 standby flooded-suction split-case pumps, 400 m³/h each at 15 m head, ~25 kW, on VSDs. Total design 1,600 m³/h, a 4-hour turnover on 6,400 m³. Inertia bases, 25 mm deflection spring isolators, twin-sphere flexible connectors both sides, acoustically lined enclosure.

**Why.** Turnover rate is the whole clarity argument, and VSDs give a true silent mode for takes without stopping circulation dead.

**Here.** About 110 kW continuous is the tank's baseload. With Landsvirkjun refusing supply to new industrial load, every kW in the plant has to be justified line by line in the connection application.

#### Silent mode and pump lock-out interlock

Two-stage control: silent mode at 30% flow (NR 18 in the stage, my target) for takes, plus a hard lock-out/tag-out on all circulation and drain pumps via key exchange between the dive supervisor's panel and the plant room whenever anyone is in the water.

**Why.** You cannot run full turnover during a take and you cannot have suction live with divers down. Both need to be physical interlocks, not procedures.

**Here.** Clarity and temperature must hold through a 40-minute silent block — worth proving, since 4 °C Icelandic make-up is unforgiving of drift.

#### Hair and lint strainers

One per pump, 316L basket, free area ≥4x the suction pipe area, dP alarm at 0.3 bar, quick-release lid, drainable to the plant gully. Duty/standby pairs so baskets can be cleared without stopping circulation.

**Why.** Set dressing, costume fibre, hair and foliage will blind a strainer in a single shooting day, and then the pump seals go.

**Here.** During an ash fall these fill with abrasive glass shards. Carry a full spare basket set — replacements are a two-week import.

#### Primary filters — glass media

8 x 3.5 m diameter vertical filters, 9.62 m² each, 77 m² total; at 1,600 m³/h that is 21 m/h, inside reg. 814/2010's 25 m/h ceiling for new, rebuilt and re-equipped Icelandic installations (the 35 m/h figure applies only to old pools). Crushed recycled glass media, sight glass, sample cocks and gauges upstream and downstream of every filter.

**Why.** 25 m/h is a hard Icelandic regulatory ceiling, not a preference. Size the filter face area from it or the design fails at permit stage.

**Here.** Stricter than common European practice — do not import a filter house sized at 30–35 m/h and expect it to be approved.

#### Coagulant dosing

Polyaluminium chloride dosed after the circulation pump and ahead of the filter, proportioned to occupancy and spread over 24 h. Reg. 814/2010 handbook requires at least 10 s contact from injection to the top of the filter with water velocity ≤1.5 m/s in that run.

**Why.** Without coagulation the filter passes everything under 10 microns and the water goes milky under a hard light. PACl is what makes 0.2 FNU achievable.

**Here.** The handbook is explicit that coagulant performance is governed by pH, and Icelandic make-up is barely buffered — the PACl dose and the pH loop have to be tuned together.

#### Heat exchangers

2 x 1.5 MW titanium plate heat exchangers, 3 MW installed. Primary: district heat at 75–80 °C flow. Secondary: tank water. Titanium, not 316L — chlorinated water at 30 °C pits stainless. Full CIP connections and a spare plate pack held on site.

**Why.** 3 MW brings 6,400 m³ from 4 to 30 °C in 65 h (193.5 MWh, my calc); 6 MW does it in 32 h. That number sets your booking lead time.

**Here.** Closed indirect heating is mandatory here. Icelandic geothermal water is silica-rich and magnesium-free; mixed with cold water it forms magnesium silicate scale that bonds to tiles, liners and acrylic — Umhverfisstofnun's handbook calls it the principal cleaning problem at Icelandic pools.

#### Geothermal primary connection and tariff

Veitur price list effective 01.01.2025: tariff H4A-C Sundlaugar at 91.63 ISK/m³ plus 2% state tax, 103.74 ISK/m³ incl. 11% VAT. Return water below 30 °C (H31) is 6.60 → 7.47 ISK/m³. Industrial process water (V4) 91.63 → 115.89 ISK/m³ incl. 24% VAT.

**Why.** At 58.1 kWh per m³ of district water cooled 80→30 °C, heating the whole tank from cold needs ~3,330 m³, about 345,000 ISK (my calc). Heat is effectively free.

**Here.** Both H4 and V4 are explicitly ekki forgangsvatn — non-priority, interruptible, valid only where Veitur judges there is enough water and transmission capacity, and V2–V5 are offered only temporarily. No rebate is given for restricted delivery, so the tank needs thermal buffer and a cold-week plan.

#### Temperature control and setpoints

Nominal 30 °C, adjustable 26–34 °C, control band ±0.3 K, stratification ≤0.5 K floor to surface. Pinewood publishes its Underwater Stage at 30 °C (87 °F), with other listings citing 32 °C. Reg. 814/2010 treats 30–34 °C as a distinct pool class.

**Why.** Below 28 °C an actor in costume loses core temperature within twenty minutes. Above 32 °C divers in exposure suits overheat and chloramine production climbs.

**Here.** Holding 30 °C against roughly 500 kW of steady loss (my estimate) costs about 207 m³ of district water a day, some 21,500 ISK. Nowhere else can you leave a 2,400 m² tank hot all year.

#### Alkalinity and hardness correction

Sodium bicarbonate to 80–120 mg/l total alkalinity as CaCO3; calcium chloride to 200–300 mg/l calcium hardness; Langelier Saturation Index held −0.3 to +0.3. Reykjavík supply is exceptionally soft — parts of the city measure 0.2–0.60 °dH, roughly 4–11 mg/l as CaCO3.

**Why.** Negative-LSI water leaches calcium from cement paste, etches grout and pits metal. Iceland's water arrives aggressive and must be deliberately made benign.

**Here.** The most Iceland-specific item in the chemistry. The Umhverfisstofnun handbook warns outright that Icelandic pool water can turn acidic, risking corrosion of pipework and plant.

#### Dechlorination

Sodium thiosulfate or sodium bisulfite dosed on the discharge line, residual free chlorine to <0.1 mg/l before any release (my target — confirm with the heilbrigðiseftirlit). Inline redox verification with a hard interlock on the discharge valve.

**Why.** Nothing chlorinated leaves the site without being neutralised. The interlock is what stops a night-shift drain becoming a prosecution.

**Here.** Reg. 798/1999 Annex I sets HOCl at ≤0.004 mg/l in rivers and lakes outside the mixing zone, and Varmá and Leirvogsá both run into Leiruvogur within about 3 km.

#### Chemical store

Separate bunded compartments for acid and hypochlorite, separate fill points, separate ventilation, 110% bund volume, chemically resistant finishes, gas detection, safety shower and eyewash to EN 15154 / ANSI Z358.1 within 10 s unobstructed travel, spill kit and neutraliser per compartment.

**Why.** Acid and hypochlorite in one bund makes chlorine gas. Separation is a physical requirement, not a housekeeping rule.

**Here.** Reg. 814/2010 requires annual training for every worker handling water-treatment chemicals — a live constraint given how small the local pool-plant labour pool is.

#### Instrumentation and datalogging

Amperometric free and combined chlorine, redox (mV), pH to ±0.1, temperature, turbidity in FNU before and after every filter, flow, filter dP, conductivity/TDS and level. Reg. 814/2010 requires automatic dosing control and continuous recording of chlorine and pH for class A pools, with the sample line drawn from the pool itself rather than the plant.

**Why.** The health board inspects the record, not the water. A gap in the log is a finding.

**Here.** The handbook is specific that comparison samples be taken where water has just passed the sensors, and that class A pools need chlorine, temperature and pH on screen at all times.

#### Water quality targets for underwater cinematography

Reg. 814/2010: turbidity ≤0.4 FNU before the filter, ≤0.2 FNU after; combined chlorine never above 1.0 mg/l; pH 7.2–7.6; TDS ≤1,000 mg/l monthly; treatment capacity ≥2 m³ per bather per hour; renewal 30 l per bather per day; shock dose 10–15 mg/l, never during occupancy.

**Why.** That is the legal floor. For a 20 m sightline in the dive well I would hold ≤0.1 FNU and require a 200 mm Secchi target readable at 25 m horizontally — my spec, since no published film-tank standard exists.

**Here.** The Icelandic handbook justifies its turbidity limit on camera grounds: high turbidity makes it harder to see underwater on cameras. I could not read Annex II's free-chlorine band — confirm the mg/l range with the health board before design freeze.

#### Pipework and velocities

Reg. 814/2010: suction to the circulation pump ≤1.0 m/s, pressure side ≤1.5 m/s. PE100 SDR11 or PVC-U buried and in plant, 316L where exposed. All wetted materials must withstand chlorinated bathing water. Flexible seismic joints at every tank-wall penetration.

**Why.** Velocity limits keep the system quiet and cavitation-free, and set pipe diameter — at 1,600 m³/h and 1.5 m/s you are into DN600.

**Here.** Seismic articulation at wall penetrations is not optional; a rigid penetration is where a tank cracks in an event.

#### Plant room

About 900 m² over two levels (my estimate), structurally and acoustically separate from the stage and off the stage's isolated slab. 4x4 m equipment door, 5 t overhead crane rail, floor gullies to the balance tank, own HVAC, own fire compartment, workshop and spares store.

**Why.** Every heavy item in the water system has to come out and go back without dismantling a wall. Plan the removal route before the pumps arrive.
