**Here.** Partly bury it and heat it off the district return at 7.47 ISK/m³. It also gives a ventilation route that can be sealed and filtered during an ash event.

#### Cold water service and fill rate

DN200 service, 47 l/s at 1.5 m/s = 170 m³/h; 6,400 m³ in 38 h at that rate, 89 h at a more realistic negotiated 20 l/s (my calcs). Veitur cold water effective 01.01.2025: 46.46 ISK/m³ usage, 52.82 ISK/m³ wholesale, building water 214.60 ISK/day, plus a daily meter charge of 55.32–328.92 ISK by meter size.

**Why.** Fill time is booking time. Malta publish 15 h for a 46 million litre deep tank; Fox's Sersen Lake filled 3 million US gallons in 100 minutes. Both had reservoirs.

**Here.** Capital-area cold water comes from the Gvendarbrunnar springs in Heiðmörk, unchlorinated and extremely soft. A studio drawing 47 l/s continuously is a material new load on a municipal network — agree it in writing before the tank is designed.

#### Drain-down, holding and transfer

2 x DN300 gravity drains from the dive well sump, 2 x 250 l/s submersible dewatering pumps, 6,400 m³ to storage in about 3.5 h at 1,800 m³/h (my calc). 3 x 2,200 m³ insulated holding tanks so a drained tank's water is kept, treated and returned rather than discharged.

**Why.** Malta publish 6 hours to empty their shallow tank. Matching that while keeping the water is the whole trick — refilling costs days, discharging costs consent.

**Here.** Retaining 6,400 m³ of 30 °C water rather than dumping it also retains about 190 MWh of heat. Even at Icelandic prices that is the environmentally defensible answer.

#### Discharge consent regime

Discharge of bathing water is starfsleyfi-able under Art. 12 of reglugerð nr. 796/1999 um varnir gegn mengun vatns. Sewer and effluent are governed by reglugerð nr. 798/1999 um fráveitur og skólp, whose Annex I caps HOCl at 0.004 mg/l in rivers and lakes outside the mixing zone. Permits issued by the local heilbrigðisnefnd under Umhverfisstofnun oversight.

**Why.** No consent, no tank. And the surface-water chlorine limit is below what pool instrumentation can measure, so the practical answer is never to surface water.

**Here.** Discharge to fresh surface water additionally requires opinions from the nature-protection and environment agencies and the fisheries authority, plus consent of the local veiðifélag. Icelandic environmental agencies were reorganised in 2025 — confirm the current competent authority before applying.

#### Receiving-water sensitivity at Mosfellsbær

Varmá, Kaldakvísl and Leirvogsá all discharge into Leiruvogur. Varmárósar is a friðland designated 1980 and revised 2012, 9.76 ha, protecting saltmarsh rush (Juncus gerardii), its habitat and the estuary wetland. Leirvogsá is a salmon river with 8 km of fishable water fished by two rods.

**Why.** These are the receptors an inspector will name, and they rule out warm, chlorinated or turbid discharge to surface water entirely.

**Here.** Route everything to the municipal foul sewer after dechlorination and temperature control, at an agreed rate — 6,400 m³ at 20 l/s is 89 hours, which has to be programmed rather than improvised.

#### Legionella control on warm aerosol systems

Written control scheme for every aerosol generator fed with 30 °C tank water — rain bars, towers, cannons, fogs. No dead legs; drain rain mains after each use; thermal or chemical disinfection weekly; monthly sampling to ISO 11731; free chlorine maintained in the rain supply. HSE HSG274 Part 2 is the best available model.

**Why.** A heated rain tower recirculating 30 °C water is a cooling tower spraying into a sealed stage full of people — the most serious under-recognised hazard on a water stage.

**Here.** Iceland has no ACOP equivalent to HSG274, so nobody will ask for the scheme, which is exactly why you write one. Geothermal-fed systems elsewhere on the lot carry their own Legionella profile.

#### Permanent underwater luminaires

IEC 60364-7-702:2010: Zone 0, inside the basin, permits SELV only — 12 V AC rms or 30 V ripple-free DC — with the transformer outside Zone 2. Luminaires to IEC 60598-2-18, IPX8. Used for house light, work light and emergency egress illumination in the dive well.

**Why.** Somebody has to be able to see to get out when the production lighting is off. These are the fixtures that stay wired in.

**Here.** Reg. 814/2010 requires posted emergency plans for earthquake, fire, chemical incident and drowning at the emergency station — egress lighting is what makes the earthquake plan executable.

#### Power-to-water lock-out

Key-exchange interlock on the deck: no underwater lighting or effects circuit can be energised while divers are in the water unless the electrical supervisor and dive supervisor have both released their keys. 10 mA RCD on every underwater circuit, monthly test and log.

**Why.** Electrocution in a tank is silent and instantaneous. The interlock removes the possibility of a well-meaning spark energising a circuit during a swim-through.

**Here.** No Icelandic regulation requires this for a film tank; it is imported stage-electrical discipline, and it is what an insurer will ask about.

#### Underwater communications

Ocean Technology Systems hardwire, 2- or 4-wire encased in braided nylon rope, with OTS Guardian full-face masks. Diver-to-diver, diver-to-talent, diver-to-surface, with a production audio feed and record. OTS founders received a National Emmy in 1991 for the effect of underwater comms on filmmaking.

**Why.** Hardwire is landline quality and does not fail when a dump tank goes off. Through-water comms will not hold in a tank full of bubbles and machinery.

**Here.** An international crew plus Icelandic-certified divers means the dive supervisor's working language has to be agreed and logged before the first dive.

#### Dive supervisor's station

Permanent station overlooking the tank with comms panel, gas panel with pressure readouts, above- and below-water CCTV to every quadrant, dive log, tender position, dive plan board, emergency phone with 112 and Landspítali +354 543 1000 posted, and the lock-out key cabinet.

**Why.** Reg. 535/2001 puts named duties on the dive supervisor including records and plan compliance — give them somewhere to do it from.

**Here.** Reg. 535/2001 requires the supervisor to hold a valid certificate with at least five years' commercial diving experience and/or 200 dives, a materially scarce qualification in a country of 400,000.

#### Dive team composition

Reglugerð nr. 535/2001 um köfun, under lög nr. 31/1996: minimum three — dive supervisor, working diver, standby diver — for SCUBA in very good conditions in clear water not exceeding 10 m. Four on surface supply to 30 m, five to 50 m. SCUBA capped at 30 m, surface supply at 50 m. Certificates A–G issued by Samgöngustofa; medicals valid 12 months.

**Why.** This is the legal floor for the dive team in Iceland, and the number the safety authority will count on the deck.

**Here.** UK HSE or US ADCI tickets are not Icelandic commercial diving certificates. Recognition has to be arranged in advance or the shoot does not dive, and diving equipment separately requires recognition by the maritime authority.

#### Safety diver provision for film work

My operating standard over and above reg. 535/2001: one safety diver per performer in the water, one in-water supervisor, and for breath-hold performers one dedicated safety diver within arm's reach with eyes on at all times. UK HSE ACOP L106 (Media diving projects, 2nd ed. 2014) defines the media diver to include stunt performers, presenters, camera, sound and lighting crew and support divers.

**Why.** The legal minimum of three is for a working diver doing a task. A tank with eight actors and a camera crew in it is a different operation entirely.

**Here.** L106 is UK law with no force in Iceland, but it is the standard the international productions you are chasing will expect. Publish that you work to it.

#### Breath-hold and shallow-water blackout control

Written breath-hold protocol: no hyperventilation, enforced surface interval at least twice the breath-hold duration, one-up-one-down, a capped number of takes per performer per day, freediving-qualified supervision, and pulse oximetry on repeated-apnoea days (my spec).

**Why.** Hypoxic blackout on repeated shallow breath-holds is the most likely way a film tank kills a performer. It is silent, and it happens on the ascent.

**Here.** No Icelandic regulation covers breath-hold performers — reg. 535/2001 addresses commercial divers, not actors holding their breath. The gap is yours to close.

#### Breathing gas supply

2 x HP compressors (Bauer Verticus class, 400–560 l/min FAD, 330 bar) plus quad storage. Air to EN 12021:2014: oxygen 21 ±1%, CO2 ≤500 ml/m³, CO ≤5 ml/m³, oil ≤0.5 mg/m³, pressure dew point not above −11 °C where usage and storage conditions are unknown and at least 5 °C below the lowest use temperature. Continuous CO monitoring, quarterly independent air tests.

**Why.** Surface supply avoids cylinder changes mid-sequence and gives unlimited bottom time at 6 m. The air purity standard is what keeps it safe.

**Here.** The −11 °C dew point clause bites here: kit stored in an unheated Icelandic store in January needs dew point set below actual storage temperature, not the default.

#### Oxygen, first aid and rescue equipment

Reg. 814/2010 Annex I required list: pocket mask connectable to oxygen, single-use barrier per staff member, AED with accessories, oxygen set with adjustable flow; desirable suction, oropharyngeal airways and bag-valve. Rescue: torus or rescue tube, floating backboard with at least three straps and head block, rigid adjustable collars in all sizes, mouldable splints, blankets. Plus 2 x DAN-type O2 kits, 20 min each at 15 l/min.

**Why.** Statutory list for an Icelandic bathing place and the right list for a tank, all at a single named emergency station.

**Here.** Annex I also requires emergency plans for earthquake, fire, chemical incident and drowning to be posted or held at the emergency station — the earthquake plan is a genuinely Icelandic line item.

#### Recompression plan

No on-site chamber. Landspítali operates a Hyperbaric and Dive Medicine Department in Reykjavík, roughly 15 km and 20 minutes from Mosfellsbær, with critical care available inside the chamber; emergency route is 112 or a diving-medicine specialist on +354 543 1000, and the department accepts dive plans submitted in advance.

**Why.** At 6 m there is no decompression obligation, but arterial gas embolism from a breath-held ascent is possible from about a metre. Surface oxygen plus a 20-minute transfer is the right answer.

**Here.** A national hyperbaric unit 20 minutes away is a real Icelandic advantage over most studio lots. Pre-notify them of every shooting block — the system exists and expects it.

#### Egress, rescue points and guarding

Ladders or recessed steps at ≤20 m spacing around the full 208 m perimeter, submerged rest bench at −1.20 m, two 1:8 entry ramps, a 500 kg davit with a rescue stretcher point at the dive well, and demountable edge protection to EN 13451-1 whenever the deck is not in use.

**Why.** Getting an unconscious 90 kg performer in wet costume out of a 6 m well is a rehearsed lift with a mechanical aid, not four people and goodwill.

**Here.** Rehearse it in Icelandic and English — a mixed local and imported crew is the normal condition here and the rescue brief has to work in both.

#### Pool plant operator and lifeguard competence

Reg. 814/2010 Annex III competence test for pool supervision, re-taken annually; swimming teachers and instructors every two years. The owner must provide regular staff training, and staff handling water-treatment equipment and chemicals must be trained annually.

**Why.** Legally required, and the licence condition the health board actually checks.

**Here.** You can import the dive team; you cannot import the plant operator or pool supervisor, who must be Icelandic-certified. In a labour pool this small, name and train them two years before opening.

#### Evaporation and dehumidification load

At 0.25 kg/m²·h over 2,400 m² that is 600 kg/h of vapour, about 405 kW latent and 14.4 t of water a day; with waves and wind machines it reaches 1,200 kg/h, 810 kW and 28.8 t/day (my calcs). Demands roughly 100,000 m³/h of dehumidifying supply air delivered at NR 20.

**Why.** The tank's real cost is not the heat, it is getting 14 tonnes a day of water vapour back out of the stage air without making a sound.

**Here.** Iceland is unusually good at this. Winter outdoor air at 0 °C and 85% RH holds about 3.2 g/kg against stage air at 24 °C and 50% RH at 9.3 g/kg, so ventilation dehumidifies for free and reheating costs almost nothing on district heat. A water stage is easier to run here than almost anywhere.

#### Trichloramine surface extraction

Slot extract at the coping running the full wetted perimeter, drawing at water level where trichloramine collects. Target ≤0.5 mg/m³ NCl3 in the breathing zone (French INRS comfort value). Interlocked to run whenever the tank is uncovered.

**Why.** NCl3 is heavier than air and sits in the first 300 mm above the surface, exactly where an actor treading water breathes.

**Here.** An outdoor pool ventilates itself; a sealed 180,000 m³ stage does not, and nothing in reg. 814/2010 addresses tank air in an enclosed building. Set your own target and monitor to it.

### Expected

#### Tank deck — dry-stage decking over the wet footprint

Removable modular deck over the full 2,400 m², flush with the stage floor ±3 mm, on demountable steel beams at 3.0 m centres. Design 10 kN/m² UDL plus a 7.5 t telehandler axle (my design assumption). Panels 3.0x1.5 m at ≤150 kg for two-person handling, or 6x3 m craned.

**Why.** Without it the tank is a 2,400 m² hole in the lettable inventory for the 70% of the year nobody is shooting in water.

**Here.** Deck steel imported; hot-dip galvanise to EN ISO 1461, 85 µm minimum. Salt-laden Atlantic air off Leiruvogur eats anything less.

#### Deck-level overflow channel

Continuous perimeter overflow (Wiesbaden/Finnish profile) around the full 208 m wetted perimeter, 300 mm wide, anti-slip grating to EN 13451-1, weir level tolerance ±2 mm over the full run, gravity fall to the balance tank at 1:150.

**Why.** A deck-level weir puts the water surface at the deck line — the only geometry that lets a camera shoot flat across the surface and gives a true horizon in shot.

**Here.** ±2 mm over 208 m on a seismically active site means the survey datum must be re-checked after any felt event.

#### Tank finish and chroma system

Base finish neutral grey (RAL 7047) two-pack epoxy over the liner, gloss ≤10 GU. Over-liner chroma in Rosco Chroma Key Blue and Green, ballasted weighted-edge panels deployable in two shifts; matt black over-liner for infinite-depth work. Full repaint cycle 7–10 days (my estimate).

**Why.** Repainting 2,400 m² between shows is the hidden cost of a water stage. Over-liners turn a ten-day repaint into a two-day change.

**Here.** Two-pack epoxy needs ≥10 °C substrate and <80% RH to cure, so any repaint needs a heated, dehumidified tank enclosure for the whole application window.

#### Medium-pressure UV

3 x full-flow medium-pressure UV on the 1,600 m³/h return. DIN 19643-4 recognises only medium-pressure UV for reducing combined chlorine; dechloramination dose 60 mJ/cm² average on actual circulation flow, with 300 mJ/cm² for substantial and 600 mJ/cm² for full chloramine destruction.

**Why.** UV is what lets you hold free chlorine at the bottom of the legal band, which means less eye irritation for talent working without goggles.

**Here.** Medium-pressure lamps are a large continuous electrical load, tens of kW. On a constrained connection this competes directly with stage lighting capacity.

#### Activated carbon polishing sidestream

10% sidestream GAC, empty bed contact time ≥8 min, for colour, THM and dissolved organic removal. The reg. 814/2010 handbook specifically recommends a carbon filter with special media to reduce THM and trichloramine.

**Why.** Colour, not turbidity, is what kills long underwater sightlines. Carbon keeps the water optically colourless.

**Here.** Carbon media is a bulk import on a 6–12 month change cycle; hold a full spare charge on site.

#### pH control by CO2

CO2 injection as primary pH control to reg. 814/2010's 7.2–7.6 band, with 32% hydrochloric acid as backup. The Icelandic handbook notes CO2 is widely used but costly if pushing pH down near 7.6, and that sodium hypochlorite itself raises pH.

**Why.** CO2 forms bicarbonate, so it buffers as it corrects. Mineral acid strips alkalinity and drives the water further into the aggressive range.

**Here.** Icelandic mains is essentially unbuffered, so hypochlorite dosing walks pH up fast. Without CO2 the loop hunts and chlorine goes ineffective above pH 7.5.

#### Degassing and micro-bubble control

Air release valves at every high point, flooded pump suctions with NPSHa ≥ NPSHr + 1.5 m, return nozzle velocity ≤0.5 m/s, and a settling window of 2–3 minutes after divers still before a take. Rebreathers or remote-exhaust surface supply for safety divers in frame.

**Why.** Micro-bubbles scatter light and read as haze. They come from cavitating pumps, entraining returns and diver exhaust, in that order of stubbornness.

**Here.** Cold make-up carries more dissolved gas and releases it as it warms to 30 °C — temper and degas make-up before it enters the tank.

#### Raw water buffer reservoir

2,000 m³ buried insulated reservoir (my spec), filled continuously at a low agreed rate and drawn down fast for a tank fill. Chlorinated and circulated so it never stagnates, with its own small filter and dosing set.

**Why.** Turns a 3.7-day fill into a 12-hour one without asking the utility for a peak that does not exist.

**Here.** The direct answer to a small municipal network. Fox built a 4 million gallon reservoir for exactly this reason at Sersen Lake in 1962.

#### Deck and stage floor drainage recovery

Perimeter overflow plus stage floor gullies on a 12 m grid around the tank, all returning to the balance tank rather than to sewer. Slot drains so the floor stays flat for dollies; falls of 1:200 only in the tank surround.

**Why.** Every litre thrown out of the tank by a wave, a dump or a rain gag is treated, heated water. Recover it or pay for it twice.

**Here.** Keeps chlorinated water inside the site boundary, which is the whole consent strategy for Varmá.

#### Rain system

12 x 6 m rain bars at perm level on the grid plus 8 mobile towers of 6–9 m. A heavy rain look is roughly 100 mm/h: over the full 2,400 m² that is 240 m³/h (67 l/s), over a 20x20 m area 40 m³/h (11 l/s) — my calcs. 2 x 40 l/s pumps at 6 bar (~35 kW each) off the balance tank, 100 µm filtered, delivered at 28–30 °C.

**Why.** Cold rain ends a shooting day faster than any technical fault. Warm rain and a recovery route keep talent in the water.

**Here.** A 6 m tower indoors is trivial; outdoors on a site this exposed, wind loading on backlot rain towers is severe and they need ballast and guying designed, not hired.

#### Dump tanks

4 x 4,500 l and 2 x 9,000 l gravity dump tanks, tipping in under 2 s, refilled from the balance tank at 40 l/s (4,500 l in under 2 min). Malta and Ciudad de la Luz both list dump and tip tanks alongside wave machines and water cannons as standard tank equipment.

**Why.** A dump tank is how you make a wall of water on cue. Nothing else delivers that instantaneous flow.

**Here.** 9 t of water 15 m up is a serious moving point load on the grid, and here it must also combine with the seismic case. It belongs in the structural brief on day one, not in the effects hire order.

#### Water cannons, wind machines and air movers

Compressed-air water cannons off a 2,000 l receiver at 10 bar; 2 x 900 mm Ritter-type wind machines; an underwater bubble grid; a fog-on-water system. Ciudad de la Luz publish wave generators, water cannons and tip tanks with a spillway on their 100x80 m exterior tank.

**Why.** The standard effects vocabulary. A tank without them books at half the rate.

**Here.** Anything on a backlot tank has to survive genuinely severe design wind loads between shoots — store indoors or specify for the site wind, not for a hire fleet.

#### Production underwater lighting

HydroFlex fixtures: HydroPar 4000W and 1200W M1 HMI, 1200W SE HMI, SeaPar 1200W HMI (Academy Technical Achievement Award 1991, rated to 240 ft / 73 m), HydroPar 5000W/2000W/1000W/650W incandescent, HydroRama 10K, SeaSun S30 SkyPanel, SeaSun Litepanels 1x1, HydroFlo fluorescent — all with integrated GFCI and wet-mateable connectors.

**Why.** Mains-voltage fixtures in the water are the only way to reach key-light levels at depth, and the GFCI is the only thing between the fixture and the performer.

**Here.** These are mains, not SELV, so they sit outside IEC 60364-7-702 Zone 0 rules and run under a temporary production supply regime (BS 7909 is the usable model). Expect to explain that to an inspector who only knows pool law.

#### Underwater camera housings and wet room

Housings for current cinema bodies (Gates, HydroFlex), a dedicated wet room with a 2 m rinse tank, fresh-water rinse, warm-air drying, silica store, O-ring bench and a vacuum leak-test rig. Positive-pressure store for optics.

**Why.** A flooded housing is a six-figure day. The rinse and test discipline is the whole defence.

**Here.** Housing service is several days away by air; hold a second body's worth of seals and ports, and a backup housing, on site.

#### Cylinder and equipment store

Ventilated cylinder store, EN 1089-3 colour coding, periodic inspection to EN ISO 18119 under TPED, a separate oil-free O2 store, drysuit and wetsuit wash and drying racks for 40, heated boot dryers, a regulator service bench and a quarantine rack for failed kit.

**Why.** Forty sets of exposure kit going back on wet and cold is how you lose a dive team to skin infection and hypothermia in week two.

**Here.** Iceland's damp cold defeats passive drying. Run the drying room off district heat — the cheapest heat in Europe and already at the door.

#### Drowning detection system

Computer-vision drowning detection to ISO 20380:2017, which applies to public pools with a basin surface area of 150 m² or more — Lon at 2,400 m² qualifies comfortably. Underwater and overhead cameras, alarm to the deck and to the dive supervisor's station.

**Why.** A second layer of supervision that does not blink. ISO 20380 is explicit that it supplements lifeguards rather than replacing them.

**Here.** It will be defeated by a black-painted tank, atmospheric fog, set dressing and twenty crew in frame. Budget for it, but never let it be treated as cover for thin safety-diver numbers.

#### Insulated floating thermal cover

Sectional insulated floating cover for the full 2,400 m² on powered reels at the tank ends, deployable by two people in under 30 minutes, cutting evaporation by 90–95%.

**Why.** A covered tank stops being a humidity problem, a heat loss and a chloramine source the moment shooting stops — which is most of the year.

**Here.** Also the ash-event defence. Cover the tank, close the outside air, and the water survives a fall that would otherwise blind every filter on the lot.

#### Volcanic ash contingency for the water system

Seal the rainwater harvest, cover the tank, close outside air, run recirculation with F7 plus F9 and activated carbon. Eyjafjallajökull 2010: fluoride in precipitation 100 km west rose from 10–20 µg/l to 90 µg/l; ash carried 23–35 mg fluoride per kg and silicon hexafluoride salts (Na2SiF6). Grímsvötn ash median particle 103 µm; Eyjafjallajökull bimodal at 45 and 350 µm.

**Why.** Ash in the tank is instant turbidity, abrasion on pump seals and acrylic ports, fluoride and sulphate loading, and blinded filters — all at once.

**Here.** Purely Icelandic; nobody designing a tank elsewhere thinks about it. Hold spare filter media, strainer baskets and a pump seal set on site permanently.

#### Askja wet provision

No permanent tank in the 6,000 m² stage. Instead: a 12x12x1.2 m recessed insert pit with a flush removable lid, floor gullies on a 12 m grid to a dedicated 200 m³ sump, a DN150 water take-off at four points, 4 x 63 A wet-rated distribution positions, and pipe routes to Lon's plant room.

**Why.** The big stage will get wet whether you plan for it or not. Drainage and a water take-off cost almost nothing at build and are ruinous to retrofit into a 6,000 m² floor.

**Here.** Connecting to Lon's plant means Askja's incidental wet work is treated and heated by plant you already own and already have permitted.

#### Wet routes across the acoustic interconnect

Where the two 2,000 m² stages interconnect acoustically, break every shared wet route: no continuous water pipe or drain between isolated boxes without a flexible section, a resilient hanger every 1.5 m, and no rigid contact through the separation.

**Why.** An acoustic interconnect that lets sound through is fine; one that lets pump hum through is not. Water pipework is the sneakiest flanking path on a lot.

**Here.** Water at 1,480 m/s makes filled pipes excellent sound conductors. On a lot already fighting driven rain on the roof, do not hand the stages a second path.

#### Independent inspection and structural monitoring

Annual visual and instrumented inspection; full drain-down, survey and liner inspection every 10 years; acrylic panel inspection against ASME PVHO-1 design-life and inspection guidance; crack-width and level survey after any felt seismic event; under-liner leak detection with a drained monitoring layer to a tell-tale.

**Why.** A liner-and-concrete tank fails slowly and invisibly. The tell-tale is what tells you it has started.

**Here.** Post-earthquake survey is a standing item here, not an exception. Write the trigger into the O&M: any felt event, survey before the next fill.

### State of the art

#### Movable floor over the shallow basin

20x20 m hydraulic movable floor (Variopool, Myrtha or similar), travel 0 to −2.00 m, positioning ±5 mm, level ±3 mm, full stroke ≤20 min, 300 kg/m² deck load when parked flush, 316L or GRP-clad structure.

**Why.** Replaces a 38-hour fill and 4-hour drain with a 20-minute move. Largest turnaround saving on the stage after the tank itself.

**Here.** Run the hydraulic power pack's heating off district heat, not electricity — the grid connection is the constrained resource here, heat is not.

#### Underwater viewing ports — cast acrylic

4 x 2.4x1.5 m ports, one per dive-well wall, sill 1.5 m above the well floor, plus one 6.0x2.4 m gallery panel. Cast (not extruded) PMMA, 100–150 mm engineered. ASME PVHO-1 design safety factors: 11.2 tension/flexure, 8.0 compression for flat panels; every panel hydrostatically tested before service.

**Why.** Dry-side camera positions are what separate a tank from a swimming pool. Without ports every setup costs a housing, a diver and a light.

**Here.** Panels ship as single crated pieces. A 6 m acrylic slab is an abnormal load from the port through Mosfellsbær — the site access geometry has to allow for it on day one.

#### Dry camera gallery behind the ports

1.8 m wide, 2.4 m headroom, blacked out, around three sides of the dive well at −4.0 m. Camera plinths on isolated pads, 63 A three-phase and video tie-lines at each port, HVAC at NR 20, ≤50% RH, positive pressure to keep condensation off the dry face of the acrylic.

**Why.** A port you cannot get a camera, a focus puller and a monitor behind is a window, not a shooting position.

**Here.** 4 °C groundwater outside the gallery wall and 30 °C water inside the acrylic guarantees condensation unless the gallery is actively dried.

#### Regenerative media polishing filter

Neptune-Benson/Xylem Defender regenerative media filter on sidestream or full flow, perlite media, filtration to 1 micron, no backwash (periodic bump regeneration). Manufacturer publishes the FP series at 107–300 US gpm (24–68 m³/h) per unit, with larger models above.

**Why.** Sand at 21 m/h will not deliver the horizontal visibility underwater cinematography needs. One micron will.

**Here.** No backwash means near-zero water loss, decisive where the mains service is rate-limited and every cubic metre must be paid for, retreated or held.

#### Backwash reclaim

Backwash one filter at a time at 45 m/h: 9.62 m² x 45 = 433 m³/h for 6 min = 43 m³ per filter, 345 m³ for a full eight-filter cycle (my calc). Reclaim by lamella settlement plus ultrafiltration at 85–90% recovery; dechlorinated residue to sewer.

**Why.** 345 m³ of chlorinated waste per cycle is both a water cost and a consent problem. Reclaim turns it into 35 m³.

**Here.** Chlorinated backwash cannot go near surface water: reg. 798/1999 Annex I caps HOCl at 0.004 mg/l in rivers and lakes outside the mixing zone, below the detection limit of pool instruments per Umhverfisstofnun's own handbook.

#### Ozone and granular activated carbon

DIN 19643-3 ozone process: 0.8–1.2 g O3/m³ of water, ≥3 min contact, full destruction on GAC before return. Ambient ozone monitoring to 0.1 ppm with automatic shutdown, in a dedicated sealed room.

**Why.** Ozone plus carbon gives the best optical water of any process, removing the dissolved organics that yellow the water and kill contrast at 20 m.

**Here.** An ozone leak inside a sealed 180,000 m³ stage is a life-safety event with no quick natural ventilation path. Put the plant outside the acoustic envelope.

#### Disinfection — on-site electrochlorination

Brine electrolysis producing about 0.8% sodium hypochlorite on demand, 20 t salt store, plus a bulk 14–15% w/v hypochlorite tank as backup. Hydrogen vented outside, explosion-safe, continuous H2 detection. Bulk hypochlorite decays — store ≤30 days, below 20 °C, vented.

**Why.** Removes a chemical supply chain to an island from the critical path. A tank that cannot chlorinate is a tank that has to close.

**Here.** It trades a shipping dependency for an electrical one, which only works if the grid connection is sized for it up front — on this site it may not be.

#### Roof rainwater harvesting

Collect off Askja (6,000 m²), Lon and the two 2,000 m² stages, about 13,400 m² total. Reykjavík's 1991–2020 normal is roughly 870–880 mm; at 0.9 runoff that is about 10,500 m³ a year (my calc), or 1.6 full tank fills. First-flush diversion, 100 µm screening, settlement, then treatment.

**Why.** The roof is already sized to shed the rain. Routing it to storage instead of the surface-water drain covers effects water, rain towers and dump tanks for free.

**Here.** Harvested rain is soft, slightly acidic and carries ash after an eruption, so it needs remineralising. It is unlikely to qualify as neysluvatn for bathing make-up under reg. 814/2010 — use it for effects and initial fill, and take bathing make-up from mains.

#### Wave generator and wave-absorbing beach

6 pneumatic wave caissons along one 40 m wall, 0.6–1.0 m wave height (my spec). Published comparable: Avatar: The Way of Water used two 19,000 lb steel wave wedges on an 800 hp hydraulic system in a 120x60x30 ft, 250,000+ US gallon tank at Manhattan Beach Studios. Opposite wall: 1:5 sloped perforated absorbing beach.

**Why.** Without an absorbing beach, reflections off the far wall return into shot as uncontrollable chop about eight seconds after every wave.

**Here.** The beach is the part everyone forgets and the one part that cannot be retrofitted into a finished concrete basin.

#### Current and flow generation

4 x 150 kW submerged thrusters (my spec). Published comparable: the Way of Water tank used two 500 hp 480 V electric motors on 25 ft x 6 in solid steel shafts driving 5 ft brass propellers at 800 rpm, giving 20–30 knot flow.

**Why.** Directional current is what sells an ocean. Waves alone read as a swimming pool with a machine in it.

**Here.** Another few hundred kW of intermittent load on a connection the utility is already reluctant to grant. Size the connection for the effects peak, not the average.

### What gets got wrong

- Heating the tank in Iceland is almost free, but only on interruptible terms. Veitur's H4A-C 'Sundlaugar' tariff (91.63 ISK/m3 + 2% state tax = 103.74 incl. 11% VAT, effective 01.01.2025) and the V2-V5 industrial tariffs are all explicitly 'ekki forgangsvatn' - non-priority, valid only where Veitur judges there is enough water and transmission capacity, with V2-V5 offered only temporarily, and no rebate is given for restricted delivery. Build thermal buffer and a written cold-week plan or you will have a 6,400 m3 tank cooling through a January peak.
- Never heat the tank by direct injection of geothermal water. Icelandic geothermal water is silica-rich and magnesium-free; the cold supply is the reverse. Mixing them forms magnesium silicate scale which Umhverfisstofnun's own pool handbook calls the principal cleaning problem at Icelandic bathing places - it bonds to tiles, pool liners and acrylic, and the only way off is acid, which etches the very surfaces you need optically clear. Closed system, titanium plate heat exchangers, always.
- Icelandic water is so soft it is aggressive. Parts of Reykjavik measure 0.2-0.60 degrees dH. With essentially no alkalinity the pH walks up every time you dose hypochlorite, chlorine efficacy collapses above pH 7.5, and the negative Langelier index leaches calcium out of the concrete and pits the metalwork. You have to manufacture alkalinity (80-120 mg/l as CaCO3) and hardness (200-300 mg/l Ca) deliberately, and control pH with CO2 rather than mineral acid.
- Nothing chlorinated can go near surface water. Reglugerd nr. 798/1999 Annex I caps HOCl at 0.004 mg/l in rivers and lakes outside the mixing zone, a figure the Umhverfisstofnun handbook notes is far below the detection limit of the instruments pools actually use. Varma, Kaldakvisl and Leirvogsa all run into Leiruvogur; Varmarosar is a designated fridland (1980, revised 2012, 9.76 ha) and Leirvogsa is a two-rod salmon river. Discharge to fresh surface water also needs agency opinions and the consent of the local veidifelag. Plan to retain and re-treat, never to dump.
- A UK or US dive crew cannot simply turn up and dive. Reglugerd nr. 535/2001 requires Icelandic commercial diving certificates (classes A-G, issued by Samgongustofa), 12-month medicals, and supervisor qualification of five years' commercial experience and/or 200 dives - and diving equipment itself requires recognition by the maritime authority. HSE ACOP L106 is the media-diving standard productions expect, but it is UK law with no force here. Sort recognition months before the first shooting block. (L106 is media diving; L107 is scientific and archaeological - people cite the wrong one constantly.)
- The regulatory frame is a swimming pool, not a film tank. If the health board treats Lon as a sund- og badstadur under reglugerd nr. 814/2010 you inherit pH 7.2-7.6, combined chlorine never above 1.0 mg/l, filter velocity capped at 25 m/h for new installations rather than the 30-35 m/h common elsewhere in Europe, 2 m3 per bather per hour of treated water, and an Annex II turnover class which for 30-34 C water above 100 m3 reads as a two-hour turnover - on 6,400 m3 that is 3,200 m3/h and is absurd. Negotiate the classification and turnover basis with the heilbrigdisnefnd in writing at design stage. I could not read Annex II's free-chlorine band; confirm it before design freeze.
- Permanent pool lighting and production lighting live under different electrical regimes and inspectors confuse them. IEC 60364-7-702 allows only SELV at 12 V AC or 30 V DC inside the basin, with IEC 60598-2-18 IPX8 luminaires. Production fixtures - HydroFlex HydroPar 4000W HMI, SeaPar 1200W and the rest - are mains voltage with integrated GFCI, rigged as temporary production supply. Both are correct; be able to explain which is which, and interlock the water so nothing is energised with divers down.
- Thermal schlieren will ruin a shot before turbidity does. Inject heated return water more than about 2 K above tank temperature and the density gradient shimmers visibly on a long underwater lens. Distribute through the whole floor at 0.5 m/s or less and hold the tank within 0.5 K floor to surface. The same applies to 4 C make-up water, which lays a cold layer straight across the shot.
- A heated rain tower is a cooling tower pointed at your cast. Atomising 30 C tank water into a sealed stage is textbook Legionella aerosol generation, and Iceland has no ACOP equivalent to HSE HSG274 that will force you to write a control scheme - so nobody will ask, which is exactly the danger. No dead legs, drain the rain mains after use, disinfect weekly, sample monthly to ISO 11731.
- Trichloramine collects in the first 300 mm above the water, which is where a treading-water actor breathes. An outdoor pool ventilates itself; an enclosed 180,000 m3 stage does not, and reg. 814/2010 says nothing about tank air in a sealed building. Put a slot extract at the coping round the full perimeter, target 0.5 mg/m3 NCl3, and interlock it to the cover.
- Fill and drain times are booking times, and a small municipal network will not give you a peak fill. 6,400 m3 at a realistic 20 l/s is 89 hours. Malta publish 15 hours for a 46 million litre tank; Fox filled Sersen Lake's 3 million gallons in 100 minutes - both off dedicated reservoirs. Build the 2,000 m3 buffer, harvest the roof (about 10,500 m3 a year off 13,400 m2 at Reykjavik's 870-880 mm normal), and keep the water rather than replacing it.
- The wave machine is easy; the absorbing beach is the part that cannot be retrofitted. Without a sloped perforated beach on the wall opposite the wave caissons, reflections return into shot as chop about eight seconds after every wave and no amount of machine control fixes it. It has to be in the concrete.
- 6,400 tonnes of water is the largest seismic mass on the lot and roughly half of it sloshes out of phase with the structure. Design to EN 1998-4 for impulsive and convective masses with real freeboard, articulate every pipe crossing the tank wall, and confirm the reference peak ground acceleration for Mosfellsbaer off the IST EN 1998-1 NA:2010 hazard map - the national peak of 0.5 g applies in the South Iceland Seismic Zone, not here, and I could not read the local value (treat 0.10-0.15 g as an unverified placeholder).
- Computer-vision drowning detection to ISO 20380:2017 covers basins of 150 m2 and up, so Lon qualifies - but it is designed for clear, evenly lit, empty pools. A black-painted tank with atmospheric fog, floating set dressing and twenty crew in frame will defeat it. Fit it, and never let anyone use it to argue down the number of safety divers.
- Everything structural is imported and the labour pool is small. Acrylic ports ship as single abnormal loads that have to physically reach the site; movable-floor, wave and filtration plant arrive with commissioning engineers who fly in; and while you can import the dive team, the pool supervisor and plant operator must hold Icelandic competence under reg. 814/2010 Annex III, re-tested annually, with annual chemical-handling training for treatment staff. Name and train them two years before opening.
- Ash is a water problem, not just an air problem. Eyjafjallajokull ash carried 23-35 mg of fluoride per kg and silicon hexafluoride salts, and fluoride in precipitation 100 km downwind rose from 10-20 to 90 ug/l. In the tank that reads as instant turbidity, abrasion on pump seals and acrylic, and fluoride and sulphate loading, all at once. Cover the tank, isolate the rain harvest, and hold spare media, strainer baskets and pump seals permanently on site.

---

## 9. Virtual production and LED volume

*Askja, Sugar Land Studios, Mosfellsbær*

### Essential

#### Main wall LED panel, film-grade 2.3–2.8 mm

ROE Visual publishes for Black Pearl BP2 V2: 2.84 mm pitch, 500×500×90 mm, 176×176 px (123,904 px/m²), 1,500 nits calibrated, 7,680 Hz refresh, 16-bit greyscale, 6000:1, 1/8 scan, 140°/140°, 190 W max / 95 W avg (760 / 380 W per m²), 9.35 kg, IP40, −20 to +45 °C, 10° concave curve.

**Why.** The wall is background and key light at once. Wrong pitch, refresh or scan and every frame carries moire, scan lines or magenta shift.

**Here.** Every panel is imported — Shenzhen to Rotterdam to Eimskip Sundahöfn. Carry 8–10% hot spares rather than the 3–5% a mainland lot runs; a single replacement cabinet is 6–10 weeks (estimate), and a dead cabinet mid-shoot is a lost stage day.

#### Ceiling raster / overhead LED

Design estimate for Askja: ~1,100 m² of 5.9–6.9 mm pitch (~28,700 px/m² at 5.9 mm) on a motorised raster at 12–16 m AFFL, driven as a separate canvas. Coarse pitch is acceptable because it is never in frustum; it exists for reflection and top light.

**Why.** Without an overhead source, car roofs, eyes, chrome, water and any wet surface read as a black hole and the illusion collapses.

**Here.** Ceiling rasters must be seismically restrained to ÍST EN 1998-1 (Eurocode 8, Icelandic National Annex), not just hung — every hoist needs a secondary bond and a horizontal restraint, which most integrators will not quote unless asked.

#### Receiver cards inside the panels

Brompton publishes Tessera G1: up to 1,000,000 px per panel, up to 1,000 fps, RGBW drive, 10 Gb fibre direct to panel, 20× the compute of the R2+, and it is what makes TrueLight per-pixel RGBW calibration possible. R2+ remains the volume workhorse.

**Why.** The receiver card, not the LED, sets bit depth at low brightness, refresh, and whether ShutterSync and Dynamic Calibration work at all.

**Here.** Fibre-to-panel matters here: copper 10GBASE-T runs are limited to 100 m and an oval wall of 85 m developed length plus riser plus machine room exceeds that on the far side.

#### Volume geometry and pixel budget

Design estimate for Askja: oval footprint 40×24 m, 300° wrap, 11 m wall height → ~84 m developed × 11 m = 924 m². At 2.3 mm (189,036 px/m²) that is ~175 Mpx of wall, plus ~32 Mpx of ceiling, plus a 200 m² floor at ~30 Mpx. Total ~237 Mpx.

**Why.** Pixel count drives processor count, render node count, power, heat and cost. Get it wrong at concept and everything downstream is wrong.

**Here.** A 30 m clear height lets the wall go to 11 m and still leave 19 m of perms for the ceiling raster, lighting grid and hoists. Most stages cannot; this is the one place the Icelandic build is easier.

#### LED processors

Brompton publishes Tessera SX40: 2U, 9,000,000 px at 36 bpp/60 Hz, 12-bit, 23.98–250 Hz, 2 frames end-to-end latency, 4× 10GBASE-T outputs, 12G-SDI + HDMI 2.0 (18 Gbps, 600 MHz pixel clock), 4096×2160 max in, HDR, Dynamic Calibration, ShutterSync, Frame Remapping, 3D LUT import.

**Why.** Everything the camera sees passes through here. Processor capacity, not panel count, is usually what caps a volume's resolution.

**Here.** At ~237 Mpx you need roughly 26 SX40s plus spares (estimate). Megapixel publishes HELIOS as an alternative: 425,000 px per port at 12-bit/60 Hz (918 Mb/s), 4,250,000 px per 10-port switch, 12-bit at 120 Hz, PQ and HLG HDR.

#### Fibre and copper data plant

OS2 single-mode to the panel risers, OM4 multimode for short runs, 10GBASE-T Cat6A only for sub-90 m tails, all to ISO/IEC 11801. Armoured and drag-chained where it crosses moving wall wagons. Every run tested and certified, results kept.

**Why.** Intermittent optics produce single-frame pixel dropouts that nobody sees on set and everyone sees in dailies.

**Here.** Dry winter air plus synthetic flooring makes ESD a live risk on SFP+ handling. Specify a bonded, ESD-safe patch area (IEC 61340-5-1) — this is not theatre, it kills optics.

#### Stage network switches

PTP-aware (IEEE 1588-2019) leaf/spine 100 GbE core with 10/25 GbE leaves, cut-through switching, separate VLANs for RenderStream, nDisplay sync, tracking, DMX/sACN and house IT. Boundary-clock, not transparent-clock, at the leaf.

**Why.** Shared switching between render traffic and tracking traffic is the classic cause of intermittent inner-frustum judder that nobody can reproduce.

#### Sync generator / PTP grandmaster

Dual redundant grandmasters running the SMPTE ST 2059-2 PTP profile, plus legacy tri-level sync (SMPTE ST 318) and black burst for camera and processor genlock, and SMPTE ST 12-1 LTC for timecode. GNSS-disciplined with a holdover oscillator.

**Why.** Camera, wall refresh, tracking and render must share one clock. A drifting phase relationship produces tearing at the frustum edge and scan lines in camera.

**Here.** GNSS at 64°N sees a low satellite elevation and real ionospheric scintillation during auroral events. Spec a rubidium or OCXO holdover of ≥24 hours, and do not rely on a single roof antenna.

#### Genlock and timecode distribution

Redundant DA chains, 75 Ω BNC to every camera position, processor, tracking head and render node, with ≤1 ns skew budget across the volume. Loop-through terminated, never daisy-chained past a fourth device.

**Why.** One untermin­ated genlock run puts a reflection on the reference and gives you intermittent frame drops on one processor only.

#### ShutterSync configuration

Brompton publishes ShutterSync as a patent-pending feature that shifts the timing of the LED refresh to match the camera's shutter. Configured per camera body and per shutter angle, stored as a named preset per show.

**Why.** Without it, narrow shutter angles and high-speed work show horizontal banding no matter how high the panel refresh rate is.

**Here.** A small crew means the preset library must be written down and version-controlled, not carried in one engineer's head. That engineer will at some point be snowed in at Mosfellsheiði.

#### Dynamic Calibration, TrueLight and OSCA

Brompton publishes Dynamic Calibration as real-time per-LED drive optimisation on the R2 Dynamic Engine with no added latency, giving brighter whites and higher contrast; TrueLight as per-pixel RGBW calibration on the G1; OSCA as on-screen colour adjustment for panel-to-panel matching.

**Why.** Uncalibrated or statically calibrated panels lose 30–40% of peak brightness and show cabinet-to-cabinet colour steps that grade cannot fix.

#### ACES / OCIO colour pipeline

ACES 2065-1 (SMPTE ST 2065-1) as interchange, ACEScg for render, OCIO config version-pinned and distributed to every node. ACES 2.0 was announced available by the Academy at NAB 2024 with an entirely new Output Transform set and improved invertibility; OCIO 2.4.0 shipped ACES 2 as a preview.

**Why.** Without one config on every machine, the wall, the render, the DIT cart and the monitor each apply a different transform and nothing matches.

#### Wall display colour target

Native gamut measured and reported; working target DCI-P3 (SMPTE RP 431-2) inside Rec.2020 (ITU-R BT.2020) container, 2.4 gamma for SDR or ST 2084 PQ (ITU-R BT.2100) for HDR delivery. Sony publishes >97% DCI-P3 for VERONA.

**Why.** An undefined wall gamut means the art department's blues and the DP's reference monitor disagree and nobody can say who is right.

#### Outer-frustum render nodes

nDisplay cluster. NVIDIA publishes RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition: 96 GB GDDR7 ECC, 1,597 GB/s on a 512-bit bus, 24,064 CUDA cores, 188 4th-gen RT cores, 752 5th-gen Tensor cores, 600 W max, passive, FHFL dual-slot PCIe Gen5. Estimate ~26 4K outputs for Askja → 12–16 nodes.

**Why.** The outer frustum is the light source. Drop frames there and the key light flickers, which is worse than a background artefact.

**Here.** 600 W per GPU, 16 nodes, two GPUs each — that is ~19 kW of GPU alone before CPU and PSU losses. All of it heat, in a building where heat is already the problem, not the shortage.

#### Inner-frustum render nodes

Dedicated GPUs per tracked camera frustum using Unreal's multi-GPU ICVFX path — Epic documents allocating the inner frustum to its own GPU so the cluster scales and each camera gets a uniquely tracked frustum. Two GPUs per camera, n+1 spares.

**Why.** The inner frustum is what the lens actually photographs. It needs the frame budget to itself or the background softens and lags on a whip pan.

#### Switchboard and cluster orchestration

Epic's Switchboard as the single control surface for every stage machine, with nDisplay config, Multi-User Editing session, and Live Link Face / Live Link tracking endpoints. Full-screen enforced — Epic documents that fullscreen is required for sync and genlock.

**Why.** Launching 16 nodes by hand costs 20 minutes per reload. On a stage at ISK 4–6 m per day that is real money every time.

#### Real-time engine and plugin licensing

Unreal Engine with the ICVFX toolset, nDisplay, Live Link, Remote Control and Take Recorder; plus Mo-Sys VP Pro or Disguise Porta for on-set scene control. Per-seat DCC: Maya, Houdini, Substance, SpeedTree, Quixel/Fab.

**Why.** Licence audits and expiring maintenance stop a stage as effectively as a dead processor.

#### Volume machine room

Dedicated acoustically isolated room, 600 mm raised floor or overhead containment, hot/cold aisle, 42U racks to EIA-310, N+1 CRAC, VESDA aspirating smoke detection, clean-agent suppression (NOVEC or IG-55 to ISO 14520), and its own access control.

**Why.** Render and processing noise is 70–80 dB(A). Putting it on the stage floor costs you the acoustic rating the stage was built for.

**Here.** Free cooling is available almost year-round — Reykjavík's mean annual temperature is about 5 °C — so an economiser-first design pays for itself, but it must have ash-rated filtration on the outside-air path.

#### UPS and conditioned power for compute

Double-conversion UPS (IEC 62040-3 VFI-SS-111) sized for 10 minutes at full render load, feeding processors, render nodes, tracking and grandmasters. Wall panels on raw mains, not on UPS.

**Why.** A 200 ms dip resets receiver cards and drops the whole wall. The cameras survive; the take does not.

**Here.** Iceland's grid is hydro and geothermal and largely stable, but Landsvirkjun has curtailed delivery to customers holding curtailability clauses. Never sign a curtailable tariff for the volume feed.

#### Primary camera tracking system

Mo-Sys publishes StarTracker Max: positional accuracy ~0.03% of height (nominal), angular ~0.003° (nominal), 130° FOV sensor, zero drift, output at a multiple of genlock up to 250 fps, rated for studios up to 100×100 m and ceiling heights over 16 m with appropriately sized and spaced stars.

**Why.** Tracking error is the one error the audience always sees — the background slides against the foreground and the shot is unusable.

**Here.** Askja's 30 m clear height is above Mo-Sys's stated >16 m guidance, so the star array needs oversized and more widely spaced retroreflective targets; get Mo-Sys to survey rather than assuming the standard star kit works.

#### Ceiling marker / star array

Retroreflective targets on a surveyed grid across the perms, positions captured by total station to ±2 mm and stored as the star map. Targets on independent brackets, not on anything that moves or heats.

**Why.** If a marker moves, the tracking solution moves with it and every shot that day has a slow background drift nobody notices until the edit.

**Here.** Askja's roof will move under Icelandic design wind and under thermal cycling between a −15 °C winter night and a heated interior. Survey the array seasonally and mount targets off the primary structure, not off purlins.

#### Lens encoders and FIZ data

Absolute encoders on focus, iris and zoom feeding the tracking system — Preston HU4/LCS, cmotion cPRO, or ARRI Hi-5 with LDS-2 / Cooke /i Technology metadata over the lens mount. Frame-accurate, on the same genlock.

**Why.** The virtual camera must match the real lens's focal length and focus distance every frame, or the frustum edge and the depth of field both lie.

#### Lens calibration and distortion mapping

Per-lens, per-focal-length distortion and nodal-point calibration shot against a printed chart grid, stored as an ST Map or a lens file. Entrance pupil offsets measured for every prime and at 5–7 points across each zoom.

**Why.** An uncalibrated lens puts a mismatch between real and virtual parallax at the frustum boundary — the classic soft, sliding edge.

#### Tracking-to-render latency alignment

Measured end-to-end delay from camera motion to wall update, typically 4–7 frames (estimate), compensated by delaying the camera's recorded image or by tracking-data prediction. Re-measured whenever a node, processor or firmware changes.

**Why.** Mismatched latency is the single most common inner-frustum complaint on set and is always blamed on the tracking, which is usually innocent.

#### Volume power distribution

Three-phase 400/230 V TN-S to IEC 60364 with assemblies to IEC 61439, distributed as 63 A and 125 A three-phase to panel risers. Design estimate for Askja: wall 924 m² at 760 W/m² peak = 702 kW; ceiling ~550 kW peak; realistic shooting average 30–40% of peak.

**Why.** Sizing the feed to average and not to peak means an all-white frame trips the volume. It happens on day one of every new build.

**Here.** Landsvirkjun has stated it is refusing supply to new industrial load on capacity grounds and the OECD's 2025 Iceland survey describes the system as running at full capacity. A ~1.3 MW peak volume connection cannot be assumed — secure it in writing before the panels are ordered.

#### Power quality and harmonics management

LED PSUs are switched-mode and non-linear. Active harmonic filters or 12-pulse rectification to meet IEC 61000-3-12 / IEC 61000-3-2, with THDi held under 5% at the point of common coupling and neutral conductors sized at 2× phase for triplen currents.

**Why.** A few hundred kW of switched-mode supplies will overheat the neutral and put visible ripple on the audio system's earth if untreated.

**Here.** Landsnet and the local DSO will measure at the PCC. A stage that fails harmonic compliance on a constrained grid is a stage whose connection gets reviewed — an unwinnable argument.

#### Volume heat rejection and air handling

Design estimate: ~500 kW of continuous heat at typical shooting brightness; essentially 100% of electrical input becomes heat. At a 10 K supply-to-return rise that needs ~41.5 m³/s of air. Delivered by low-velocity displacement from the perimeter at ≤2 m/s face velocity for acoustic reasons.

**Why.** Hot panels shift colour and shorten life; the wall's own convection plume also makes a shimmer in long lenses across the volume.

**Here.** Ambient air is a free chiller for most of the year, but ash events make outside-air economiser mode a trap. Spec ISO 16890 ePM1 ≥70% final filtration with an ash-event bypass to full recirculation plus mechanical cooling, and pressure-drop alarms on every bank.

#### Ash and particulate protection for the panels

Panels are IP40 (ROE published for BP2 V2) — dust-resistant, not dust-proof. Volume kept at positive pressure relative to the stage, stage positive to the lot, with an airlock at the volume entry and sealed cable penetrations.

**Why.** Volcanic ash is angular silicate and mildly conductive when damp. It gets behind the mask, sits on the SMD packages and causes creep and eventual failure.

**Here.** Iceland gets ash events on a scale nowhere else in Europe does. Write an ash protocol: shutdown sequence, recirculation mode, panel covers, and a post-event inspection of every filter bank and every panel face.

#### Volume superstructure and seismic restraint

Independent internal structure carrying wall and ceiling loads, designed to ÍST EN 1998-1 (Eurocode 8) with the Icelandic National Annex for a real design earthquake, plus EN 1991-1-4 wind for the enclosing shell. Every panel string with a secondary safety bond.

**Why.** A 924 m² wall of 9.35 kg panels is about 34 tonnes of hardware hanging over the crew. It is a life-safety structure, not a scenic one.

**Here.** The Reykjanes–Mosfellsbær area is seismically live. Panel restraint must resist horizontal acceleration, not just gravity — most LED integrators quote gravity-only frames and will need to be told otherwise.

#### Lighting console and DMX/sACN network

MA Lighting grandMA3 full-size with a full-size backup, running ANSI E1.31 (sACN) over the lighting VLAN, ANSI E1.11 DMX512-A to local gateways, ANSI E1.20 RDM for feedback, fixture data via GDTF and stage layout via MVR. Disguise documents Art-Net, sACN, Enttec and KiNET DMX drivers.

**Why.** The wall, the light cards and the conventional rig have to be one lighting system with one cue stack, or the gaffer is fighting the Unreal operator all day.

#### Contamination control: blacks, negative fill, flags

Full-height blackout drapes on motorised tracks behind and outside the wall opening, black floor when no LED floor is down, and a stock of solid and net flags. Volume interior surfaces matte black, LRV <5%.

**Why.** A 900 m² light source bounces off everything. Without negative fill the volume has no contrast and every shot looks flat.

**Here.** Sub-arctic summer means 21+ hours of daylight in June; the stage's own light-tightness carries the whole burden. Any daylight leak into a volume at 4am destroys the black level the panels were bought for.

#### The brain bar: crew roles

VP supervisor, Unreal/nDisplay operator (×2 for multi-camera), LED systems engineer, tracking engineer, imaging scientist / colour, systems and network engineer, plus the DIT. Eight to ten people before a single department head arrives.

**Why.** A volume without a permanent crew degrades in months. The knowledge is in people, and the equipment is worthless without them.

**Here.** Iceland's labour pool cannot supply eight specialist VP engineers. Plan for imported senior staff on rotation plus a structured apprenticeship with Kvikmyndaskóli Íslands or Reykjavík University, and budget for the housing.

#### Capital cost of the volume

Estimate for Askja: wall 924 m² at €3,000/m² ≈ €2.8 m; ceiling 1,100 m² at €1,200/m² ≈ €1.3 m; floor ≈ €1.0 m; 28 processors ≈ €0.9 m; render and media ≈ €1.5 m; tracking (two systems) ≈ €0.3 m; structure, motion and hoists ≈ €3 m; power, cooling, network ≈ €2 m. Total €12–16 m.

**Why.** Owners routinely budget the panels and forget that structure, power, cooling and compute together cost more than the LEDs.

**Here.** Add 12–18% (estimate) for freight, Icelandic installation labour at imported rates, and the crane time a 30 m stage demands. Published market figures for an 80 ft × 30 ft volume run USD 8–16 m excluding labour, which brackets this.

### Expected

#### Fine-pitch hero section, 1.5–1.6 mm

ROE publishes Ruby RB1.5F: 1.56 mm, 320×320 px per 500×500×73 mm panel, 1,000 nits calibrated, 7,680 Hz, flip-chip, 240 W max / 120 W avg (960 / 480 W per m²), 9.06 kg. Sony publishes ZRD-VP15EB: 1.56 mm, 500×500×93 mm, 1,500 cd/m², >97% DCI-P3, 7,680 Hz, 60 W avg at 30% brightness (240 W/m²).

**Why.** Short lenses and tight coverage resolve the pixel grid. Without a finer centre run you cannot shoot closer than roughly 4–5 m on a 35 mm lens.

**Here.** Two panel families means two spare pools, two calibration datasets and two firmware trains — a real burden with a small crew. Budget a second LED engineer, not just a second stock room.

#### LED light cards / soft source panels

Separate low-resolution, high-output cards (typically 9–15 mm pitch, 2,000–5,000 nits, estimate) addressed as DMX fixtures rather than as screen. Driven from the same processing spine so colour matches the wall exactly.

**Why.** The wall itself is a poor key light — too dim, too far, wrong shape. Light cards give the gaffer controllable soft sources that are already in the wall's colour space.

**Here.** With December daylight near zero in Mosfellsbær, the volume is the crew's only daylight for weeks. Spec ≥4,000 K working white and EN 12464-1 compliant task lighting outside the volume, or you will have a fatigue problem, not a lighting problem.

#### Processing redundancy and failover

N+1 hot-spare processors on a patched 10G fibre matrix, dual data feeds to every panel string (loop-back topology), and dual PSU cabinets so one dropped feed loses no pixels. Redundancy configured per-port, not per-rack.

**Why.** A single processor failure on a 26-unit system dark-fields a 9 Mpx wedge of the wall mid-take; with loop-back it is invisible.

**Here.** Nearest Brompton or Megapixel field service is the UK or the Netherlands. Hold a full spare processor on site and a spare of every SFP+ optic; a two-day AOG shipment is a lost shooting week.

#### Panel calibration and recalibration programme

Factory calibration at install, then camera-based recalibration on a documented cycle — 12 months or 2,000 operating hours, whichever first (estimate). Every recalibration logged against panel serial so drifting cabinets can be retired.

**Why.** LEDs age non-uniformly. A wall two years old with no recalibration has visible blotching under a flat grey and a colour shift in the blues.

**Here.** Recalibration needs a stable 20–25 °C soak. Book it for a period when the district-heat-fed AHUs are not fighting a −15 °C outside air condition, or the thermal state during calibration will not match the thermal state during shooting.

#### Spectroradiometer and reference colorimeter

Konica Minolta CS-2000/CS-2000A class spectroradiometer (380–780 nm, 1 nm pitch, luminance from 0.003 cd/m²) or a JETI specbos 1211, plus a working colorimeter for daily checks. Traceable calibration certificate renewed annually.

**Why.** You cannot characterise a wall with a colorimeter alone — narrowband LED spectra defeat filter-based instruments and you will chase a colour error that is in the meter.

**Here.** Annual recertification means shipping the instrument abroad. Buy two, stagger the certification dates, or you lose colour QA for a month each year.

#### On-set colour management software

Pomfort LiveGrade Pro or Colorfront On-Set Dailies / Express Dailies on the DIT cart, with FilmLight Prelight or Daylight for look development. CDL and LUT round-trip to the render cluster so the wall and the monitor move together.

**Why.** The DP grades the camera and the wall as one image. Split tools and the wall is graded twice, once by the DIT and once by the Unreal operator.

#### Reference mastering monitor

Sony BVM-HX3110 (dual-layer LCD, 4,000 cd/m² peak HDR / 1,000 cd/m² in a second mode, 31-inch, 4096×2160) or Flanders Scientific XMP550 OLED at the DIT cart, plus a matched client monitor. Profiled on the same schedule as the wall.

**Why.** Colour decisions made on an unprofiled monitor are made twice — once on set and again, expensively, in the DI.

#### Media server and show control

Disguise publishes RX III: 2 RU, AMD EPYC 16C/32T at 2.45 GHz, 96 GB RAM, 1.92 TB NVMe, NVIDIA RTX 6000 Ada, and states it handles double the RX II workload. VX 3 for playback with three 4K outputs; RenderStream for cluster rendering across RX nodes.

**Why.** Someone has to own timeline, cueing, plate playback and the handoff between pre-rendered and real-time. Unreal alone does not.

#### Shared storage and content network

All-NVMe shared storage, ≥2 PB usable, ≥40 GB/s aggregate read, 100 GbE to the render cluster with RDMA. Separate tier for plates and HDRI capture. Snapshot and offsite replication.

**Why.** Sixteen nodes cold-loading a 400 GB level off spinning disk is a 12-minute stage stop every time the scene changes.

**Here.** Offsite replication over the FARICE-1 / IRIS / DANICE subsea cables is fine for cold data but latency to a London or Frankfurt facility makes remote real-time review awkward; keep review local.

#### Version control for Unreal content

Perforce Helix Core with a stage-local edge server, typed workspaces per node, and a locked build promoted to the volume — never the artist stream. Every shoot day tagged by changelist.

**Why.** Without a promoted build you cannot reproduce yesterday's background for a pickup, and pickups are where volumes make or lose money.

#### Secondary / redundant tracking

stYpe publishes RedSpy as inside-out ceiling-marker tracking with ~20 s start-up, no recalibration, ceiling heights up to 42 m, and PTP sync plus genlock in the current version. OptiTrack publishes PrimeX 22: 2048×1088, 360 Hz, 2.8 ms latency, ±0.15 mm in a 9×9 m volume, 21 m passive / 30 m active range.

**Why.** A single tracking system is a single point of failure that stops the stage. A second system on a second technology also cross-checks the first.

**Here.** RedSpy's stated 42 m ceiling capability is the one system comfortably inside Askja's 30 m without special measures — worth being the primary rather than the backup here.

#### Humidity and ESD control in the volume

Relative humidity held 40–55% RH. Bonded, earthed working surfaces and wrist straps for any panel or card handling, to IEC 61340-5-1. Static-dissipative flooring in the machine room.

**Why.** Below about 30% RH, a technician walking across a floor can carry several kV. One discharge into an exposed receiver card destroys it silently.

**Here.** Geothermal-heated air in a cold, dry Icelandic winter runs extremely dry indoors. Humidification is not optional here the way it is in Los Angeles.

#### Ceiling hoists and raster motion

Variable-speed hoists to EN 17206:2020 / DIN 56950 with load cells, secondary suspension and a supervised control desk. Positional repeatability ±5 mm so the ceiling can return to a surveyed height.

**Why.** The ceiling must clear a crane, drop for a low interior, and return to a known height so the light rig and the tracking solution stay valid.

#### Wall regions exposed as DMX fixtures

Regions of the wall and every light card published to the console as addressable fixtures with intensity, RGB/RGBW, CCT and position parameters, so the board op can pull a window's brightness without going through the render.

**Why.** Making the gaffer ask a programmer to change a light is the single biggest efficiency loss on a badly integrated volume.

#### Supplementary fixtures colour-matched to the wall

ARRI SkyPanel X, Creamsource Vortex, Astera Titan and similar, each measured with the spectroradiometer and given a correction so their spectrum sits with the wall's narrowband RGB in the taking camera. TM-30-20 Rf/Rg recorded per fixture.

**Why.** LED wall spectrum and LED fixture spectrum are both spiky and differently spiky. Uncorrected, faces lit by both read as two different skin tones.

#### Scenic blend and set extension kit

Physical set pieces built to marry to the wall at the seam, matching paint and texture reference shot under wall light, and a run of translight/blend panels. Plus a stock of 2.8 m and 3.6 m blue and green for the shots the volume cannot do.

**Why.** The seam between practical set and wall is where audiences catch a volume. Blending is a craft budget line, not an afterthought.

#### HDRI and plate capture package

Ricoh Theta / Insta360 Pro for scout HDRI, plus a proper bracketed capture rig (Canon EOS R5 + 8 mm fisheye on a nodal head, 7-stop brackets) and a chrome/grey ball set. LiDAR (Leica RTC360 or NavVis VLX) for set scans.

**Why.** Content is the volume's real cost. Without your own capture you buy every environment, and bought environments never match the practical set.

**Here.** Iceland is the reason half the clients come. An in-house capture unit that can shoot Landmannalaugar, Vatnajökull and the Reykjanes lava fields as native volume content is a genuine commercial moat.

#### Volume QC test pattern and burn-in regime

Daily pre-call run of flat fields at 5%, 18%, 50% and 100%, a Brompton test-pattern set, a moire chart at the shooting lens, and a dead-pixel scan logged by cabinet serial. Firmware versions locked and recorded per show.

**Why.** Dead pixels and drifting cabinets are cheap to fix at 7am and catastrophic to discover in the DI six weeks later.

#### Operating cost per shoot day

Estimate: ~500 kW average over a 12-hour day = 6 MWh. Iceland's published business electricity price was ISK 12.8/kWh (about USD 0.106/kWh) in December 2025 per GlobalPetrolPrices, so ~ISK 77,000 (about USD 640) of electricity. Crew, licences and content dwarf it.

**Why.** People assume power is the running cost of a volume. It is not — it is the eight-person brain bar and the content that never stops being built.

**Here.** Cheap, low-carbon electricity is the genuine Icelandic advantage and worth marketing: the same volume day in Los Angeles or London costs several times as much in energy and carries a far worse carbon figure.

#### Depreciation and refresh cycle

Estimate: panels written down over 7 years, processors 5, render nodes 3–4, tracking 5. LED brightness typically falls to 50% at 50,000–100,000 hours; a volume running 2,000 h/year reaches useful end-of-life on colour uniformity long before on brightness.

**Why.** A volume priced without a sinking fund for refresh is a volume that becomes uncompetitive in year five and cannot afford to replace itself.

#### Insurance, warranty and service contract

Manufacturer warranty extended to 5 years on panels and receiver cards, a spares-holding agreement with guaranteed 48-hour dispatch, and business-interruption cover written to include volcanic and seismic events.

**Why.** Standard film production insurance excludes a lot of what Iceland actually does. Finding that out after an ash event is expensive.

**Here.** Icelandic insurers price volcanic and seismic risk explicitly; the Natural Catastrophe Insurance of Iceland (Náttúruhamfaratrygging Íslands) scheme covers some property perils but not production downtime. Read what is excluded, twice.

#### Acoustic treatment of the volume enclosure

The wall is a hard, flat, 900 m² reflector. Absorption behind and above it, and broadband absorption on the volume's outer face, to hold reverberation inside the stage's rated figure. Panel fan noise specified at ≤NR20 at 3 m (estimate).

**Why.** A volume can wreck the acoustics of a stage that cost a fortune to make quiet. Production sound will notice on day one.

**Here.** This compounds the rain-on-roof problem Askja already has. Do not let LED cooling fans eat the noise budget the roof build-up was designed to protect.

### State of the art

#### LED floor / walkable deck panels

Design estimate: 200–400 m² of 2.6–3.9 mm walkable cabinets rated ≥1,000 kg/m² point-load with anti-slip masking, laid flush to a sprung sub-deck. Deployed only for reflective-floor work; otherwise a matched painted deck.

**Why.** Reflections on wet tarmac, marble and polished floors have to come from somewhere. Painted floors cannot do it and a floor comp costs more than the panels.

**Here.** Volcanic grit tracked in on boots is abrasive silicate and destroys walkable masks faster than ordinary dust. Spec a hard-surface entry mat run of ≥8 m and a boot-wash at the volume airlock.

#### Camera-to-wall characterisation LUTs

A measured 3D LUT per camera body / sensor / panel-type pair, shot as a Macbeth and ramp series through the taking lens, loaded into the processor (SX40 supports 3D LUT import) or into the render. Rebuilt whenever a camera or panel family changes.

**Why.** Every sensor sees LED primaries differently. Without per-camera characterisation, skin against the wall reads magenta on one body and green on another.

#### Performance and prop motion capture

OptiTrack PrimeX 41 / PrimeX 120 or Vicon Valkyrie array covering the volume floor, for actor-driven creatures, driven set pieces and vehicle motion. Passive retroreflective markers, sub-millimetre accuracy per OptiTrack's published figures.

**Why.** Anything in the scene that must react to a performer — a creature, a stunt rig, a virtual vehicle — needs its own capture, not camera tracking.

#### Motorised wall wagons and reconfiguration

Wall segments on motorised wagons with absolute position feedback, control to EN 60204-1 and lifting/load-bearing machinery to EN 17206:2020, with dual-channel E-stop and a fixed safe-operating envelope. Position fed back to the render as stage geometry.

**Why.** A volume that cannot open up, split into a cove, or shrink to a small set is booked for one job at a time and sits idle between them.

**Here.** With a small local labour pool, motorised reconfiguration replaces crew you cannot hire. It is the highest-return capital item on the whole volume after the panels themselves.

#### Interconnect to Lon and the two 2,000 m² stages

Fibre and genlock/PTP trunks from the volume machine room to Lon's tank stage and to both 2,000 m² stages, so a tracked camera on another stage can render against volume content, and so the volume can serve a second unit.

**Why.** A volume that only serves one stage is idle whenever that stage is between jobs. Trunked, it becomes a lot-wide resource.

**Here.** Lon's 2,400 m² tank and 20×20 m, 6 m dive well plus volume content is a genuinely rare combination — underwater and water-interactive ICVFX. Nowhere in Europe offers both at this scale; it is the thing to sell.

### What gets got wrong

- Sizing the electrical feed to average brightness rather than to all-white peak. ROE publishes BP2 V2 at 190 W max against 95 W average per panel — a 2:1 ratio. A 924 m² wall is 702 kW peak, and a white-out frame will find the undersized breaker on the first day.
- Assuming 7,680 Hz refresh alone kills scan lines. It does not — the camera shutter must be phase-locked to the refresh. Brompton's ShutterSync exists precisely because high refresh rate is necessary and not sufficient.
- Treating moire as a panel defect. It is an interference between the sensor's photosite grid and the LED grid, and it is fixed by pitch selection, camera distance, focus plane and lens choice — not by anything the LED vendor can sell you.
- Buying one pixel pitch for the whole wall. Coarse pitch in the ceiling is free money; fine pitch in the hero section is what lets you shoot close. A uniform wall is either too expensive or too coarse.
- Forgetting that the receiver card, not the LED, sets low-end bit depth. Panels driven at 10% brightness on a weak card band visibly in shadows, which is exactly where volume content lives.
- Mounting tracking markers on anything that moves. Askja's roof will deflect under Icelandic design wind and thermal cycling; a star array surveyed in July and not re-surveyed in January produces a slow, un-diagnosable background drift.
- Sharing switching between render traffic and tracking traffic. It works in commissioning and fails intermittently under load, and the symptom — occasional inner-frustum judder — gets blamed on the tracking system for weeks.
- Putting the LED panels on the UPS and the render nodes on raw mains. It is the wrong way round: a dip that resets receiver cards is survivable, a dip that resets the render cluster is a 20-minute stage stop.
- Skipping annual panel recalibration because the wall 'looks fine'. It looks fine on content and blotches on a flat 18% grey, which is what a DP will eventually put up.
- Characterising the wall with a colorimeter. Narrowband LED spectra defeat filter-based instruments; you need a spectroradiometer of the CS-2000 or specbos class, and it needs traceable annual recertification, which from Iceland means shipping it abroad.
- Running one OCIO config on the render cluster and a different one on the DIT cart. The wall, the monitor and the render then each apply a different transform and the resulting argument is unwinnable because everyone is right about their own machine.
- Assuming grid capacity is available because Icelandic power is cheap. Landsvirkjun has publicly refused supply to new industrial load on capacity grounds and the OECD's 2025 Iceland survey describes the system as at full capacity. Secure the ~1.3 MW connection contractually before ordering panels — and never on a curtailable tariff.
- Specifying an outside-air economiser without an ash bypass. Free cooling is genuinely near-free at 64°N, but a single ash event ingests abrasive silicate straight into the panel plenum. ISO 16890 ePM1 ≥70% final filtration plus a full-recirculation mode with mechanical cooling is the minimum.
- Believing IP40 means the panels are protected. IP40 per IEC 60529 keeps out objects above 1 mm. It does nothing about ash, which is fine, angular and mildly conductive when damp.
- Quoting LED support steel for gravity only. ÍST EN 1998-1 with the Icelandic National Annex requires horizontal seismic restraint on 34 tonnes of panels hanging over the crew. Most LED integrators do not price this unless told to.
- Ignoring humidity. Geothermally heated Icelandic winter air indoors is extremely dry; below about 30% RH a technician carries kilovolts, and one discharge into an exposed receiver card kills it silently.
- Letting panel cooling fans eat the acoustic budget. A stage built to solve rain-on-a-long-span-roof can be undone by 900 m² of fans; specify panel noise and treat the volume's outer face.
- Budgeting the panels and not the rest. Structure, power, cooling, compute and motion together exceed the LED cost. Published market figures of USD 8–16 m for an 80 ft × 30 ft volume are the whole system, not the wall.
- Building a volume with no permanent crew. Eight to ten specialists keep it alive, and Iceland's labour pool cannot supply them — imported seniors on rotation plus a real apprenticeship pipeline is a capital cost, not an HR footnote.
- Assuming StarTracker will work out of the box at 30 m clear height. Mo-Sys publishes guidance for ceilings over 16 m 'with appropriately sized and spaced stars' — Askja needs a surveyed, oversized array, or a system like RedSpy that stYpe rates to 42 m.
- Running a single tracking system. It is the one failure that stops the stage dead, and a second system on a different technology also cross-checks the first, which is how you catch a drifting star map before it reaches the edit.
- No promoted, version-controlled build. Without a Perforce changelist tagged per shoot day you cannot reproduce a background for a pickup, and pickups are where volume economics are actually decided.

---

## 10. Sound stage complex

*workshops and people spaces (construction mill, paint, plaster, metal, prop/set stores, wardrobe, hair & makeup, dressing rooms, green room, catering, unit base, production offices, casting, medical, stores). Askja/Lon, Sugar Land Studios, Mosfellsbaer, Iceland.*

### Essential

#### Grid connection agreement and site load budget, secured before design freeze

Negotiate with Landsnet (transmission) and Veitur (distribution), not Landsvirkjun. Estimate: workshops+catering+laundry 1.5–2 MW, unit base 1 MW, stages 3–5 MW; 6–10 MVA ask. Underground all site HV.

**Why.** If the connection is refused or capped, the mill extraction, spray booth fans, laundry and unit base are the loads that get shed. Everything else in this spec depends on it.

**Here.** Grid at capacity and supply refused to new industrial load. Consequences: push every thermal load onto geothermal, add 5–10 MWh battery for peak shaving, 3 x 2 MVA HVO gensets for storm/peak, N+1 incomer, hard load-shedding BEMS.

#### Indirect hot water: plate heat exchangers on every potable/process circuit

Geothermal supply (est. 75–80 C at the capital-area network) on the primary side only. Secondary side is cold potable water. Never plumb district hot water to laundry, dye, hair backwash, showers-for-hair, or any food circuit.

**Why.** Geothermal water carries hydrogen sulphide. It smells, tarnishes silver and copper, shifts dyes and colour-treated hair, and leaves odour in fabric.
