**Here.** Veitur confirms a deliberate small H2S excess is maintained to scavenge oxygen and prevent pipe corrosion. The single most-missed Icelandic detail in a costume or hair department.

#### Construction mill building

3,000–4,000 m2 floor, 9–12 m clear under haunch, 200 mm power-floated slab to Concrete Society TR34 FM2, 50 kN/m2 UDL, steel-fibre + mesh, geothermal underfloor loop in the slab.

**Why.** Two-storey set pieces and 20 ft flats must be trial-assembled standing up. Under 9 m you build lying down and discover errors on stage.

**Here.** Heated slab stops frost heave at door thresholds and dries the floor; heat is nearly free so run it continuously rather than on demand.

#### Mill main doors

12 m x 8 m sliding-folding on bottom track with wind posts, insulated, motorised, plus an airlock vestibule. EN 13241 CE-marked, wind resistance class 5 to EN 12424 (>=1,000 Pa).

**Why.** A leaking or flapping door lets driven rain and ash into the timber store and makes the mill unheatable.

**Here.** At 1,000 Pa a 12 x 8 m leaf carries ~96 kN. Sectional doors flap and leak in 40 m/s gusts — use sliding-folding in a recessed, canopy-protected bay.

#### Panel saw (sliding table)

3,200 mm crosscut, scoring blade, 5.5 kW, digital fence. Altendorf F45 / Martin T75 class. Guarding to EN ISO 19085 series.

**Why.** Everything sheet-based starts here; an undersized or worn panel saw is the mill's real bottleneck.

#### Straight-line rip saw

5.5–11 kW, feed 10–40 m/min, anti-kickback fingers, riving knife. EN ISO 19085-6.

**Why.** Dimensioning imported stock; without it every rip goes through the panel saw and the queue doubles.

#### Surface planer and thicknesser

500–630 mm surface planer with spiral cutterblock; 630 mm thicknesser, 7.5 kW.

**Why.** Imported timber moves in transit and in store; nothing is straight out of the pack.

**Here.** Sea-freighted stock arrives with more movement than road-freighted stock. Budget more machining allowance per board.

#### Spindle moulder with power feed

Tilting, 5.5 kW, integral power feed, tooling to MAN/BG-TEST limited-cut. Guarding to EN 848-1.

**Why.** Non-standard profiles and curved work. Limited-cut tooling is the difference between a nicked finger and an amputation.

#### Bandsaws — resaw and cutting

900 mm resaw (7.5 kW) plus 600 mm cutting bandsaw with tilting table.

**Why.** Curved scenic work, resawing to thickness, recovering stock.

#### Flat-assembly tables and benches

One 12 x 4 m flat table with integral pneumatic frame clamp; 6 x 4 ft and 12 x 6 ft benches with vacuum clamping; 40 m of bench run total.

**Why.** A flat assembled out of square on the floor is square nowhere on stage.

#### Central LEV dust and chip extraction plant

Est. 45,000–60,000 m3/h at 60% diversity across 30–40 drops (800–2,500 m3/h each). Reverse-jet cartridge filter, residual <0.1 mg/m3, dust class M to EN 60335-2-69. Duct 3 mm mild steel, earthed/bonded, 20–25 m/s transport velocity.

**Why.** Hardwood dust is an IARC Group 1 carcinogen; the EU binding OEL fell from 3 to 2 mg/m3 inhalable on 17 Jan 2023 under Directive 2017/2398 amending 2004/37/EC.

**Here.** Run once-through and dump the air rather than recirculating. Makeup air of 50,000 m3/h at dT 20 K is ~330 kW, trivial on district heat — geothermal removes the whole economic argument for recirculating carcinogenic dust.

#### Dust plant explosion protection

Wood dust is St1, Kst up to ~200 bar.m/s. Venting to EN 14491, isolation to EN 16447 (back-pressure flap or chemical barrier), spark detection and extinguishing (VDI 2263 / FM-approved), ATEX 2014/34/EU equipment and 1999/92/EC zoning.

**Why.** A dust deflagration in the filter propagating back down the duct into the mill is the way studios burn down.

**Here.** Vent ducts must be wind-rated and their outlets sited so a 40 m/s crosswind does not hold the vent panel shut or drive rain into the filter.

#### Compressed air plant

2 x 55 kW oil-free rotary screw duplex plus standby, 7–10 bar g, est. 600–900 l/s FAD site-wide. ISO 8573-1 Class 1.4.1 for spray air (oil-free, +3 C PDP, 1 micron), refrigerant dryer plus a desiccant leg for the booth. 63 mm aluminium ring main, drops every 6 m.

**Why.** Oil carry-over into a spray gun contaminates a whole day of paint; wet air freezes tools.

**Here.** Keep receivers and dryers indoors; trace-heat any external run. An outdoor receiver in a January gale will freeze its drain and dump condensate into the ring main.

#### Conditioned timber store

400 m2, cantilever racking for 12 m lengths, 45–55% RH held, plus a 10 m3 conditioning/kiln chamber. 50 t sheet-goods store with flat stack, A-frames and a vacuum sheet lifter.

**Why.** Kiln-dried stock at 8% MC reaches 16–18% in Icelandic maritime air. Every flat built from it twists after paint.

**Here.** No commercial forestry — all timber imported by sea via Sundahofn on a 7–10 day cycle from Scandinavia/Baltic. Hold 8–12 weeks buffer, not the 2 weeks a UK mill runs.

#### Materials handling fleet, all-electric

3.5 t counterbalance electric FLT, 3 t side-loader for 12 m timber, 2 x electric pallet trucks, 10 m and 12 m electric scissor lifts (EN 280).

**Why.** Diesel indoors poisons the mill; LPG is a bottled-supply problem here.

**Here.** Electricity is cheap and low-carbon, and there is no piped gas in Iceland. All-electric is both the clean and the cheap answer. Size the charging load into the constrained connection.

#### Cordless tool crib and Li-ion charging room

200+ tools, charging bay in a fire-rated cabinet/room (EN 14470-1 Type 90 class or purpose-built Li-ion cabinet), thermal monitoring, outdoor quarantine cage for damaged packs.

**Why.** Charging lithium packs in a room full of wood dust is the most likely single ignition source in the mill.

**Here.** Fire service response to Mosfellsbaer is an estimated 8–12 minutes from Slokkvilid hofudborgarsvaedisins — verify. That response time argues for containment at source, not detection-and-wait.

#### Mill acoustic decoupling from the stages

Separate foundation, no shared slab, no shared duct or conduit route, physical gap between buildings. Machine enclosures on the panel saw and moulder. Mill machines run 95–105 dB(A).

**Why.** A moulder transmits through ground and structure into a live stage 300 m away and ruins a take.

**Here.** Also drives the noise-at-work case: Directive 2003/10/EC action values 80/85 dB(A), limit 87 dB(A), enforced in Iceland by Vinnueftirlitid. Audiometry programme required.

#### Paint shop building

800–1,200 m2, 9–10 m clear so a 6 m flat sprays standing. Sealed, coved, slip-resistant floor (DIN 51130 R11 wet areas), bunded, falls to interceptor.

**Why.** Paint at floor height on trestles is slower and worse than paint at height on a standing flat.

#### Large scenic spray booth

Walk-in/drive-in, 12 x 8 x 6 m internal. Standard: EN 16985:2019 (which superseded EN 12215, EN 13355 and EN 12981). Downdraught 0.3 m/s at the working plane; crossdraught to NFPA 33 needs >=0.5 m/s (100 ft/min) face velocity and atmosphere below 25% LEL.

**Why.** Overspray without containment coats the whole shop, and solvent vapour above 25% LEL is an explosion.

**Here.** Do the arithmetic: 12 x 8 m at 0.3 m/s = 96 m3/s = ~345,000 m3/h. Elsewhere the supply-air heater kills it. Here a geothermal water-to-air coil at 75–80 C makes full downdraught affordable — a genuine Icelandic capability no other studio has.

#### Spray booth filtration, fire and ATEX package

3-stage extract: paint-arrestor + bag + activated carbon for VOC. Supply air ISO 16890 ePM1 60% plus a ceiling plenum filter. Sprinkler or water-mist inside booth and ducts per NFPA 33 / EN 16985. Zone 1 inside during spraying, zone 2 around; AMCA 99 spark-resistant fans, motors out of airstream.

**Why.** Overspray in a duct is a fire; a sparking fan in a zone 1 atmosphere is a detonation.

**Here.** Ash events load the supply filters fast — specify differential-pressure monitoring and a slam-shut outside-air damper with a recirculation mode.

#### Paint mixing room

Separate EI 60/REI 60 compartment (EN 13501-2), >=10 ACH mechanical ventilation, sparkproof, bunded floor, ATEX zone 2 electrics, 0.1 g dispensing scales, gyroscopic shaker, tinting system.

**Why.** A tinting system in the open shop is a solvent-vapour hazard and a colour-accuracy failure.

#### Flammable liquids store and solvent recycler

External bunded store to 110% of largest vessel; EN 14470-1 Type 30/90 cabinets inside; est. 2,000–5,000 l of solvent-based product held. Distillation gun-wash recycler at 60–120 l/day. Enclosed recirculating gun-wash station with LEV.

**Why.** Uncontrolled solvent stock is the largest fire load and the largest waste cost on the lot.

**Here.** Hazardous waste must be EXPORTED for treatment under the Basel Convention and EEA rules — there is no domestic capacity for most solvent waste. That shipping and paperwork is a design constraint. Prefer water-based systems.

#### Paint effluent settlement and pH plant

Multi-chamber settlement interceptor plus pH correction before sewer discharge; solids to hazardous waste. Consent agreed with Veitur/Mosfellsbaer in advance.

**Why.** Pigment and solids discharged to sewer breach the trade-effluent consent and stop the shop.

**Here.** Iceland's sewer authority will not take pigment load, and there is no local sludge treatment route for paint solids.

#### Scenic artists' studio with full artificial daylight

300 m2, easels, wall mounting rails, 6 x 3 m light table. Overhead rig at 5,600 K, CRI >=95, TM-30 Rf >=90, 1,000 lux on the working face, flicker-free, DALI-2 dimming.

**Why.** Colour judgement under the wrong light produces a set that reads wrong on camera and cannot be fixed in grade.

**Here.** Reykjavik area gets about 4 h 07 m of daylight on 21 December and about 21 h in late June. North-light studios are a temperate-latitude idea. Here the rig IS the daylight for five months.

#### Plaster shop

400–600 m2, separate room and separate air from the mill and paint shop. Slab falls to a 3-chamber plaster settlement trap.

**Why.** Gypsum sets in drains and destroys them; plaster dust in a paint shop ruins finish; sawdust in wet plaster ruins the cast.

#### GRP / composites bay

Separate fire compartment, >=15 ACH forced ventilation, bench LEV, resin and gelcoat store, MEKP catalyst stored separately from any accelerator. Styrene monitoring against the current Icelandic Vinnueftirlitid limit list (UK WEL is 100 ppm 8h TWA / 250 ppm STEL — confirm the Icelandic value).

**Why.** MEKP and cobalt accelerator together detonate. Styrene vapour is the shop's main inhalation exposure.

**Here.** Chemical labelling must be in Icelandic under CLP national requirements; SDS in Icelandic and English.

#### Stone and concrete carving bay with RCS control

Wet cutting, on-tool extraction to EN 60335-2-69 class M/H, water suppression. EU binding limit for respirable crystalline silica 0.1 mg/m3 (8h TWA) under Directive 2017/2398.

**Why.** Silicosis is irreversible and the dust travels; dry-cutting a stone facade contaminates the whole workshop block.

**Here.** Icelandic basalt and local aggregate are used for real-stone dressing; crystalline silica content varies — assay before assuming it is low.

#### Metal shop and welding bays

600–900 m2 in its own fire compartment. 6–10 welding bays at 4 x 4 m, each with transparent screens to EN ISO 25980, a downdraught table and a 100 mm steel weld platen zone. MIG/MAG, TIG, MMA and pulse-MIG for aluminium.

**Why.** Hot work adjacent to wood dust is how a lot burns down; separation is not optional.

#### Welding fume extraction to EN ISO 21904

On-torch extraction 40–60 m3/h per torch, articulated arm hoods at 800–1,000 m3/h each, plus general push-pull. Filter class W3 for high-alloy per EN ISO 21904-1. Welding fume was classified IARC Group 1 (carcinogenic to humans) in 2017, Monograph Vol. 118 — including mild steel.

**Why.** In the EU welding fume is handled as a carcinogen, so LEV is a legal control, not a comfort measure.

**Here.** Do not vent to a recirculating heat-recovery loop to save energy. Heat is free here — vent once-through and take the geothermal makeup-air hit.

#### Hexavalent chromium control (stainless welding and grinding)

EU binding OEL for Cr(VI) under Directive 2017/2398 is 0.005 mg/m3 as 8h TWA, with a higher transitional value that applied to welding and plasma-cutting processes until 17 January 2025 (dates and the Icelandic transposition to be verified — ESTIMATE as stated).

**Why.** Cr(VI) is the specific carcinogen in stainless fume and the limit is an order of magnitude below general fume control.

**Here.** Confirm the Icelandic transposition through Vinnueftirlitid before writing the risk assessment; EEA transposition dates can lag the EU.

#### Welding gas manifold room and external gas cage

External ventilated cage, oxygen and fuel gases segregated, argon and CO2 manifolded and piped to the bays, flashback arrestors at both ends of every hose.

**Why.** Cylinders in the shop are a projectile hazard; loose hoses are the commonest hot-work fire.

**Here.** Anchor and roof the cage against wind. Industrial gases are available domestically (Linde/ISAGA) but specialty mixes are imported — hold deeper stock than a European lot.

#### Rigging engineering bench and proof-load test rig

Chain hoist servicing, load cells, a proof-test rig to 1.25 x SWL, register of thorough examinations. Hoists to EN 14492-2, suspended access to EN 1808.

**Why.** Every point that carries a person or a set piece needs a traceable test certificate, and the nearest test house is abroad.

**Here.** There is no local third-party load-test house at scale. In-house test capability is effectively mandatory rather than a convenience.

#### Prop store

2,000–4,000 m2 at 8 m, 3-tier mezzanine racking to EN 15635 with in-rack sprinklers, narrow-aisle order picker. Held at 18–22 C and 45–55% RH. Positive pressure relative to outside.

**Why.** Upholstery, leather, paper, books and timber furniture mould in a single damp winter.

**Here.** The Icelandic ambient is cool and DAMP. The cost here is dehumidification, not heat — the opposite of the assumption every temperate-climate spec makes. Positive pressure also keeps volcanic ash out.

#### Prop store category layout and asset register

Furniture, soft furnishings, drapes, practicals, ceramics/glass, kitchenalia, artificial greens, books and paper, pictures and frames, rugs, clocks, luggage, smalls, action props, period electricals. RFID/barcode register with condition photography.

**Why.** An unregistered prop store is a warehouse you cannot search, so buyers rebuy what you already own.

#### Set store (stock scenery)

3,000–5,000 m2 at 10 m, vertical A-frame bays for 6 m and 8 m flats, rostra, staircases, doors, windows, brick and stone sheet, columns, cornice. Telehandler or 5 t crane access. Tagged register with dimensions, finish and last credit.

**Why.** Reusing stock scenery is the single biggest cost and carbon saving on a lot; untagged stock is not reusable.

**Here.** Set reuse matters more here because replacing it means importing timber by sea. Material passports are logistics, not greenwash.

#### Set store fire protection

ESFR sprinklers to EN 12845 / NFPA 13, K360 heads, dedicated 500 m3 tank and a pump set to EN 12845 / NFPA 20, with freeze protection on tank and any dry sections.

**Why.** 5,000 m2 of racked timber flats is the largest single fire load on the lot.

**Here.** Water is abundant and cheap. But the tank, the pump house and every external main must be frost-protected and wind-rated — a frozen sprinkler main is no sprinkler main.

#### Costume department

1,200–1,800 m2 total, split into cutting, sewing, fitting, laundry, dye, breakdown, shoes/millinery, store and office. Positive pressure relative to outside.

**Why.** Wardrobe is the department most often squeezed and the one most exposed when a fitting runs late.

**Here.** Positive pressure keeps ash and damp out of the store.

#### Cutting room

4 x 12 m cutting tables at 1.6 m wide and 900 mm high. Overhead 5,600 K rig at CRI >=95, TM-30 Rf >=90, plus a switchable 3,200 K bank; 1,000 lux at the table.

**Why.** Fabric reads differently at 3,200 K and 5,600 K; you must judge it at the temperature it will be shot under.

**Here.** Daylight is unavailable for a third of the year. The artificial rig is the primary reference, not a backup for cloudy days.

#### Sewing room

20–30 stations: industrial lockstitch (Juki DDL-9000 class), 5-thread overlockers, coverstitch, blind hemmer, buttonholer, bartack, walking-foot for leather, plus 4 domestic-format machines for fine work. Task light >=1,000 lux at the needle (EN 12464-1 indicates 1,500 lux for very fine work). Chairs to EN 1335, sit-stand benches, anti-fatigue matting.

**Why.** Twenty people sitting ten hours a day; poor light and poor chairs are the department's injury profile.

#### Fitting rooms

8–12 rooms at 3 x 4 m minimum: 3-way full-length mirror, 300 mm fitting platform, lockable door, rail, chair, mid-grey (approx. N5) continuity wall, camera mount, power and data, individual temperature control. Lighting switchable 2,700/3,200/4,000/5,600 K, CRI >=95, from vertical soft sources at face and body height — not downlights.

**Why.** Downlights put an actor's face in shadow and make every fitting decision wrong.

**Here.** Geothermal underfloor heating at ~24 C surface temperature. Actors stand in underwear in a building where the outside is 2 C and the wind is 20 m/s; a cold fitting room shortens every fitting.

#### Costume store

>=2,000 m2 hanging on double-tier rolling rails at 16–18 C and 50 +/- 5% RH. Moth control by pheromone trapping plus the -30 C freezer chamber. Positive pressure.

**Why.** Moth and mould are the two ways a costume store is lost, and both are humidity-driven.

**Here.** Dehumidification, year-round, is the dominant plant load here — not heating. Every temperate-climate costume store spec gets this backwards.

#### Costume laundry

Washer-extractors at 25, 35, 60 and 110 kg; 4 tumble dryers; drying cabinet; flatwork ironer; 2 wet-cleaning machines; 1 hydrocarbon or siloxane dry-cleaning machine (not perc) with solvent store and vapour control to EN 12921. Capacity est. 1,400 kg/day; a big show generates 300–600 kg/day.

**Why.** Costume continuity depends on same-day turnaround; an undersized laundry forces duplicate builds of every costume.

**Here.** Three Iceland effects: (1) cold water is exceptionally soft, so detergent dosing drops sharply and fabrics last longer — a genuine advantage; (2) H2S means all hot water must be indirect via plate heat exchanger; (3) store metal trims and costume jewellery in sealed tarnish-inhibiting boxes.

#### Dye room

Separate tiled room, 4 stainless dye vats at 100–300 l, fume canopy at 2,500–4,000 m3/h, chemical store, scales to 0.01 g, pH neutralisation before discharge, effluent consent agreed with Veitur.

**Why.** Dye effluent and dye vapour cannot share a room or a drain with anything else.

**Here.** Specify an RO/DI plant (est. 500 l/h) for dye water and final rinses. Dyeing in H2S-bearing geothermal water shifts colour and is not viable. This is not optional — it is the reason to build the water plant at all.

#### Covered, wind-screened loading docks — every department

Level-access dock with a canopy, side wind screens, a heated air curtain, and dock levellers. One each to mill, paint, costume, props, set store and catering.

**Why.** Everything that crosses a dock — costume, backings, props, food — is damaged by rain.

**Here.** An uncovered costume load-out in driving rain ruins a day's work. Treat covered docks as a site-wide rule, not a per-building choice.

#### Hair and makeup rooms

12–20 makeup positions plus 6–10 hair positions, as rooms of 4, 6 and 8, plus 4 private single rooms for principals. Position footprint 2.4 x 2.0 m minimum — chair, mirror, bench, trolley and room to walk round.

**Why.** Under 2.4 m the artist works across the actor rather than around them, and the room jams at a crowd call.

#### Makeup chairs

Hydraulic, 5-star base, footrest, height 480–620 mm, removable headrest. Include 4 transfer-friendly/accessible chairs and 2 full-recline prosthetics chairs (dental-style) for lifecasting and appliance work.

**Why.** Appliance work on a standard chair wrecks the artist's back and takes twice as long.

#### Hair backwash stations with treated water

6–10 reclining backwash basins. Water: cold potable heated indirectly via plate heat exchanger, plus a point-of-use carbon/KDF filter at each basin.

**Why.** Geothermal water affects bleached and colour-treated hair and leaves the sulphur smell in it.

**Here.** Icelandic salons manage this daily — it is a known, practical problem, not a theoretical one. Any spec that plumbs district hot water to a backwash has failed.

#### HMU hygiene, sterilisation and ventilation

Towel washer/dryer, brush steriliser (barbicide/UV), autoclave for any skin-penetrating tools, clinical waste stream. Room ventilation 10–15 ACH with extract taken at BENCH level, plus local extract at each airbrush and hairspray position. Floor sealed and coved, slip resistance DIN 51130 R10.

**Why.** Powder and aerosol fall; a ceiling-only extract pulls it past every face in the room first. Aerosol propellant is LPG — a fire risk as well as an exposure.

**Here.** Salon hygiene is inspected by the local Heilbrigdiseftirlit (public health authority); registration applies to any hair/makeup operation.

#### Standby HMU room on the stage

30–40 m2 immediately adjacent to the Askja floor, INSIDE the acoustic envelope, 2 positions with the full lighting spec, plus a costume standby rail.

**Why.** A touch-up between takes cannot involve a 200 m walk.

**Here.** The governing Iceland adjacency rule: any people-space link that would be a covered walkway elsewhere must be an enclosed, heated, internal connection here. Actors in costume must never go outdoors.

#### Dressing rooms — supporting cast

12 rooms at 18–25 m2, each with ensuite shower/WC, mirror, wardrobe, sofa, desk, fridge, safe, 2.5 m of rail, ironing board and steamer, mid-grey continuity wall, independent HVAC at NR 25–30 so a line-learn or a self-tape is possible.

**Why.** An audible corridor makes the room useless for the thing actors actually do in it.

#### Background artist dressing rooms and holding

4 rooms at 60–80 m2 taking 20–30 each (est. 2.0–2.5 m2/head including rail), ventilated lockers, shower block; plus a 600 m2 indoor holding area for 300 SAs with seating, WCs, phone charging, tannoy and catering access.

**Why.** 300 background artists with nowhere to sit is an AD department's worst day and a safety problem.

**Here.** In California SA holding is a tent. Here it is a heated building with a boot room and a drying area. Non-negotiable.

#### Blackout, every sleeping and rest space

Motorised blackout blinds with side channels achieving >=99.9% occlusion in every dressing room, star suite, green room rest area, meeting room and production rest space.

**Why.** An actor on a 5 a.m. call cannot sleep in daylight, and a screen in a meeting room is unreadable.

**Here.** In June the sun in Mosfellsbaer sets around midnight and rises around 03:00 with civil twilight all night. Blackout is a functional requirement here, not a hotel nicety.

#### Green room

150–250 m2 adjacent to but acoustically separated from the stage (door lobby, NR 30). Soft seating for 40, tea point, commercial bean-to-cup with 2 grinders, fridges, water, a piano, script table, quiet corner, TV with video-village feed and a tannoy/talkback repeat.

**Why.** Without the talkback repeat the ADs spend the day walking to the green room to fetch people.

#### Drying rooms for wet outerwear

One per building: 40 m2 at 35–40 C with high air change, rails and boot dryers, sized for 100 sets of waterproofs and boots. Geothermal heated.

**Why.** Wet gear worn all day is the reason people go home ill.

**Here.** Almost never on a British or American spec and absolutely essential here. Crew arrive soaked. Free heat makes it cheap. It pays for itself in the first winter.

#### Boot wash and mud rooms at every external door

Grating, boot brush, hose, coat and boot store, walk-off matting at 6 m run minimum.

**Why.** Volcanic ash and Icelandic grit are abrasive and end up on the stage floor, in the camera and in the lens store.

**Here.** Ash is the reason this is not just housekeeping — abrasive fines tracked into a camera department cost lenses.

#### Locker rooms and showers

200 lockers per building with 12 showers, benches; gender-neutral and gendered options; WC provision est. 1 per 15 people plus urinals — roughly 60 WCs for a 900-person lot.

**Why.** Undersized WC and shower provision is the most common and most resented welfare failure on a lot.

#### Crew dining room

400 seats at 1.2–1.5 m2/seat over 2 sittings = 500–600 m2, plus 60-seat cast/VIP dining. Reverberation controlled to T <= 0.8 s (EN ISO 3382-2) with Class A absorbers.

**Why.** A 400-seat hard-surfaced dining room runs at 85 dB(A) of chatter and nobody uses it. Lot population est. 600–900 with a big unit running.

**Here.** Icelandic workplace norm is a subsidised hot lunch (motuneyti), not an American craft-service model. Design and price the dining room as a canteen everyone eats in, every day.

#### Production kitchen

350–450 m2 to Regulation (EC) 852/2004 with a documented HACCP plan, registered with the local Heilbrigdiseftirlit. 2 x 20-grid combi ovens, 2 x 120 l bratt pans, 2 x 150 l boiling pans, pass-through plus flight dishwasher (2,000 plates/h), 80 kg blast chiller, 30 m3 chiller at 2–4 C, 20 m3 freezer at -20 C, 60 m2 dry store, allergen-segregated prep, separate vegan line.

**Why.** Feeding 400 twice a day with allergen control is an industrial operation, not a canteen.

**Here.** Specify ALL-ELECTRIC INDUCTION. There is no piped natural gas in Iceland; the alternative is bottled LPG. Induction also removes the combustion load from the canopy and roughly halves the extract rate, which helps the constrained connection.

#### Kitchen canopy extraction and suppression

Sized to DW/172 (BESA) or VDI 2052 — est. 20,000–30,000 m3/h for 400 covers. Grease filters to EN 16282-6, UV or ozone treatment, stainless duct with access doors at 3 m. Fire suppression: wet chemical to UL 300 / EN 16282-7.

**Why.** A grease-duct fire in a building attached to the dining room is the classic catastrophic studio fire.

**Here.** Induction cooking cuts the required extract volume substantially, which is a direct electrical and thermal saving.

#### Kitchen goods-in, cold store depth and standby power

Covered level-access dock, walk-in chiller and freezer on generator-backed circuits, dry store sized for 2–3 weeks of stock rather than 3 days.

**Why.** A January storm power cut destroys a full cold store; a road closure empties a 3-day larder.

**Here.** Most food is imported via Reykjavik port on weekly sailings, and Route 1 and the Mosfellsheidi routes close in storms. Stock depth is a design parameter, not a purchasing preference.

#### Servery, allergen labelling and waste yard

4-lane servery: hot wells, salad bar, carvery, grill, deli, barista. Digital allergen labelling per Regulation (EU) 1169/2011. Waste yard with segregated food waste, cardboard, plastics, metals and hazardous streams, plus a baler and compactor.

**Why.** Allergen mislabelling is the highest-consequence catering risk on the lot.

**Here.** Source separation is required by Icelandic waste law. Food waste goes to SORPA's GAJA biogas and composting plant at Alfsnes — a genuinely good local route that most territories do not have.

#### Unit base hardstanding

15,000–20,000 m2 for 40–60 vehicles. 200 mm reinforced concrete with air entrainment at 4–6% and frost-resistant aggregate, graded and drained, with the snow-melt loops embedded.

**Why.** Trailers and 14 m artics on a failing surface sink, and a stuck truck blocks a load-out.

**Here.** Freeze-thaw destroys asphalt in Iceland. Air-entrained concrete or you resurface every ~5 years (estimate).

#### Unit base shore power pedestals

Bollards at 20 m spacing with 63 A and 125 A three-phase CEE outlets to IEC 60309 at 400 V 50 Hz, plus 32 A single-phase, RCD-protected, with per-outlet energy metering for chargeback.

**Why.** Without shore power every trailer runs a diesel generator, which is noise on a live stage, fuel cost and carbon.

**Here.** Est. 1 MW for 50 trailers at 20 kW average. On a capped connection this is a load-management problem: meter it, shed it, and back it with the battery.

#### Production office floor area

8–10 m2/person NIA open plan, 12–14 m2 cellular. A feature office is 60–140 people (1,000–1,800 m2); a large series 100–200. For 2–3 concurrent productions plus studio operations: 4,000–6,000 m2 NIA in separable, independently secured, lockable suites.

**Why.** Two productions on one lot must not see each other's schedules, budgets, scripts or dailies.

#### Production office suite composition

Per production: production (line producer, PM, 3 coordinators, 3 assistants, 4 PAs, 2 travel), accounts in a separately locked room (accountant, 1st, 2nd, 2 payroll), AD (1st, 2nd, 3rd, crowd, base), locations (manager, 2 assistants, map/pin room), costume, HMU, script supervisor, casting, stunts, VFX, editorial, transport, H&S, sustainability, publicity and stills with a lockable image server.

**Why.** Miss the accounts lock or the stills server and you have a leak, not an office.

#### Art department office

Production designer's office, supervising art director, 4 art directors, 6 draughtsmen at dual 32-inch colour-managed monitors, set decorator, 3 buyers, graphic designer, model-making bench, A0 plotters, and a BIM/virtual-art-department room with a 4K review wall. Lighting CRI >=90 at 500 lux, UGR <=19.

**Why.** The art department is the mill's input; a slow or colour-blind art department starves the CNC.

#### Office building services and envelope

EN 12464-1: 500 lux at desk, UGR <=19, CRI >=80 (>=90 colour-critical). Open plan to EN ISO 3382-3: distraction distance rD <=10 m, spatial decay D2,S >=7 dB. Fresh air >=10 l/s/person to EN 16798-1 Cat II — est. 29,000 m3/h at 800 people. Triple glazing Ug <=0.8 W/m2K.

**Why.** An open-plan production office with no acoustic design is unusable for the phone calls that are its entire function.

**Here.** Free cooling works essentially all year — Reykjavik-area annual mean is about 5 C and summer highs rarely exceed 20 C — so mechanical refrigeration is close to unnecessary. Heating is geothermal. Offices are the cheapest thing on this lot to run.

#### Wind- and rain-rated facade and glazing

Pressure-equalised rainscreen. Watertightness to EN 12208 class E1200 or better (beyond class 9/E750); wind resistance to EN 12210 class C5/E; air permeability EN 12207 class 4.

**Why.** Driven rain at 40 m/s finds every joint; Icelandic buildings fail at the window seals first.

**Here.** Standard European curtain-wall classes are not sufficient here. Specify above the top of the published class range and mock-up test.

#### Site data and connectivity

Dual-diverse fibre to the site, media-grade 100 Gbit/s+ link, Cat 6A to every desk, WiFi 6E/7, on-lot machine room with UPS.

**Why.** Dailies, VFX pulls and remote review all fail on a single link.

**Here.** Genuine Icelandic advantage: multiple submarine cables (FARICE-1, DANICE and IRIS, commissioned 2023). Latency to London est. ~20 ms, to New York est. ~40 ms. A production can genuinely cut and finish here — and cheap power plus free cooling make Iceland the obvious place for the render farm, except that the grid cap fights you. Size honestly.

#### Meeting rooms

Per suite: 1 x 20-person boardroom, 2 x 8-person, 4 x 4-person, 6 focus booths, plus a 60-person production meeting room with screen and lectern. All at Dw >=45 dB with 4K VC, beamforming mic array, eye-height camera and a whiteboard wall. Motorised blackout throughout.

**Why.** A 45 dB partition is the difference between a confidential casting conversation and a public one.

**Here.** Blackout is a screen-legibility requirement in June, not just a privacy one.

#### Nursing and family rooms

One per building: fridge, sink, lockable door, comfortable chair, changing table.

**Why.** Basic, cheap, and its absence is what people remember.

#### Medical centre

80–120 m2: treatment room with examination couch, consulting room, 2-bed recovery room, clean store, dirty utility, assisted-access WC, and a stretcher-capable route to a vehicle. Paramedic or nurse on every shooting day.

**Why.** The nearest emergency department is Landspitali Fossvogur in Reykjavik, est. 20–25 minutes by road.

**Here.** Road closure in a storm can extend that indefinitely. On-site clinical capability is a resilience measure here, not a convenience.

#### AEDs and first aid provision

AEDs to EN 60601-2-4 with self-test and remote monitoring, sited so no point is more than 3 minutes' round trip — at least 4 on the Askja perimeter and 2 on the gantries, plus one per building. Kits to DIN 13157/13169 or BS 8599-1, one per 25 people per area. Train 1 in 10 of the workforce.

**Why.** Survival from cardiac arrest falls roughly 10% per minute without defibrillation.

**Here.** Ambulance response to Mosfellsbaer is longer than to central Reykjavik, which raises the value of on-site AEDs and trained responders.

#### Emergency showers and eyewash

Plumbed emergency showers to EN 15154-1 (>=60 l/min for 15 min) in paint, plaster, dye and metal shops; plumbed eyewash to EN 15154-2 and portable units to EN 15154-4 within 10 seconds' reach of any chemical hazard. Tepid water supply.

**Why.** A 15-minute shower in cold water will not be completed, which defeats the whole control.

**Here.** Tepid water is easy and free here via the heat exchangers — but every external or unheated run must be trace-heated or the shower is frozen when it is needed.

#### Burns kits and shop-specific response

Burns kits in the mill, welding shop, paint shop and kitchen; spill kits matched to each shop's inventory; a documented CLP/REACH chemical inventory with SDS in Icelandic and English.

**Why.** A generic first aid kit does nothing for a 200 C powder-coat oven burn or a 4 bar resin pot failure.

**Here.** Icelandic CLP labelling requirements apply — labels and SDS in Icelandic.

#### Occupational health surveillance programme

Audiometry (Directive 2003/10/EC, action 80/85 dB(A), limit 87), spirometry and health surveillance for wood dust, welding fume, Cr(VI), silica, solvents and isocyanates. RPE face-fit testing for EN 149 FFP3 and EN 12941 powered hoods.

**Why.** Health surveillance is the only way to find sensitisation before it ends someone's career.

**Here.** Diisocyanate users (2-pack paints and adhesives) have required documented training under the REACH restriction since 24 August 2023 — this applies in the EEA, so it applies in Iceland.

#### Volcanic ash protocol and air intake protection

Motorised slam-shut outside-air dampers with a recirculation mode on every AHU; final filters ISO 16890 ePM1 >=80% with ePM10 >=70% pre-filters and a coarse bag; differential-pressure monitoring; cleanable inertial pre-separator at intakes; intakes sited high and on the lee side. Positive pressure held in costume store, prop store, HMU and camera stores. FFP3 stock for the whole workforce.

**Why.** Volcanic ash is fine, abrasive and conductive when wet. It contaminates a costume store, a prop store and a makeup room permanently.

**Here.** Standing ash procedure tied to Vedurstofa Islands (Met Office) and Almannavarnir (civil protection) warnings: close dampers, recirculate, mask up, seal camera and lens stores, stop outdoor work.

#### Storm and civil protection plan

Written plan tied to Vedurstofa Islands orange/red warnings and Almannavarnir alerts: road closure triggers, shoot stand-down rule, seismic protocol, ash protocol, and a pre-agreed decision authority. Indoor refuge muster points designated as an alternative to open ground.

**Why.** An outdoor muster point in a red-warning storm is more dangerous than the incident being evacuated from.

**Here.** Iceland loses working days to weather. A best-in-class lot prices that into the schedule and publishes the rule, rather than arguing about it at 6 a.m.

#### Fire strategy and works fire team

Fire main with hydrants at <=90 m spacing, dry risers to every gantry, EI/REI 60 compartmentation between each workshop, a pre-plan agreed with Slokkvilid hofudborgarsvaedisins, and a trained works fire team — or an on-site tender.

**Why.** A mill full of wood dust, a set store of racked timber and a paint shop of solvent are three separate large fire loads on one site.

**Here.** Est. 8–12 minute attendance to Mosfellsbaer (verify with SHS). That response time is the argument for containment, on-site capability and compartmentation over detect-and-wait.

#### Central stores and consumables

600 m2 bin racking with a counter and a stock system: fixings, abrasives, tapes (gaffer, camera, spike), rope, chain, cable ties, sheeting, adhesives, PPE.

**Why.** A mill that runs out of 50 mm screws stops.

#### Expendables store, deep-stocked

Grip and electrical expendables: gels, diffusion, blackwrap, clamps, sandbags, lamps. Stock depth: 3 months, not 3 days. Est. 400–600 m2 with a counter and a reorder trigger tied to sea-freight lead time.

**Why.** A production that cannot get diffusion on Tuesday shoots something else on Tuesday.

**Here.** The single biggest workshop-operations difference Iceland forces. A UK or US lot orders overnight; here it is 7–10 days by sea or expensive air freight. Run a much larger stores operation than an equivalent UK lot.

#### Segregated chemical store

Segregation matrix for acids, alkalis, oxidisers, flammables and toxics; bunded shelving; ventilated; inventory to REACH and CLP with SDS in Icelandic and English.

**Why.** MEKP next to accelerator, or acid next to hypochlorite, is a straightforward way to make chlorine gas or a detonation.

#### Battery store and Li-ion fire compartment

Dedicated fire compartment for camera, lighting and tool batteries with aerosol or water-mist suppression, thermal monitoring, cell-level state-of-health logging, and an outdoor quarantine cage for damaged packs.

**Why.** Lithium packs are now one of the largest fire risks on any lot and thermal runaway is not extinguishable by conventional means.

**Here.** With an est. 8–12 minute fire response, containment and early detection carry the whole load.

#### Multilingual safety signage and documentation

All safety signage, method statements, SDS, inductions and permit systems in Icelandic and English, with Polish where the construction workforce warrants it. Signage to ISO 7010.

**Why.** Safety information a worker cannot read is not safety information, and Icelandic labour law requires it be comprehensible to the worker.

**Here.** Polish speakers are the largest immigrant group in Icelandic construction. A trilingual safety regime on the mill and site is realistic, not box-ticking.

#### Integrated access control and one manned control room

Single control room, mobile credentials, turnstiles, ANPR at the gate, visitor management with NDA capture, phone lockers for secure productions, and an air-gapped MPA-CSP-compliant secure media handling room for dailies.

**Why.** Four separately manned lobbies is four shifts you cannot staff.

**Here.** The small labour pool means security must be designed to be run by few people. Automate the perimeter and concentrate the humans.

#### Working hours and rostering model

Design rosters and welfare provision against a 36-hour norm rather than 40. Iceland's 2015–19 trials cut public-sector weeks to 35–36 hours at full pay; by June 2021, 170,200 of 196,700 union members were covered by shorter-hours contracts or mechanisms to negotiate them (BSRB: 36 h standard, 32 h for shift workers).

**Why.** Crew room, locker, dining and welfare sizing all follow from how many shifts you run, and a 36-hour norm changes the headcount per shift.

**Here.** A film unit's long-day culture collides directly with the Icelandic working-time norm. Settle this with the unions before you size the building, because it changes the answer.

### Expected

#### 5-axis CNC router, gantry

3,100 x 6,200 mm bed minimum, 12–15 kW HSD spindle, 24-position ATC, 10-zone vacuum bed, 5-axis head. AXYZ / Anderson / Biesse class.

**Why.** Full 10 x 5 ft sheets and 20 ft scenic runs in one setup; without 5-axis every compound-angle moulding is hand-cut.

**Here.** Service engineers fly in. Carry a critical spares kit (spindle, drives, ATC) — a 10-day wait for a spindle stops the mill.

#### 3-axis nesting CNC router (second machine)

3,100 x 2,100 mm, 9 kW, vacuum bed, auto tool change, nesting software post-processed from the art department's Rhino/Revit models.

**Why.** Sheet-goods throughput. One machine means the 5-axis does nesting work and the sculpted work queues behind it.

#### Four-sided moulder

5–6 head, 230 mm x 120 mm capacity, Weinig Powermat class, with a tooling grinder and profile store.

**Why.** Period cornice, architrave, skirting, glazing bar. Buying mouldings in is a 3-week lead time from Europe.

**Here.** Makes you self-sufficient in profiles. Buying-in means sea freight and a fortnight, so the moulder is worth more here than in the UK.

#### Wide-belt sander and edge sander

1,350 mm two-head wide belt (calibrating + finishing), plus a 2.6 m edge sander with dust hood.

**Why.** Paint-ready surfaces. Hand-sanding 300 flats is not a plan.

#### Mortiser, tenoner, dowel machine and wood lathe

Chain/chisel mortiser, single-end tenoner, line boring machine, 2 m bed pattern lathe with 400 mm swing plus a copying attachment.

**Why.** Balusters, columns, joinery-grade doors and windows for hero sets that hold up in close-up.

#### Chip silo and briquetting press

60–100 m3 external silo, ATEX rotary valve, level indication, heated cone; briquetter at 60–100 kg/h.

**Why.** Without a silo the mill floods with chip by Wednesday.

**Here.** Wood-waste burners, standard in UK/US mills, make no economic sense here — there is no fuel-cost case against free geothermal. Briquette and send to offtake. Heat the cone: damp chip freezes and bridges.

#### Mill overhead crane and jibs

5 t SWL double-girder to EN 15011, full-span, plus 2 x 1 t jib cranes at the CNC and the assembly bench. Annual thorough examination.

**Why.** Set pieces leave the mill as 2–4 t assemblies; without a crane they leave as kits and get built on stage at stage rates.

#### Mill drawing office and BIM station

Construction manager and HOD offices, 2 x A0 plotters, 3D scanning rig, CAM post-processing station, a 4K review wall. Cat 6A and colour-managed monitors.

**Why.** Set drawings arrive as Rhino/Revit; somebody must turn them into toolpaths and cutting lists or the CNC idles.

#### Small prop/downdraught booths

4 x 3 x 3 m downdraught booth with 1.5 m turntable, plus 2 x bench-top downdraught tables (2 x 1 m) for touch-in and airbrush.

**Why.** Sending a lamp base through the 345,000 m3/h booth is absurd; small work needs small containment.

#### Drying and curing room, paint

200 m2 at 40 C, forced air, racking for flats and props, geothermal heated.

**Why.** Rushed paint is the commonest cause of a set failing on camera.

**Here.** Free heat means you can afford a genuinely large drying room and stop rushing cure times. This is a real quality advantage.

#### Texture, ageing and distressing bay

Enclosed, heated, 300 m2, washdown slab with drain interceptor, high-pressure water, sandblast cabinet, dirt/mud tank, airbrush stations with LEV.

**Why.** Ageing and dirtying is wet, filthy work that cannot share air or floor with clean paint.

**Here.** Everywhere else this is a yard. Here it must be an enclosed heated building — nobody ages a set in a 25 m/s crosswind with horizontal rain, which is a normal Tuesday.

#### Graphics, signage and large-format print bay

3.2 m flatbed UV printer, vinyl cutter, laminator, silk-screen bed, sign-writing bench, letterpress for period work.

**Why.** Every set has 50 pieces of graphic dressing; outsourcing them means a week's lead time per change.

**Here.** Outsourcing to Europe is 7–10 days by sea or expensive air freight. In-house graphics is disproportionately valuable here.

#### Running bench, moulding tables and fibrous plaster bench

6 m running bench with running mould and horse; 2 x 4 x 2 m moulding tables; fibrous plaster bench for lath-and-canvas cornice and ceiling roses; 200 l paddle mixers.

**Why.** Cornice, roses and enrichments are still cast, not carved; without the bench every period ceiling is bought in.

#### Mould-making bay — silicone and rubber

50 l vacuum degassing chamber, 40 l pressure pot at 4 bar, platinum- and tin-cure silicone store, LEV at bench, temperature-controlled store (silicone shelf life is temperature-sensitive).

**Why.** Bubbles in a mould become bubbles in every cast off it.

**Here.** Silicone and catalyst ship as regulated goods; lead time from Europe is 2–3 weeks. Hold deeper stock and watch shelf life.

#### Vacuum former

2,400 x 1,200 mm platen, twin quartz heaters, for brick/stone sheet, panelling and scenic mouldings. Shared with props and armour.

**Why.** Repeat scenic texture at scale — 400 m2 of brick sheet is not hand-sculpted.

#### CNC plasma cutting table

4 x 2 m, 130 A, downdraught water table with fume extraction and slag removal, with a CAM link from the art department.

**Why.** Structural and scenic steelwork cut to drawing rather than by eye.

#### Sheet metal forming line

135 t x 3,100 mm CNC press brake with 6-axis backgauge; 3,100 x 6 mm guillotine; section rolls; plate rolls; 50 mm mandrel tube bender; 90–120 t ironworker.

**Why.** Set steelwork, handrail, ducting and effects mechanisms all start as flat sheet or straight tube.

#### Machine shop

1,500 mm centre lathe; CNC vertical machining centre 1,000 x 500 mm; double-mitre 350 mm bandsaw; cold saw; pedestal drill, linisher, pedestal grinder, tapping arm.

**Why.** Rigging fittings, effects mechanisms and one-off engineering parts. Every alternative is a week's freight.

**Here.** With everything imported, the ability to make a part on a Tuesday afternoon rather than order it is worth far more here than on a UK lot.

#### Powder coat and metal finishing bay

2 x 2 x 4 m powder booth with cyclone recovery, curing oven to 200 C, phosphate/etch pre-treatment bay with bunded drainage and effluent treatment.

**Why.** Durable finish on anything that will be handled or stand outdoors; wet paint on steel props chips in a week.

**Here.** Curing oven is one of the few loads that cannot use geothermal (200 C is above the district-heat temperature) — it is electric, so it counts against the constrained connection.

#### Special effects workshop

300 m2 with hydraulics, pneumatics, machining, electronics and a wet bay. Rain bars, snow machines, Ritter-class 1.8 m wind fans, smoke and haze machines, fluid store with LEV.

**Why.** Effects rigs are built, tested and maintained, not hired; a failed rig on stage costs a shoot day.

**Here.** Iceland is the one place you can shoot real wind and rain for free — but you still need controllable, repeatable effects indoors for continuity.

#### Pyrotechnic magazine

Bonded steel magazine at the separation distance required by Icelandic explosives regulation (est. 30–50 m from occupied buildings — verify with the authorities), licensed storekeeper, inventory control, lightning protection to EN 62305.

**Why.** Unlicensed or unseparated pyro storage closes the lot.

**Here.** Lightning risk is low in Iceland but wind is extreme — anchoring, not lightning, is the governing load. Get the licence via the local police and fire authority before you build.

#### Armoury

Separate secure room, safe to EN 1143-1, ammunition and blank store separated, CCTV, access log. Iceland's Firearms Act No. 16/1998 governs; police liaison required for prop and blank-firing weapons.

**Why.** Prop weapons crossing an international border into Iceland without prior clearance are seized.

**Here.** Icelandic firearms law is strict and there is no informal route. Build the compliance path into pre-production, not into the shooting week.

#### Prop quarantine, disinfection and fumigation room

Sealed room with its own extract, disinfection station, and a walk-in freezer chamber (-30 C, 20 m3) for pest kill — 72 h at -30 C kills all moth and beetle life stages.

**Why.** One infested chair introduces moth to a 4,000 m2 store and you lose the soft furnishings.

**Here.** Not optional here: MAST (the Icelandic Food and Veterinary Authority) enforces genuinely strict biosecurity. Used riding tack, hay, straw and untreated timber need disinfection certificates on entry. This room is a customs requirement, not housekeeping.

#### Covered build and pre-rig yard

2,000 m2 under a light roof, open-sided with 6 m windbreak fencing at ~50% porosity, heated slab, drainage, 10 t crane coverage.

**Why.** Set assembly and pre-rigging need to happen somewhere that is not a live stage and not a storm.

**Here.** Porous windbreak (50%) is more effective than a solid wall, which generates lee turbulence. Standard agricultural practice, directly applicable.

#### Backing and translight store

Climate-controlled at 16–20 C / 45–55% RH, 12 m and 30 m motorised storage drums, no folding.

**Why.** Painted backings crack if they freeze and go slack if damp; a folded translight shows the crease on camera forever.

**Here.** An unheated container store here will freeze a backing and destroy it in one night.

#### Anchored external container storage

20 and 40 ft ISO containers on hardstanding with cast-in anchor eyes, 4 x 5 t chain per unit, ground-level access.

**Why.** Overflow storage and per-production lock-ups.

**Here.** Containers do blow over in Iceland. Anchoring is a real calculation, not a precaution: a 40 ft box presents ~30 m2 of side area.

#### Star fitting suites

2 rooms at 6 x 5 m, private entrance, ensuite WC, sofa, the full fitting lighting spec, and a discreet approach not visible from the general lot.

**Why.** A lead being fitted in a shared corridor is a dignity and a security problem.

#### Crowd fitting hall

400 m2, 20 changing cubicles, 300 m of rail run, sizing bay, a photo bay with a fixed camera and grey wall, and a sign-in desk. Capacity: fit 200 supporting artists in a day.

**Why.** Fitting 200 SAs through a normal wardrobe stops the department for a week.

**Here.** Needs its own coat and boot store and a drying area — 200 people arriving wet is a normal fitting day.

#### Costume breakdown and ageing bay

Covered washdown bay with interceptor, sandblast cabinet, airbrush station with LEV, dirt and mud tanks, and a fire-retardant spray bay. FR treatment to EN 13501-1 / NFPA 701 for anything near a flame effect.

**Why.** Breakdown is filthy and solvent-heavy; doing it in the sewing room contaminates every clean costume.

**Here.** Must be indoors and heated, like the scenic ageing bay, for the same reason.

#### Shoes, millinery, leather and armour bays

Cobbler's bench with lasts, stretchers, sole press and leather sewing machine; millinery with steamers, blocks and individually boxed hat store; leather and rivet bench sharing the vacuum former.

**Why.** Period footwear and hats cannot be bought; every one is made or altered.

#### Hair colour bay

Separate room with an LEV canopy over the colour bar, scales to 0.1 g, colour store, and 5,600 K CRI >=95 lighting for colour judgement.

**Why.** Bleach and ammonia vapour in a shared room is an exposure and a headache generator.

#### Wig room

Knotting stations with magnifiers at 1,500 lux, wig blocks, wig ovens at 60–80 C, blocked-head store at 45–55% RH, ventilating benches, steam supply.

**Why.** A wig dressed in the wrong humidity drops its set before the first take.

**Here.** Wig ovens and the drying cabinet run on geothermal — free heat, so run them continuously rather than batching.

#### Heated smoking shelter, bike store and sheltered terrace

Enclosed-on-three-sides heated smoking shelter; 60-space covered heated-floor bike store with e-bike charging; a glass-screened, geothermally heated outdoor terrace off the dining room.

**Why.** Without a shelter people smoke in a stage doorway. Without a terrace nobody gets outside at all in winter.

**Here.** Iceland's Tobacco Act bans indoor workplace smoking. Icelanders use outdoor space readily when it is sheltered and heated — a heated terrace gets used in February.

#### Craft services points

4 stations near the stages at 15 m2 each: hot water, commercial coffee, fridge, snacks, and a permanent soup station.

**Why.** A crew that has to cross the lot for a coffee loses ten minutes a head per break.

**Here.** The soup station is the Icelandic detail — hot soup available all day through the winter does more for a crew than anything else on the trolley.

#### Artist trailers, arctic-specified and anchored

12–20 double- and triple-banger units, 12–14 m, each room with shower/WC, makeup mirror, sofa bed, HVAC, 32 A shore power. Arctic insulation pack, double glazing, heated waste tanks and water lines. Ground anchors or ballast: a 14 m trailer presents ~40 m2 of side area; at ~1 kPa that is ~40 kN lateral.

**Why.** A standard US trailer freezes its waste tank solid in December and moves in a gale.

**Here.** Chocks are not anchorage. Specify cast-in ground anchors at every bay and chain every unit.

#### Permanent unit base welfare block

400 m2 single storey: WCs, showers, warm-up room, drying room, lockers, tea point. Standing, not trailered.

**Why.** Crew welfare must not depend on a trailer that may be off on a location or frozen.

**Here.** The correct Icelandic answer to a trailer-based welfare model.

#### Transport workshop and heated truck garage

Heated garage for 10 vehicles, inspection pit, tyre bay with winter/studded tyre changeover, jump-start and block-heater points, plus a vehicle wash bay with interceptor and heated water.

**Why.** A diesel that has sat at -15 C in a gale does not start, and a 6 a.m. call does not wait.

**Here.** Studded or winter tyres are legally required in season. Ash plus road salt together corrode vehicles fast, so the wash bay is maintenance, not cosmetics.

#### Fuel store and generator provision

20,000 l double-skinned bunded HVO/diesel tank to EN 12285 with a 110% bund and card-controlled dispenser. Any generators HVO-fuelled with acoustic enclosures at <=65 dB(A) at 7 m, mandated by studio rule.

**Why.** An unsilenced generator on the unit base ruins sound recording on the adjacent stage.

**Here.** All liquid fuel is imported. HVO is available but at a premium — the gensets exist for storm resilience and peak shaving, not for daily running.

#### Rehearsal and stunt rehearsal room

200–300 m2, sprung floor, 6 m clear height, mirrors on one wall with draw curtains, piano, PA, and a rigging grid with 5 t points plus crash mat storage.

**Why.** Stunt and fight rehearsal on a live stage costs stage time at stage rates.

#### Child performer facilities

Schoolroom/tutor room at 25 m2 with desks and whiteboard, a rest room, a chaperone office, and a separate child waiting area at casting.

**Why.** Child working hours and tuition requirements are statutory and the schedule is built around them.

**Here.** Icelandic law and the collective agreements set child working hours — check with the union and the labour inspectorate before scheduling, not after.

#### Stables and animal facilities

12-box stable with vet room, feed store, muck store and a paddock, plus a wrangler's office.

**Why.** Productions will bring animals into the schedule whether or not you planned for it.

**Here.** A hard constraint: MAST's import rules are among the world's strictest, and an Icelandic horse that leaves the country can never return — the population is closed. A production must use Icelandic horses and cannot bring its own. Build for that.

#### PPE store and 24/7 vending

Industrial PPE vending with usage tracking, full size range held deep, including FFP3, powered hoods, welding PPE, and cold-and-wet-weather outerwear for the whole workforce.

**Why.** PPE that is not available at 6 a.m. is PPE that is not worn.

**Here.** Everything is imported, so order depth is the whole problem. Cold-and-wet outerwear is site-issued PPE here, not a personal item.

#### Waste and recycling centre

Segregated bays, baler, compactor, WEEE, hazardous waste, and a scenic-waste sorting bay so set strike is reclaimed rather than landfilled.

**Why.** Set strike is the largest single waste stream a studio produces.

**Here.** Landfill capacity is constrained and hazardous waste must be exported under Basel. Designing for reuse and material passports is logistics here, not marketing.

#### Tool crib, calibration register and gas detection

Controlled tool issue; calibration register for torque tools, load cells and gas detectors; portable multigas detectors for confined-space and tank work (relevant to Lon's 6 m dive well and any plant room).

**Why.** An out-of-calibration load cell or gas detector gives false assurance, which is worse than none.

**Here.** H2S is naturally present in Icelandic geothermal infrastructure. Any plant room, pit, sump or valve chamber on the district-heat side is a potential H2S confined space. Fixed and portable H2S detection is an Iceland-specific requirement most studio specs would never include.

### State of the art

#### Enclosed scenic transport link (scene dock corridor), mill to stage

12 m wide x 9 m clear, heated, fully enclosed, level power-floated slab, 4-leaf sliding doors each end, 10 t floor loading. Not a covered walkway — a sealed building link.

**Why.** A 6 x 3 m flat is an 18 m2 sail. Moving scenery outdoors is impossible on a large share of days; without the link you lose set-up days to weather.

**Here.** The defining Iceland item in this domain. Telehandler/crane makers typically stop large-area lifts at ~9–12 m/s wind (verify per machine); Mosfellsbaer exceeds that routinely. No Californian lot builds this; here it is mandatory.

#### Geothermal snow-melt (snjobraedsla) to all hardstanding and pedestrian routes

PEX loops at 250–300 mm centres under ~20,000 m2 of unit base, docks, stage aprons and walkways. Design output 200–300 W/m2 (est.), i.e. 4–6 MW thermal, fed from ~35 C district-heat RETURN water.

**Why.** Snow clearance failure loses shoot days, blocks trailers and docks, and is the main slip-injury source on a winter lot.

**Here.** Reykjavik already runs pavement snow-melt off district-heat return. It uses heat you would otherwise throw away, so the marginal cost is near zero. Unthinkable anywhere else, near-free here.

#### 5-axis foam/EPS carving cell with hot wire

4 x 2 x 2 m envelope, hot-wire plus router head, dedicated non-sparking extraction, antistatic ducting.

**Why.** Sculpted scenic, rock, cornice and hero props. EPS dust is combustible and will not behave in a wood-dust system.

#### Paint frame (counterweighted scenic frame)

12 x 8 m working face, motorised or counterweighted, running in an 8 m deep pit so a backdrop passes through the floor. Edge protection to EN ISO 14122-3, fall arrest at the pit.

**Why.** A 12 m backcloth painted flat on the floor cannot be judged; the frame brings any part of it to bench height.

**Here.** Pit needs tanking and a sump pump — the water table and the driven rain will find it.

#### Colour-matching booth, dual-standard

ISO 3668 / BS 950 Pt 1 viewing at D65 6,500 K with 5,000 K alternative, CRI >=90; PLUS a cine-referenced bank at 3,200 K tungsten and 5,600 K daylight, CRI >=95, TM-30 Rf >=90.

**Why.** Scenic paint is matched to what the camera sees, not to a paint-trade standard. A match that is right at D65 can be wrong at 3,200 K.

**Here.** No usable daylight reference for five months. The artificial rig is the primary reference here, not the check.

#### Sculpting studio and digital fabrication

Clay benches, armature bench, turntables; structured-light 3D scanner at +/-0.1 mm; FDM/SLA print farm to 1 m3 total build volume; 6-axis robot arm on a 3 x 2 m bed for foam carving.

**Why.** Hero props and creature work now round-trip between hand sculpt and digital; without the scanner you cannot scale or mirror a sculpt.

#### Fibre laser cutter

3 kW, 3 x 1.5 m bed, nitrogen/oxygen assist, enclosed class 1 to EN 60825-1.

**Why.** Cleaner, quieter, more accurate than plasma, and it cuts thin sheet plasma cannot.

**Here.** Assist gas (nitrogen, oxygen) is available domestically through the industrial gas supplier, but specialty mixes have lead time — manifold and hold stock.

#### Practicals workshop with dual-frequency supply

Rewiring bench for period lamps; test to EN 50678 / EN 50699. Iceland is 230 V 50 Hz, TN-C-S, CEE 7/4 Schuko. Provide a 120 V transformer supply AND a 60 Hz frequency-converted supply (est. 30–50 kVA) for US-brought practicals and motor-driven props.

**Why.** A US practical or a synchronous prop motor on 50 Hz runs at 5/6 speed or burns out; the timing of any mechanical prop shifts.

**Here.** A genuinely Icelandic gotcha. US productions arrive with 120 V/60 Hz kit and nobody thinks about it until the clock on the hero set runs slow on camera.

#### Geothermal greens glasshouse

500 m2 heated glasshouse with supplementary HPS/LED grow lighting, for live picture plants and greens, plus a separate artificial greens store.

**Why.** Live greens on a set need to arrive healthy; shipped plants arrive dead or quarantined.

**Here.** This is exactly what Icelandic horticulture already does — geothermal-heated, artificially-lit glasshouses growing year-round (Fridheimar and others). Free heat and cheap light make an on-lot greens house uniquely cheap here.

#### Standing sets — build strategy

Three honest options: (a) scenic standing sets INSIDE a covered unlit structure; (b) a permanent street built in real construction — structural steel, rendered blockwork facades, engineered to the full IST EN 1991-1-4 wind case; (c) demolish-and-rebuild per production. Recommend (b) for a permanent street, (a) for anything scenic.

**Why.** A plywood-and-plaster facade on an Icelandic backlot is destroyed in its first winter.

**Here.** The core Iceland finding for set storage. A Californian backlot is simply not buildable here. Iceland's basic wind velocity under IST EN 1991-1-4:2005/NA:2010 is high — a default figure of 36.0 m/s appears in Dlubal's tool for the Icelandic annex (indicative, verify per site).

#### Pressing stations on live geothermal steam

4 stations with vacuum boards and gravity-feed irons, plus garment steamers, fed from the geothermal system via a clean steam generator or an indirect steam raiser.

**Why.** Pressing is continuous and steam demand is high; an electric boiler adds load to a constrained connection.

**Here.** One of the cleanest geothermal wins on the lot — but raise clean steam indirectly. Never put H2S-bearing geothermal steam onto costume.

#### Makeup mirror lighting specification

1.0–1.2 m mirror, lamps on three sides. Switchable 2,700/3,200/4,300/5,600 K. CRI Ra >=96, TM-30-20 Rf >=93 and Rg 98–102, and critically R9 (saturated red) >=90. 800–1,200 lux vertical at the face from broad sources at +/-45 deg at eye height, negligible downlight. Flicker <1% at all dim levels, no PWM below 3 kHz. DALI-2/DMX.

**Why.** Skin tone is red-dominant. A lamp with good Ra and poor R9 makes every makeup decision wrong, and it is only visible on camera.

**Here.** The whole department works in darkness from November to February. There is no window to sanity-check against, so the spec has to be right.

#### Makeup bench and dual-voltage power

800 mm deep Corian or laminate bench, sink every 2–3 positions, drawers. Per position: 4 x 230 V 50 Hz Schuko plus 2 x 120 V 60 Hz outlets on the frequency-converted supply.

**Why.** US-brought irons, clippers and airbrush compressors run hot, slow or not at all on 230 V 50 Hz.

**Here.** Same gotcha as prop practicals, and it bites harder here because HMU tools arrive in every US kit.

#### Prosthetics laboratory

Lifecast room with shower and plumbed eyewash; mould room; silicone and foam-latex lab with planetary mixer; curing oven (foam latex ~70 C for 2–3 h); vacuum degassing chamber; downdraught airbrush bay; adhesive and remover store (Pros-Aide, Telesis, isopropyl myristate) with LEV.

**Why.** Prosthetic adhesives and their removers are the department's main inhalation risk, and lifecasting needs a shower within reach.

**Here.** Foam latex curing at 70 C sits exactly in the geothermal temperature band — a rare case where a process oven can run on district heat instead of electricity, which matters on a capped connection.

#### Star suites

4 suites at 80–120 m2: private lobby, lounge, rest room with full-size bed, ensuite with bath and rain shower, kitchenette, private makeup position to full spec, desk and broadcast-grade VC, acoustic isolation >=50 dB Dw from neighbours and corridor, private terrace, discreet vehicle drop.

**Why.** Sleep between a 5 a.m. call and a night shoot is the actual function; everything else is decoration.

**Here.** Terraces need glass wind screens and a geothermal-heated floor to be usable at all.

#### Circadian and bright-light provision, winter

Bright-light panels at 10,000 lux at 400 mm, 4,000–6,500 K, in the green room, each star suite, the crew rest areas and the dining room; dining-room scheme reaching 750–1,000 lux at table at 6,500 K in the morning.

**Why.** A crew that arrives in the dark, works under a stage roof and leaves in the dark for three months has a measurable morale, error-rate and retention problem.

**Here.** About 4 h 07 m of daylight on 21 December in the Reykjavik area. Pair with vitamin D provision, flexible winter start times and an explicit SAD policy. A best-in-class Icelandic lot treats light as an occupational health control.

#### Crew sauna and hot pots

12-person sauna, 2 hot pots at 38 and 40 C, cold plunge, changing and shower block. Geothermal fed via heat exchanger, treated and monitored to Icelandic pool hygiene rules.

**Why.** Recovery facility for a physically hard job, and the highest-value morale investment per krona on the lot.

**Here.** Not a joke. It is what an Icelandic workforce expects, running costs are near zero on geothermal, and with a small labour pool the lot competes on conditions, not only on pay.

#### Permanent serviced trailer bays instead of honeywagons

Mains water, mains foul drainage, mains power and data at every trailer bay. Retain only 2 honeywagons (6–10 bay, WC/urinal/basin, heated tanks) for location work off the lot.

**Why.** Tanked honeywagons on a permanent lot are more expensive, less reliable and worse for crew than plumbed bays.

**Here.** It also removes the frozen-tank failure mode entirely, which is the honeywagon's characteristic Icelandic failure. This is a real design change from the US template.

#### Anemometers and a wind-decision rule

Anemometers at the yard, at each stage door and at the unit base, logged, with alarms and a documented threshold for stopping lifts, door operation, working at height and scenery moves.

**Why.** Without an agreed number, the decision to stop is made by whoever is most tired.

**Here.** Manufacturers commonly stop large-area telehandler and crane lifts around 9–12.5 m/s (verify per machine). Mosfellsbaer exceeds that often enough that the rule must be automatic and pre-agreed, not negotiated on the day.

#### Casting suites

2–3 suites. Each: separate cast entrance and waiting area, sign-in desk, casting studio 6 x 8 m x 4 m with mid-grey cyclorama or clean backing, silent key/fill rig at 5,600 K CRI >=95, room at NR 20 and RT60 <=0.4 s, locked-off 4K camera and reader's chair, an observation/control room with monitor and talkback, plus a self-tape booth.

**Why.** A casting tape with room slap or a noisy fan is unusable, and a cast queue past the production office is a security breach.

**Here.** Waiting area needs a coat and boot store and a drying area — sixty actors arriving wet is a normal audition day.

#### Screening room and post suites

40–60 seat DCI theatre calibrated to SMPTE ST 2080 / ISO 26431 at 14 fL (48 cd/m2) 2D, Dolby Atmos or 7.1, RT60 0.25 s, NC-20. Plus a 12-seat rushes theatre, 4 edit suites at 30 m2 and Dw >=55 dB, a sound suite, and a grading suite at D65 with 5 cd/m2 surround and matte grey walls at ~60% reflectance to ISO 3664 / ITU-R BT.2035.

**Why.** If the lot cannot show a reliable image, every review decision moves abroad.

**Here.** Free cooling for the machine room is nearly free; grid capacity is the only limit on how big the render and storage tier can be.

#### On-site childcare (leikskoli) or a funded place scheme

Either an on-lot preschool for 40, or funded and reserved places in Mosfellsbaer, plus a guaranteed-hours policy that matches shoot days.

**Why.** Without it the lot cannot recruit from the local pool at the hours the work demands.

**Here.** Iceland has very high female labour-force participation and a strong municipal childcare norm. With a small labour pool this is a recruitment instrument, not a benefit.

#### On-lot training school and apprenticeship programme

400 m2 training workshop with 12 benches and a classroom, running scenic carpentry, scenic art, plaster, metalwork and prop-making apprenticeships in partnership with Taekniskolinn (the Technical College of Iceland) and Listahaskoli Islands (Iceland University of the Arts).

**Why.** Without a pipeline the mill, paint shop and plaster shop cannot be staffed at all.

**Here.** Arguably the single most important item in this whole domain for this site. The labour pool is small; a lot of this scale needs 200+ skilled trades who do not currently exist in Iceland in that number. You cannot import them all, so you build them.

#### Crew accommodation and shuttle

Est. 120-room crew accommodation on or adjacent to the lot, plus a scheduled shuttle from Reykjavik (~15 km, est. 20–25 min) timed to call times and wrap.

**Why.** Imported crew with nowhere to live and no way to get in at 5 a.m. do not turn up.

**Here.** Mosfellsbaer housing stock is limited and Reykjavik summer hotel rates are punishing. The shuttle also removes 200 cars from an iced road at 5 a.m.

### What gets got wrong

- Plumbing geothermal district hot water directly to costume laundry, dye vats, hair backwash, showers or anything food-related. The H2S smell transfers into fabric and hair, tarnishes silver and copper trim instantly, and shifts dyes and colour-treated hair. Always indirect: cold potable water heated through a plate heat exchanger against the geothermal loop. This is the most-missed Icelandic detail in a wardrobe or hair department.
- Assuming the electrical connection is available. The grid is at capacity and supply has been refused to new industrial load. Get the Landsnet/Veitur connection agreement before the architect starts, push every thermal load onto geothermal (heating, hot water, drying, curing, snow melt, laundry, kitchen), and add battery peak-shaving so the contracted capacity is lower.
- Specifying an outdoor backlot, an outdoor unit base or outdoor ageing bays to a Californian template. Telehandler and crane makers typically stop large-area lifts around 9-12.5 m/s and Mosfellsbaer exceeds that routinely. Scenic facades in plywood and plaster do not survive an Icelandic winter.
- Not enclosing the mill-to-stage scenery route. A 6 x 3 m flat is an 18 m2 sail. Without a 12 m x 9 m heated enclosed scene dock corridor you cannot move scenery on a large fraction of days.
- Forgetting 120 V / 60 Hz. Iceland is 230 V 50 Hz Schuko TN-C-S. US productions arrive with 120 V 60 Hz practicals, HMU tools, airbrush compressors and prop motors. Provide a frequency-converted supply to props, practicals and every makeup position.
- Ordering timber, expendables and consumables on a UK or US lead time. Everything arrives by sea via Reykjavik on a 7-10 day cycle. Hold 8-12 weeks of timber and 3 months of expendables, not 3 days, and size the stores building accordingly.
- Ignoring the light. Blackout to >=99.9% occlusion in every sleeping and rest space is mandatory for June, when the sun sets near midnight and rises near 03:00. Bright-light provision at 10,000 lux and a written SAD policy are mandatory for December, when the Reykjavik area gets about 4 hours 7 minutes of daylight.
- Recirculating hardwood-dust-laden air to recover heat. Heat is nearly free here, so there is no economic argument, and hardwood dust is an IARC Group 1 carcinogen with an EU binding limit of 2 mg/m3 inhalable since 17 January 2023. Run once-through and take the geothermal makeup-air hit.
- Treating volcanic ash as an HVAC-only problem. Costume stores, prop stores, camera stores and makeup rooms need positive pressure and slam-shut outside-air dampers with a recirculation mode, or one ash event contaminates them permanently.
- Sizing dining, locker and rest spaces to a temperate-climate standard. Crew arrive soaked. A 40 m2 geothermally heated drying room per building, sized for 100 sets of waterproofs and boots, is essential and appears on almost no British or American spec.
- Discharging gypsum or pigment to the municipal sewer. Plaster sets in drains and destroys them; Veitur will not accept pigment load. Multi-chamber settlement traps and pH correction, with the trade-effluent consent agreed before construction.
- Planning to bring animals in. MAST's rules are among the strictest in the world, and an Icelandic horse that leaves the country can never return - the population is closed. A production must use Icelandic horses and cannot import its own.
- Specifying a gas kitchen. Iceland has no piped natural gas network; the alternative is bottled LPG. Go all-electric induction, which also roughly halves the canopy extract rate.
- Under-anchoring anything outdoors. Trailers, ISO containers, the chip silo, the gas cage and the pyro magazine all need calculated ground anchorage, not chocks. A 14 m trailer presents about 40 m2 of side area, roughly 40 kN of lateral load at 1 kPa.
- Assuming a British-sized labour pool. There are not 200 skilled scenic trades in Iceland waiting to be hired. Build the training school and the apprenticeship programme with Taekniskolinn and Listahaskoli Islands as part of the capital project, provide crew accommodation and a Reykjavik shuttle, and run safety documentation in Icelandic, English and Polish.
- Treating hazardous waste as a disposal line item. Solvent, paint sludge, MEKP and most chemical waste must be exported for treatment under the Basel Convention and EEA rules. Design toward water-based systems and on-site solvent recovery.
- Buying a downdraught scenic spray booth without doing the arithmetic. A 12 x 8 m working plan at 0.3 m/s is 96 m3/s, about 345,000 m3/h. Geothermal heat is what makes it affordable here; fan electricity is what it costs you, and electricity is the constrained resource.
- Timber moisture. Kiln-dried imported stock at 8% MC reaches 16-18% in Icelandic maritime air. Without a conditioned store at 45-55% RH every flat built from it twists after paint.
- Asphalt hardstanding. Freeze-thaw destroys it. Use air-entrained concrete at 4-6% with frost-resistant aggregate, with the snow-melt loops cast in.
- Designating an outdoor muster point. In a red-warning storm the muster is more dangerous than the incident. Designate heated indoor refuges and write the decision rule down in advance.
- Sharing a slab, a foundation or a duct route between the mill and a stage. A four-sided moulder transmits through structure and ground into a live stage hundreds of metres away.
- Forgetting the diisocyanate training requirement. Users of 2-pack paints and adhesives have needed documented training under the REACH restriction since 24 August 2023, and it applies across the EEA.
- Ignoring H2S as a confined-space hazard on the lot's own plant. Every district-heat plant room, pit, sump and valve chamber is a potential H2S space. Fixed and portable detection is an Iceland-specific requirement no standard studio spec includes.
- Sizing the building before settling working hours. Iceland's norm moved to 35-36 hours at full pay for most union members; a film unit's long-day culture collides with it directly, and the answer changes headcount per shift and therefore every welfare space.

---

## 11. Fire, safety, compliance and operations

*Askja (6,000 m2 / 180,000 m3), Lon tank stage, Stages 3+4 interconnect; Sugar Land Studios, Mosfellsbaer, Iceland. VERIFICATION NOTE: WebSearch budget was exhausted this session; primary-source WebFetch was used where reachable (island.is Byggingarreglugerd 112/2012, shs.is, uos.is, island.is/Vinnueftirlitid). Everything not verified is marked ESTIMATE.*

### Essential

#### Performance-based fire engineering strategy (brunahonnun)

Byggingarreglugerd nr. 112/2012, 9. hluti, kafli 9.2 permits fire-engineered design in place of prescriptive rules. Framework: ISO 23932-1:2018, PD 7974-0:2019. Signed by a registered brunahonnudur. Deliverable is a brunahonnunarskyrsla issued to the building official and the fire service.

**Why.** A 180,000 m3 single volume 30 m tall breaches almost every prescriptive rule in Part 9. Without an approved engineered case there is no building permit and no occupancy.

**Here.** HMS (Husnaedis- og mannvirkjastofnun) is the national building authority; the Mosfellsbaer byggingarfulltrui issues the byggingarleyfi; SHS is statutory consultee under Log um brunavarnir nr. 75/2000.

#### Design fire scenario set and heat release rate basis

Soundstage design fires commonly 5-20 MW peak: set build, flats, soft goods, cable, foam. Growth 'fast' t-squared (alpha 0.047 kW/s2) for scenery, 'ultra-fast' (0.188 kW/s2) for drapes and foam. NFPA 140 is the only standard written specifically for soundstages. ESTIMATE: HRR to be fixed by the fire engineer.

**Why.** Smoke exhaust rate, detector spacing, ASET/RSET and water density all derive from the design fire. An understated HRR produces a strategy that fails in the real event.

#### CFD smoke and egress modelling

FDS 6.x (NIST) for smoke filling and layer height; Pathfinder or STEPS for egress. Tenability per ISO 13571:2012: smoke layer above 2.5 m, visibility >=10 m to illuminated signs, FED <0.3, radiant flux <2.5 kW/m2, air temp <60 degC at head height.

**Why.** In a 30 m volume smoke stratifies rather than descends. Hand calculation over- or under-sizes the exhaust by a factor of two and gives a false ASET.

**Here.** Model the winter case: cold roof, warm stage air, and a stable thermal inversion that stalls a weak plume below the roof.

#### Fire strategy drawings and the fire safety file

Zone plans at every level, compartment lines, escape routes with measured walked distances, detection and suppression layouts, cause-and-effect matrix, smoke control schematic. Maintained as a live document; O&M fire file per the building regulation and handed to SHS.

**Why.** An unmaintained fire strategy is the reason engineered buildings drift out of compliance. It is also the first document an insurer or investigator asks for.

**Here.** Icelandic and English versions. SHS crews will read the Icelandic set on arrival.
