#### Fire risk assessment (ahaettumat) and annual review

Assessment per the Icelandic fire regulations and the employer's duty under Log nr. 46/1980. Reviewed annually and re-issued on any material change: new standing set, tank fill, interconnect opened, pyro work, change of stage use.

**Why.** The stage changes use every few weeks. A static assessment stops describing the building within one production cycle.

**Here.** Vinnueftirlitid (Administration of Occupational Safety and Health) enforces the workplace risk assessment duty; SHS enforces the fire side.

#### Eigid eldvarnaeftirlit (self-inspection fire regime)

Icelandic fire law requires the operator to run and record its own periodic fire inspection alongside the fire service's eldvarnaeftirlit. Checklists, frequency by risk, signed records retained. ESTIMATE: implementing regulation number to be confirmed with SHS.

**Why.** Failure to keep the self-inspection record is the most common enforcement finding against Icelandic commercial premises.

**Here.** Statutory in Iceland and distinct from UK/US practice, where self-inspection is good practice rather than law.

#### Eldvarnafulltrui (fire safety manager) and named deputies

One accountable person plus deputies covering every shift across four stages, the mill, the tank and the backlot. Authority to stop work, to refuse a hot work permit and to cancel a haze inhibit. Trained to a recognised fire safety management standard.

**Why.** Without a named person with stop-work authority, the fire strategy is advisory and the first AD outranks it.

**Here.** Deputy depth is the hard part. Iceland's labour pool is small; cross-train over-generously and expect turnover.

#### Stage-as-compartment strategy

Each stage its own brunaholf. Separating construction REI 120 loadbearing / EI 120 non-loadbearing to EN 13501-2. Classification EI 120 for walls between Askja and adjoining production support; the stage is deliberately isolated from mill, wardrobe, paint and offices.

**Why.** A 6,000 m2 stage is already far beyond any normal compartment size. If fire can reach the mill or the paint store the loss becomes the whole lot.

**Here.** Justified by fire engineering under kafli 9.2 rather than by the prescriptive compartment sizes in kafli 9.6.

#### Fire-rated elephant doors with position monitoring

Main scenery doors typically 12 x 10 m (ESTIMATE for Askja). Fire-rated leaf or an adjacent rated shutter to EI 60/120 with fail-safe closure on alarm; position monitored to the fire panel; personnel escape door in-leaf, outward-opening, EN 1125 panic bar.

**Why.** An open elephant door defeats the compartment and the smoke control at the same time, and is exactly what happens on a load-in day.

**Here.** Wind: the personnel door must be openable single-handed against the design gust. Fit door closers rated for the pressure and consider a wind lobby.

#### Acoustic interconnect wall, Stages 3 and 4

Movable wall rated both acoustically (Rw target set by the acoustician) and for fire (EI 120 or a rated shutter in series). Position-monitored, interlocked to both fire panels, fail-safe closed on alarm in either stage. Combined open area 4,000 m2 is a separate declared compartment case.

**Why.** Open, the two stages are one compartment and the whole compartment analysis, egress plan and exhaust sizing change. Unmonitored, nobody knows which state the building is in.

#### Penetration sealing and fire stopping

Tested systems to EN 1366-3 (penetration seals), EN 1366-4 (linear gaps), EN 1366-2 (dampers). Every cable, pipe, duct and rigging penetration through a compartment line. Third-party installed, photographically recorded, register maintained.

**Why.** A stage envelope gets drilled continuously for cable and rigging. Unrecorded penetrations are how a 120-minute wall becomes a 5-minute wall.

#### Structural fire resistance of the long-span roof

Design to EN 1993-1-2 (steel, fire) and EN 1991-1-2 (actions on structures exposed to fire), with the Icelandic National Annexes. R 60 or R 90 for the primary trusses depending on the engineered case; intumescent or no protection where a fire-engineered temperature analysis permits it.

**Why.** A 100 m span truss losing capacity brings 30 m of steel down on the stage floor and on any firefighters inside.

**Here.** The fire case combines with snow and wind. EN 1990 accidental combinations with Icelandic snow (EN 1991-1-3 NA) and wind (EN 1991-1-4 NA) loads.

#### Reaction-to-fire classification of linings and acoustic treatment

EN 13501-1 Euroclasses. Target A2-s1,d0 for permanent wall and roof linings; acoustic absorbers must not be the fire load. Smoke class s1 and droplet class d0 are the ones that matter in a tall volume, not the A/B letter alone.

**Why.** A stage is lined floor to roof with absorption. If the lining burns or drips, the building's own acoustic treatment becomes the fire.

**Here.** Imported systems must carry CE marking and a Declaration of Performance under Regulation (EU) 305/2011; Icelandic building control will ask for the DoP.

#### Scenery and drape flame retardancy regime

EN 13773 classes for textile drapes, DIN 4102 B1, or NFPA 701 Test 1/Test 2 for US-sourced goods. Certificates per batch, re-treatment after cleaning, site testing by match or field kit. Register of treated goods with expiry dates.

**Why.** Scenery is the fire load. Untreated muslin and foam are what turn a 2 MW fire into a 20 MW one inside 90 seconds.

**Here.** Incoming goods arrive from multiple jurisdictions with mismatched certificates. Set one accepted standard in the stage hire terms and test on arrival.

#### Fire dampers and smoke control dampers

EN 15650 (fire dampers) and EN 12101-8 (smoke control dampers), tested to EN 1366-2 and EN 1366-10. EI 120 (ve ho i<->o S) at compartment lines. Motorised, fail-safe, position-monitored, cycle-tested automatically.

**Why.** HVAC ducts are the most efficient smoke path in the building. A jammed damper defeats an otherwise perfect compartment.

**Here.** Volcanic ash fouls damper blades and actuators. Specify ash-tolerant actuators and increase the auto-cycle frequency after any ashfall.

#### Powered smoke and heat exhaust ventilation (SHEVS)

Fans to EN 12101-3, classification F400 (400 degC for 120 min) for a 30 m stage; F300/60 minimum. Sized from the CFD to hold the layer above 2.5 m for the RSET. Dual power feeds, fire-rated cabling to EN 50200 / IEC 60331.

**Why.** Natural ventilation cannot be relied on in Icelandic wind. Powered exhaust is the only means with a predictable volumetric rate at any wind condition.

**Here.** This is the Icelandic inversion of normal practice: elsewhere natural vents would be the default. Here wind makes them unreliable and powered exhaust becomes the primary system.

#### Make-up (replacement) air provision

Inlet area sized so replacement air velocity at the fire does not exceed about 1.0 m/s (NFPA 92 guidance) to avoid plume disruption. Motorised low-level louvres and elephant doors as engineered inlets, interlocked and monitored.

**Why.** Exhaust without make-up air depressurises the stage, stalls the fans, and can make escape doors impossible to push open.

**Here.** Low-level inlets are acoustic holes and driven-rain holes. Acoustic labyrinth plus a driven-rain louvre to EN 13030 Class A.

#### Aspirating smoke detection (ASD)

EN 54-20 Class A (very high sensitivity) for the stage volume; sampling pipe network at multiple heights with capillary droppers. Multi-stage thresholds: Alert / Action / Fire 1 / Fire 2. Filtered sampling to protect the detector chamber.

**Why.** Early warning in a volume where point detectors are 30 m from the fire. ASD is the only technology that gives useful minutes on a big stage.

**Here.** Volcanic ash and fine construction dust will load ASD filters fast. Hold spare filter cartridges and monitor flow-fault trends as an ash indicator.

#### Manual call points

EN 54-11 Type A (direct operation). Sited on escape routes, at final exits and at each stage exit, travel distance to a call point not exceeding 45 m (BS 5839-1 guidance; 30 m where mobility is restricted). Protective covers on the stage floor to prevent accidental operation.

**Why.** A person who sees the fire is the fastest detector in the building. On a 100 m long stage they must not have to run 50 m to raise it.

#### Haze and atmospherics inhibit protocol ('shoot mode')

Documented, time-limited, auto-expiring inhibit of the optical scatter channel only. CO, IR3, heat and manual call points stay live throughout. Maximum inhibit 60 min before it self-reverts (ESTIMATE). Every activation logged with requester, compensating measures and clearance time.

**Why.** The single most dangerous routine act on a soundstage. An inhibit that does not expire by itself will eventually be left on overnight.

**Here.** Agree the protocol with SHS in writing at handover, not with the duty crew at 23:00.

#### Fire alarm control and indicating equipment

EN 54-2 (CIE) with EN 54-4 power supply and EN 54-13 system compatibility certification. Networked panels, one per stage plus a master at the gate. Fire-resistant network cabling to IEC 60331 / EN 50200 PH120. Battery standby 24 h quiescent plus 30 min alarm.

**Why.** The panel is the building's nervous system and drives every cause-and-effect. A non-EN 54-13 assembly of certified parts is not a certified system.

#### Voice alarm / PAVA system

EN 54-16 (VACIE), EN 54-24 (loudspeakers), EN 54-32 (planning, design, installation, commissioning, service). Speech intelligibility STI-PA >=0.50 (EN 60268-16 Common Intelligibility Scale C) throughout escape routes and the stage floor.

**Why.** Tone sounders in a 180,000 m3 reverberant volume are unintelligible and ambiguous. Spoken, zoned instructions are the only way to run a phased evacuation.

**Here.** Messages in Icelandic and English. The crew mix here is international and the fire service is Icelandic-speaking.

#### Alarm audibility over working noise

Target 65 dB(A) minimum, or 5 dB(A) above any noise persisting more than 30 s (BS 5839-1). A working stage can hold 95-105 dB(A) with wind machines and rain bars, so design to that, plus an automatic work-light and playback kill on general alarm.

**Why.** An alarm nobody can hear over a rain bar is not an alarm. Killing the noise sources is as important as raising the level.

**Here.** Rain bars and wind machines are used more here, not less, because productions come to Iceland for weather. Treat the loud case as normal.

#### Visual alarm devices (VADs)

EN 54-23, coverage categories C (ceiling), W (wall) and O (open). Coverage volume declared by the manufacturer, e.g. C-3-15 = 3 m mounting, 15 m diameter. Required wherever audibility is compromised.

**Why.** Half a stage crew are in ear defenders, IEMs or headsets and will not hear a sounder at any level.

#### Automatic alarm transmission to 112

Monitored transmission path to Neydarlinan (112) and the alarm receiving centre, dual-path (IP plus cellular) per EN 50136. ARC to EN 50518. Verified response time and a documented no-delay path for confirmed fire signals.

**Why.** An unmonitored alarm at 03:00 on an empty lot is a building that burns to the ground before anyone notices.

**Here.** 112 is the single Icelandic emergency number, operated by Neydarlinan ohf. SHS Mosfellsbaer (Skarholabraut 1) is the first-due station.

#### Evacuation drills and records

Minimum two full drills per year, plus a drill within the first week of any major production move-in. Timed to the RSET assumed in the model. Recorded: date, population, clearance time, deficiencies, corrective actions.

**Why.** The RSET in the fire model is a claim. A drill is the only evidence it is true, and it will not be true once a standing set is in.

**Here.** Run one drill in December darkness. Clearance times and assembly-point behaviour are different when it is pitch dark at 15:00 with drifting snow.

#### Roof-level automatic sprinkler system

EN 12845:2015+A1:2019. Hazard class for a stage realistically HHP1-HHP2: 7.5-10.0 mm/min over an assumed maximum area of operation of 260 m2, 90 min duration. Alternative NFPA 13 design. Glass bulb sprinklers, K-factor and temperature rating per the ceiling temperature analysis.

**Why.** NFPA 140 expects automatic sprinkler protection, and insurers will require it. It also protects the roof steel and limits fire spread to the structure.

**Here.** Geothermal district heat makes trace-heating the roof mains almost free, so the system can stay wet where a cold climate would normally force dry pipe and its 60-second water delivery penalty.

#### The 30 m ceiling problem (why ESFR is unavailable)

NFPA 13 lists ESFR sprinklers only for ceilings up to 13.7 m (45 ft) with storage to 12.2 m (40 ft). EN 12845 has no equivalent listing. At 30 m no sprinkler is listed to suppress a floor fire; roof sprinklers give control and structural protection only.

**Why.** Specifiers write ESFR by default and get rejected late. The honest position is that roof sprinklers do not put out a floor fire at 30 m.

#### Fire pumps

EN 12845 clause 10 / NFPA 20. Duty electric plus standby diesel, each capable of full design flow and pressure. Weekly automatic start test. Diesel with 6 h fuel minimum, block heater, twin batteries. Pump room heated and accessible without entering the stage.

**Why.** The mains alone will not deliver HHP densities plus hose streams. No pump means the sprinkler design density is fiction.

**Here.** With Landsvirkjun refusing new industrial load and the grid at capacity, treat the diesel as a primary, not a formality. Winter-grade fuel and block heaters are mandatory, not optional.

#### Sprinkler water storage tank

EN 12845 Annex A / NFPA 22. Full-capacity Type A suction tank. Rough sizing for HHP1 at 7.5 mm/min over 260 m2 plus hose allowance gives order 700-1,000 m3 for 90 min (ESTIMATE; to be calculated). Vortex inhibitor, low-level alarm, automatic refill.

**Why.** Town main capacity in a small municipality cannot be assumed to deliver 400-500 m3/h on demand. Stored water is the only defensible supply.

**Here.** Frost protection on the tank and suction pipework. Geothermal return water is a cheap heat source for tank tempering.

#### Kitchen and commissary wet chemical suppression

EN 16282-7 for fire suppression in commercial kitchen ventilation; nozzles over fryers, ranges and griddles with automatic gas and power shut-off. Grease duct cleaning at a documented frequency.

**Why.** Cooking oil fires are class F and cannot be put out with water or a standard sprinkler. The duct is also the fastest route into the roof void.

#### Seismic restraint of fire protection systems

Sway bracing, flexible couplings at seismic joints and 25 mm (1 in) clearance at penetrations, per NFPA 13 seismic provisions, with design forces from EN 1998-1 and the Icelandic National Annex. Applies to sprinkler mains, standpipes, fire pumps and gas cylinder banks.

**Why.** An unbraced 200 mm main at 30 m failing in an earthquake floods the stage and disables the sprinkler system at the moment it is most needed.

**Here.** The South Iceland Seismic Zone produced two Mw 6.5 events in June 2000 and a further event in 2008. European sprinkler contractors often do not price seismic bracing at all; write it into the specification explicitly.

#### Fire hose reels

EN 671-1 (semi-rigid hose reels), 19, 25 or 33 mm hose up to 30 m, minimum flow per the selected nozzle (commonly 24-36 L/min at 2-4 bar). Sited so every point of the stage floor is within reach of one reel plus nozzle throw. Maintenance to EN 671-3.

**Why.** Gives the stage crew a continuous water supply for a small fire without waiting for the fire service, which is a 10-15 minute ask even from Skarholabraut.

**Here.** Frost protection on any reel in an unheated area or loading bay. Geothermal trace heating is the cheap fix.

#### Wet risers and landing valves

EN 671-2 hose systems with lay-flat hose for fire service use; wet risers charged and pressure-maintained. Landing valves at perms and catwalk levels so crews do not haul hose 30 m up a ladder. Typical 2.5 in / 65 mm instantaneous outlets to the fire service's fitting.

**Why.** At 30 m, a dry riser takes too long to charge and a hauled hose line takes longer still. Wet risers are the difference between a 5 minute and a 20 minute attack.

**Here.** Wind makes external aerial attack unreliable, which pushes almost all firefighting inside. Internal water at height therefore matters more here than in a calm climate. Confirm outlet couplings match SHS equipment exactly.

#### Portable fire extinguishers

EN 3-7:2004+A1:2007. Water/foam 21A 183B for general areas, CO2 for electrical and camera, dry powder for the mill and generators, class F wet chemical for kitchens. Travel distance to an extinguisher not exceeding 30 m for class A risks (BS 5306-8 guidance).

**Why.** Most stage fires start small and are put out by whoever is standing there. The extinguisher has to be within 30 seconds' reach of everywhere.

**Here.** Signage per EN ISO 7010 F001. Bilingual instruction labels; the crew will not all read Icelandic.

#### Fire blankets

EN 1869. Kitchen, wardrobe, hair and make-up, SFX prep and any area with open flame or hot tools. 1.2 x 1.8 m for kitchen; larger person-sized units where costume fire risk exists.

**Why.** Costume and hair fires in the make-up and wardrobe departments are a real and recurring stage injury. A blanket is the only correct first action.

#### Private fire main ring and hydrants

Looped private main to EN 805 / NFPA 24 so any single break can be isolated without losing supply. Pillar hydrants to EN 14384 (or underground to EN 14339). Typical design 1,500-2,000 L/min per hydrant with two hydrants running (ESTIMATE; confirm with SHS).

**Why.** A dead-leg main means one excavator strike disables the whole lot's fire water. A loop with section valves survives it.

**Here.** Frost-proof self-draining hydrants, buried below frost depth, with 2 m marker posts so they are findable under drifted snow. This is standard Nordic practice and it is not optional here.

#### Fire service vehicle access and hardstanding

Access roads to within 18 m of every fire main entry (BS 9999 guidance; confirm the Icelandic figure with SHS), minimum 3.7 m carriageway width, 4.0 m headroom, turning circle for the largest appliance, and hardstanding rated for appliance axle loads.

**Why.** An appliance that cannot reach the building, or sinks into a verge, is a fire engine parked 200 m from the fire.

**Here.** Snow clearance to the fire service route is a named, prioritised duty with a designated person and a plant contract, not a best-effort. Grit and clear before the office car park, every time.

#### Fire service inlets and breeching

Twin or four-way breeching inlets to the wet riser and to the sprinkler system, in a marked, lit, unobstructed and unlocked cabinet at the fire service entry point. Couplings matched to SHS equipment.

**Why.** If the pumps fail, the fire service pumping into the system is the fallback. Mismatched couplings make that impossible.

**Here.** Verify coupling type physically with SHS. Do not assume a UK or US instantaneous fitting is what they carry.

#### Travel distance compliance and the walked route

Byggingarreglugerd 112/2012 gr. 9.5.6 sets maximum escape travel distance by notkunarflokkur: approximately 60 m for flokkar 1 and 2, 40 m for flokkur 3 (assembly), 30 m for flokkar 4 and 5. A stage falls in flokkur 3. VERIFY against the current consolidated text.

**Why.** A 100 x 60 m floor cannot meet 40 m walked in the corners without either more exits or an engineered justification under kafli 9.2.

**Here.** Verified from island.is text of Reg. 112/2012 via model-mediated extraction; the fire engineer must confirm the figures against the consolidated regulation.

#### Protected perimeter escape aisle

A permanently clear aisle of not less than 1,500 mm (ESTIMATE; minimum 1,200 mm per gr. 9.5.8) around the full stage perimeter, marked on the floor, written into the stage hire agreement as a non-negotiable, and policed by the stage manager daily.

**Why.** This is the only way travel distances stay compliant when a standing set can be built in any geometry. Without it the escape plan is re-invented weekly.

#### Exit capacity, door widths and number of exits

Byggingarreglugerd 112/2012 gr. 9.5.8: escape routes minimum 1,200 mm wide, doors in escape routes minimum 775 mm clear. Exit capacity calculated from the design occupant load. VERIFY figures against the consolidated regulation.

**Why.** A 6,000 m2 stage with 300 crew plus 500 background needs exit width calculated from occupancy, not from the minimum door table.

**Here.** Verified from island.is text of Reg. 112/2012 via model-mediated extraction; confirm before design freeze.

#### Panic and emergency exit hardware

EN 1125 push-bar panic devices where the public or unfamiliar occupants are present (background artists, studio tours). EN 179 emergency exit devices (lever or pad) where all occupants are trained. Both CE marked and cycle-rated.

**Why.** A background artist in a crowd will not find a lever handle. EN 1125 is what works when people are pushing.

**Here.** Hardware must be operable against wind pressure on the leaf. Specify devices and closers rated for the Icelandic design gust.

#### Escape from the perms, catwalks and galleries

Two means of escape in different directions from every catwalk level, protected stair or external escape stair, and a documented maximum time to ground. Escape ladders are not a means of escape for the general population.

**Why.** Riggers and lighting crew work at 25-30 m routinely. A single stair from the perms is a single point of failure over a fire below.

**Here.** An external escape stair in Icelandic wind and ice is hazardous in its own right. Enclose it, heat-trace the treads with geothermal water, and light it.

#### Escape from the Lon tank and dive well

Ladders or steps at maximum 15 m spacing around the tank perimeter (ESTIMATE), continuous grab rail, non-slip edge treatment, and a dedicated exit from the 6 m dive well independent of the main tank. Lit to emergency lighting standard when flooded.

**Why.** A person in a wet, cold, 6 m deep well in low light with a costume on has minutes. Exit spacing is the whole safety case.

**Here.** Cold water shock and hypothermia onset are much faster than crews from warmer countries expect, even in a heated tank if the heating fails.

#### Refuge areas and evacuation chairs

Refuges at every protected stair landing above ground, minimum 900 x 1,400 mm clear wheelchair space without obstructing the escape route, two-way emergency voice communication, and an evacuation chair at each refuge.

**Why.** A wheelchair user or an injured person on the gallery has nowhere to go without a refuge. It is also where the fire service will look for them.

#### Emergency voice communication (EVC) system

Type B outstations at refuges and Type A master at the fire service entry point, to BS 5839-9 / EN 54 principles. Monitored lines, battery backed. Disabled refuge alarms, lift car phones and tank-side call points on the same system.

**Why.** A refuge without communication is a cupboard. The occupant must be able to tell someone they are there and hear that help is coming.

#### Final exits, external escape route and assembly points

External routes maintained to the assembly point: lit, surfaced, drained, gritted and snow-cleared. Assembly points sited upwind where possible, at least 1.5 x building height from the facade (ESTIMATE), with shelter and a roll-call station.

**Why.** Getting people out of the building is half the job. Getting 800 people to stand somewhere safe for 40 minutes is the other half.

**Here.** This is where Iceland bites hardest. An unsheltered assembly point in 25 m/s wind, sleet and darkness at minus 5 degC produces casualties of its own. Provide heated shelter or a designated warm refuge building, and plan for a wind direction choice between two assembly points.

#### Personal Emergency Egress Plans (PEEPs)

Individual written plan for any person who cannot use the standard escape route unaided: permanent disability, temporary injury, pregnancy, or a performer in a restrictive costume or prosthetic. Generic plans (GEEPs) for visitors and tour groups.

**Why.** A performer in a full creature suit cannot self-evacuate, cannot hear and cannot see. That is a PEEP, and productions routinely forget it.

#### Emergency escape lighting

EN 1838:2013 and EN 50172:2004. Escape route >=1 lx on the centre line; open area (anti-panic) >=0.5 lx excluding a 0.5 m border; high-risk task >=10 percent of maintained illuminance and never below 15 lx. Uniformity max:min <=40:1. Ra >=40. 50 percent of level in 5 s, 100 percent in 60 s. Duration >=1 h.

**Why.** A 6,000 m2 floor in a blackout with a full set built on it is a maze. Anti-panic illuminance is what stops the crush.

**Here.** Specify 3 h duration rather than 1 h. Grid supply is reliable but the winter dark means a failure has no daylight fallback for most of the working day.

#### High-risk task area emergency lighting

EN 1838 high-risk task lighting at >=10 percent of normal maintained illuminance, minimum 15 lx, response 0.5 s. Applied at the tank edge, the dive well, the perms, the mill machines, the dimmer room and any pyro prep.

**Why.** Someone operating a saw, rigging at 28 m or diving when the lights fail needs enough light to make the operation safe before they leave it.

#### Escape route and safety signage

EN ISO 7010 registered signs: E001/E002 emergency exit, E003 first aid, E010 AED, F001 extinguisher, F002 hose reel, F005 call point. Illuminated or externally lit exit signs per EN 1838. Viewing distance from sign height per the standard.

**Why.** The pictogram is the only element every nationality on a crew reads at speed.

**Here.** Icelandic wording is expected by the authority; add English. Lead with the ISO 7010 graphic so language is never load-bearing.

#### Permit-to-work system

Written permit regime with a single controlling authority (the stage manager or duty safety officer), numbered permits, time limits, named issuer and acceptor, isolation certificates, and a hand-back signature. Covers hot work, height, confined space, electrical, diving, pyro and roof access.

**Why.** Multiple contractors working simultaneously on one stage without a permit system means somebody energises a circuit while somebody else is inside it.

**Here.** Everything structural is imported, which means imported contractors unfamiliar with the site. The permit system is the only thing that makes a transient workforce safe.

#### Hot work permit and fire watch

Permit for all welding, cutting, grinding, brazing and hot cutting. 11 m (35 ft) clearance of combustibles or protected screening, detection inhibit only with compensating watch, extinguisher and charged hose at the work, fire watch during and for 60 minutes after (NFPA 51B), plus a final 3 h check.

**Why.** Hot work is the leading cause of major industrial fires. The post-work watch period is the part everyone cuts, and it is where the fires start.

#### Pyrotechnics and special effects permit

Named licensed pyrotechnician, product inventory with UN classification and net explosive quantity, magazine storage separated by distance from occupied buildings, rehearsal without charge, detection profile change, fire service notification, standby crew with charged lines.

**Why.** Pyro is deliberate fire inside the design fire's building. It must be a controlled, notified, separately assessed event every single time.

**Here.** Explosives import, storage and use are licensed in Iceland through the police and the relevant authority. Build the licence and magazine into the lot's standing infrastructure rather than into each production's schedule, or you will lose shoot days waiting.

#### Naked flame and gas effects permit

Separate permit class for candles, torches, fire gags and propane flame bars. LPG cylinder quantities limited and stored externally in ventilated cages, EN 60079 / ATEX zoning where a flammable atmosphere can form, gas detection and automatic shut-off.

**Why.** Gas flame effects are the commonest source of an out-of-control stage fire because they are treated as set dressing rather than as pyro.

**Here.** Icelandic wind through open elephant doors can move a flame effect metres sideways. Wind speed limits must be written into the effect's permit.

#### Working at height permit and fall protection

Anchors to EN 795 (Types A-E), harnesses EN 361, energy absorbers EN 355, retractable fall arresters EN 360, work positioning EN 358, rescue descenders EN 341, rescue lifting EN 1496. Anchor points designed, proof-loaded and certified as part of the structure, not retro-fitted.

**Why.** The perms at 30 m are a working level used daily. Fall protection designed into the steel is the difference between a safe stage and a fatality.

**Here.** Falls and near-misses rise in the dark season and in wind gusting through open doors. Set a wind speed at which perms work stops (ESTIMATE: 15-20 m/s at the working level) and enforce it.

#### Suspended-worker rescue plan and rope rescue capability

Written rescue plan for every height task, rescue equipment pre-rigged, and a trained rescue team on site whenever anyone is aloft. Competence to NFPA 1006 / NFPA 1670 technical rescue level or a European equivalent. Target retrieval under 15 minutes.

**Why.** Suspension trauma can become life-threatening in 15-30 minutes (ESTIMATE). A rescue plan that starts with calling 112 is not a rescue plan.

**Here.** SHS at Skarholabraut is a municipal fire service, not a dedicated rope team. The Coast Guard helicopter from Reykjavik airport is wind-limited. The capability must be in-house.

#### Confined space permit and entry regime

Applies to the Lon tank when drained, the dive well, sumps, ducts, under-floor trenches and the sprinkler tank. Atmospheric testing before and continuously during entry (O2 19.5-23.5 percent, LEL <10 percent, CO, H2S), forced ventilation, entry controller, top man, retrieval harness and tripod.

**Why.** Drained tanks accumulate CO2 and depleted oxygen at the bottom and give no warning. Confined space deaths are usually the would-be rescuer's.

**Here.** Iceland is volcanically active and CO2 and H2S can accumulate in low-lying enclosed spaces from geological sources, not just from the work. Gas testing is non-negotiable even when the space looks clean.

#### Lock-out / tag-out (LOTO) and energy isolation

EN ISO 14118:2018 (prevention of unexpected start-up, superseding EN 1037), with EN ISO 12100 risk assessment. Personal locks, group lock boxes, isolation register, stored-energy dissipation, and try-out verification before work starts.

**Why.** A hoist, a turntable, a wind machine or a rain pump starting while someone is inside it is the classic stage fatality.

#### LOTO for suspended loads

Secondary mechanical restraint (dead-hang steel, safety bond to EN 17206) applied to any suspended load before its hoist is electrically isolated. Isolation alone de-energises a brake without removing the stored gravitational energy.

**Why.** De-energising a chain hoist under load leaves half a tonne of set on a brake you just turned off. This catches out even experienced riggers.

#### Electrical safety and arc flash

EN 50110-1:2013 operation of electrical installations; arc flash PPE to IEC 61482-2 with arc rating in cal/cm2 from an incident energy study; NFPA 70E as the working practice reference. Installation to HD 60364 / IEC 60364 series.

**Why.** Stage power is large (multi-megawatt services with generator backup). An arc flash at a 4,000 A switchboard is survivable only with rated PPE and a boundary.

**Here.** HMS is the Icelandic electrical safety authority. Installations and periodic inspection fall under its regime, and imported US 120 V equipment on a 230/400 V 50 Hz supply is a standing hazard on an international lot.

#### Rigging, hoists and stage machinery inspection

EN 17206:2020 Entertainment technology - machinery for stages and other production areas. Annual inspection by a competent person, safety functions to a determined Performance Level or SIL, secondary safety bonds, load testing records. DIN 56950-1 and DGUV Vorschrift 17 as supporting references.

**Why.** A grid carrying tens of tonnes over a crew's heads is the highest-consequence system in the building. EN 17206 is the standard that governs it.

**Here.** Vinnueftirlitid registers and periodically inspects lifting appliances in Iceland. Confirm whether stage hoists fall inside that regime or are handled under EN 17206 alone.

#### Lifting accessories and thorough examination

Slings, shackles, steels, spansets and beam clamps examined by a competent person at 6-monthly intervals for equipment lifting persons and 12-monthly otherwise (LOLER convention; confirm the Icelandic interval). Colour-coded tagging, register with unique IDs, quarantine for failed items.

**Why.** A single unmarked, unexamined shackle in a truss bundle is the failure point. The colour code is what makes it visible from the floor.

#### Mobile elevating work platforms (MEWPs)

EN 280:2013+A1:2015. Operators trained and certificated (IPAF or equivalent), harness with restraint lanyard in boom-type platforms, daily pre-use check, thorough examination at 6 months. Rescue plan for a stranded platform.

**Why.** MEWPs are how most stage rigging is reached below 12 m, and entrapment against the grid steel is a recurring fatality mode.

**Here.** Vinnueftirlitid registers work machines and issues operator certificates (vinnuvelarettindi) in Iceland. Foreign IPAF cards may need conversion; check before crew arrive.

#### Scaffolding and temporary works

EN 12811-1 (performance and design), EN 12810 (facade scaffolds), design by a competent person, handover certificate, and inspection before first use, after alteration, after adverse weather, and at intervals not exceeding 7 days.

**Why.** Set-build scaffolding is erected and struck constantly, and the post-weather inspection is the one that gets skipped.

**Here.** 'After adverse weather' is a weekly event here, not an exception. Write wind-triggered re-inspection into the procedure with a named trigger speed.

#### Woodshop and mill dust extraction

LEV to EN 12779 (chip and dust extraction for woodworking), ATEX Directive 2014/34/EU equipment and 1999/92/EC workplace zoning for the dust collector, explosion relief venting to EN 14491, spark detection and extinguishing on the ducting.

**Why.** Wood dust is an explosive atmosphere and the collector is the bomb. Spark detection on the duct is what stops a saw spark reaching it.

#### Paint and spray finishing booth

EN 12215 (spray booths for organic coating materials) and EN 13355 (combined booths). ATEX zoned, interlocked so spraying cannot occur without extraction, EX-rated lighting and fans, solvent vapour monitoring, and filter change records.

**Why.** Solvent vapour plus a non-EX fan motor is a deflagration. It is also the highest chronic exposure risk on the lot.

**Here.** VOC emissions fall under the starfsleyfi. Abatement (carbon or thermal oxidiser) may be a permit condition; check with the health inspectorate before design freeze.

#### Flammable liquid store

External or fire-rated store, EN 14470-1 safety cabinets (Type 90) for internal quantities, bunded to 110 percent of the largest container, ATEX zoned, no ignition sources, and a maximum quantity held inside the stage envelope written into the fire strategy.

**Why.** Paint, thinners, alcohol and fuel accumulate on a stage floor during a build. Limiting the inside quantity is the only way the design fire stays valid.

#### Generator compound and fuel storage

Bunded tanks to 110 percent capacity, overfill prevention and leak detection to EN 13160, separation distance from occupied buildings, fire-rated compound, spill kits, and interstitial monitoring on double-skinned tanks.

**Why.** A fuel spill reaching the surface water system is an environmental prosecution; a fuel fire next to a stage is a total loss.

**Here.** With the grid at capacity and Landsvirkjun refusing new industrial load, on-site generation is likely to be a routine operating mode rather than an emergency one. Size the compound, the bunding and the permit accordingly.

#### First aid provision and AEDs

Trained first aiders per shift sized to the population and risk (ESTIMATE: minimum four on a shooting day across the lot). AEDs at maximum 3 minute round-trip from any working position, signed EN ISO 7010 E010, with monthly checks and pad expiry tracking.

**Why.** Cardiac arrest survival falls roughly 10 percent per minute without defibrillation. On a 6,000 m2 floor with a set built on it, 3 minutes is a short distance.

#### Emergency showers and eyewash

EN 15154-1 (body showers) and EN 15154-2 (eyewash). Minimum flow of the order 60 L/min for a body shower and 6 L/min for eyewash, 15 minute duration, tepid water 15-37 degC, within 10 seconds' travel of the hazard. Weekly flush and record.

**Why.** Paint shop, tank chemical dosing and the mill all carry chemical eye injury risk. Untempered water in Iceland will cause cold shock and the casualty will step out early.

**Here.** Geothermal supply makes tempered water trivially cheap here, but it also means the hot supply can be scalding. Thermostatic mixing to EN 15154 is essential, not optional.

#### Water rescue and lifeguard provision at Lon

Qualified lifeguard or rescue diver on duty whenever the tank is wet and occupied, throw lines, rescue tube, reaching pole, spinal board rated for in-water use, and an underwater-visible emergency stop for wave and current generators.

**Why.** A performer in costume, weighted or restrained, in 6 m of water is a drowning in under a minute, and the crew watching will be looking at a monitor.

#### Commercial diving management for the dive well

Dive supervisor, standby diver, documented dive plan, surface-supplied or redundant gas, comms, and a recompression access plan. Operations under the Icelandic diving regulation (ESTIMATE: Reglugerd um kofun; confirm number and scope with Vinnueftirlitid).

**Why.** Underwater film work in a 6 m well is commercial diving in law, whatever the stunt team's recreational qualifications say.

**Here.** Confirm with Vinnueftirlitid whether film diving is inside the occupational diving regime. Iceland's small diving industry means competent supervisors may need importing and booking well ahead.

#### Cold water and hypothermia management

Heated tank water with a controlled minimum (ESTIMATE: 28-30 degC for extended performer immersion), immersion time limits by temperature, warm-up facilities adjacent to the tank, dry robes, hot drinks, and a named person tracking each performer's time in water.

**Why.** Cold shock and progressive hypothermia degrade judgement and swimming ability long before the performer reports feeling cold.

**Here.** Geothermal heat makes maintaining tank temperature almost free here, which is a genuine Icelandic advantage. The risk is the heating failing, not the cost of running it.

#### Access control system

Card or mobile credential, anti-passback at the perimeter, zone-based rights (lot, stage, post, vault, tank plant), audit trail retained, and integration with HR on/off-boarding so a wrapped crew member's card dies the same day.

**Why.** An access system that does not revoke on wrap is an open door. Content security and life safety both depend on knowing who is inside.

**Here.** Access control must fail-safe (unlock) on fire alarm at every escape door, per the fire strategy. Mag-locks on escape routes need a green break-glass and a fire alarm interface.

#### Guard force and security control room

24/7 manned control room with CCTV, access control, intruder, fire panel repeat and EVC master in one place, plus a documented handover between shifts and an incident log. Guard force trained in fire warden duties and first aid.

**Why.** On a small-labour-pool site the guard force is the out-of-hours emergency response. Train them for that role rather than pretending a separate team exists.

**Here.** With a small labour pool, multi-skilling the security team into fire and first aid roles is not cost-cutting, it is the only way the roster is fillable.

#### Waste segregation at source

Iceland requires source separation. Streams: paper and card, plastic, organic food waste, mixed residual, plus glass, metals, textiles and hazardous. Under Log um medhondlun urgangs nr. 55/2003 as amended; capital region collection and treatment via SORPA bs.

**Why.** Non-compliant mixed skips are rejected at the gate and the lot pays residual rates on everything.

**Here.** Source separation into four or more streams became mandatory across Iceland from 1 January 2023 (VERIFY exact scope). SORPA's GAJA plant at Alfsnes takes the organic fraction for gas and compost.

#### Hazardous waste management

Paint, solvents, adhesives, aerosols, batteries, fluorescent and HID lamps, oils, tank treatment chemicals and contaminated absorbents. Segregated, labelled to CLP Regulation (EC) 1272/2008, stored bunded, consigned to a licensed carrier with transfer documentation.

**Why.** Hazardous waste in a general skip is an environmental offence and will be traced back through the transfer notes.

**Here.** Iceland's limited domestic hazardous waste treatment means some streams are exported under the Basel Convention and Regulation (EU) 2024/1157 on shipments of waste. Lead times are long; plan storage capacity accordingly.

#### Operating permit (starfsleyfi)

Required under Log nr. 7/1998 um hollustuhaetti og mengunarvarnir. Covers emissions to air and water, noise, waste handling and chemical storage. Conditions become binding operating constraints.

**Why.** Operating without or outside the starfsleyfi is an offence and the permit conditions dictate what the paint shop, the generators and the tank may actually do.

**Here.** Issued by the local health and environment inspectorate or by the national body depending on scale. Note the 2025 reorganisation: Umhverfisstofnun and Orkustofnun were dissolved at end-2024 and replaced by Umhverfis- og orkustofnun (UOS). Confirm which body holds the file. The local inspectorate for Mosfellsbaer is Heilbrigdiseftirlit Kjosarsvaedis (ESTIMATE, confirm).

#### Surface water drainage and oil interceptor

Full-retention separator to EN 858-1/-2, Class I (residual hydrocarbon <=5 mg/L) at the vehicle yard, generator compound and fuel area. Automatic closure device, oil level alarm, silt trap, and a documented emptying frequency.

**Why.** Hydrocarbons reaching the storm system is a reportable pollution incident and the source is always traceable to the yard.

**Here.** Sizing must reflect Icelandic rainfall intensity and snowmelt, not a temperate default. Also fit a penstock or shut-off valve so a firewater run-off event can be contained rather than discharged.

#### Tank dewatering discharge consent

Agreed discharge route, rate and quality for the 4,000-5,000 m3 (ESTIMATE) Lon tank volume. Dechlorination or debromination before discharge, holding volume, and a maximum discharge rate that the municipal sewer can accept. Regulated under Log nr. 7/1998 and the sewerage regulation (nr. 798/1999, VERIFY).

**Why.** You cannot empty a film tank into a storm drain, and dumping it at full rate into the foul sewer will surcharge the network and back up into somebody's house.

**Here.** Agree this with the local health inspectorate and the utility (Veitur) before the tank is first filled, not before it is first drained.

#### Tank water treatment and chemical handling

Filtration, UV and controlled halogen dosing. Chemical store bunded and segregated (acid from hypochlorite), COSHH-equivalent assessments, gas detection where chlorine gas is possible, and a chemical spill plan. Water quality tested to a defined standard before performers enter.

**Why.** Mixing incompatible pool chemicals produces chlorine gas. Untested water gives a stage full of performers a gastrointestinal outbreak and shuts the production.

**Here.** Legionella risk in a heated tank fed from a geothermal-warmed supply is real. Set a control scheme and test regime; warm water and aerosol-generating effects are the exact conditions Legionella needs.

#### Volcanic ash response plan

Trigger levels from Vedurstofa Islands (Icelandic Met Office) ash advisories. Actions: close outside air dampers, go to full recirculation, deploy pre-filter banks, seal elephant door gaps, cover the tank, suspend external rigging, and inspect roof drainage for ash loading.

**Why.** Ash blinds filters in hours, abrades every moving seal, and when damp it becomes conductive and corrosive across electrical gear.

**Here.** This is the Icelandic hazard with no equivalent anywhere else in the studio world. Hold a full spare filter set (F7/F9 and pre-filters) permanently on site; you will not be able to buy them during an event.

#### Severe wind operating limits

Written wind speed thresholds with named actions: stop perms work, stop MEWP use, stop crane and telehandler operations, close elephant doors, secure the backlot, suspend external rigging. Anemometer at roof level feeding an alarm to the control room. ESTIMATE thresholds: 15 m/s stop height work, 20 m/s stop lifting, 25 m/s close and secure.

**Why.** An elephant door open in a 35 m/s gust will tear off its track, and a suspended flat becomes a sail.

**Here.** Mosfellsbaer sits in one of the windiest inhabited regions on earth. Wind limits are a daily operating control here, not an annual storm procedure. Build the anemometer feed into the call sheet.

#### Snow load monitoring and roof clearance

Roof snow load design to EN 1991-1-3 with the Icelandic National Annex, including drift and sliding cases. Snow depth or load monitoring on the 100 x 60 m roof with an alarm threshold, and a safe clearance method that does not require people on the roof in wind.

**Why.** Drifted snow behind a parapet or in a valley can multiply the uniform load locally, and a long-span roof has little reserve.

**Here.** Drifting rather than depth is the Icelandic problem: wind redistributes snow into deep local accumulations. Geothermal roof heating is cheap here and worth considering as the primary clearance method.

#### Seismic response and post-event inspection protocol

Design to EN 1998-1 with the Icelandic National Annex (ESTIMATE: reference peak ground acceleration of the order 0.1-0.15 g for the capital region, up to 0.4 g in the South Iceland lowlands; VERIFY with the NA). Post-event procedure: shutdown triggers, structural walkdown, sprinkler and gas leak check, lift and hoist re-certification before re-use.

**Why.** After a felt earthquake somebody must decide whether it is safe to put 300 people back inside. That decision needs a pre-written protocol, not an on-the-day judgement.

**Here.** Two Mw 6.5 events struck the South Iceland Seismic Zone in June 2000. Seismic gas shut-off valves and sway-braced fire mains are the two most commonly omitted items in European-led design.

#### Rain noise on the roof (acoustic, but it is an operations problem)

Rain noise is the single worst acoustic failure mode on a long-span stage. Mass-loaded, damped, multi-layer roof build-up; rain noise tested to EN ISO 140-18 (sound generated by rainfall on building elements). Operationally: a rain-stop protocol and a sound department veto.

**Why.** If the roof drums, the stage cannot record dialogue in driven rain, which in Iceland is a large fraction of the year. The business case fails, not just the take.

**Here.** Iceland's heavy driven rain and sleet make this the defining acoustic risk. It belongs in operations because the fallback is scheduling around weather, which costs real days.

#### Statutory inspection calendar (the master document)

Single register listing every statutory and insurance-required inspection with its standard, frequency, responsible person, last and next date, and the access window required. Reviewed monthly. Drives a work order system, not a spreadsheet somebody remembers.

**Why.** Nothing on this list fails because it was unknown. It fails because the stage was busy and the date slipped twice.

**Here.** Access is the binding constraint: write named inspection windows into the stage hire agreement or productions will defer them indefinitely.

#### Sprinkler and water system inspection, testing and maintenance

EN 12845 clause 20 or NFPA 25. Weekly: pump auto-start test (electric 10 min, diesel 30 min), water supply gauges, valve positions. Monthly and quarterly checks. Annual: full flow test, main drain test, alarm valve test. 3-yearly tank inspection. 10-yearly internal pipe inspection and tank drain-down.

**Why.** A sprinkler system is the only life-safety system that sits unused for decades and must work perfectly once. Only the test regime proves it will.

**Here.** Diesel pump weekly runs matter more in cold: block heater function, battery condition and fuel gelling are the failure modes here.

#### Fire alarm system servicing

BS 5839-1 convention or the Icelandic equivalent: user weekly test of a different call point each week, 6-monthly service by a competent engineer covering a proportion of devices, and an annual service covering 100 percent of devices over the year. Panel event log reviewed monthly.

**Why.** Detector drift, battery decay and failed sounders are invisible until the day they matter. The weekly call point test is what catches a dead transmission path.

**Here.** Review the false-alarm and inhibit log at every service. A rising inhibit count is the leading indicator that the haze protocol is breaking down.

#### Emergency lighting testing

EN 50172 / BS 5266-8: monthly short function test of every luminaire, annual full rated duration test (1 h or 3 h as designed), with results logged. Automatic test systems to EN 62034 satisfy and document both.

**Why.** A 3 h rated battery that holds 20 minutes is worse than none, because the escape plan assumed 3 hours.

**Here.** Schedule the annual duration test in the dark season and treat it as a rehearsal: it is the only time you will see what the lot actually looks like on emergency light in real darkness.

#### Electrical installation periodic inspection

Periodic inspection and testing of the fixed installation to IEC/HD 60364-6, at an interval set by the risk (ESTIMATE: 3 years for a studio lot, annually for temporary and generator distribution). Portable appliance testing for the vast inventory of production equipment.

**Why.** Stage power is repeatedly re-patched, extended and abused by incoming productions. The fixed installation drifts from its as-installed condition fast.

**Here.** HMS is the Icelandic electrical safety authority. Note the hazard of incoming US productions bringing 120 V equipment onto a 230/400 V 50 Hz supply; a documented incoming equipment check should be a standing gate.

#### Lifting, rigging and machinery periodic examination

EN 17206 annual inspection of stage machinery by a competent person; 6-monthly thorough examination of equipment used to lift persons; 12-monthly for other lifting equipment and accessories; MEWP thorough examination at 6 months. Registers with unique asset IDs.

**Why.** The grid, the hoists and the personnel lifts are the systems whose failure kills immediately and without warning.

**Here.** Vinnueftirlitid registers and inspects work machines and issues operator certificates in Iceland; confirm the boundary between its regime and EN 17206 for stage-specific machinery.

#### Fire service statutory inspection (eldvarnaeftirlit)

Periodic inspection by the local fire authority under Log um brunavarnir nr. 75/2000, at a frequency set by risk classification. Findings become enforceable notices with deadlines.

**Why.** This is the enforcement visit. Findings here close stages if unresolved.

**Here.** Conducted by SHS. Build the relationship before the first inspection: involve them in the fire engineering review, the haze protocol and the Premises Information Box so the first visit is a confirmation rather than a discovery.

#### Insurance and underwriter survey

Annual or biennial risk survey by the property insurer's engineer. Recommendations are graded and typically carry compliance deadlines tied to cover. Covers protection systems, housekeeping, hot work control, and business interruption exposure.

**Why.** The insurer's recommendations are commercially binding in a way regulations sometimes are not. A declined recommendation can void cover on the exact exposure it addressed.

**Here.** Underwriters will price Icelandic volcanic, seismic and windstorm exposure explicitly. Documented ash, wind and seismic protocols are worth real premium reduction, so write them to be shown to an underwriter.

#### Incident reporting, investigation and RIDDOR-equivalent notification

Internal reporting of all incidents and near misses, root cause investigation for anything above a threshold, and statutory notification of serious injuries, dangerous occurrences and fatalities to the enforcing authority.

**Why.** Near-miss data is the only leading indicator of a serious injury. A lot that only records injuries is flying blind.

**Here.** Serious workplace accidents are notifiable to Vinnueftirlitid under Log nr. 46/1980. Fires are notifiable to the fire authority. Confirm the current thresholds and timescales with both.

### Expected

#### Smoke stratification design case

Multi-level detection at approx. 8 m, 18 m and roof soffit (ESTIMATE). Plume from a smouldering fire loses buoyancy against a cold ceiling layer and never reaches roof-mounted detectors. Recognised in BS 9999 and NFPA 92 guidance on tall spaces.

**Why.** Roof-only detection in a tall cold-climate volume will miss the incipient fire entirely. Detection delay is the whole ballgame on a stage.

**Here.** A sub-arctic roof surface can sit 20-30 degC below stage air in winter (ESTIMATE). Stratification is a year-round design case in Iceland, not an edge case.

#### Fire and smoke curtains

EN 12101-1 for smoke curtains (D classification, DH for heat), EN 13501-2 / EN 16034 for fire curtains. Used to form smoke reservoirs at roof level and to sub-divide the interconnect and any high-level bridge links. Gravity fail-safe descent on power loss.

**Why.** Smoke reservoirs are what make a 30 m exhaust system work. Without curtains the layer spreads over 6,000 m2 and thins below the detection and exhaust threshold.

#### Natural smoke and heat exhaust ventilators (NSHEV)

EN 12101-2 with the Icelandic classes that matter: WL (wind load) at the top class, SL (snow load) at the top class, T(-25) low temperature, RE 1000 reliability cycles, B300/30 heat. Aerodynamic free area Aa declared, not geometric area.

**Why.** Ventilators that will not open under snow load, or that are torn off in a gust, are worse than none because the strategy assumed them.

**Here.** Two Icelandic failure modes at once: drifted snow prevents opening, and windward placement pressurises rather than vents. Heat-trace vent perimeters with geothermal water and treat NSHEVs as secondary.

#### Smoke reservoirs and screens at roof level

Roof volume sub-divided into reservoirs sized from the CFD, typically bounded by fixed or drop-down screens to EN 12101-1. Reservoir depth chosen so layer temperature stays high enough for the exhaust fans to move mass efficiently.

**Why.** Without reservoirs, smoke over 6,000 m2 cools, loses buoyancy, thins and descends. Exhaust then moves cold air instead of smoke.

**Here.** Cold roof makes the layer cool faster than in a temperate climate, so reservoirs matter more here.

#### Firefighting stair and lobby pressurisation

EN 12101-6 / EN 12101-13: 50 Pa differential with all doors closed, 0.75 m/s through an open door, maximum 100 N door-opening force. Serves the firefighting shaft and any protected stair from the perms level.

**Why.** A firefighter climbing 30 m into a smoke-logged stage needs a clean stair. Without pressurisation the shaft fills and internal firefighting stops.

**Here.** External aerial firefighting is wind-limited here, which pushes more of the tactical load onto internal shafts. Pressurisation becomes more important than it would be elsewhere.

#### Fire service control panel and smoke control override (FSCP)

Single panel at the fire service entry point showing zone status, damper and vent positions, fan run status, sprinkler valve status and lift positions, with manual override of every smoke control device. Mimic diagram in Icelandic.

**Why.** Incident commanders take manual control. If they cannot see and drive the smoke system from the entry point they will fight blind.

**Here.** Interface agreed in advance with SHS (Skogarhlid 14, 105 Reykjavik; tel. 528-3000), whose Mosfellsbaer station is at Skarholabraut 1.

#### Multi-sensor detectors

EN 54-29 (smoke + heat), EN 54-30 (CO + heat), EN 54-31 (smoke + CO + heat). Deployed in stage support areas, corridors and the grid where point detection is viable. Algorithm-switchable between a haze-tolerant and a normal profile.

**Why.** Single-criterion optical detectors in a hazed environment will either false-alarm or be inhibited. Multi-sensor gives a profile that stays live during haze.

#### Optical beam smoke detectors

EN 54-12. Reflective or end-to-end beams across the roof void and at intermediate levels. Obscuration thresholds typically selectable 25 / 35 / 50 percent. Useful for the stratified-layer level where point detection is useless.

**Why.** Covers long horizontal runs cheaply and detects a stratified layer at a chosen height, which point detectors at the roof cannot.

**Here.** Beam alignment drifts with structural movement in high wind and with thermal cycling. Specify auto-aligning heads and monitor drift as a structural indicator.

#### Linear heat detection cable

EN 54-22 (resettable line-type heat detectors). Routed in cable trays, the dimmer room, the tank plant room and along the perms where there are cable bundles. Fixed-temperature and rate-of-rise variants; addressable fibre-optic (DTS) for metre-level location.

**Why.** Cable fires start inside trays where no smoke detector will see them until the tray is well alight.

#### Alarm verification and staged (investigation) response

Two-stage: Alert to staff, then general evacuation after an investigation period, typically 3 minutes with a hard maximum of 5 (BS 5839-1 staff alarm guidance). Investigation period runs only when a trained warden is on site and free to respond.

**Why.** Without a staged response every haze puff evacuates 300 crew, and after the third time nobody evacuates for real.

**Here.** Staffing the investigation role on every shift is the constraint. If it cannot be staffed, the delay must be removed, not assumed.

#### Detector cleaning, drift compensation and sensitivity audit

Addressable analogue detectors with automatic drift compensation and a maintenance-alert threshold. Annual sensitivity print-out from the panel; clean or replace heads that have drifted. ASD filters changed on flow-fault or scheduled, whichever first.

**Why.** A drifted detector either false-alarms constantly or has quietly desensitised itself out of usefulness. The panel knows; somebody has to read it.

**Here.** Ash and construction dust accelerate drift. Expect a shorter cleaning interval than a manufacturer's temperate-climate default.

#### Zoning and phased evacuation plan

Stage-by-stage zoning so a fire in Stage 3 does not evacuate Askja mid-take. Alert in adjacent zones, evacuate in the fire zone, extend on escalation. Cause-and-effect matrix signed off by the fire engineer and SHS.

**Why.** Blanket evacuation of the whole lot for a single-stage event costs a day of shooting and trains everybody to ignore the alarm.

**Here.** The Stages 3/4 interconnect must be treated as one zone whenever the wall is open, and two when closed. The matrix has to read the wall position.

#### Deluge protection over high-risk zones

NFPA 15 / EN 12845 deluge over the paint and foam areas, the SFX and pyro prep bay, and the flammable liquid store. Open sprinklers, design density 10-12.5 mm/min (ESTIMATE), released by the detection system with manual release stations.

**Why.** Concentrated fire load in small areas needs a different answer from the open floor. A foam or solvent fire will outrun any sprinkler density sized for scenery.

#### Gaseous suppression for MER, data and dimmer rooms

EN 15004 / ISO 14520. Inert gas IG-55 or IG-541, or FK-5-1-12. Design concentration and hold time from the standard; room integrity (door fan) test at commissioning and every 12 months. Pressure relief venting sized for the discharge.

**Why.** Water in a machine room destroys the facility's brain. Gaseous suppression with a verified hold time is the only clean answer.

**Here.** Do not specify HFC-227ea: Regulation (EU) 2024/573 phases HFCs down and Iceland is in scope through the EEA. Refills will become scarce and expensive.

#### Wheeled (mobile) extinguishers

EN 1866-1 (powder and foam) and EN 1866-2 (CO2). 50 kg powder or 45 kg CO2 units at the generator compound, fuel store, pyro prep and the mill. Positioned for a two-person deployment.

**Why.** A 6 kg portable will not touch a running fuel fire or a generator fire. Mobile units bridge the gap between portables and the fire service.

#### Firefighting shaft and firefighters' lift

Protected shaft with pressurised lobby, wet riser outlet at each level, firefighters' lift to EN 81-72 with a fire service recall switch, secondary power supply and a minimum rated load (typically 630 kg, 1,100 mm x 1,400 mm car).

**Why.** Carrying a casualty or equipment down 30 m of stair is not a plan. The lift is what makes a high-level rescue survivable.

**Here.** With aerial appliances wind-limited, the internal shaft is often the only route to height. Design it as the primary, not the backup.

#### Premises Information Box and site fire plan

Weatherproof, fire-service-keyed box at the main gate holding: site plan, fire strategy drawings, hazardous materials inventory, tank status, isolation points, contacts, and current standing set layouts. Updated at every production changeover.

**Why.** An incident commander arriving at 03:00 needs to know what is in the tank, where the pyro store is and whether the interconnect is open, in under two minutes.

**Here.** Icelandic-language primary. Keep a Gasmagn/HazMat sheet for any SFX chemicals and any propane for gas effects.

#### Emergency lighting automatic test system

EN 62034 / IEC 62034 automatic test system, addressable, reporting per-luminaire. Monthly function test and annual full-rated-duration test performed and logged automatically per EN 50172. Central battery or self-contained with central monitoring.

**Why.** Manual testing of several thousand luminaires across four stages will not happen reliably. Automatic testing is the only way the logbook is true.

**Here.** Also removes the need to send a technician round a dark, wind-blown lot in January to key-test fittings.

#### On-site medic and treatment room

Dedicated room with examination couch, oxygen, suction, spinal board, vacuum splints, burns dressings and a hypothermia kit. Staffed by a qualified paramedic or nurse on shooting days, with water rescue and diving medical competence when Lon is wet.

**Why.** Ambulance response to Mosfellsbaer is real but not instant, and a stunt injury, a fall or a near-drowning needs intervention in the first minutes.

**Here.** Landspitali's trauma centre at Fossvogur in Reykjavik is roughly 15-20 km away (ESTIMATE). The Coast Guard operates SAR helicopters from Reykjavik airport but they are wind-limited, so ground transport is the reliable path.

#### Occupational health surveillance

Audiometry for crew exposed above the EU Physical Agents (Noise) Directive 2003/10/EC action values (lower 80 dB(A), upper 85 dB(A), limit 87 dB(A)), respiratory surveillance for mill and paint, HAVS for power tool users, skin checks for make-up and paint departments.

**Why.** Noise-induced hearing loss and wood dust sensitisation are the two chronic injuries a studio lot actually produces.

**Here.** Haze and atmospherics exposure: glycol aerosols have occupational exposure debate. Monitor and record, since a studio running haze daily accumulates exposure that a location shoot does not.

#### Occupancy accountability / roll call

Access control feeding a live occupancy count per stage, exportable to a tablet at the assembly point for roll call. Supplemented by department head headcount because badge data is never complete.

**Why.** The incident commander's first question is how many people are inside and are they all out. Badge counts are the only scalable answer for 800 people.

**Here.** Doubly valuable in the dark season, when a visual sweep of a 6,000 m2 stage at 16:00 is a night search.

#### Perimeter security and vehicle mitigation

Perimeter fence, vehicle access control with under-vehicle inspection at the main gate, hostile vehicle mitigation bollards or blockers to IWA 14-1 / PAS 68 at pedestrian-dense points, and a separate goods gate.

**Why.** A studio lot is a target for paparazzi, for content theft and, for high-profile titles, for genuine threats against talent.

**Here.** Fence and gate structures must be designed for the Icelandic wind load (EN 1991-1-4 with the Icelandic National Annex). Standard fence panels will be destroyed in the first winter.

#### CCTV system

EN 62676 series (video surveillance systems for security applications), graded and with defined image quality per task (monitor, detect, observe, recognise, identify). Retention set by policy and justified. Encrypted storage and access logging.

**Why.** Evidential value depends on the image meeting the identify standard at the point it matters, not on having a camera somewhere nearby.

**Here.** GDPR applies in Iceland through the EEA, enforced by Personuvernd (the Icelandic Data Protection Authority). Workplace CCTV needs a lawful basis, a DPIA, signage, and consultation with staff representatives. Do not point cameras at a set without a written agreement with production.

#### Intruder detection system

EN 50131 series, graded (Grade 3 typical for a commercial site with high-value content; Grade 4 for the vault). Environmental class selected for unheated and external areas. Signalling to an EN 50518 alarm receiving centre.

**Why.** Insurers and the MPA content security regime both specify a graded, monitored intruder system, not a domestic alarm.

**Here.** Environmental class matters here. External detectors must tolerate driven rain, blown snow and wind-shaken mounting structures without false-alarming nightly.

#### Content security programme (TPN / MPA)

Assessment against the MPA Content Security Best Practices through the Trusted Partner Network (TPN), covering physical, digital and management security. Blue Shield self-assessment and Gold Shield assessed status. Supported by ISO/IEC 27001:2022 for the information security management system.

**Why.** Major studios will not place a title on a lot without a current TPN status. This is a commercial gate, not a nice-to-have.

**Here.** Iceland's advantage is cheap, low-carbon power for on-site post and storage; the obligation that comes with it is a TPN-grade secure data centre, not a cupboard with a server in it.

#### Key and lock management

Restricted-profile patented key system with legal copy protection, master key hierarchy documented, key cabinet with electronic audit trail, and a documented recovery procedure for lost keys including re-suiting costs held in budget.

**Why.** Mechanical keys are the failure mode nobody audits. One lost grand master compromises the whole lot and costs six figures to re-suite.

#### Set strike and construction waste diversion

Target diversion rate written into the stage hire terms (ESTIMATE: 70-80 percent by mass). Timber, steel, plasterboard, plastics and paint separated at strike. Re-use store for flats, steels and hardware. Waste transfer notes retained.

**Why.** Set strike is the single largest waste stream a studio produces and it arrives in a 48 hour burst. Unless segregation happens at the strike it never happens.

**Here.** Everything structural is imported, which makes re-use disproportionately valuable here. A flat re-used is a flat not shipped 2,000 km. It is also the strongest line in the carbon account.

#### Firewater containment

Capacity to contain the calculated firewater volume (sprinkler design flow plus hose streams for the design duration) so it cannot reach the storm system. Penstock valves, a containment lagoon or the drained tank volume, with a documented activation procedure.

**Why.** Firewater carrying paint, solvents and combustion products is the classic post-fire environmental prosecution, and it happens while everyone is watching the fire.

**Here.** Icelandic surface waters and groundwater are exceptionally sensitive and the regulatory tolerance is correspondingly low. Containment should be automatic on fire alarm, not a manual action during an incident.

#### Noise, light and air emission limits

Boundary noise limits set in the starfsleyfi; obtrusive light managed per CIE 150 / ILP guidance; generator emissions to the relevant NRMM or stationary engine limits. Complaints log and a named contact for neighbours.

**Why.** Night shoots with 24-hour generators, wind machines and lighting balloons next to a 13,700-person town generate complaints that become permit conditions.

**Here.** Two Icelandic specifics: the long summer nights mean night exteriors run in the small hours, and dark-sky value is high because the aurora is a local amenity. Expect stricter light spill conditions than a mainland lot would face.

#### Volcanic gas monitoring (SO2, HF, CO2)

Fixed SO2 monitoring at outside air intakes with automatic damper closure, plus portable detectors for crew working externally. Trigger thresholds aligned to the Icelandic public health advisory levels (VERIFY current values with the health authority).

**Why.** Eruption gas plumes reach the capital region and SO2 at working concentrations causes respiratory injury within minutes for susceptible people.

**Here.** Reykjanes peninsula activity has repeatedly delivered SO2 to the capital region in recent years. This is an operating reality, not a contingency.

#### Civil protection integration (Almannavarnir)

Site emergency plan aligned with the national civil protection framework and the Mosfellsbaer municipal emergency plan. Named liaison, contact tree, and a mutual understanding of the lot's role in a regional event (eruption, severe weather, seismic).

**Why.** In a regional emergency the lot is either a resource or a liability. Pre-agreeing which avoids a 3 a.m. negotiation.

**Here.** A 6,000 m2 heated, geothermally supplied, powered building with catering and welfare is an obvious regional evacuation shelter. That is worth negotiating deliberately rather than having it happen to you.

#### BREEAM or LEED certification

BREEAM International New Construction 2021 (Excellent >=70 percent, Outstanding >=85 percent) or LEED v4.1 BD+C (Platinum >=80 points). Assessor appointed at concept stage; credits for energy, water, materials, waste, health and wellbeing, and land use.

**Why.** Studio tenants and financiers increasingly require a rating, and the credits are only achievable if pursued from concept, not retrofitted.

**Here.** BREEAM is the established system in Iceland, supported by Graenni byggd (the Icelandic Green Building Council). Iceland scores exceptionally on operational energy carbon (geothermal heat, hydro power) and poorly on embodied carbon, because everything structural is shipped in.

#### albert certification for productions

BAFTA albert carbon calculator and certification, applied per production rather than per building. The lot provides the data: metered energy per stage, waste weights by stream, water use, and transport data.

**Why.** Productions arrive already committed to albert or equivalent. A lot that cannot supply per-stage metered data forces them to estimate, and loses the pitch.

**Here.** Iceland's electricity and heat are close to carbon-free at the point of use, so a production shooting here can post a genuinely exceptional albert figure. That is a sales asset and it needs metering to prove it.

### State of the art

#### Carbon monoxide fire detection

EN 54-26 CO fire detectors. CO is the discriminator that solves the haze problem: glycol, glycerol and mineral-oil haze scatter light exactly like smoke but produce no CO. Combustion produces CO. Cross-zone CO with optical scatter before alarming.

**Why.** This is the technical answer to the haze false-alarm problem. Without it the only option is inhibiting detection, which is what gets buildings burned down.

#### IR3 triple-infrared flame detectors

EN 54-10 Class 1. Sense the 4.3 micron CO2 emission band of a flame, with two reference bands to reject blackbody sources. Immune to theatrical haze, and to the tungsten and HMI sources that defeat UV detectors. Typical range 60 m to a 0.1 m2 n-heptane fire (manufacturer-dependent).

**Why.** Flame detection sees a flaming fire in seconds where smoke must travel 30 m to a detector. It is also the sensor that stays live when haze is running.

#### Video smoke and flame detection (VSD/VFD)

Camera-based analytics on the existing high-level CCTV, cross-zoned with CO and IR3. Gives the incident commander an image of the fire, not just a zone number. Not a substitute for EN 54 certified detection; used as a confirmation layer.

**Why.** Turns a two-minute investigation walk into an instant visual confirmation, which is what makes a short alarm-verification delay defensible.

**Here.** Also earns its keep for the dark season, when a duty officer cannot see the far end of the stage on a routine walk.

#### Atmospherics interlock to the fire panel

Hazer and smoke machine power routed through a monitored contactor that reports state to the fire alarm panel via DMX or dry contact. Panel knows when haze is running and switches detector profiles automatically rather than waiting for a person.

**Why.** Removes the human from the loop. The panel's inhibit tracks reality instead of tracking somebody's memory.

#### Tactile and pager alerting for department heads

Vibrating pagers or radio-linked alert to first AD, gaffer, key grip, sound mixer, stunt coordinator and SFX supervisor, triggered by the panel. Redundant to VADs and voice alarm, not a substitute.

**Why.** The people who can actually stop the set and start the evacuation are the ones most likely to be wearing headsets.

#### High-level automatic fire monitors (water cannons)

Oscillating monitors with IR/UV or thermal-imaging targeting, mounted at roof or high perms level. Typical flow 500-2,000 L/min per monitor at 8-10 bar, throw 40-70 m (ESTIMATE, manufacturer-dependent). Auto-target and auto-release with manual override at the FSCP.

**Why.** This is the technology that actually delivers water onto a floor fire from 30 m, and it is what atria and aircraft hangars use for the same reason.

#### Lon water tank as fire water reserve

2,400 m2 tank plus a 20 x 20 m dive well at 6 m holds roughly 4,000-5,000 m3 (ESTIMATE). Dedicated fire suction sump with a protected low-level reserve that production drawdown cannot reach, plus a fire service draft point.

**Why.** Turns a production asset into the largest fire water reserve on the lot at near zero extra cost, and gives the fire service an open-water draft point.

**Here.** Ice cover in winter needs a heated or continuously circulated suction zone. Geothermal heat makes keeping the sump ice-free trivially cheap.

#### Water mist for cable voids and plant rooms

EN 14972-1:2020 with the relevant fire test protocol. High-pressure (typically 70-140 bar) systems using 10-20 percent of sprinkler water volume. Suited to confined, cluttered, cable-dense spaces where a sprinkler cannot wet the seat of the fire.

**Why.** Cable voids and under-floor trenches are exactly the geometry sprinklers cannot reach, and the water damage from a sprinkler there is disproportionate.

**Here.** Very small nozzle orifices are intolerant of particulates. With ash and volcanic dust in the water catchment, specify the full filtration train the manufacturer requires.

#### Lithium-ion battery storage and charging enclosure

Separate rated enclosure outside the stage envelope for camera batteries, e-bikes, EV charging and generator start banks. Thermal-runaway detection (CO plus off-gas sensing), dedicated suppression, gas venting, and a quarantine cage for damaged cells.

**Why.** Li-ion thermal runaway self-oxidises, reignites hours later and produces HF. It defeats normal suppression and must be kept out of the stage entirely.

**Here.** Cold-soaked batteries charged immediately on arrival indoors are a documented runaway trigger. Enforce a warm-up period before charging.

#### Photoluminescent low-location wayfinding

ISO 16069 safety way guidance systems. Floor-level photoluminescent marking on escape routes and stair nosings, charged by the normal lighting. Supplements, does not replace, electrical emergency lighting.

**Why.** Smoke collects at high level first, so signs at head height disappear before floor-level marking does.

**Here.** Photoluminescent material needs a charging light source. In a dark stage with the work lights off for days, verify the charge regime or the marking is dead when needed.

#### CCTV coverage for safety, not only security

Cameras on the tank, dive well, perms, mill and loading areas monitored live during operations, with a dedicated safety monitor position separate from the security wall.

**Why.** On a stage the camera earns its keep watching a diver or a rigger, not watching a gate. That is a different monitoring task and a different operator.

**Here.** In the dark season, low-light and thermal cameras are the only way to supervise external areas of the lot for most of the working day.

#### Drone detection and airspace management

RF detection and direction finding for unauthorised drones over the lot. Own drone operations under Regulation (EU) 2019/947 with Samgongustofa (Icelandic Transport Authority) as the national aviation authority, operator registration and remote pilot competence.

**Why.** A paparazzi drone over an exterior set is the standard content leak, and an uncontrolled drone over a stunt is a safety event.

**Here.** EU drone rules apply in Iceland through the EEA, administered by Samgongustofa. Note that active jamming is generally unlawful; detection and response is the legal option.

#### Management system certifications

ISO 45001:2018 (occupational health and safety), ISO 14001:2015 (environmental), ISO 9001:2015 (quality), ISO 50001:2018 (energy), ISO 22301:2019 (business continuity), ISO/IEC 27001:2022 (information security, supporting TPN).

**Why.** These are what turn a set of good intentions into an audited, evidenced system that an insurer, a studio client and a regulator will all accept.
