**Here.** ISO 22301 has unusual value here: eruption, ashfall, storm closure and seismic events are credible business interruption scenarios that a mainland lot does not have to plan for.

#### Embodied carbon assessment of the imported structure

Whole-life carbon assessment to EN 15978 / RICS methodology, with EPDs (EN 15804) for the imported steel, cladding and acoustic systems. Includes shipping emissions from the fabrication yard to Reykjavik or Grundartangi.

**Why.** For a building with near-zero operational carbon, embodied carbon is the entire footprint. Ignoring it makes the sustainability claim hollow.

**Here.** Iceland's structural imports mean transport is a material share of embodied carbon, which is the opposite of the usual pattern. Low-carbon steel sourcing and design for disassembly are the two levers that actually move the number.

#### Business continuity and stage-loss plan

ISO 22301 aligned. Scenarios: stage fire, ashfall closure, storm closure, seismic event, prolonged grid outage, tank failure. Pre-agreed alternative stage arrangements, production insurance interfaces, and a communications plan for talent and studio clients.

**Why.** Losing a stage mid-production is a contractual catastrophe measured in millions per week. The plan is what converts it into a manageable claim.

**Here.** Iceland stacks credible interruption scenarios that most lots never face. This plan is more valuable here than anywhere else, and it is a selling point to risk-averse studio clients.

### What gets got wrong

- The haze inhibit gets left on. This is the single most common soundstage fire-safety failure worldwide. A first AD calls 'haze up', someone puts the stage into shoot mode, the day wraps at 02:00 and nobody takes it out. The compensating fire watch went home at midnight. Engineer it so the inhibit auto-expires on a timer (60 min max, ESTIMATE) and re-arms itself, rather than relying on a person to reverse it.
- ESFR sprinklers do not exist above 13.7 m (45 ft) ceiling per NFPA 13, and EN 12845 has no equivalent listing at all. Specifiers routinely write 'ESFR' into a 30 m stage because it is the default for warehouses. No manufacturer will certify it and the AHJ will reject it late, after the roof steel is fabricated.
- Roof-level sprinklers at 30 m protect the roof, not the floor. A 2-5 MW floor fire's plume will carry the droplets sideways and evaporate most of them before they land. Treat roof sprinklers as roof/structure protection and exposure control, and put the actual floor-fire answer somewhere else: high-level aimed monitors, fast detection, and a trained on-site response.
- Sound isolation and smoke ventilation are directly opposed. Every smoke vent, every make-up air inlet and every fire damper is a hole in a 65-70 dB NR envelope. If the acoustician and the fire engineer are not in the same room from RIBA Stage 2, one of them will win and the stage will either be noisy or unventable. Acoustic smoke vents with lined plenums exist but cost several times a plain one.
- Icelandic wind inverts the natural-ventilation calculation. A natural smoke vent on the windward roof slope at 40 m/s does not exhaust, it pressurises the stage and drives smoke down and sideways. EN 12101-2 wind-load classification (WL) is a durability rating, not a performance guarantee in wind. Powered EN 12101-3 F400 fans with directional control are the defensible answer here; natural vents alone are not.
- Snow sits on the roof vents. An EN 12101-2 NSHEV has a snow-load class (SL) and will not open under a load beyond it. Icelandic drifting snow on a 100 x 60 m roof will exceed casual assumptions. Either heat-trace the vent perimeter with geothermal water (nearly free here) or accept powered exhaust as the primary means.
- Travel distance is measured as walked, not as flown. The 40 m limit for notkunarflokkur 3 in gr. 9.5.6 of Reg. 112/2012 is a walked route. A standing set on a 100 x 60 m floor can turn a 35 m straight line into an 80 m walk around flats, and it changes every fortnight. This is why a large stage needs an escape strategy that survives arbitrary set geometry: a protected perimeter aisle that production is contractually barred from blocking.
- Production fills the clear floor the fire strategy assumed was clear. The design fire, the exhaust sizing and the egress model all assume something. Write the assumption into the stage hire agreement as a numeric fire load limit (MJ/m2) and a maximum set height, and make the stage manager enforce it, or the engineered case is void from the first shoot day.
- The acoustic interconnect between Stages 3 and 4 is a fire compartment breach that also happens to be a movable wall. It must be rated both ways (EI/REI and Rw) and it must be position-monitored, interlocked to the fire alarm, and fail-safe closed. If it is open, the two 2,000 m2 stages are one 4,000 m2 compartment and the compartment case changes. Most designs get the acoustics right and forget the monitoring.
- Elephant doors are only exits if one person can open them in the design wind. A 12 x 10 m sliding door against a 35 m/s gust needs power assist, and power assist fails in a fire. Provide a personnel escape door within the elephant door leaf, outward-opening, EN 1125 panic hardware, tested against the design wind pressure.
- Alarm audibility fails on a working stage. EN 54 voice alarm design assumes a background you can beat by 10 dB(A). A wind machine, a rain bar and a generator on a 6,000 m2 floor can hold 95-105 dB(A). Add to that half the crew in ear defenders or IEMs, and audible alarm alone is a fiction. You need VADs, a tactile/pager channel to department heads, and a hardwired work-light kill on alarm.
- Tank dewatering is a discharge consent, not a drain. 4,000-5,000 m3 of brominated or chlorinated tank water at Lon cannot go to the storm system, and dumping it in one go will also hydraulically overwhelm the municipal sewer. You need dechlorination, a holding volume and an agreed discharge rate written into the starfsleyfi before the tank is first filled.
- Working in the Lon dive well is commercial diving. Not recreational, not 'the stunt team have PADI cards'. It needs a dive supervisor, a standby diver, a documented dive plan, surface-supplied or fully redundant gas depending on the job, and a chamber-access plan. Underwater filming crews get this wrong more often than any other department.
- LOTO on a hoist with a suspended load is not a padlock. Isolating power to a chain hoist carrying half a tonne of set leaves the load suspended on a brake you have just de-energised. Secondary mechanical restraint (a dead-hang or a safety bond) goes on before the isolation, not after. EN ISO 14118 covers the isolation; it does not cover the energy still stored in the load.
- SHS will not get someone off the perms quickly. A casualty suspended in a harness at 28 m has roughly 15-30 minutes before suspension trauma becomes life-threatening (ESTIMATE, figures vary). The nearest station (Skarholabraut 1, Mosfellsbaer) is a municipal fire service, not a dedicated rope rescue team. You must hold your own NFPA 1006-equivalent rope rescue capability on shift whenever anyone is aloft.
- Volcanic ash blinds filters in hours and is conductive when damp. An ash event does not announce itself in time to buy filters. Hold a full set of F7/F9 spares on site, fit differential-pressure alarms, and have a written trigger to go to full recirculation and shut outside-air dampers. Ash also destroys door seals and track rollers on the elephant doors, and it is abrasive on any wet surface including the tank.
- HFC-227ea is the wrong gaseous agent to specify now. Regulation (EU) 2024/573 (the recast F-Gas Regulation) phases down HFCs, and Iceland is in scope via the EEA. Specify inert gas (IG-55 / IG-541) or FK-5-1-12 for the MER and data rooms instead, or you are designing in a refill problem for the 2030s.
- Blank-firing weapons run into Icelandic firearms law, not film convention. Vopnalog nr. 16/1998 governs, and permits come through Logreglan / Rikislogreglustjori. A US armourer's letter means nothing. Build the armoury, the permit route and the named licence-holder into the lot's standing operations, not into each production's schedule, or you will lose shoot days.
- The fire warden roster is the binding constraint, not the equipment. Iceland's labour pool is small and the lot needs trained wardens on every shift across four stages plus the mill and the tank. Cross-train, over-train, and budget for it; a fire strategy that assumes six trained responders at 03:00 on a Sunday in February is a strategy you cannot staff.
- In December the external escape routes are night routes from about 15:30. Reykjavik's shortest day gives roughly four hours of daylight (sunrise near 11:22, sunset near 15:30). Every external escape route, assembly point and fire service access road needs to be lit, gritted and snow-cleared to a night standard for most of the working day, for four months of the year.
- Seismic bracing of sprinkler mains gets skipped in European-led design. NFPA 13 has an explicit seismic chapter; EN 12845 does not emphasise it, so a European sprinkler contractor may not price sway bracing or flexible couplings at all. In the South Iceland Seismic Zone, unbraced 200 mm mains at 30 m are a post-earthquake flood waiting to happen.
- Geothermal heat solves the frozen-sprinkler problem that would otherwise force you to dry pipe. Elsewhere a cold roof void means a dry or preaction system and the EN 12845 60-second water-delivery penalty. Here, trace-heating the roof main with district hot water is almost free. Take the win: keep the system wet.
- Grid constraint means the diesel fire pump is not a formality. With Landsvirkjun refusing new industrial load, do not assume you can lean on an electric duty pump and a grid that will always be there. Size and fuel the diesel standby pump (6 h minimum run, block heaters, winter-grade fuel) as if it is the primary.
- Signage has to be in Icelandic and the crew often cannot read it. Icelandic law will want Icelandic wording; an international crew will want English; ISO 7010 graphical symbols are the only part everybody reads. Lead with the pictogram, dual-language the text, and never rely on words alone for an exit.
- The statutory inspection calendar drifts because production owns the stage. Annual sprinkler flow tests, alarm servicing and emergency lighting duration tests all need the stage dark and empty. If inspection access is not a written condition of the stage hire with named windows, it will be deferred shoot by shoot until something lapses and the insurer voids cover.
